PREFACE 

The following volume is the revision of a manuscript used for three years by the author as a text in social psychology; the manuscript was used in a mimeographed form. 

The text has been revised completely, the order of the chapters altered, some new material added and the subtitle changed. The purpose of the present volume is to present to the reader the field of personal and social relationships from the point of view of psychology. The more exact point of view and emphasis is set forth in the Introduction which constitutes Chapter I of the text. It is hoped that the work in this form will be of use to: (1) students of social psychology, social ethics and the social or moral applications of education; (2) conference groups such as 

Y.W. or Y.M.C.A. groups interested in a more basic understanding of personal and social relations; (3) reading circles of teachers or others interested in social problems. 

The attempt is to present the material in a way understandable to those who have an elementary knowledge of psychology.

A rather full index and bibliography has been supplied and references and suggestive questions appended to the close of the chapters. Much of the material of the text has been worked out by groups of seminar students under the direction of the writer. To these students both present and past I feel deeply indebted. It is impossible for anyone to express adequate appreciation to the long list of teachers and writers who supply the background for his thinking. I wish to express special gratitude to those seminar students who aided me in the reading of the manuscript and the checking up of references. Especially am I VII

VIII Preface indebted to my colleague Dean Cora Orr and Dr. G. R. 

Johnson of Parsons College. I have received definite help and council from both of these. Toward the revision of the work I have been greatly aided by detailed criticisms received from Dr. L. T. Spencer of the Department of Psychology, Yale University. To these especially and to many other helpful friends I feel deeply indebted for assistance but hold none of them responsible for either the thought of the text or the form of its presentation. 

New Concord, O. 

November 7, 1929.

SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY

SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY The Psychology of Social Attraction and Repulsion

CHAPTER I Introductions: The  of Social Psychology and an Approach to Its Study. 

This chapter aims to introduce the field of social psychology. The reader should keep in mind such questions as these: What is psychology? What is the scope of social psychology? For what purpose do we study social pyschology? How and from what sources should we secure our information for this science? What approach to the subject is used in the present text? 

1. The Nature and Function of Social Psychology. 

It is well at the beginning of any study to attempt to designate its nature and function and to indicate the point of view from which it is to be approached. The attempt is to do this briefly in the introductory chapter. 

1. The Nature of Social Psychology. 

Psychology as a science is comparatively new. Because of this it is still in a period of very active growth and change and hence has not yet taken the more stable form of certain of the older sciences. There is nothing to be regretted about such a condition any more than there is about the growing and hence changing status of the human 38

Social Psychology infant. But such condition of rapid development does make rather difficult a clear statement as to the present status of the science. 

In fact at present there is a real difficulty connected with the defining of the term psychology. Different writers have classified psychology as a science dealing with each of the following fields: the soul, the mind, the self, the conscious or near conscious, and animate behavior. The 

What is the field of student should not conclude hastily social pyschology? that these terms setting forth the main subject matter of psychology are mutually exclusive. Such a conclusion would assume the situation much more conflicting than actually is the case. Most of these statements of subject matter refer definitely to the subjective aspect of the life of man and the higher animals; the study of animate behavior implies a living organism but views it from an objective angle. 

The psychic realm is that of knowing, feeling and striving in contrast to the physical realm with its objects which occupy space and possess such characteristics as length, color, weight, etc. In time it may be ascertained by a more philosophical inquiry that the psychic and physical realms are not as distinct as they now appear to be but the contrast suggested is helpful ordinarily to the beginning student. 

Social psychology applies to the field of human relationships. This tends to eliminate much of the need for emphasis on neural, glandular and muscular behavior which emphasis is necessary in connection with any careful study of the mind of the lower animals. It is possible to stress a distinction between the minds of groups and the interplay of individual minds in social relations. In well organized groups there develops a group consciousness which partakes of certain of the characteristics of the unified individual self. Whether such a group conscious-

ness properly is called a mind or self depends upon the definitions assigned to these terms. It is better to think of the term self or mind as being used in a relatively figurative sense when applied to a social group rather than to an individual. The text following tries to illustrate the principles of group relationships by referring most frequently to the better known groups but intends the principles developed to apply to the relationships of all kinds of groups.

Social psychology bears close relations to the other social sciences such as history, sociology, ethics, political science, anthropology, economics, etc. It is to be taken as a matter of course that all the social sciences deal with many of the same social problems. They all are attempting to lay a scientific foundation upon which can be built a better social order. The difference in any case consists largely in 

How distinguish social a varying emphasis on a particular psychology from the point of view or in the developother social sciences? ment of a selected portion of the whole social field. History for the most part has been political and economic but at present shows a tendency to include a larger range of human problems. Economics lays its emphasis upon scientific statistics. Sociology treats social life from the point of view of objective facts. As its name indicates political science is concerned with the state, its subdivisions and international relations. Ethics as a branch of philosophy treats of the problems of the good in a more general or inclusive way; the extent to which such general principles find concrete applications depends upon the particular text or teacher. If psychology is to be true to its name it must deal with the psychic elements of social problems. It is evident that actual social relations are determined largely by the nature of the being sustaining those relations. The

Social Psychology other social sciences have a right to expect from psychology facts and principles bearing upon the basic nature of human life. Any definite trend of human life is likely to produce a distinct social influence. But we must distinguish between accidental manifestations and the basic or essential nature of human tendencies. Styles of haircut or of dress we expect to change readily but we do not expect a coming generation of college students to develop a distinct antagonism between the sexes. The trends of human life which are the most definitely social are the feeling-striving trends. So in social psychology we may expect to study more about emotion and impulse than about knowing or understanding: the understanding expression of mind is discussed mainly as it relates to the other expressions of psychology. If you were to travel around the world you would find the different peoples having little in common in the way of their thought but possessing many of the same impulses and emotions. Because of this fact and its social significance social psychology finds it necessary to give a great deal of attention to impulses and emotions and to their organization into the larger patterns of the sentiments. Emphasis on these expressions of the psychic implies only their social significance and not that they are to be accounted absolutely superior to expressions of mind in the way of understanding. 

2. The Function of Social Psychology. 

The basic function of social psychology can be determined from its nature; its functioning must be in accord with its basic nature. But for the sake of plainness it is well to try to designate certain functions more in detail. 

Social psychology should contribute a psychological foundation for the other social sciences. There may exist different

forms of foundations for social study. History lays a basis for the social sciences by revealing the facts of previous social happenings. Economics in like manner gives the social sciences its own type of foundation. So psychology

What is the function in contributing its type of foundaof social psychology? tion is not entirely distinctive. But many difficult social problems can be solved only after adequate psychological facts are ascertained. Does a large army and navy further international peace or tend toward international war? This is a psychological problem and to answer it we must know normal trends of group reactions to certain forms of stimuli. Does shaving the head of a social offender, dressing him in horrible striped clothing and shutting him up in a pen tend to make him penitent or does it tend to make him a hardened social offender? The answer calls for a thorough understanding of the psychological problem of the freedom of the will and a knowledge of the place of self-respect as a factor in the form of right-mindedness that is socially effective. Is it the severity or the certainty of punishment that has the greater effect in the prevention of crime? The answer certainly must be based upon the facts of the normal working of the human mind. Will the refusal to grant so many divorces tend toward the bettering of home conditions? Will divorces increase or decrease with the increasing education of women? Are the various groups of strikers interested mainly in the increase of wages or in the will to power and the realization of conditions making self-respect possible? Would the lowering of railroad rates produce a smaller or a larger income? The answer to many such problems can be determined in advance of costly social experimentation only after a clear understanding of the principles of human psychology. 

Psychology is developing such an understanding of

Social Psychology human life and social psychology attempts to apply such understanding to the realm of social relationships. 

Where should the emphasis be laid in social pyschology?

In order to accomplish such a purpose psychology must concern itself with a consideration of the most potent tendencies in human life. There must be a determined effort to realize truth and to follow the leadings of truth regardless of personal benefit, traditional or party theories. Too long have we assumed instinctive tendencies in human life to suit the demands of a particular philosophy, national or economic situation. If slavery is the fashion of the day then we must create a psychological myth to the effect that men are created some of clay and others of iron, silver or gold. If it seems desirable for big business to protect its investments in foreign lands then it is convenient to create in imagination an unchanging fighting impulse which must express itself through the historical channels of war. In the realm of psychology, as in that of all sciences, we must not only respect and trust the truth but we must positively and vigorously espouse the truth and cleave to it. This seeking after an assured psychological basis has been characterized by one critic as spending all the time in getting ready for the journey without actually starting. There are several dangers to be avoided in this connection: spending all the time in packing the bag for the journey; putting into the bag that which is of no use during the journey; putting useful things into the bag but never using them during the journey. The present text attempts to avoid these dangers. 

Any psychological truth probably has some social bearing. 

But the primary demand is that we ascertain what are the major dynamic tendencies of human life. The impulses of human life are many and varied. It can be said with truth that in any particular social issue human motives are

certain to be mixed. In order to ascertain what type of human behavior is likely to predominate it is necessary to determine which of the dynamic tendencies of human life are major and which minor. 

Further, it is necessary that we distinguish between the independent functioning of basic impulses and their functioning when organized into larger patterns or systems. 

The gestalt school of psychology is teaching us to note the significance of the larger over the smaller organizations or patterns. Acquired complex tendencies organizing within their system potent racial elements have important social bearings. The present text emphasises the system of feeling-striving tendencies which is called a conscious complex or sentiment.

II. The Field of Social Pyschology and the Approach of the Present Volume to It. 

The problem of the field of social psychology is somewhat different from the problem of the function of this science. A brief discussion of this problem is pertinent at this point. 

1. The Field of Social Psychology. 

The field of social psychology has not as yet been marked off very definitely. A review of the different books bear- 

What is the field of ing this title indicate this. No social psychology? outstanding writer has as yet succeeded in listing a series of topics which are in the main followed by other writers; a review of books in general or educational psychology shows that these fields are much more definitely outlined than is social psychology. There is value in this situation in that it calls

Social Psychology for more thought on the part of each writer as to what topics should be treated under this heading. The present text is written on the assumption that social psychology should deal with the relationships of both individuals and groups. The individual is related socially to other individuals and to small and large social groups. Further, social relationships are traced between groups both small and large. An introductory consideration is given to the historical and pychological meaning of the principal terms used. This is followed by a consideration of the nature, content and development of the potent acquired complexes. 

Then individual, individual-group and group-group relationships are traced. The final chapter is supplementary and need not be related to the others.

The text centers the discussion of social psychology mainly about social attraction and repulsion. The idea of social attraction is expressed mainly in terms of friendship Can social psychology and that or repulsion in terms of be expressed in terms disgust or hatred. The term of attraction and resentiment is used frequently; its pulsion? meaning is explained in a preliminary way in Chapter II and more fully in chapters following. Emphasis is laid on friendship more than on hatred because of the constructive social qualities involved in it. The type of sentiment termed respect or regard also has a rather prominent place in the discussion. The attempt is made to explain not only the types of sentiments which develop the stable trends of social life but also to analyze the affective and dynamic elements which constitute their make-up. Synthetic charts are presented for both the impulses and emotions. Among other forms of classification, sentiments are classified as being social and antisocial. There are central trends

to sentiments which enables us to speak of them as promoting or inhibiting the welfare of their objects. Emphasis is placed upon these acquired complexes which have harnessed the potency of certain innate impulses because of the distinct social effect of their development. Mere emotion or impulse has but scant influence upon social institutions or the social order unless its potency is stabilized by the definite set of the larger complex. Such stabilization normally is along either social or antisocial lines; hence the emphasis on the attraction or repulsion of sentiments. 

2. A Scientific Approach to the Field of Social 

Psychology. be scientific?

Pyschology formerly was considered a branch of philosophy. Even yet its relation to philosophy and to the physical sciences is not defined clearly in the minds of all. In 

Why must psychology many schools it is closely associated with philosophy. It is quite evident that psychology must be based on ascertained fact rather than deduced from speculative philosophy or manufactured to support social partyism. The most careful and exact inquiry must be made as to the facts and equal care must be exercised in formulating them into principles and applying them to social situations. One statement which the various schools of psychology are in entire agreement upon is that psychology must be a science and hence must proceed with all the carefulness and exactness of any other science and with equal detachment from individual dogmatism or party interests. 

The development of experimental and animal psychology together with the definite attempt to make psychology a

Social Psychology 

Can psychology emphasize the subjective and be scientific? science has tended to shift the emphasis from the subjective to the objective. Hence a type of behavioristic psychology has developed which emphasizes motor expression as the subject matter of psychology. We at least can say concerning this type of psychology that it attempts in a very definite way to be scientific. But some behavorists have even gone to the extent of disregarding consciousness and a few to the point of denying its existence. There are certain dangers involved in this method of attempting to make psychology scientific. It is folly to attempt to be more exact or objective than the nature of the field in which we are working permits. Many distinctions can be stated in a mathematical form which cannot be imagined or distinguished mentally; I may say that .00001 mm is longer than .000001 mm but the assertion is meaningless on the basis of life experience. In like manner probably it does not add to our understanding of reactions to attempt to reduce them from the animate to the mechanical form. 

We understand psychic distinctions better than neural or muscular distinctions. This is true simply because we live in the psychic realm and to a much less extent in the neural and motor and only at second hand in the mechanical. Because of this we are lead to believe that the way to psychological truth is not along the way of exclusive emphasis on the neural, glandular and motor. 

To welcome every detailed contribution which is made through this line of study is very different from saying that this field compasses the realm of psychology. A much more promising attack is to deal in a strictly scientific way with the subjective data of psychology. Both historically and etymologically the term psychology relates to the subjective and conscious. The attempt to make psy-

chology strictly objective is in fact to deny the existence of psychology in the ordinary sense of the term. We believe it better to maintain the original meaning of this term but to utilize every assistance that the physiological or motor approach to it can give. In its final analysis to be scientific means to be careful and exact-to rule out prejudice, dogmatism, partisan interests, to have confidence in the worth of truth and to seek it earnestly. This can be done with the data of the realm of the subjectives as well as with that of the objective. 

Certain positive values should develop from the attempt to deal with social relations in terms of attraction and repulsion. Friendship has been praised by most of the classical writers of the Greek and Roman periods and by many of the more recent writers of classical 

What values should literature. Friendship has been result from the psy. generally recognized as one of the chological study of most desirable of social relationsocial attraction and ships but has not been analyzed repulsion? carefully nor have its laws been stated in psychological terms. It is perfectly obvious that the unity and stability of the home rests upon the lasting qualities of the friendships existing among its constituent members. A little more thought will convince anyone that the relationships which he sustains to his church, political or social party may be expressed in terms of friendships or other forms of sentiments. Also the inter-group realationships such as church to church, state to state or in general group to group may be considered to advantage in terms of social or antisocial sentiments. Many of the difficulties existent in the social order would not exist were it possible to introduce relationships of friendship between the parties involved. We have a 

Negro and a Japanese problem. Why these and not a

Social Psychology 

Chinese, Russian or Italian problem? Is it not evident that we have created certain international and interracial problems and have not developed others where such development is altogether possible? This means that certain conditions cause the development of such problems. So if it is possible to understand the conditions of the development of such relations it may be possible to introduce counteracting forces or even to undo relatively stable tendencies which now are developed. We offer this as an approach to social psychology and in no sense are asserting that it is superior to various other approaches which have been or could be made. It is suggested as a somewhat different approach to social psychology which seems to have in it elements of value.

CHAPTER II 

A Consideration of the Main Terms Involved in the Text. 

Why should we pay careful attention to terms? What is the meaning of the term "sentiment"? Sublimation? 

Cognitive? Affective? Conative? Neural arc? Neural levels? Neural pattern? What has the term "friendship" meant in history? The term "love"? Do these two terms differ essentially in meaning? With what meaning should the terms "friendship" and "love" be used at the present time?

I. An Introductory Statement Concerning the Value of 

Clear Terminology.

It is fitting at the beginning of this chapter to say a few words concerning terminology. We depend upon words to arouse in the minds of others the ideas which we strive to express. On this account we must be concerned as to whether our words do in fact arouse the ideas which we intend that they should. 

1. Reasons for Confused Terminology in a Democracy.

One of the items of cost in a democracy is a varied use of terms. Where no standardizing agencies exist, such as the 

Why do we find our

French Academy, there is likely to terms used so vari- develop considerable variation in ously? the usage of a term. This is true because each writer reserves the right to pass upon the question of the proper usage of a 45

Social Psychology term. Such a term as instinct has been used by relatively careful writers in a surprising number of ways. (See 15:) This is a situation which must be recognized as a fact and dealt with as such. 

2. Undesirable Effects Resulting from the Use of a Term in Several Senses.

Several types of undesirable effects result from such varied usage of a term. There is likely to develop a lack of understanding among writers and speakers using the term. 

Such misunderstanding gives the impression of disagreement where such may not exist in actuality. There results also an unnecessary amount of discussion concerning terms and this gives the impression to the general public that scientists What results follow are laboring in a realm of confusuch varied sion; there of may in fact be a measterms? ure of unclearness in meaning but the degree of confusion is likely to usage be much less than the varied terminology indicates. Also when a term is used in several senses it makes it very difficult for a reader to determine the extent to which the writers are in agreement. All this may lead to controversy and resultant partyism where such in fact is not necessary. 

Especially is such confusion apt to be disturbing to the minds of younger students. Before a students obtains a complete mastery of an idea and is able to trace it in its varied relationships he is dependent to a large degree upon a symbol used in a special sense. If we state that man has more instincts than any of the lower animals then the truth of the statement depends upon the meaning assigned to the term instinct. If instinct is used to mean a complex act, each element of which is so dominated by germinal determiners as to prevent it from being other than what it is, then it must seem obvious that the human being pos-

sesses very few instincts. But if instinct is used to mean an innate tendency to react to a certain type of stimulus and in the direction of a certain type of end, there being no germinal domination of the means or details of the action, then it can be said that the human being possesses a very large number of instincts. An attempt to enumerate and classify instincts prior to the determination of the sense in which the term is to be used must result only in failure. 

What is true of the term instinct is true as well of any other important term. Hence it is well to inquire definitely as to the meaning of the main terms under consideration. 

3. The Purpose of the Present Inquiry Concerning 

Terms.

The attempt in this chapter is: (1) to state the meaning of certain terms briefly; (2) to give more extended treatment to other terms. The terms that are treated briefly 

What does this chap. are either terms that will receive ter show concerning terms? a fuller treatment in a later section or are terms the meaning of which is comparatively standardized but may not be known to all readers. For the terms treated at greater length we attempt: (1) to show historical and present usages; (2) to indicate the pyschological elements stressed in a certain type of usage; (3) to develop as far as possible clearness and definiteness of usage. Of course, all forms of historical usage cannot be indicated. In the attempt to relate the historical usage of a term to the functions of consciousness, recognition is given to the fact than none of the major tendencies of consciousness ever function entirely apart from the others; striving, understanding and feeling are involved always to some extent in every major expression of consciousness. But the em-

Social Psychology phasis may be prevailing on one or the other of the major tendencies of consciousness.

II. An Introductory Explanation of Certain Terms. 

The main terms treated in this connection are the terms sentiment, sublimation, the terms expressive of the aspects of consciousness and those relating to neural mechanisms and patterns. 

What is a sentiment?

The term sentiment is given a rather extended explanation in Chapter III and needs only a preliminary statement at this time. It implies mainly a steady set of the life toward a particular object and with a particular attitude or motive. It is a complex organization of feelings and impulses but requires understanding of the object toward which it is directed. It is an acquired complex and hence differs from an instinct, disposition or temper; it is acquired in relation to a particular object but all normal human beings have original tendencies making easy the acquirement of such sentiments as love, hate or regard. It differs from the automatic complex, such as the phobia or inferiority complex, in that there is a conscious understanding of its relation to its object and something of the reason for its organization. It differs from a complex emotion such as reverence in that there is no fusion of its elements but any expression of the sentiment is through one of its contained elements. Reverence tends to blend its emotional elements of tenderness, fear, wonder and subjection. Love may express itself through tenderness or fear for its object but not by combining these elements into a fused expression. 

As suggested above, the commonly recognized sentiments are love, hate and regard or respect.

One of the most important terms coming from the school of psycho analysis is the term sublimate. A

What does it mean to basic or racial impulse may become fixated as an expression and thus form a habit, it may take a downward, more primitive or degenerate expression, or it may be directed so as to take an upward, socialsublimate an impulse? ized, moralized expression. When the upward expression of an impulse becomes standardized so as to be comparatively automatic the impulse is said to be sublimated. This is not to be confused with the right regulation of an impulse by self-control. The sublimated impulse responds to a right stimulus or seeks a right expression without selfcontrol hence the sublimation of impulses makes the problem of self-control easier. Two phases of sublimation are recognized. These are the perceptual or afferent and the motor or efferent. For an impulse to undergo perceptual sublimation means that the impulse is aroused by a social or moral form of stimulus. For instance fear is aroused by a common drinking cup or such active social danger; this in contrast to the fear of work or the fear of having a tooth treated which are primitive and socially harmful forms of fear. Motor sublimation means that the impulse when aroused expresses itself to the good of the individual or social group. Fear in the case of a burning dormitory expresses itself not in fainting and hysteria but in energetic action which tends to save property and life. The desirable situation is that an impulse be sublimated on both its perceptual inlet and its motor outlet. 

We find some students who do not have clearly in mind the terms relating to the main aspects of consciousness; 

What are the major those who deal with psychology expressions of cononly in behavioristic language will sciousness? perhaps tolerate a brief historic interpretation. We use the term cognitive to refer to understanding or comprehending

Social Psychology meaning. This includes the ability to perceive, conceive, reason or judge; the mental operation may involve induction, deduction, abstraction, and be called cognitive. The term is a general one covering all or any of these expressions of knowing or understanding. The term affective is used in a general way to refer to the ability of the conscious mind to feel. This refers mainly to sensation and emotion but may refer to mood, temper, intuition, disposition, in as far as these express feeling. We do not use the term in its more narrow sense of the agreeable and disagreeable or the feeling of attraction and repulsion. Rather we use it in its most general sense including this and the other forms of feeling. Conation or the conative aspect of consciousness refers to the ability of mind to strive or to express itself in a dynamic way. This involves all expressions of impulse whether neural or mental and the experssions of self along lines of decision or volition. It may refer also to the dynamic expression of a sentiment or other form of complex. We believe the student does well to master the general terms cognitive, affective and conative. 

The younger reader may be somewhat confused at first by certain neural terms. These have come from physiological psychology into rather general psychological usage. 

The neuron is the structural unit of the nervous system and is a nerve cell made up of a cell body and extended fibers called axon and dendrite(s). The unit of function of the nervous system is the reflex arc which is made of a sensory, What do these neural a connecting and a motor neuron which arc joins a sense end-organ terms mean? (such as a point on the skin) to a nerve center (a group of cell bodies and synapses perhaps within the spinal cord) and hence to a muscle (perhaps a muscle in the wrist or arm which by contraction will jerk the finger away from a hot surface). Nerve centers are

made up mainly of cell bodies with their dendrites and connecting synapses and have the power to control (facilitate, inhibit, direct) the neural current. These nerve centers in the brain and spinal cord are arranged in a hierarchy of levels; beginning with the upper and forward part of the brain the higher centers have an influence (which they exercise with more or less constancy and completeness) over the centers below them. Generally three levels are recognized: the lower level of centers either in the spinal cord or at least not above it; centers on the level of the brain stem (including that portion of the cerebral cortex which extends below the top of the brain stem); centers in the cerebral cortex (the gray matter which constitutes the outside of the brain) which is above the level of the top of the brain stem. The lower level of the centers is apt to function without or with a minimum of consciousness. Affective consciousness seems to accompany mainly the second level of centers and the higher forms of cognitive and conative consciousness (conscience and volition) seem to accompany the functioning of the higher level of centers. Neural arcs become connected to each other and thus form complex neural patterns sometimes called "neurograms" or simply neural complexes or patterns. The student should keep in mind that the new school of Gestalt* psychology uses its term "Gestalt" to cover a much wider form of pattern than the neural. We are not attempting to explain the meaning of this word in this connection but to prevent an identification of the idea of the neural pattern with it. In so far as the term complex or pattern is used in the text we believe its context will make its meaning plain. * This German word is translated by form, pattern, configuration or some synonymous word.

Social Psychology III. An Historical and Psychological Consideration of the Terms Friendship and Love. 

We have reserved the terms friendship and love for fuller consideration in this chapter. We quote many of the historical usages of these terms and attempt at the same time to classify them under the main aspects of consciousness. At the close of the historical-psychological presentation a statement is made as to the meaning of these terms. 

The way in which the literature has mingled these terms together makes it practically impossible to separate them during their investigation; their differentiation is attempted at the close of the presentation. 

1. Historical Emphasis on the Conative Elements of Friendly Alignments. 

The conative emphasis is differentiated as it relates: (1) to the place of the sex impulse in the friendly alignment; (2) to the place of self-assertion in the form of loyalty as a factor in friendship. 

(1) The Place of the Sex Impulse in Friendly Alignments. 

Distinctions made upon a quantitative basis are susceptible only of such rather crude designations as much and little. First we mention such terminology as seems to place much emphasis on the sex factor. 

A. The Tendency to Place Much Emphasis on Sex in 

Relation to Love. 

Several references follow which imply that sex is a basic element in certain types of love. Plato says, "Men whose bodies only are creative, betake themselves to women 

How have writers associated sex with love? and beget children-this is the character of their love; their offspring as they hope will preserve their memory and give them the blessedness and

immortality which they desire in the future". (B106:339).

Edward Carpenter says, "Sex is the allegory of love in the physical world. It is from this fact that it derives its immense power". (26:26. Gray says, "From the very first love expresses itself as a reaching after intimacy—And then- -at some point of its growth love summons passion into life". (55:33-34). Gulick writes, "There is nothing more important about sex and marriage than its relation to the developement of love. There are those that say that, because sex is primarily a reproductive matter, sex and sex activity should occur only with the intention and expectation of producing children. The rest of us say that there are two uses of sex-to produce children and to develop love and character". (B60:92). Freud contends that, "The neucleus of what we mean by love naturally consists-in sexual love with sexual union as its aim. In its origin, function, and relation to sexual love, the 'Eros' of the philosopher Plato coincides exactly with the love force, the libido, of psychoanalysis,—and when the apostle Paul, in his famous epistle to the Corinthians, prizes love above all else, he certainly understands it in the same 'wider' sense". (B49:37-39). Gulick asserts that sex has an important relation not only to love between men and women but also to "love between parents and children, and love between comrades-three fundamental kinds of love". (B60:92).

Most of the writers mentioned stress the importance of socializing or sublimating the sex impulse in order to

Also Gray says, "Mere lover's love is a selfish thing.-But from the point where they bend together over a baby's cradle they take a step up in life, and their love becomes a call to service, whereby its selfishness is purged away". (55:36-37).

Freud observes that, "Anyone who considers sex as something mortifying and humiliating to human nature is at liberty to make use of the more genteel expression 'Eros' and 'erotic'.-I cannot see any merit in being ashamed of sex; the Greek word 'Eros', which is so often the affront, is in the end nothing more than a translation of our word 

Liebe (love); and finally, he who knows how to wait need make no concessions". (B49:39-40). Further, Trumbull says, "A true friendship between a husband and a wife may precede the love which led to their marriage union, or, again, it may follow that love as the choicest of its incidental results; but whether it come earlier or later than mere wedded love as such, there, as everywhere, the love which is friendship transcends all other loves". (B133:113).

It is necessary always for the reader to consider carefully what a writer means by the term sex. It is very evident that many writers who speak much of sex do not intend to convey to their readers any primitive or crude conceptions. 

The sex impulse tends towards creativeness in beauty but is in no sense limited to merely physical beauty or form. It rises as Plato says, "from fair forms to fair actions, and from fair actions to fair notions, until from fair notions he arrives at the notion of absolute beauty, and at last knows what the essence of beauty is". (B106:342). The general impression of these passages is that these writers intend us

to think of sex on sublimated levels. 

B. The Tendency to Condemn or to Disregard Sex or to Shift Emphasis from It.

There are different ways of thinking of friendship on its higher levels. Some seem to conceive the soul or psychic life as existent apart from sex or such primitive impulse while others think of it as involving manifestations of such basis impulses which are expressed

Emerson friendship as detached on sublimated levels. from sex? suggests that there may be friendly relationships between souls. He says, "Every soul is a celestial Venus to every other soul". (41:98). He shows how real love has in it a tendency to separate as well as to unite. "The crude village boy teases the girls about the schoolhouse door;-but to-day he comes running into the entry, and meets one fair child arranging her satchel; he holds her books to help her, and instantly it seems to him as if she removes herself from him infinitely, and was a sacred precinct". (41:100). This psychic separation he says is due to respect for "each other's personality". From quotations given above it is evident that 

Plato intended to say that souls are creative and that there is an ascending order of attachments leading finally to a comprehension of and attachment to absolute beauty. 

Plato says, "love is only birth in beauty, whether of body or soul". (B106:336). It is not of beauty only but, "The love of generation and birth in beauty". (B106:337).

A creative tendency productive of worthy forms of beauty and goodness is assumed by Plato to abide in love, for "all men will necessarily desire immortality together with good, if love is of the everlasting possession of the good". (B106:337). This way of thinking of friendly relationships does not involve the expression of sex in its most crude and

Social Psychology primitive forms. But the exact relation between this psychic attraction and primary sex attraction may be interpreted differently.

Some authors seem to think of Platonic love as a false or abortive form of attraction.
