Besides thinking of pyschic attractiveness as a false or abortive form of sex, it is possible to think of it as a higher expression of sex or as a bond supplementary to that of sex attraction. Hocking, speaking as an interpreter of Plato, says that, "Sex-love, completely understood, has no psychological need of physical relationship nor of marriage; and 

Plato seems to speak in total accord with the voice of early 

Christianity". But Hocking adds, "there are few today who accept the interpretation of Plato as complete. Nor does it seem to me complete, nor equivalent to the purport of Christianity". (A67:364). It is to be noted here that

Hocking interprets the Platonic idea of psychic comradeship as "sex-love, completely understood". In much the same way Gulick seems to think of psychic attractiveness

as a guarantee of the stability of sex attraction. He says that, "the man in love who fails to find intellectual and aesthetic comradeship in the woman has small promise of durable happiness with her". (B60:21). Freud speaks of "the unsexual heavenly love and the sexual, earthly love."

He suggests further that, "the depth to which anyone is in love, as contrasted with his purely sensual desire, may be measured by the size of the share taken by the inhibited instincts of tenderness". (B49:73). It follows that

Freud also thinks of the psychic element in tenderness as being supplementary to the more primitive form of the sex impulse. Such interpretation is more in accord with the original ideal of Plato who thought of physical and psychic creativeness as being kindred tendencies promoting the creation and hence immortality of the good and beautiful. 

What was the attitude of the ascetics toward the sex impulse?

But at times there has developed very distinct opposition to permitting the sex impulse to have a place at all in the relation of friendship or love. The most distinctive condemnation of sex in its more primitive expression developed on the basis of the ascetic type of thought and life. Lecky on the basis of an extended study of the attitude of the monks toward women concludes, "that the cardinal virtue of the religious type was not love, but chastity. And this chastity, which was regarded as the ideal state, was not the purity of an undefiled marriage. It was the absolute suppression of the whole sensual side of our nature". (B83:122). Plotinus in his Enneades concludes, "This, therefore, is the life of the Gods, and of divine and happy men, a liberation from all terrene concerns, a life unaccompanied with human pleasures, and a flight of the alone to the alone". (A110:175). Taylor says that, "Unquestionably in the

Social Psychology monk's eyes passionate love between the sexes was mainly lust. Within the bonds of marriage it was not mortal sin; but the virgin state was the best. Here, as we shall see, life was to claim its own and free its currents". (AA128:354). The general attitude of the monk towards carnal pleasures and hardships is well expressed by Guigo in the following maxim: "As many carnal pleasures as thou spurnest, just so many snares of the devil dost thou escape. As many tribulations-especailly those for truth's sake as thou dost flee, so many salutary remedies thou spurnest". (AA128:405). It is hard for us to realize with what terrible earnestness the monks sought to free themselves from any carnal contact with the female sex even though the females be their own mothers or sisters. The following examples from Lecky are typical of their attitude but not as extreme as other examples he mentions. "The mother of St. Marcus persuaded his abbot to command the saint to go out to her. Placed in a dilemma between the sin of disobedience and the perils of seeing his mother, St. 

Marcus extricated himself by an ingenious device. He went to his mother with his face disguised and his eyes shut. 

The mother did not recognize her son. The son did not see his mother. The sister of St. Prior in like manner induced the abbot of that saint to command him to admit her to his presence. The command was obeyed, but St. 

Prior resolutely kept his eyes shut during the interview". (B83:128-29). Saints refused audiences with their mothers even in cases in which the life of the mother was at stake. 

To the mind of the monk there was a distinct conflict between the creative tendencies of the sex impulse and the submissive tendencies of the self to the divine spirit. 

The monk never thought of the possibility that the religious emotions of hope, faith and belief may have their motivation or dynamic in the despised sex impulse. During the

moral degeneracy which accompanied the fall of Rome the monk saw how terribly destructive sensuality is of spiritual values. The ascetic did not realize that even the harlot has more chance of coming into the Kingdom of God than the speculative idealist who refuses to vitalize his life through the sublimation of its natural dynamic, creative elements which when properly expressed tend to make and keep the life pure and strong.

Asceticism was a dominant force over the entire extent of the Christian world for a thousand years and more. To what extent does its attitude toward love and sex still maintain? McFayden says, "Asceticism, which for centuries dominated the minds if not 

Have we outgrown tude toward sex? fully the ascetic atti- of Christians at least of professionals of the church, still remains a living ideal; to many Christianity is still the pursuit of the empty life. They glory in renunciation, and measure their progress in the Christian race by their success in emptying life of every interest not in the narrow sense spiritual". (92:134-35). But much opposition is developing to the ascetic view. 

Ellis says, "Nowadays the pseudo-chastity that was of value on the simple ground that any kind of continence is of higher spiritual worth than any kind of sexual relationship belongs to the past, except for those who adhere to ancient ascetic creeds". (F39:168): Mathews writes that, "The church, as the representative of the spiritual ideals, has no war with any amusements which are healthful and uninjurious to its own ideals. Religion, we now see clearly, is not ascetic". (A92:210). Peabody points out that, "The supremacy of the religious sentiment was never more manifest than in its power to dictate bodily sacrifices and rejoice in bodily sufferings". But he adds, "Yet the final judgment of history can but conclude that Christian as-

Social Psychology ceticism, with all its heroism, was one of religion's glorious mistakes". (102:168). The main influences of asceticism which still affect our thinkers are to be found in the major premises of their thought; such premises as have not been considered consciously and thus recast in modern terms are apt to be freighted with this prevailing thought of the middle ages. But wherever this issue has been faced squarely in thought the prevailing tendency of our day is to say that the dynamic of the sex impulse can be sublimated and hence given an expression in accord with the best standards of religion. Sex in itself is not an enemy of the higher order of social fellowship or religious love but as a primitive dynamic stands ready to contribute its power and so to vitalize these friendly relationships as to make them potent and lasting. (2). The Place of Self-Assertion in the Sentiments of 

Love or Friendship.

The higher factor of conation considered in our historical review is volition or self-assertion. We are considering the place which the writers on love and friendship tend to assign to this factor. The discussion following centers about the emphasis on moral character and on loyalty. Any emphasis on character and loyalty in connection with friendship assumes the need of volition or self-control in the development and maintenance of the sentiment. 

First, let us note the tendency to demand moral standards on the part of the participants in an individual friendship. Although it is rather hard to be sure when Plato has expressed his final judgment on an issue, he seems to conclude, "that the good only is the friend of the good, and of him only; but that the evil never attains to any real friendship, either with good or evil". (C106:66). The reason for this is, "For the more a bad man has to do with

Is moral character essential to individual friendship? a bad man, and the more nearly he is brought into contact with him, the more he will be likely to hate him, for he injures him, and injurer and injured cannot be friends". (C106:65). 

Aristotle, after pointing out that individual alignments may be for the purpose of either utility or pleasure, concludes, "That then is perfect Friendship which subsists between those who are good and whose similarity consists in their goodness:so the Friendship between these men continues to subsist so long as they are good; and goodness, we know, has in it a principle of permanence". (4:186-87). Epictetus writes that, "the ruling principle of a bad man cannot be trusted, it is insecure, has no certain rude by which it is directed, and is overpowered at different times by different appearances". (A84:200). Seneca also expresses a high evaluation of moral standards. He says, "That friendship where men's affections are cemented by an equal and by a common love of goodness, it is not either hope or fear, or any private interest, that can ever dissolve it: but we carry it with us to our graves, and lay down our lives for it with satisfaction." (A120:184-85). Modern writers also emphasize the essential relationship of moral character to friendship. King says, "For, first of all, I am afraid it needs to be said, in order to have a friendship worthy the name, there must be a vital integrity of spirit, the loving purpose itself, the simple intention in this new relation to be a good frend". (79:22). Griggs insists, "The first law, on which all else rests, is that of moral reality". (58:20).

Miss Condé also put morality in first place. She says, "The first question that every girl needs to ask is this: 'Do my friends hold me to my best?' Or, to be concrete, 'Do my friends ever interfere with my work and the doing of my duty?'". (29:46). Mrs. Spencer says, "Friendship at its

Social Psychology best is a task as well as a gratification". (126:125). 

There is no point on which the writers on friendship agree better than in the contention that moral character is a prerequisite to worthy and permanent friendships. The development and maintenance of such character calls for a large measure of volition or self-control. 

A somewhat different form of the insistence upon selfcontrol as a factor in friendly alignments is found in the emphasis on loyalty in friendships. Gulick stresses

What place should loyalty as an element in friendloyalty have in pership. He says, "Loyalty, then, sonal friendships? we look upon as the primary and most splendid quality of manhood. And without loyalty there is no friendship". (B60:46).

Mrs. Bosanquet says that to bring the relationship of husband and wife to its best there must be "the discovery of a new strength to uphold the other's weakness". (19:258).

If we understand Royce correctly he uses "loyalty to loyalty" in much the same way as supreme devotion to the highest good. We are to assume that, "My fellow wants to be loyal. This is his deepest need. If I am loyal to that need, I therefore truly delight him". (A116:160) "But benevolence, sympathy, what some people love to call altruism,these are mere fragments of goodness, mere aspects of the dutiful life. What is needed is loyalty". (A116:218). But what is the bearing of loyalty upon self-culture and selfcontrol? Royce says, "Much of the art of loyalty, consequently, depends upon training yourself to observe the loyal who are all about you, however remote their cause is from yours, however humble their lives". (A116:287). No one would call the disloyal person a true friend. But to assume the burden of loyalty to the highest good of another person is a sober responsibility with far-reaching demands upon wisdom and self-control. The person loyal in this sense

must control his own impulses and moods so as not to lead his friend into wrong channels of life. He must resist such tendencies on the part of his friend which obviously are unworthy of him and so encourage the expression of the better qualities of his friend. Loyalty in this sense demands sober judgment and rigid self-control. 

To clarify this matter of loyalty somewhat further it is well to attempt to relate it to the impulses of tenderness. 

What relation has Some have assumed tenderness to worthy loyalty to be in its primitive nature altruistic. 

Ribot says, "Tender emotion tenderness? marks an important stage in the evolution of affective life; with it we pass beyond the period of the purely egoistic emotions". (112:234). In another place Ribot identifies the altruistic tendency with the tender emotion. (112:292-93). Norsworthy and Whitley speaking of motherly behavior say, "Among the crude, violent, often selfish natural tendencies, the maternal instinct with its accompanying kindliness and sympathy stands out in strong relief. This group is one of the roots of humaneness; it is the source of altruism, and of the consciousness of the brotherhood of man". (97:59). This quotation implies that the maternal impulse of tenderness will prompt action to the essential good of its object. But we incline to agree with Bernard that such is not of necessity the fact. The instinctive tendencies of human mothers do not always prompt them to act for the good of their children. Bernard says, "We have an abundance of examples of the difficulty encountered in inducing a diseased ignorant mother to wean her child from the breast when its life may depend upon her taking the step. It is also difficult to teach ignorant mothers not to rock their children to sleep, not to jounce them, and not to respond to the petulant or hunger cries of the baby with complex foods suitable only to adults,

Social Psychology or even harmful to the adults themselves". (15:334-35).

The parental impulse expressive of the emotion of tenderness is as crude and primitive in its expressions as that of any of the human innate tendencies. Maternal love in its racial expression is likely to be blind to the hygienic, moral and permanent interests of the child and alive only to its temporary and physical interests. All innate tendencies are equally in need of socialized sublimation. This demand puts a heavy burden upon thoughtfulness and selfcontrol. Faithfulness to the best interests of a friend must be maintained in spite of the present moods, attitudes, or character trends of the friend. We recognize that the 

Prophet Hosea was true to the basic demands of friendship when he went to the slave market and purchased his outcast degenerate wife that she might be saved from utter degeneracy. Does any one imagine that he thus faced individual disgust and social condemnation in accord with the prompting of primitive tenderness? Rather it was a last desperate assertion of self (going the second mile and more) to preserve some little semblance in reality of the implications of his own ideal of what his "other self" and the mother of his children should be. Under trying conditions any friendship may demand the spirit of the martyr to defy impulse and desire and to maintain the principles involved in duty and friendship. Self-control with its assertion of volition in accord with the demands of duty thus stands in the background of friendly relations in order to preserve them from degeneracy and decay; the hope is that in most cases the friendship will develop properly without the necessity of the expression of this type of loyalty. 

2. Historical Emphasis of the Affective Elements of 

Consciousness Involved in Friendly Alignments.

To overlook the feeling element in the sentiment of love

or friendship would be to disregard utterly the testimony of practically all writers. Next we attempt to discover just what emphasis has been placed on the feeling elements of positive social alignments. 

(1). The Stressing of Tenderness or Kindness and of In tuitive Judgments in Friendships.

Although in its primitive form tenderness may prompt only the care for the physical, temporal welfare of its object yet it is capable of sublimation and on any level manifesting great power and producing striking types of sacrifice. Novelists vie with each other to set hard tasks for personal love and then to show how love is sufficient to overcome them. The most primitive personal desires for food, clothing and shelter are disregarded for the sake of love. The right hand, right eye and even physical life itself is sacrificed in the interests of the 

What is the function of tenderness in love? beloved. Constrained by their love of Christ the ascetics were enabled to endure terrible sufferings. They measured the strength of their love by their power to endure. James writes, "Suso then tells how, to emulate the sorrows of his crucified Lord, he made himself a cross with thirty protruding iron needles and nails. This he bore on his bare back on his shoulders day and night". (C73:308).

Various forms of severe pain were endured constantly by this saint for a period of some forty years. In the case of certain of the saints there came to be a positive rejoicing in pain. James says further, "Of the founder of the Sacred 

Heart order, for example, we read that 'Her love of pain and suffering was insatiable' ". (C73:310). The psychoanalytical school of psychology calls our attention to a type of sex perversion, called masochism, in

Social Psychology which the individual craves pain at the hand of his beloved. The attitude of the saints mentioned above give some suggestion of this. The seeking of experiences productive of such extreme pain cannot be explained by ordinary self-control, even though the whole force of the life be given to such effort. We must assume the expression of a powerful, though in many cases misdirected, emotion of tenderness with its kindly and sympathetic impulses. Besides tenderness there are various other feeling elements in the sentiment of love. Drummond interprets Paul's analysis of love into the elements patience, kindness, generosity, humility, courtesy, unselfishness, good temper, guilelessness and sincerity. (37:22). Assuming this a proper interpretation of love at its best it is easy to note the complexity of the feeling elements involved. Tenderness seems to dominate the whole and to give the tone of its motivation to the other impulses and emotional elements involved in the complex.

There is a common recognition that love in a sense is blind to the ordinary defects of its object. Hocking says that this is not ordinary blindness but merely a confidence Are the intuitive judg. of the power of love to recreate its ments of love trust. object. He says, "Love does not worthy? want a perfect object; it wants an object to be made over, fit for its own power of re-creation". (A67:366). According to

Hocking love tends to destroy what it finds and to recreate its object according to its own pattern of beauty. "More than this, such a transformation is what love actually, though subconsciously, and more or less permanently, achieves". (A67:366). The trustworthiness of the selective promptings of this blind or confident love depends largely upon the trustworthiness of the intuitive tendency in general. Emerson asserts that, "Our spontaneous action

is always the best". (41:181). Vedder reminds us that, "The method of Jesus was the intuitional. He relied on the religious intuitions, his own first of all, but next on those of his hearers.- -Jesus never argued. Jesus never proved. Open the gospels where you will, and you will find him simply announcing truth, leaving those who have ears to hear, to hear". (A135:30). Thilly points out how our moral judgments become largely feeling judgments. "The terms, murder, robbery, theft, benevolence, veracity, sacrifice, stand not merely for acts and modes of conduct and dispositions of the will, but for feelings and impulses in reference to them". (129:92). The reliance on intuitive judgments in the realms of mathematics, chemistry or history would be extremely foolish. But we are inclined to recognize worth in intuitive judgments in the aesthetic and moral realms. But even here the character of the individual making the intuition needs to be good; the emotional promptings of the dissipated life are practically sure to be wrong. The feeling judgment, This is more beautiful than that, or, This man is more trustworthy than that, is probably as reliable as its corresponding logical judgment. In the choice of a life mate or friend the racial promptings probably are as reliable as any logical conclusion that the individual possessing the intuitions is likely to formulate. If the individual were wiser and knew more about the laws of heredity, personality and human ontogeny he might form logical judgments relatively much more reliable than his intuitive judgments. Certainly we are not advocating that logical thought be disregarded and that love be blindfolded in addition to being blindly optimistic but merely are saying that in the realm of the racial, aesthetic and personal the intuitive judgments have a distinct right to be heard. Many of the great moral advances of the race-Abraham's revolt against child

Social Psychology sacrifice, Ezekiel's placing of responsibility upon the individual, Lincoln's denunciation of slavery, Jesus's announcements of the higher spiritual laws-have been based in the main on intuitive judgments. (2). The Assertive Nature of the Affective Aspect of 

Friendship in Disregard of Its Immediate Satisfaction.

There are cases in which love with optimistic confidence lays claim to a personal object in entire disregard of the Does love demand rewill of that object. A marked case ciprocity for its satis of this sort is shown in "Love Letfaction? ters of a Portuguese Nun" from which we quote a paragraph. "I refuse to yield credit to all those signs which might convince me that you no longer love, and I feel much more disposed blindly to abandon myself to my passion, than to dwell upon the reasons, which you give me, to complain of your want of attention".-After speaking of his coldness and indifference and condemning him for his early hypocrisy she says, "I see plainly that the least excuse is sufficient for you; and, even without your taking the pains to make any to me, my love serves you so faithfully that I can only consent to think you culpable, that I may enjoy the delightful pleasure of justifying you myself". (A85:46-47).

It is possible to say in such a case that love is seeking and even demanding reciprocity but certainly it must be said that love does not readily take "No" for a final answer.

This condition of affairs may be explained in several ways. 

Love is eternally young and blindly confident in his power to conquer. Love is creative and seeks that which is to be rather than that which at present exists. Love is assertive and claims possession of its object to the disregard

of all other claims; this includes even the claims of selfcontrol and self-direction in those cases in which the object is a person. It is this feeling of power and of creative assertiveness that causes perfect love to cast out all fear and never to fail. But it is quite plain that the total task is not completed and that the friendly situation remains abnormal until the conquest of the will of the object is made and a worthy return in love secured. Love seeks and in fact demands reciprocity but may be content to pursue the quest for a long time before acknowledging defeat. 

3. Historical Emphasis on the Cognitive Elements of 

Consciousness Involved in a Friendship.

In addition to the elements of striving and feeling that are stressed in connection with friendly alignments many authors emphasize the importance of knowing or understanding. This factor may be stressed in several ways. 

We consider (1) understanding the meaning and value of friendship; (2) understanding the essential nature of friend and of self; (3) understanding the necessity of parallel lines of development of the parties to the friendship. (1). Emphasis on the Importance of Comprehending the Meaning and Value of Friendship.

It seems rather plain that if a friendship is to develop to a high plane and remain stable the parties to it must comprehend something of its value. Speaking of wrong systems of evaluation Epictetus says, "And let every man among you who has seriously resolved either to be a friend himself or to have another for his friend, cut out these opinions, hate them, drive them from his soul". (A84:201).

Other writers express the thought that friendship implies

Social Psychology 

1 a considerable degree of likeness in thinking or at least in the major premises of thought which are the stable as- 

Why think about the values and conditions of a friendship? sumptions back of the ordinary 

King says, thought processes. "The third element in the basis of any true friendship is some deep community of interests". (A79:221). Cicero asserts that this community of interests should involve "a perfect conformity of opinions upon all religious and civic subjects, united with the highest degree of mutual esteem and affection". (A27:177). Griggs also claims that, “To make a high friendship possible, there must be a certain identity of personality, a common range of taste, interest and activity, to draw the two lives together in mutual understanding". (58:21). Careful thought is needed so as to avoid a situation likely to spoil the relation of friendship. 

Friendship should be cast upon a high level and must be so cast if it is to remain stable. Thoreau suggests, "It is the merit and preservation of Friendship, that it takes place on a level higher than the actual characters of the parties would seem to warrant". (131:19). It is with a kindred thought in mind that Plutarch says, "We must also beware of speaking too freely to a friend in the company of many people, remembering the well known remark of 

Plato. For when Socrates reproved one of his friends too vehemently in a discussion at table, Plato said, 'Would it not have been better to have said this privately?' Whereupon Socrates replied, 'And you too, sir, would it not have become you to make this remark also privately?'" (107:194). That the ideas presented in this paragraph are not ancient in contrast to modern is indicated by a recent article by Edson suggesting attitudes proper in a young man towards his future life partner. He insists that there should exist "a community of tastes, ideals and standards",

"a willingness to give and take", "a disposition to give fair consideration to her judgment", "a wealth of things to say and do together". (A38:273-75). If friends are to take careful thought as to the conditions under which their friendship will prosper they must have a definite, conscious realization as to the worth of the friendly relationship. 

The valuation of friendship is something apart from the appreciation of a particular friend. We make costly investments only in products we consider to have great worth. 

One prepares to develop and maintain a friendship in an adequate way only if he is thoroughly convinced of the worth of that relationship. (2). Emphasis on the Importance of Understanding the

Essential Nature of Friend and of Self. 

Beside appreciating the worth of friendly relations and understanding how to develop and maintain them there is need to understand thoroughly the object of the friendship. 

Miss Condé says that the reason that some girls were not To what extent need "divinities but temptations" is we think of the nature that, "These girls had lost the fine of a friend? sense of responsibility for helping their friends to be true to the highest standards and were thoughtlessly weakening the powers of moral resistance and self-control in their boy friends". (29:48). This lack of responsibility seems due to a failure to think concerning the real nature of the object of friendship. After a careful inquiry into this subject the 

Platonic Socrates concludes, "Here is a jest; you two boys, and I, an old boy, who would fain be one of you, imagine ourselves to be friends, and we have not as yet been able to discover what is a friend". (C106:79). If as keen an investigator as Socrates had real difficulty in detecting the nature of a friend surely the subject is one worthy of con-

124 

Social Psychology templation. There is much to be said in favor of the contention that the object of a friendship is my own ideal created on the basis of a particular individual. Emerson tends to stress the idealization of the object of the friendship. He says, "The essence of friendship is entireness, a total magnanimity and trust. It must not surmise or provide for infirmity. It treats its object as a god, that it may deify both". (41:125). That the friend is an ideal and that the friendship is idealistic is indicated by this passage from Emerson. "I do, then, with my friends as I do with my books. I would have them where I can find them but 

I seldom use them. We must have society on our own terms, and admit or exclude it on the slightest cause". (41:124). It appears that one must use his mental powers of imagination and abstraction to formulate an idea of his friend. My total content of meaning depends quite as much upon my own contribution as upon the nature of the stimuli I receive from contact with the individual friend; it is quite evident that my mental operations are set going by the sensory stimuli. The more extended my generalization process the more lofty and idealistic does my friend appear to me.

Personal friendship involves an adjustment between two individuals. We have been pointing out the fact that to adjust to a friend one must understand the nature of a 

What does friendship friend. There is need also for an demand in the way of understanding self? understanding of self. If my friend is growing away from me I may so idealize his life as to maintain its adjustments to mine or I may so alter my life as to maintain its adjustments to his. Trumbull says, "Because friendship always includes a reverent admiration of a friend's ideal-the ideal seen in the friend, seen by the friend, or seen for the friend, therefore it follows that

every added indication of that ideal's realizing is added cause for rejoicing on the part of him who loves his friend as the embodiment of that ideal". (B133:39). It is well to keep in mind that it is the ideal suggested by the person and not the actual present attainments of the person that should determine the changes undertaken in self. Thus moral standards constantly must dominate friendly relations as Aristotle asserts, "because good men, being stable in themselves, are also stable as regards others, and neither ask degrading services nor render them, but, so to say, rather prevent them: for it is the part of the good neither to do wrong themselves nor to allow their friends in so doing". (4:196). So one must consent to lose any friend whose life tendencies are divergent from right standards. But if the change in my friend is idealistic I should so study my own life as to maintain a friendly touch with such idealistic tendency of growth. This may demand only gradual development or may call for radical adjustments depending on the situation. But if my friend rises to a new level of idealism I must adjust my life to that advance or lose my friend. Friendship in a real sense can be maintained only by those whose major moral tendencies are on kindred planes of idealism. (3). Emphasis on Understanding the Necessity of

Parallel Lines of Development for Self and Friend. 

We have indicated that friendship implies the development and maintenance of adjustment of the parties to it. 

There remains to be stressed the need of understanding the necessity of the constant adjusting of these parties to each other. Thoreau suggests that, "The Friend asks no return but that his Friend will religiously accept and wear and not disgrace his apotheosis of him. They cherish each

Social Psychology other's hopes. They are kind to each other's dreams". (131:16). It is to be noted that if the friend is to make such return as to be "kind" to the 

Must friends grow to. "other's dreams" he must develop gether intellectually? in accord with such dreams. The writers on this subject show a considerable tendency to insist that friends be and remain alike as to the substantial moral attributes of their lives. Cicero contends that, "Whoever is in possession of a true friend sees the exact counterpart of his own soul". (A27:179. If friends are to retain their friendship for a considerable length of time it is necessary that the lines of their mental growth be parallel if not convergent. Gulick seems to mean that such growth together is comparatively rare. In speaking of an especially cherished friend he says, "He has been a rare friend, in that he has let me grow and change my ideas. Most of my friends seem to demand that I remain what they first knew and liked me for". (B60:44). Such parallel growth should be along the lines of the major interests and purposes of life and should not interfere with that desirable supplementation of powers which comes with the development of minor differences. Differences as well as points of likeness between friends are very desirable. Griggs says, "The third law of friendship is thus the law of unlikeness, or complementary relationship of personalities". (58:23).

But such supplementation must relate to minor issues and details and not to the major issues of life and character. 

The friends need to grow in likeness of their appreciation of the good, the true and the beautiful. They must think somewhat alike as to the place morality and religion has in life. Their appreciation of personality and individuality must be akin. Assuming a likeness in such great essentials it seems to be true that the friendship is furthered by minor differences which are in their nature supplementary.

4. Conclusions as to Psychological Elements Involved and as to the Meaning of Love and Friendship.

In summing up this historical-psychological study we draw conclusions concerning: (1) the influence of philosophical points of view on psychological emphasis; (2) the expressions of mind involved in friendly alignments and their functional relations; (3) resulting statements as to the meaning of these terms. 

(1). Conclusions as to Historical Attitudes concerning the Psychology of Friendly Alignments. 

Whether a writer recognizes it or not his writing is influenced by his philosophical point of view. Our mention of schools of philosophy in this connection is suggestive only and not intended to set forth with any completeness the historical attitude of such schools. 

A. Influence of the Different Schools of Philosophy upon the Place Granted to the Psychological Elements of Friendships.

The study of the literature on friendship and love shows rather plainly the influence of quite a group of philosophical What schools of phil- schools. Those who look at life osophy have influ- against the background of a type enced interpretations of cultured and refined Epicureanof friendship? ism find it easy to give the impulse of sex and such basic impulses a place in friendship. If we assume that basically human beings have a right to enjoyment it is not difficult to understand how such an impulse can have a place in life. The stern attitude of the Stoic philosophy shows itself in the demand for justice and loyalty in friendly relations and in the maintenance of vows regardless of the effort or suffer-

Social Psychology ing involved. It is plain how the general ascetic point of view casts discredit on any form of personal intimacy and any appreciation of the beauty or strength of the human body. The trend of Philosophy toward mysticism, absolute idealism or transcendentalism tends to emphasize the friend as an ideal and to construe the relationship as existing on an idealistic or psychic level. The influence of Christian idealism or optimism shows itself in the assumption that the elements of original nature are basically good and are capable of such sublimation as to give them a form of expression favorable to moral character and social welfare. 

Also it is to be noted that philosophical rationalism tends to emphasize the importance of knowledge and wisdom in establishing a strong and stable friendship. Those who have confidence in a general way in racial and intuitive promptings in the message of the prophet, poet, musician-tend more readily to emphasize the significance of the feeling elements involved in the relationship of love and friendship.

B. Summary Statement of the Expressions of Mind 

Involved in Friendly Alignments and of their Functional Correlations.

From our historical review it seems evident that a friendly alignment of a personal nature involves a conscious complex, including all types of the expressions of mind. We have found evidence of the assertion of impulses, desires, emotions and of the self as well as the powers of imagination, reason and 

What mental powers are involved in friend. ship or love? judgment. It will be understood that many of these expressions of mind always function together and that it is

only a matter of emphasis when we speak of the functioning of one phase apart from the functioning of its correlated phases of mind. The expressions of mentality are not always involved to the same degree in every friendly alignment nor are they expressed on an equally high level in different cases. But it is important to have clearly in mind that love or friendship is of the nature of a conscious complex involving in some way and degree all of the basic powers of life. 

How are the component elements related in a friendship?

In this conscious complex the function performed by each expression of mind is in accord with the natural function of that mental power. We agree with Bergson (B12:142) and with 

McDougall (90:45) that there is an instinctive dynamic element in life which supplies the driving power for life expression on all levels of development. We are not assuming any definite amount of directive power of this dynamic: only in a very general way does it determine ends on the part of human beings. An expression of this life dynamic constitutes the stable impulse of friendship. This relationship will be explained further in Chapters III and IV. The cognitive powers of mind (attention, imagination, perception, conception, reason) are involved in detecting the nature of the object of friendship, in idealizing its possibilities, in understanding the laws of friendship and the values of such attachments and in that degree of self-analysis which makes continued adjustment to a friend possible. The affective elements of consciousness (emotions, organic sensations, moods, dispositions, tempers) make the attachment enjoyable and by the release of endocrine hormones stimulate certain major impulses and motives to the end of establishing a positive social relationship to the ideal and an

Social Psychology optimistic blindness to the actual or personal object of the friendship. Volition or self-control maintains the implications of the friendly attachment under special conditions; this may be needed at times of emotional disturbances or when the friendship is undergoing change to such an extent that the normal functioning of the feeling elements of attachment cannot be relied upon. Volition maintains friendly expressions regardless of moods, tempers or whimsical desires. The complete unification and hence full development of such conscious complex involves a considerable process of adjustment. In any particular friendly alliance this adjustment may have developed to any one of a number of actual stages. It is possible to designate certain of the most outstanding of such genetic stages of development. 

(2). Conclusions as to Distinctions in Friendly Situa tions and as to Resultant Terminology. 

A rather obvious distinction between friendly attachments is that made by contrasting the crude or primitive with the cultured or sublimated form of the sentiment. 

How distinguish the If we speak of love in a crude or crude from the subli. primitive way we tend to emphamated love or friend. size the organic sensations associated with the sex impulse. It we ship? mention love on its more sublimated or spiritualized level we tend to think of the emotion of tenderness with its accompanying parental care and altruistic tendencies. Friendship on its crude or primitive level is primarily an attachment to a concrete actuality which is temporary in nature. The crude friendship is likely to be formed for the purposes of utility and for the time being to be agreeable to the participants. Friendship

at its highest and best has an ideal as its object, it is more stable in its nature, and where persons are involved inclines more to be reciprocal and involves submission of all parties concerned to moral standards. In either case probably there are various intermediate stages between the crude and the developed form of the complex. 

First we attempt to state the meaning of the term love when it takes the form of a crude and partially formed 

What is love in its sentiment. The term love should crude or early begin. not be applied to the attachment ning form? in any case until a sufficient complexity is developed to make the alignment of the nature of a sentiment. In the crude and beginning form of love the complexities of impulse and organic sensation center around the impulse to reproduce with its emotion of lust. So we may say in its crude, early form love is a partially developed sentiment made up of a central sex impulse with a variety of associated elements centering about an individual as an object and constituting a relatively stable basis of attraction. The object is likely to be idealized only to a very minor degree. The attractive elements are likely to be temporary in their nature and hence likely to produce a temporary rather than a permanent satisfaction to the individuals involved in the alignment.

In distinction to this the crude form of friendship seems to develop about a larger variety of impulses. Impulses prompt- 

What is friendship in ing any of the forms of utility or its crude beginnning pleasure (sensory and temporary) form? seem to suffice in this case. The impulse prompting the desire for economic or social advantage or prompting the pleasure of association in work together or association in play or diversion, may constitute a basis for the developing complex.

Social Psychology 

So we may say in its crude early forms friendship is a partially formed sentiment based upon the impulse of a personal desire or desires and directed toward an object which promises some temporary but rather immediate form of satisfaction. As it develops this sentiment tends more to idealize its object, to make more permanent its attachment to it and to subordinate the whole relationship to inclusive moral principles. 

What is love at its best?

From crude beginnings the sentiments of love and friendship seem to develop in a somewhat gradual way until they attain a sublimated status. We make no attempt to designate such stages as there are no commonly used terms applied to them. We attempt rather to state the nature of the sentiment at its most developed stage. 

Fully developed and sublimated love is a sentiment expressing itself through moralized emotions and motivated by an impulse which functions to that end which the lover believes to be for the highest welfare of the object of the sentiment. The sentiment has no expression except through the instinctive-emotional units built into this system. Its central impulse or motive is protective and productive of factors of character and conditions of life that the lover believes to be for the welfare of the object of the sentiment. Fear as a factor in the sentiment tends to protect the object, anger in the sentiment tends to remove danger from the object, and in like manner each emotional-instinctive unit involved in the sentiment uses its natural tendency to the welfare of the object of the sentiment. 

We conclude the study by attempting a statement of the nature of friendship in its most developed and worthy form. The sublimation of the primary impulses into the psychic realm makes the higher expressions of love and friendship very much alike. The more carefully one

searches in the classical literature for fundamental differences in the meaning of these terms the more he is convinced that such basic differences do not exist; certain 

What is friendship authors speak in terms of contrast developed to its best? but do not express real differences of meaning. Self-assertion, gregariousness or sex functioning on a moralized psychic level promote much the same attitude toward their object. My friend is of most utility and pleasure to me when he develops his character to its highest and best. In like manner it is only after he is so developed that I can commune with him in the fullest sense and thus satisfy my gregarious impulse most fully. So it is these tendencies of self-assertion and gregariousness when developed to their best that lend their impulses to strengthen the creative impulse of sex when functioning at its best. The creative impulse is likely not to be recognized as sex when expressed in the psychic realm; thus the ascetic craving spiritual immortality thinks that this desire is counter to the impulse of sex which promotes racial immortality. So it happens that love and friendship functioning at their best approach each other very closely. 

Fully matured and sublimated friendship is a complex sentiment expressing itself through moralized, emotional channels and motivated by a group of basic impulses (selfassertion, gregariousness, sex) the tendencies of which have combined to promote what is believed by the friend to be the welfare of the object of the sentiment. A fuller understanding of social relations is likely to convince the friend that the welfare of the object of his personal friendship also constitutes the best interests of himself and of society in general. Or the statement may be so reversed as to read, The highest welfare of self and of society can be taken as a norm to determine what is in reality the best interests of the friend.
