CHAPTER III. 

The  

In the chapter preceding, the terms love and friendship were spoken of as sentiments. It is proposed to think of dislike, animosity, hatred, respect, regard, liking, as sentiments more or less completely formed. A preliminary statement as to the meaning of the term sentiment has been given. This chapter considers such questions as these: 

How has the term sentiment been used and how will it be used in this text? What are the different types of sentiments? What types of emotion and what emotions enter into the different sentiments? How may we visualize the total relationship of sentiments and emotions? 

I. The Nature of the Sentiment. 

To develop clearness we bring out in a measure of contrast something of the different ways in which the term sentiment has been used. The reader is warned against using the adjective sentimental as a cue to the meaning of the noun sentiment. 

1. Differing Conception as to the Nature of the Sentiment.

We quote several statements from psychological writers of the present or near past. Pillsbury says, "Sentiment is ordinarily used to designate the milder, more lasting forms of emotion". 104:492). McDougall says, "In common speech and in much psychological writing, 'sentiment' is 85

Social Psychology 

How conceive of sentiment as emotion or feeling? not clearly distinguished from 'emotion'" (C90:418).

Breeze says, "Sentiments are relatively permanent feelings which grow up around more or less complete systems of ideas and intellectual processes". (A19:375). Warren says, "A sentiment is an experience which is made up of systemic sensations and ideas". (A137:218-19. Bogardus says, "A sentiment is a complex of emotional reactions which appears in organized ways. Sentiments are organized emotions with social values". (18:77). While Warren and Bogardus recognize that a sentiment is in some ways different from an emotion the exact difference is not made plain to the reader. That they are not using the term in the same sense that Shand and McDougall use it is made plain by the fact that Warren says, "They are generally weak and unimportant in mental life" and that Bogardus calls such emotions as pity, shame, jealousy, revenge, examples of sentiments. The usage referred to in this paragraph makes no clear-cut distinction between sentiment and emotion. Also there is considerable difference of opinion as to the psychological significance and exact meaning of the term sentiment. 

2. The Meaning of Sentiment as Used in the Present 

Recent English writers on psychology have developed a somewhat different meaning for the term sentiment. The development of the more recent usage of the term is accredited by McDougall (90:126) and by Stout (227:576) to 

A. F. Shand. Shand says that emotions, "are in a sense adjectival and qualify a more stable feeling. Whereas the specific organization of our sentiments,-so far from being mere adjectives and qualifying other feelings, are the rela-

tively stable centers to which the first attach themselves, the substantives of these adjectives, the complex wholes which contain in their possible lifehistory the entire gamut of the emotions". (127:576-77). Mc 

Dougall lays considerable stress 

How conceive of sentiment as a physio- logical complex? upon, "the distinction between facts of mental structure and facts of mental functioning or activity". He says, "The emotion is a mode of experience, a way of functioning, and a fact of activity; the sentiment is a fact of structure, an organized system of dispositions, which endures, in a more or less quiescent condition, between the occasions upon which it is brought into activity". (C90:418).

Shand in another place speaks of the sentiments as, "those greater systems of the character which organize and direct the lesser primary systems". (121:35). McDougall points out further that our emotional dispositions "tend to become organized in systems about the various objects and classes of objects that excite them". (90:126). Stout says, “Such a sentiment as friendship cannot be experienced in its totality at any one moment. It is felt only in the special phase which is determined by the circumstances of the moment. Thus we may say that so far as actual experience is concerned the sentiment is constituted by the manifold emotions in which it manifests itself". (127:578). In other words the sentiment is a physiological complex or pattern which builds into its system such emotions or instinctive tendencies as may be used to further its purposes. 

If our national government were only a union of states and if it expressed itself only through state agencies, then as a figure of speech we might think of the nation as a sentiment and each state as an instinctive-emotional channel for its expression. In such figure of speech it would be necessary to assume that certain states (such as those of

Social Psychology 

New England for example) give their tone to the union as a whole and that after the formation of such trend the state joining the union must submit to the principles of the union and use its power only on behalf of the union and that the union has no form of expression other than through a state or states. Or we may think of the sentiment as the hand which itself may be fixed in a stationary position but which expresses itself through the movement of the fingers; the fingers correspond to the dynamic-emotional units which are the agencies of expression of the sentiment (in this figure of speech the hand) which is the unifying and controlling agency. Translate such a figure of speech into neurones, neural arcs and patterns (perhaps involving essential glanular stimulation) and you have the conception of the sentiment as a physiological complex. 

The nature of the sentiment may be further clarified by giving a paragraph to the consideration of its functioning with special reference to its control over its involved elements. This includes some consideration of the importance of this acquired form of organizing agency. McDougall in speaking of its value says, "The conception of the sentiment -enables us at once to reduce to order many of the facts How does the senti- of the life of impulse and emotion, ment function in its a province of psychology which form as an organized hitherto has been chaotic and obcomplex? (90:126). The present scure. writer is convinced that the conception of the sentiment is one of the most important that has been introduced into psychology within comparatively recent times. This is true especially in the case of social psychology because the sentiment organizes the main elements of experience with which social psychology deals; it is conceded rather generally that the dynamic-feeling elements of life have most to do with social relationships.

Lest we misrepresent McDougall as to the extent to which a sentiment dominates over its involved emotions we quote from a private letter which he was kind enough to address to the writer on December 20, 1924. He says, "I cannot quite accept the phrase 'the central impulse of a sentiment' since for me each sentiment has as many central impulses as there are primary emotional dispositions entering into its composition, the one may be dominant as normally in the love of the mother for her child. But in regard to respect there is commonly no such one dominant impulse unless it be the impulse of submission in some cases". But in order to recognize a type of sentiment at all each sentiment of that type necessarily must have some characteristic peculiar to it. We have somewhat the same idea as 

Shand when he says, "Every sentiment tends to form a type of character of its own". (121:123). At least there must be enough of direction about this central government to organize the expression of primary instincts and emotions without conflict among themselves and to stabilize the action of all the impulses so as to maintain the same attitude toward the object of the sentiment. It is plain that anger expressed toward the enemy of the beloved is not in itself an expression like that of fear felt when the beloved is in danger or sorrow when the beloved suffers but the expression of each embodied emotion sets forth the same tendency of the sentiment-to further the best interests of the beloved. It is this stable tendency of the sentiment which characterizes its type that we are calling its central impulse.

We must not come to think of the sentiment as related to impulse and feeling only. It is a conscious complex and a phase of the consciousness involved is cognitive. A particular sentiment must have an object which takes the form of an idea; the idea may be in the form of a percept or that

Social Psychology of a concept. This means that the self through its powers of cognition must give sufficient interpretation to the stimulus of sense-which through imagination and visceral acarouses certain of the 

How does the senti- tivity ment become related primary emotions-to be able to to a particular object? recognize that object under various situations. Some perceptual element must be involved even in the functioning of a primary emotion; before a mouse fears a cat there must be the perception at least that it is a cat and not a goat. Probably it is true as McDougall insists (90:54) that emotion follows perception with little or no interference from volition but it should be kept in mind that emotion does not follow sensation without perception. What is true for the smaller system of the emotion is more evidently true for the larger system of the sentiment. An object may not be chosen consciously previous to the experience of emotional attachment to it but it must be interpreted consciously and acknowledged by the self as the object of the sentiment; if this is not true the complex is automatic and should not be called a sentiment. Such recognition of an object normally involves in a human mind some evaluation and idealization. 

Before the main forces of life are organized about a particular object that object must be considered worthy of such a relation to the life. We do not consider it worth while to vow our love to every tree and flower we see nor do we consider it worthy of us to express hatred toward every dog that growls at us. The tendency is not only to understand the object of the sentiment but also to idealize it. The beloved object becomes surrounded to the view of the lover with a halo of perfection not discernable to others. The hated object is supposed to be fearful and disgusting in a way entirely incomprehensible to its friends. So it follows hat the powers of mind which enable us to perceive, to ab-

stract, to idealize, to evaluate, are involved in the organization of a sentiment and are used to some extent in the functioning of the sentiment. In a sense the sentiment is a unit element of life and hence involves all the factors which go to make up human life; the human self may be thought of as made up of a hierarchy of sentiments. 

II. The Types of Sentiments. 

We consider the classification of sentiments: (1) as to the nature of their objects; (2) as to the nature of their impulses. 

1. Types of Sentiments Distinguished as to the Nature of their Objects.

It is normal that during its early stages a sentiment builds its system about, a concrete object. This is true 

What is a concrete sentiment? because in the natural order of learning percepts precede concepts; before the child knows the word book in general he has become acquainted with a picture book, a reader and thus several particular books. The percept is to be understood as bearing with it its sensory setting in the way of objectively or subjectively caused images; the tree, for example, has its sensory attributes caused in the main by the present functioning of sense end-organs but in part by a cortical production of images. The concrete objective sentiment has a concrete existent object which may be of various types. One may respect, love or hate a particular tree, mountain, stream, dog, boy, house, farm, etc. All such concrete things constantly are being made the objects of our sentiments. One may regard, hate or love an imagined character as well as a real person. Lasting attachments may be formed for the hero of a novel or

Social Psychology for a concrete idea involving an invention or reform of some sort. Any concrete idea carrying with it its definite setting, whether existing in the external world, or in the mind only, may become the object of a sentiment. We are calling such complex a concrete sentiment. Or It is possible also to have a collection of objects as the object of a sentiment. Beside forming an attachment for a particular child one may become attached to children, say 

What is a collective of the age of the child to which the first attachment was made. sentiment? one may come to hold trees and mountains in high respect or come to hate snakes or submarines. The formation of a sentiment about the idea of a series of concrete objects may be called a collective sentiment. In such case a particular attitude is taken towards the whole group of objects; this is likely to come from the development of that attitude toward one of the group and to make it easy to develop the same attitude toward any other member of the group.

What is an abstract sentiment?

In addition to the concrete and collective idea there is also the abstract idea. The process of obstraction (ab plus trahere, to draw from) is the intellectual process which draws the central idea away from its concrete sensory setting. Thus the abstract idea becomes free from a particular sensory setting. Our little girl when first learning the word door was accustomed to run and lay her hand on a particular door which, of course, had a particular location, size, color, shape, etc. As is normal, she later learned to recognize objects as doors regardless of their setting, size, shape, etc.; thus the door idea became detached from its particular setting. This is typical of the genesis of all abstract ideas. Abstract ideas may refer to either the objective or subjective realms. We think in

the abstract of the home, state, church, of the flower, bird, tree, ship, of travel, sport, study, etc. These we are terming objectively abstract because they are developed from the concrete objects of experience by the process of abstraction. On the other hand we have abstract ideas of truth, goodness, beauty, democracy, freedom, etc., which seem to have their original basis in subjective experiences and their intellectual interpretation. The first class of ideas constitute the objects for the abstract objective sentiment, the second class of ideas the objects for the abstract subjective sentiments. It will be noted that it is not the sentiment that is abstract but the idea which is the object of the sentiment. It need only be suggested that ideas, either concrete, collective or abstract, are very numerous and hence that there is the possibility, at least as regards their objects, of forming a great number of sentiments. 

2. Types of Sentiments Distinguished as to the Nature of their Impulses.

In addition to distinguishing sentiments by the nature of their objects it is possible also to distinguish them by the attitude they take toward their objects. The same type of object such as man may call forth very different attitudes such as friendship, antipathy, respect, etc. This complicates the situation very considerably and suggests a further consideration of types of sentiments. 

It must be kept in mind that a sentiment is very complex and the determination of its central impulse is a matter of interpretation. We have mentioned McDougall's objection to the phrase, "the central impulse of a sentiment". 

Indeed the task of determining such a tendency is somewhat hazardous. It is in a way akin to the task of determining the central aim of the United States or of determining our basic implication when we use the words feminine or mascu-

Social Psychology line. But without an interpretation of its central tendency the nature of the sentiment is comparatively unknown. 

And in fact we do not hesitate to interpret in all such cases Can the central motive as those mentioned above. I speak sentiment be of trusting this man because he is my friend or I speak of this or that as being characteristically mascuof a stated? line or feminine. There is no doubt that some impulses are easier to interpret than others. In the case of play we have a number of theories-excess energy, preparation, atavistic, rivalry, biological utility, relaxation, to which Gulick adds an idealistic theory. (See A60:266-81). In the case of fear, anger, disgust and other emotions we have developed no such list of theories presumably because in such cases the impulse involved is easier to interpret. In like manner in certain of the types of the sentiments the interpretation of the central tendency is much easier than is the case with other types. The major difficulty is not so much to distinguish the main types of sentiments but rather to differentiate the subdivisions of a type.

The first of the more general types of sentiments is designated friendship or love or by some term synonymous with What is the central these; we have pointed out that these sentiments are very much impulse of friendship alike on their higher levels and for or love? purposes of considering their central tendencies their differences may be disregarded. After such a complex becomes conscious its impulse seems to prompt that which the friend or lover believes to be for the welfare of the object of the sentiment. If the understanding of the friend is limited his sentiment is likely to promote only the temporary and physical rather than the permanent and mental welfare of its object. Mistakes may be made as to that which actually is for the welfare of the

object and objectives promoted which for the time being are believed to be for the object's welfare. There always is a tendency to idealize the object into a more favorable status. Attention is kept by the impulses of the sentiment upon the more favorable expressions of the object. The hidden motives of the one loved are believed to be good. His undesirable expressions are interpreted in the most favorable light. The potentialities and unknown qualities are given a high evaluation. Attention and thought are kept away from the defects of the friend; such tendency may be potent enough to develop the forgetting of such defects. 

That there is a central tendency operative within the whole complex is indicated by the effect of the whole upon 

What is the organizits subordinate elements. The ing power of the central impulse organizes and difriendly sentiment? rects the impulses of the subordinate units of the sentiment so as to promote the major tendencies of the larger unit. When organized in such complex, fear is no longer self-protective only but also guards the object of the sentiment from danger. The force of anger is so organized as to help the object of the sentiment overcome his difficulties and to guard him from threatening dangers; the same holds for an institution or ideal as does for a person. Elation and joy is felt at the success of the object and subjection or sorrow at its defeat. This same truth may be expressed by saying that the object of the sentiment has been taken into self and now is considered a part of self. Whether we think of the sublimation as taking this form, or whether we think in accord with the more customary meaning of altruism, makes little difference as to the truth of our main contention here. What properly constitutes success or failure may be misinterpreted by the self. Also in any crisis the self may misjudge what will promote the welfare of the ob-

Social Psychology ject. But in any case the self when prompted by a friendly impulse promotes that which is believed to be the welfare of the object.

It is recognized commonly that there is a hostile type of sentiment the attitude of which toward its object is practi- What is the central cally the opposite of that of the impulse of hatred? friendly type of sentiment. Such sentiment is known as hatred. The tendency of this sentiment is to oppose the welfare of its object. Bogardus says, "Sentiments, diametrically opposed, are hate and love. Hate is an organization of emotional energy against a person or a group believed to be hostile". (A18:17). Shand indicates the objective of hatred more definitely when he says, "Shylock says, 'Hates any man the thing he would not kill'? Destruction then becomes the prominent end of hatred". (121:58). But hatred may develop toward an institution or social group without the self entertaining any hope of bringing about the destruction of the group. Instead of working directly for the death of the object the sentiment tends rather to impede its welfare. 

In fact at times hatred prompts keeping the object alive in order that the tendency of hatred may be the more completely satisfied; kings have protected the lives of their captives in order to punish them further, certain temperance workers keep reviving the saloon in imagination in order to have the added satisfaction of defeating it. 

Several of the same emotional units may be organized into the sentiment of hatred that become a part of the What are the organi- sentiment of love. This fact shows zation tendencies of plainly the influence of the larger pattern over the smaller. For in the case of hatred the tendency of the primary emotion is directed in a different way as regards the object of the sentiment. The self now becomes hatred?

afraid of the object of the sentiment rather than fearing that the object might suffer injury as in the case of love. 

Anger is turned against the object of the sentiment and its power used to the annoyance or injury of that object. It is quite possible that the whole program of hatred in reality works to the injury of self more than that of the object but this fact is apt to be overlooked by the person who hates. 

The injury done the object is likely to be more objective and hence obvious while that suffered by self is more subtle, affecting motives and character trends, and hence not so easily recognized. All other instinctive-emotional units involved in the sentiment of hatred are so organized as to impede the welfare of the object of the sentiment. 

Further, the sentiment of hatred has very important influences over the cognitive powers of mind. Darwin notes that, "Few individuals, however, can long reflect about a How does hatred in- hated person, without feeling and fluence cognition? exhibiting signs of indignation and rage". (31:237). Conversely the emotions of vengeance, malice, jealousy, after they have become organized into the system of hatred, exercise a potent influence on understanding and thought. These emotions encourage derogatory interpretations of the object of the sentiment. If the object is personal the motives of the individual are depreciated or conceived to be evil. The trend of thought in the case of hatred depends upon the estimate of self as to the power of the object of the sentiment. Darwin points out that, "if the offending person be quite insignificant, we experience merely disdain or contempt. If, on the the other hand, he is all-powerful, then hatred passes into terror, as when a slave thinks about a cruel master, or a savage about a bloodthirsty malignant deity". (31:237). So there is a mutual casual relation between the judgments of cognition and the secondary

Social Psychology emotions normal to the sentiment of hatred. If the object of hatred is impersonal attention is kept by the sentiment upon its undesirable qualities; these are in general such qualities as arouse disgust, anger or fear. There is a deepseated feeling of opposition which promotes planning to the injury of the object. Beside the extreme form of hatred involving vengeance there is a graded series of sentiments of this type including antipathy, dislike and presumably other forms for which we do not as yet have definite names, terminating in a very mild form of opposition to the object. 

It is coming to be recognized by some writers that there is a third type of sentiment which usually is called regard or respect. Bogardus says, "Respect is perhaps the most rationalized sentiment. Self respect means that one has given thought to his behavior and has justified it in the light What is the nature of of social standards.-Respect for the sentiment of reanother person means that one has analyzed the activities of the other gard? person and approved them". (A18:15-16). Bernard says, "McDougall properly speaks of the role of the self-regarding sentiment in the development of character. The self-regarding sentiment is indispensible to character formation". (15:485). As is indicated in this quotation McDougall lays considerable emphasis (See 90:179-270) upon the self-regarding sentiment as the organizing factor for the self and the source of the power which the self uses in maintaining its ideas through the operation of vôlition; the ultimate source of this power 

McDougall finds in the primary instincts. It is recognized commonly that regard does not have in it as potent dynamic elements as love and hate. As Bogardus suggests it is closely allied to admiration. (A18:15). Instead of the dominant, assertive tendencies of tenderness (in love) and disgust (in hate) regard stresses submission. This is rather the oppo-

site to self-assertion and gives to the sentiment a contemplative rather than an assertive attitude. It is not so much that it is highly rationalized as Bogardus (above) suggests as that the primary tendency of subjection favors submission to the object of the sentiment. The object may seem to me ideal only in a certain particular but that is sufficient to cause me to submit to that extent. If even a small measure of patience is shown under very trying conditions I am likely to admit that I could not do so well under such conditions. So the attitude is one of submission to the standards of the object rather than a definite assertion of self, either on behalf of, or against the object. If subsequently the standards of the object are lowered instead of the cherishing redeeming attitude of love ("that will not let me go") regard tends to shift to another object more worthy of the submission of self. There must, of course, be an estimation of the worth of the object but such estimate is made more in the form of an intuitive than a rational judgment.

We have noted that the extent of the organization of the sentiment of regard is not as extensive as that of the senti- What are the organiz. ments of love and hate. The eming tendencies of rephasis which the sentiment places gard? upon submission rules out of its organization such vigorous assertive impulses as accompany tenderness and disgust. This tendency to retreat from the object and to remain in rather a passive attitude of submission toward it probably is what gives the impression that this is an intellectualized or contemplative sentiment. There is no reason for believing that the self that holds an object in regard gives more intellectual attention to that object than if it were the object of love, for instance; the absence of tenderness and disgust from the sentiment may cause the contemplation to be less

Social Psychology 

"blind" than if these dynamic factors were functioning.

Self-respect is rather clearly differentiated from self-love. 

McDougall says, "respect is clearly marked off from love by the fact that shame is one of its strongest elements". (90:166). He further remarks that, "Self-love is fortunately a comparatively rare sentiment; it is the self-regarding sentiment of the thoroughly selfish man, the meaner sore of egoist. Such a man feels a tender emotion for himself, he indulges in self-pity; he may have little positive self-feeling and may be incapable of shame". (90:166-67).

We recognize that the situation just described is very undesirable and readily to be distinguished from the very desirable tendency of self-respect. This conclusion suggests that regard has little of the sex impulse in it. Instead of the desire to take an object and remake it there is the tendency to accept it in its present form as idealistic. Selfrespect then to be good must take as its object a lofty idea of self and not a present embodiment of self. I have respect for my potential nature rather than for my present accomplishments; I respect what I have the possibility of becoming rather than what at present I am. So the tendency of respect is to develop an attitude of confidence and assurance toward its object. I am ready to submit to the leadership of another. I am ready to trust my own potentialities and to undertake hard tasks with the feeling of assurance that I can succeed.

Next we think in a more definite way of the emotions which are embodied in the sentiments. For this purpose we use the classification of emotions, (1) primary, (2) secondary, (3) derived.

III. The Types of Emotional Elements Involved in the 

Sentiments.

We attempt here: (1) to distinguish the types of emo-

tions; (2) to relate the types to each other and to the sentiments. The chart showing this larger pattern of relationship is reserved until after the discussion of the emotions. 

1. The Primary Emotion and Its Relation to the 

Sentiment.

Of the many ways suggested by different writers for the classifying of emotions the headings primary, secondary and derived seem to the present writer most promising. The first attempt is to make plain the nature of the primary emotion. Ribot says truly, "There is no need to point out that a simple emotion (fear, anger, etc.) 

What is a primary emotion? is, in itself, a very complex phenomenon, and that 'simple' means irreducible by analysis to any other emotion". (112:260). We believe that this observation makes impertinent the whole line of criticism of Bernard when he says that he is able to "show that the so-called primary or elementary emotions are complex organizations of feelings, organic and peripheral sensations and perceptions, even of concepts in the most extreme cases, and that they are as variable and as loosely organized in content as the activity or value complexes to which they correspond". (15:456).

This whole line of thinking misses the point. We are not saying that the primary emotion does not develop on the background of various organic and kinaesthetic sensations and that it is not closely associated with various images and ideas. What we are saying is that the emotion as emotion is unitary. We consider the emotion to be the reaction (e plus movere, to move out) of affective consciousness to organic and kinaesthetic sensations and not the summation of such sensations; just as perception is a conscious interpretation of sensations, so emotion is a definite reaction to certain groups of sensations. There is no necessity

Social Psychology of attempting to include in the emotion all the sensations, impulses, images, ideas and actions which are in one way or another involved in its functioning; to do this is as foolish as to try to involve all the other parts of the engine in the definition of a spark plug. An affective reaction with a distinct feeling quality can properly be spoken of as a unitary emotion. It is unitary if it is one emotion and not made up of other emotions. emotion?

It is well to consider certain of the tests for detecting what emotions are primary. Shand suggests the following 

How detect a primary tests: "(1) Its early appearance in child life; (2) Its diffusion in the animal world; (3) Its irresolvability into other emotions; (4) Its innate excitability by sensory stimuli, and (5) its manifestation in some form of instinctive behavior". (121:xx). McDougall suggests further, "we must inquire in each case whether the emotion and impulse in question occasionally appear in human beings with morbidly exaggerated intensity, apart from such general hyper-excitability as is displayed in mania”. (90:51). If the emotional tendency persists in dimentia the indication is that the emotion is unitary and does not break up with the general disruption of self. But even with such an extended series of tests it is not easy to be sure whether certain emotions are primary. For instance there are certain reactions of cats and dogs which give a clear suggestion of jealousy. But jealousy seems on analysis to be made up of different emotions and to imply comparative judgments not attributed ordinarily to cats and dogs. So we may expect to find some differences in the lists of primary emotions given by different authors. 

Under the title "The Tendencies of the Primary Emotions", Shand mentions fear, anger, joy, sorrow, disgust, repugnance, surprise, curiosity. (121:177-454). In his

What primary emo tions should be recog nized? "Introduction to Social Psychology", McDougall relates in a diagram the instincts, repulsion, fear, pugnacity, curiosity, subjection, self-assertion, and the parental instinct. (90:129). 

In his "Outline of Psychology", McDougall gives a fuller list of the primary emotions. Here he names fear, anger, disgust, tender emotion, distress, lust, curiosity, feeling of subjection, elation, feeling of loneliness, appetite, feeling of ownership, feeling of creativeness, amusement. (C90:324).

Woodworth says, "Anger, fear, lust, the comfortable state appropriate to digestion, grief (the state of the weeping child), mirth or amusement, disgust, curiosity, the 'tender emotion' (felt most strongly by a mother towards her baby), and probably a few others are 'primary emotions' ". (142:134). We speak of the emotions as the unitary components of the sentiment rather than assigning the instincts to this central place. This is true because the emotional patterns possess stable qualities whereas the instinctive expressions are extremely varied on the part of humans; this is true both of the stimulus which arouses them and of their expression in action. It is desirable not to think and speak of all of the above names as primary emotions. Some of them seem to us not to be emotions at all and others are expressions of a greater or less sublimation of the same innate tendency. We think of lust as being a more primitive expression of tenderness and of creativeness as being the impulse involved in both lust and tenderness. We think of appetite as a persistent impulse or desire and distress as a retrospective derived emotion which has a backward look toward defeat. A certain form of amusement may be a primary emotion (although we do not find it in animal nature) but it has no particular connection with the sentiments. Loneliness is related in a general way to gregari-

Social Psychology ousness and hence is in the general background of friendliness but the connection is not direct enough to consider it an important component element. Ownership is in some way connected with the creative, protective trend of tenderness and hence does not deserve separate mention in this connection. Also it seems to us that the distinction between repulsion and disgust is not sufficient to warrant the mention of both terms; on the general synthetic chart (see p. 113) repulsion is mentioned as being a more elementary form of disgust, only disgust is mentioned as a component element of the secondary emotions. So for purposes of relating the primary emotions to the secondary ones and to the sentiments we are calling the following primary: tenderness (lust as its organic basis), elation, subjection, wonder, fear, anger (rage as a primitive form of the same emotion), disgust (repulsion as indicative of its primitive impulse). 

Surprise is a condition of neural excitement coming in between sensation and its perception; this condition may lead quickly to perception of the situation, imagination concerning it, visceral activity, organic sensations and hence to some form of emotion. But certainly the surprise itself should not be considered an emotion. 

2. The Secondary Emotion and Its Connection with the 

Sentiment.

We consider next the secondary emotion and its relation to the sentiment. The secondary emotion is a compound into which there has been blended 

What is a secondary emotion? the particular characteristics of two or more primary emotions. 

Such combination results on account of experience and hence its neural pattern is not to be thought of as innate; the separate patterns may be innate and the compounding

merely a closure or joining together of such patterns. But whenever the secondary emotion comes to its full development it is found to have its own quality as an emotion. 

But ordinarily the blending is not sufficient to prevent our detecting of the emotional elements which have entered into the combination. This analysis may be performed by introspection of the separate qualities of the emotional components or by observation of the action tendencies of the secondary emotion and thus noting the diverse elements of which it is composed. 

What secondary emo tions shall we recognize?

Obviously it must be difficult to detect and tabulate all the secondary emotions. McDougall says, "Since the primary emotions may be combined in a large number of different ways, and since the primaries that enter into the combination of the secondary emotions may be present in many different degrees of intensity, the whole range of complex emotions presents an indefinitely large number of qualities that shade imperceptibly into one another without sharp dividing lines". (90:131). The following emotions are recognized by McDougall as being secondary: admiration, awe, reverence, gratitude, scorn, contempt, loathing, envy, reproach, jealousy, resentment, revenge, shame, bashfulness, pity. (90:132-63). Joy, sorrow, surprise, happiness and remorse are discussed in this connection but it seems not to be Mc- Dougall's intention to call these secondary emotions. We shall mention again joy, sorrow and remorse in connection with the derived emotions. Warren gives quite a list of "Human Emotions". (A137:215). He is not classifying on the basis under consideration here but mentions some terms not stated above which might be considered secondary emotions. Ecstasy, exuberance, exultation, seem very akin to joy or are perhaps attempts to express degrees of

Social Psychology strength of this emotion. Suspicion and dread are of the derived type of emotion. Detestation is a term expressive of the sentiment of hatred and in so far as it refers to feeling is an expression of sex aversion on the part of the female under certain conditions. The selection made among these emotions and the analysis of the secondary ones is indicated in our synthetic chart of emotions and sentiments. (See p. 113). McDougall considers that certain of the secondary emotions must be embodied in the system of the sentiments and that the others may be so embodied. (See 

90: 132ff, 141ff). We are not stressing this distinction but are indicating the emotions which are found most commonly in a particular type of sentiment; this is indicated on the synthetic chart referred to above. 

3. The Derived Emotion and Its Association with the Sentiment. 

What is a derived emotion?

The remaining type of emotion has been termed derived. 

This is because it derives its existence from the operation of a stable impulse or desire against a changing mental background. Desire may be thought of as a persistent impulse. Shand says, "Desire is then a very complex emotional system which includes actually or potentially the six prospective emotions of Hope, Anxiety, Disappointment, Despondency, Confidence and Despair". (121:463). These emotions are conditioned upon the operation of a persistent impulse toward an end with a conscious recognition both of the objective and of the environmental conditions making its realization more or less likely. 

In this connection we are thinking of the central tendency of the sentiment as representing the stabilized impulse and the object of the sentiment as representing the object of desire. In speaking of the list of derived emotions quoted above McDougall says, "With this I am in entire agree-

ment, save that I would enlarge the class by including in it the retrospective as well as the prospective emotions of desire; thus we should add regret, remorse and sorrow. Joy, I submit, occupies a peculiar position, in that it belongs to both groups; there are restrospective as well as prospective joys". (90:395). We use the suggestion to classify the derived emotions under the headings "prospective" and "retrospective". We incline also to take into consideration the actual or assumed fact of victory or defeat. Certainly the restrospective emotions take a different form depending on whether the backward look is toward victory or toward defeat. In the case of the backward look to victory there may be joy, encouragement, comfort, satisfaction or pride. In the case of the backward look to defeat there may be discouragement, regret, grief, sorrow, chagrin, or remorse. In the case of the contemplation of defeat the question of responsibility and the extent of the loss sustained are important factors. If the loss is supposed to be slight the emotion is likely regret. If the loss is estimated as great and the responsibility does not seem to rest upon a particular individual, the emotion is likely to be grief or sorrow; grief is more painful and concentrated than sorrow. If the loss is judged supreme and the responsibility rests squarely upon self, the emotion is remorse. If the loss is thought to be comparatively slight but if an enemy has conquered through some needless blunder of self, the emotion is chagrin. If defeat is repeated or is due to some unchangeable quality of self, the emotion is discouragement. 

The emotions produced by the backward look to victory constitute more of a graded series as the question of responsibility need not be so definitely in consciousness; whether the emotion is joy, encouragement, comfort or pride depends mainly on whether the self judges the victory exceptional or expects it to be repeated. In the case of the

Social Psychology forward look to defeat, in which self is forced to conclude that the chances of victory are growing less and less, the emotions are likely to be confidence, belief, hope, suspicion or anxiety, dread or worry, despondency, distress and despair; this is based on the assumption that at the beginning of the forward look conditions appeared very favorable to victory but that they seemed to grow increasingly less favorable. If the forward look starts with apparently a very discouraging situation which gives a gradually increasing promise of victory the emotions are likely to be practically the reverse of those just named only starting with distress and despondency and culminating in confidence and joy. Doubt seems to be a fluctuating tendency between belief and dread; the cause of the mental fluctuation may be partially objective but is mainly a subjective balance of decision without a definite mental set. 

IV. A Graphic Presentation of the Major Types of Senti ments with Their Involved Emotional Units. 

We are presenting on page 113 a summary synthetic chart showing the relations of the major sentiments to each other, the tendencies of their central impulses and their emotional content. 

1. The Psychological and Social Value of the Considera tion of Such Charting of Sentiments and Emotions. 

The writer has been working for several years with his pupils on this chart and recognizes something of the difficulties involved in the task of building these units into a single inclusive pattern. It is to be understood that this chart is suggestive only but we believe that it presents to the eye most of the results that have been worked out thus far along this line. Also we are convinced that there is a

definite value for psychology in this type of work. The gestalt psychology is calling to our attention the importance of understanding wholes and of relating the whole to its What are the psycho- involved parts. There is no field logical values of chart- of psychology in which there is a ing sentiments and greater need that this be done than in the field of the affective and dyemotions? namic consciousness. It is rather generally conceded that this division of psychology is most difficult and least understood. Those who are content to think of emotion as "useless action" will, of course, see no point in trying to comprehend this difficult subjective field. 

But for those who think of emotion as a factor of real value in life, any approach to its fuller comprehension must be considered of value. The questions of relationship involved here are basic to the understanding of human ontogeny: the development of self and the formation of character center in the unification of the instinctive-emotional systems and their organization into the larger systems of the sentiments. There can be little comprehension of the nature of volition without an understanding of the source of its power and without a comprehension of the genesis of the self which finds expression through it. Human character is mainly an organization of the sentiments into a hierarchy. Thus to understand character we must understand something of these relationships.

Also we believe there are definite social values involved in the attempt to organize and chart the emotions and sentiments. Too long have we merely pretended to supply a psychological basis for social relations. Instincts and desires have been invented at will to suit social theories political and economic needs. Polygamy, slavery, the saloon, the duel, racial hatred, war, and practically every other social evil has been assumed to rest on human instinctive

Social Psychology What social values are involved in charting sentiments and emotions? tendencies. Any theorist who so desired has invented unchanging human tendencies to suit the needs of his theory. 

If we are to have a science at all it is plain that we must ascertain what tendencies of human life are major and what minor. If this determination is to be scientific it must be done prior to the special demands of particular situations. For this reason we believe that it is of social importance that detailed study be given to the organization and trends of the major patterns of life. 

2. A Synthetic Chart of Sentiments and Emotions and Its Explanation. 

The following suggestions and explanations apply to the chart on page 113. The main attempt is to relate the types of sentiments-classified as How visualize the relation of the types of their impulses to each other and sentiments? to to show the emotional elements involved in each of the sentiments. 

The arrow after each major type of sentiment indicates that the sentiment finds its expression through, and only through, its embodied emotions. The length of the line from the sentiment to the primary emotion is intended to show the relative prominence of that emotion in the sentiment. It will be noted that the primary emotions common to love and hate are in reverse prominence. Both love and hate omit the element most prominent in the other and regard omits the two emotions (tenderness and disgust) one or the other, of which is most prominent in love or hate. Wonder holds a prominent place in each of the sentiments because it calls the attention of self to an object which later becomes the object of the sentiment. The placement of lust, rage and repulsion on the lines of tenderness, anger and disgust

should be taken to indicate that the former are somewhat more primitive forms of expression of the latter. The fact that the sentiment has no form of expression except through one of its contained emotions causes its expression to be somewhat different when the different emotional channels are used. But the sentiment exercises such control over its emotions as to cause the emotions of love, although functioning along their own lines, to promote the welfare of the object and the same emotions in hate to function to practically the opposite end. Certain emotions have to be omitted from the complex because their tendencies cannot be directed to the support of the main end of the sentiment. 

Our chart attempts to show the main lines of these tendencies and to state as accurately as possible the meaning of their central trend. It should be understood that each main type may involve any number of individual sentiments of that type.

How visualize the relation of the second. ary emotions to the sentiments?

The secondary emotions which we believe are most commonly embodied in each type of sentiment are placed on the long line which represents the basic impulse of that sentiment. We list only such secondary emotions as are found at least occasionally in a particular 

We are not type of sentiment. following McDougall's distinction of secondary emotions as to whether they must, or may be, embodied in the system of a sentiment. In the cases in which the star for the secondary emotions are found on two or more lines of the sentiments, the suggestion is that the emotion may be embodied in either or both of the sentiments. The letters following the secondary emotions are intended to show the primary emotions entering into their composition; the letters are the initial ones of the main list (not including the three primitive emotions) of primary emotions used. The analysis of the secondary emotions

Social Psychology follows for the most part that given by McDougall (90:132158) but some changes are introduced or alternatives suggested. We are not taking space in this connection to develop this matter fully; the analysis of each one of these emotions is deserving of very careful study. Awe and reverence certainly do not occupy all forms of regard but only regard for objects which are considered worthy of worship. We are convinced that psychology would profit from more investigation, discussion and comparison of ideas concerning the content of the compound emotions. 

An examination of the chart will reveal our suggestion as to the relation of the derived emotions to each other and to the sentiments. The two types of derived emotions pro- How visualize the reposed by McDougall (90:395) are lation of the derived indicated on the chart and their relation to both victory and deemotions to the sentiments? feat suggested. The idea of a derived emotion is that it develops from a partial or complete blocking of a desire. The tendencies of the sentiments come to constitute the main lines of desire. On this account we have inserted the derived emotions at the place of junction of the lines representing the tendencies of the sentiments. The dotted lines through the block of derived emotions indicate the unimpeded working of the basic impulses of the sentiments. 

The two lines of prospective derived emotions suggest gradations tending toward the crisis either of victory or defeat. The same suggestion is intended in the case of the retrospective emotions only in this case the gradations are not as definite as in the other; this is true especially of the trend toward defeat. The derived emotion felt does not depend upon the actual objective situation but upon the interpretation of that situation by the individual. CHAPTER IV 

 Involved in the Sentiment and Related to It.

This chapter considers power elements of life related to sentiments. Such questions are raised as these: What is the source, or what are the sources, of the power elements of life? What relations do these basic life impulses sustain to sentiments? To what extent does the sentiment develop, organize or direct vital power? How does the functioning of a life impulse differ in the case of a sentiment from that of an automatic complex?

I. The Origin of the Basic Life Impulses and their Rela tions to the Sentiment. 

How think of life in dynamic terms?

It is helpful to think of life itself as a dynamic impulse. 

Bergson says that, “life is like a current passing from germ to germ through the medium of a developing organism". (B12:28). 

Further he says that, "When a tendency splits up in the course of its development, each of the special tendencies which thus arise tries to preserve and develop everything in the primitive tendency that is not incompatible with the work for which it is specialized". (B12:125). Bergson says that realization comes through abandonment as well as through creation. There is the coming into being of a life which in a certain sense already exists. "Thus souls are continually being created, which, nevertheless, in a certain sense pre-existed. They are nothing else than the little rills into which the great river of 117

Social Psychology life divides itself, flowing through the body of humanity. 

The movement of the stream is distinct from the river bed, athough it must adopt its winding course". (B12:284).

Such creative evolution has a definite bearing on the forms of instinctive and of acquired tendencies; it must be remembered that the acquired tendency is definitely based upon the instinctive. We have attempted on an included chart (see opposite page 118) to indicate certain of the major trends of this differentiating life impulse. 

1. A Schematic Representation of the Major Dynamic

Elements Making up Human Life.

The included chart refers only to human impulses; both additions and subtractions would have to be made if the whole range of animal life were included. 

The chart suggests three main channels of the dynamic factor in life. The central one of these (to be active) is of a more general nature and is possessed by the human in common with many of the other animals. The upward branch of the chart (to be social) is intended to express the impulses of human life that are more favorable to the social. order and to individual life at its best. Since individual life tendencies are characterized by plasticity or loose-linkage, it must be assumed that an increasing amount of cognition is necessary for the development of the impulses shown towards the upper part of the chart.
