diagram is arranged on the assumption that there is a check and balance system of the impulses; impulses functioning without the aid of self-control can be kept from destroying the life only by the assertion of counter impulses. On the chart an arrow indicates the forward movement of an impulse and a reversed arrow the backward movement of the counter impulse. At certain places an emotion closely connected with the impulse is stated in parenthesis; other words in parenthesis are intended to clarify the meaning. The inserted diamond toward the upper part of the chart is intended to suggest the meeting of sublimated egoism with altruism. It is to be understood that the differentiating tendency of life into "little rills" or branches extends much beyond the place indicated on the chart. We attempt to represent only the impulses which are supposed to be major. Our main concern in dealing with the social order is to determine which of the impulses of life are major and which relatively minor; any prophecy of social reactions to certain environmental situations must be based upon a determination of major human impulses. 

2. The Relation of Instinctive Dynamic Elements to the Complex.

We use the term complex to include sentiment as well as automatic complex; we think of the sentiment as a con- 

How relate instinctive scious complex. The chart sugimpulses to the comgests that the dynamic elements plex? of life are derived basically from the force in life itself: to be alive is to possess either potential or kinetic energy. The energy of life is maintained and made kinetic through food, the dynamic element of which can be traced back through animal and plant life to the energy of the sun. The available impulses of life have the more immediate source of their power in the systems of the primary emotions. This

Social Psychology way of thinking does not conflict seriously with Mc- Dougall's thought when he says, "it is not gravely misleading to speak of such emotions as becoming organized within the sentiments; although it is more strictly correct to say that the affective-conative disposition of the corresponding instinct becomes thus organized or incorporated". (C90:346). (C90:346). We do not think of instinct or emotion as the ultimate source of the energy; rather it makes the basic life energy available for particular purposes under the demands of environmental conditions. This is akin to what the electric dynamo does in taking the energy from the revolving machine, which in turn gets it from the steam and this from the fire and so back to coal, to plant life and to the sun. In case of the abnormal automatic complex, such as the fear or inferiority complex, there is a visceral-emotional disturbance due to the blocking of the channel of expression of one of the basic impulses. In the case of fear the exact stimulus for the first fright is forgotten or never completely understood, and hence the fear comes to be aroused by a type situation; as a young child one is frightened in a tunnel, and having forgotten this particular experience, later comes to feel fear in every narrow channel through which he passes. This type stimulus for fear is normal in the case of animals which, of course have not the intellectual ability to pick out the exact cause of their fear. The conscious complex or sentiment differs from this automatic one in that there is a definite realization of the object of the sentiment and nothing within the organism itself which is serving to impede the movement of the basic impulse. In either case there is a grouping of impulsive tendencies to the development of an attitude toward an object; in the latter part of this chapter we point out more definite comparisons between these two types of complex. Only such impulses are incorporated into the complex as are useful to it and as

further its attitude in some way. 

How does heredity influence the complex?

Some consideration seems fitting as to the relation of the hereditary life impulses to the acquired complex. We may think of racial determiners as cellular, neural or glandular. 

In the nucleus of the parent cell from which the individual develops are certain determiners determiners called Chromosomes; these are called also "genes", "factors" and the smaller divisions "idants", "ids" and "biophores". In the later development of the individual life these determiners may become neural or glandular. We think of the neural determiner as being made up of afferent or sensory, central and efferent or motor nerve cells. Of such a determiner the central part in the case of humans is the most stable. McDougall says, "the constitution of this part determines in the main the distribution of the nervous impulses, the nervous activities of this central part are the correlates of the affective or emotional aspect or feature of the total psychic process.-It is probable that these central affective parts of the instinctive dispositions have their seat in the basal ganglia of the brain. The evidence for this view has been greatly strengthened by the recent work of Pagano". (90:34). Some recent vivisection work on cats by W. B. Cannon confirms the above conclusion from Pagano. Shand speaks of the instinct as a part of the system of the emotion but seems to think of the neural basis for the determiner in much the same way as does McDougall.

There is developing another way of thinking of hereditary determiners. According to this way of thinking the endocrine hormone (chemical compound secreted by a ductless gland) is the chief determiner of visceral activity, impulse and emotion. Berman says, "A man's nature is essentially his endocrine nature. Primarily, when he is born, he

Social Psychology represents a particular inherited combination of different glands of internal secretion. They, constituting the inventory of his vital stock in trade, start him in life". (14:167).

Or Bandler asserts the idea no less positively when he says, "A child is born with instincts, emotions and endocrines of a type contributed by its parents and inherited from them and their ancestors. Its emotional experiences are evidence of, and are associated with endocrine activity. The type of mentality and mental development are evidences of normal function, hypofunction, or excessive function,for instance of the thyroid and pituitary". (9:9). Certainly it is not well to disregard the accumulating evidence that the vital organs are excited to activity by chemical elements coming into the blood from the endocrine glands. 

Such visceral activity is practically certain to be a cause of organic sensations and hence of emotions. Also there is an increase in the activity of the dynamic elements of life due to the release of glycogen from the liver which counteracts the fatigue elements of the blood and provides the impulse for the emotions; any one recognizes the added strength accompanying the emotion of anger. 

The writer finds no pronounced contradiction in these two explanations of the functioning of innate determiners. 

It is quite possible that a central neural pattern of neurogram exists in the basal ganglia of the cerebrum or elsewhere in the central nervous system and that such series of arcs needs the stimulation of hormones or autocoids (Schafer, Watson, 137:182) from the pineal, pituitary or other endocrine glands. Whatever the exact form of the determiner or determiners it is true that there are innate impulses which make certain actions or achievements satisfactory to human beings. We think of these objectives as being determined innately in only a rather general way and the exact means of their attainment left to be deter-

mined by the postnatal experiences of the individual human being. These innate impulses come to have one of their main channels of expression through acquired complexes; the functioning of these complexes on the highest levels of reaction produces the sentiments. 

II. The Expression of Life Impulses through the 

Sentiment. 

What is the nature of an impulse?

First let us be clear as to the nature of an impulse and then ascertain its function in relation to the sentiment. 

As we use the term, impulse refers to the dynamic or power element back of the action. McDougall says, "It takes the form of mere craving for some undefined goal, of definitely directed desire, of conflict of desires, of resolving, choosing, willing; and, when we are actively occupied in working toward our goal, either by thinking or by bodily activity, this conative experience is complicated and obscured for introspection by the kinaesthetic sensory qualities set up by muscular strains". (C90:320). As is suggested here the impulse may not in any particular case be distinguished from the complex of elements associated with it. It may be true that the impulse as such has no particular feeling quality of its own; most forms of powersteam, electricity, gravity-have not a discernable quality aside from the dynamic attribute. 

1. The Major Impulse of the Sentiment Considered as 

Its Central Organizer.

The main impulse of the sentiment organizes it both as to its content and as to its goal. A statement quoted frequently from McDougall is that, "The instinctive impulses determine the ends of all activities and supply the driving

Social Psychology power by which all mental activities are sustained; and all the complex intellectual apparatus of the most highly de- How does the impulse veloped mind is but a means toward these ends, is but the instrument organize the sentiby which these impulses seek their ment? satisfactions, while pleasure and pain do but serve to guide them in their choice of the means". (90:45). We are not assuming such complete innate dominance as this on the part of the impulse; we think of the innate impulse as supplying the power and in a very general way suggesting the goal satisfactory to the individual. 

Intelligence may have to clarify the goal, choose between different satisfactory goals as well as determine the means for reaching a goal under particular environmental conditions. Disregarding the distinction between innate and acquired we may say that what the basic impulses do for life as a whole, the central impulse of the sentiment does for its organized system. We do well to think of the sentiment as one of the organized units which enters into the composition of the whole self. The main dynamic trend of the sentiment organizes it in two ways: (1) first as to the instinctive and emotional elements which compose it; (2) as to its major attitude toward its object. No instinctive or emotional element can be admitted into the sentiment unless it is in accord with the main impulse of the sentiment. For this reason tenderness must be omitted from the system of hate and disgust from that of love. Also through the automatic working of its major impulse there is developed in the sentiment a type of attitude toward its object. Through the working of wonder and subjection, excited by definite environmental stimuli, one builds up admiration which by gradual stages passes into the sentiment of regard and hence causes the self to maintain a submissive attitude toward certain standards of the object

held in regard. In like manner the distinctive tendencies of love and hate center about the automatic (in part acquired) operation of certain dynamic tendencies and thus promote the growth of these types of sentiments. 

2. The Impulse as Determiner of the Force and Stability of the Sentiment. 

How does the impulse of the sentiment promote stability?

Further, the dynamic tendency of the sentiment promotes a steady type of relation between self and the object of the complex. In their unorganized innate form human impulses suggest ends in only a very vague way. This makes possible the more ready adjustment of the growing life to its changing environs. But as a substitute for the instinctive guidance necessary to the lower animals there develop sentiments on the part of humans. This implies that cognitive tendencies are bound to be more potent in the determination of human ends as compared to the objectives of the lower animals. 

Human ends developing in relation to artificial and environmental conditions are too varied and complex to be dominated by native impulses. Various ends are suggested to consciousness by native impulses but must be evaluated and chosen by cognition and volition. Also native tendencies among humans are subject to sublimation and degeneration and hence to marked variations. It is in connection with the sentiment or conscious complex that the impulse becomes so directed as to function automatically and constantly with a certain tendency toward a certain object. This automatic functioning has the effect of eliminating deliberative tendencies and logical decisions and hence of promoting stability of operation. Thus the deep-seated and regular functioning of the sentiment maintains steady attitudes on the part of self.

Social Psychology III. The Influence of the Sentiment Itself upon Its Con tained Impulses.

It is recognized generally that moral character is an individual achievement rather than a racial endowment. As the sentiments are important elements in the makeup of character it is natural to assume that they are acquired in their form as sentiments. To what extent does a sentiment represent an individual achievement? 

1. Individual Differences in Sentiments of the Same

General Type. 

Heredity determines the types of sentiments which may be acquired through individual growth and experience. As was indicated above, the racial determiners on the part of 

How is a sentiment individualistic? the growing individual consist, at least partially, in neural patterns and depend in part upon the development of certain endocrine glands. At least there are certain determiners which give to human beings standardized types of emotions and impulses through which the life energy finds expression; also these emotions and impulses have an inherent tendency to become systematized into larger complexes which we call sentiments. Starch says, "Differences among human beings are quantitative rather than qualitative. That is, all human beings have the same reflexes, instincts, and capacities; all have the powers of perception, discrimination, attentiveness, retentiveness, reasoning, and so forth". (B126:26). On this account we expect to find the types of sentiment such as love, hate, and regard, prevalent in the life of every normal human being; at least it is normal for these types of sentiments to show a tendency toward development under standardized environmental conditions. But the prevalence of sentiments

of each type and the object of any particular sentiment is an individual matter. Sentiments of love and of hate may prevail to a very different extent in the lives of two individuals. Also the love of a particular type of object may exist on a very crude or on a very sublimated level. 

Two individuals may love music, but the one music of a very worthy and the other of a very crude type. Hatred may be in both of two lives, but express itself almost constantly in one and but very rarely in the other. Thus any particular sentiment may be individualistic and individuality may be shown as to the prevalence of any one of the types of sentiments. 

2. The Organization of Instinctive Impulses within the 

System of the Sentiment. stinctive tendencies?

It is coming to be recognized rather commonly that instinctive tendencies on the part of human beings are not definite as to stimulus and response; this frequently is 

Why does a sentiment spoken of as adaptability, modineed to organize in- fiability, plasticity, or loose-linkage. Authors differ as to the extent to which they assume human nature to be organized by individual experience. After considering a list of instincts named by Thorndike, Watson says, "We are inclined to take the point of view here also that most of these asserted instincts are really consolidations of instinct and habit". (137:261). Bernard says, "This view of emotion is in conflict with the theory of Mc- Dougall which holds that the emotion is the consciousness aspect or correlate of the unchanging central segment of the instinctive action mechanism". (15:471). (15:471). For instance in the case of fear Shand points out that it expresses itself in concealment, silence, clinging to something, a

Social Psychology shrinking from something, immobility or simulation of death, crying out for protection, defense by aggressive action, disinterested action on behalf of young, as well es in flight. (121:xix). We may term flight a typical expression of fear but have little other reason for connecting the two specifically. The type of protective action carried out by the animal depends upon its anatomy and physiology; think, for instance, of a snail expressing fear in flight.

Apart from crying, swallowing, digesting and other forms of visceral activity, the human being has no instincts if we use the term in the sense of a definite response to a definite stimulus. But certainly the human has many complex innate impulses and seeks to attain or avoid various ends. 

But the means by which these ends are attained or avoided are developed by individual experience. This makes the human infant, as compared with the young of the lower animals, very little prepared by heredity for the struggle of life. But this inequality is apparent rather than real. 

With every generation of human beings cognition promotes better ways of attaining human ends, whereas the effectiveness of such animals as the ant and bee remains practically stationary through the ages. But cognitive-voluntary control of life reactions is not sufficient to meet life situations. 

Reactions to meet life needs must not only become automatic, as in habits, but must become automatic with the power of a life impulse. To meet this demand there develops the organizing tendency of the sentiment. By such scheme of organization there is retained most of the values of animal instinct and is added the factors of cognition and individuality.

Let us consider somewhat more definitely how the sentiment builds up its automatic system for the control of the impulses and emotions. A sentiment starts with the prevalence of one of the native impulses. Tenderness be-

1 gins to prevail in the impulse which is to develop into the central tendency of love. The impulse associated with disgust or anger constitutes a natural starting place for the How does the senti- sentiment of hatred. But just as ment organize innate any animal or plant upon taking food into its system at once transtendencies? forms it for its own purposes and towards its own ends, so the growing pattern of the sentiment comes readily to redirect the impulses taken into its system. An impulse always asserts its own basic tendency but in accord with the demands of the sentiment; anger always asserts energy against an opponent, but in the system of hate against the object of the sentiment while in the system of love it works against the opponents of the object of the sentiment. Or two primary impulses may so combine within the sentiment as to create a new tendency; anger and tenderness may combine and produce reproach.

McDougall insists that certain of such combinations are possible only within the system of a sentiment. Again the basic impulse may be sublimated and take a form not easily recognized; as indicated on our chart the sex impulse or play may be basic to various forms of creative idealism. 

Certain impulses because of their essential nature cannot be admitted into the sentiment; this is no more remarkable than that certain forms of plants and animals cannot absorb certain foods but either reject them or are poisoned by them. Tenderness as an impulse is too divergent from hate to be admitted into its system as also disgust is too radically opposed to love; this assumes no more cognition of ends than the rejection of poisons by the stomach or type of building material by a bird. But if we desire to think of the selection or rejection in a more intellectualized way we may think of how readily a splendid man who is rich and popular will be selected by a fraternity or the objections

Social Psychology raised to the admission of a polygamous or slave state into our union.

Since such an organization may develop either on an automatic or conscious basis it is well to consider to what extent the formation of a sentiment is under the control Are sentiments de. of cognition and volition. There veloped consciously? is likely to be very little conscious recognition of the selecting or rejecting of elements entering into the various relationships. 

However the selecting of an object for the sentiment involves some form of attention and is to a greater or less degree a conscious process. Also cognition is used in the interest of impulses to discover elements in the object which accord with the tendencies of the impulses; to love an enemy we must make a conscious effort to look below the surface to discover qualities which are lovely. For the most part however attraction or repulsion is closely confined to types. One is attracted to his own race, species, age, moral type, supplementary type, etc. In like manner he is repelled by the opposite of that by which he is attracted. If one is attracted to two friends and must choose between them intellect is likely to exercise some part in the estimation of the worth of each. Also if one recognizes the desirability of building up a certain form of attractionto the environment in which he has to live, to his business associates, to a study for which he has enrolled-his cognition may take the lead in detecting "signs of loveliness" in the object. But it is true for the most part that cognition works under the control of an impulse or of impulses during the formation of a sentiment or series of sentiments. This does not imply the dominance of heredity over environment as the type of impulse stimulated depends upon the total environmental situation; the conditions of child labor may produce cynicism and hatred while happy home and school

life are likely to produce trust and love. 

3. The Tendency of the Sentiment to Originate Its Own Power.

We have considered in a general way the origin of vital force. We consider here more definitely the relation of the 

Where does the senti- sentiment to the power elements of life. Love and hate frequently are ment get its power? spoken of as forces; it is asserted that love never fails and hence is equal to all opposition brought against it. Note the impression of quiet power involved in Paul's description of love. "Love is patient and kind. Love knows neither envy nor jealousy. Love is not forward and self-assertive, nor boastful and conceited. She does not behave unbecomingly, nor seek to aggrandize herself, nor blaze out in passionate anger, nor brood over wrongs". (Weymouth). We are assuming that the sentiment utilizes the power inherent in its embodied elements; our chart traces this power ultimately to the life impulse itself. Power which is potential may be made available in several different ways. We may think of the dynamic of life as coming directly from the food digested and absorbed into the blood. We may think of the sense end-organs as translating the dynamic of the stimulus and thus transforming it into a neural current. We may think of the central neural patterns of the instincts as being the immediate dynamos for the transformation of life energy into usable form, or the hormones of the endocrine glands the adrenals, for example-as releasers of energy stored in potential form in certain of the vital organs such as the glycogen of the liver. Probably we are not able at present to distinguish definitely between producing and releasing energy or to pass with assurance among these different possibilities as to the immediate source of the energy of the impulse.

Social Psychology 

We conclude that the sentiment as such is not the original source of the power elements which it utilizes. 

But there still remains the possibility that the organized Does the sentiment in- sentiment may utilize or bring to bear more effectively the power increase the power of its herent in it. The United States as elements? a nation certainly is more effective as a world power than would be all of the states operating separately. This is true partially because it can prevent friction among its contained states and can concentrate its power under one leadership. In like manner the sentiment, because of its organization, can bring a more steady application of power upon a particular place and thus secure a result which otherwise would not be secured; again we would warn the reader not to assume that this must be done consciously as it would be in the case of the United States. 

Also the sentiment by exerting control over its constituent elements may give more potent expression to one of such by inhibiting its natural counteractants; as our chart indicates we are conceiving the basic impulses to be arranged on a check and balance system. Thus anger may be more potent if fear is inhibited from exerting its natural restraint. 

Further, it is possible that more power may be concentrated upon a definite point by uniting two power elements at that point; anger at the opposition and fear of the intentions of the enemy readily may concentrate our antipathy and increase our power of opposition. So it seems that a sentiment without increasing the potential energy of the life can make more effective the distribution and application of that energy.

IV. Life Impulses not Involved in the Sentiment But 

Related to It. 

We consider here: (1) the life impulse functioning apart

from any complex; (2) the dynamic of the automatic as compared to the conscious complex. 

1. The Life Impulse Functioning apart from the 

Organized Complex.

A life impulse is not certain to become organized in any system. Various forms of mania are recognized and classified; the mania usually is characterized by an expansive emotional state with excitement and a violent impulse to action. The kleptomaniac is af- 

Should a life impulse function apart from organized an plex? com- fected with a violent and insane impulse to steal and may do so regardless of the need of the article stolen. Any strong impulse of life may come to such a violent detached form of expression as to give grounds for the assumption that it is uncontrollable and hence that the individual is insane in that particular. 

It is quite evident that impulses functioning apart from the organized systems of character are dangerous to character and to individual and social life. A muscle may function more powerfully in a cramp or under the influence of hypnotism but such functioning is not normal or beneficial. 

In like manner an impulse may express great power in an unregulated way but such power is apt to be destructive to the values involved in moral character as well as to the unity of the social order. 

2. The Automatic Complex and Its Relation to the 

Sentiment.

The term "complex" is coming to be used rather commonly; it is used perhaps most frequently in relation to fear or inferiority complexes. It refers to a deep-seated organization of impulses and feelings which functions in a

Social Psychology prevailingly automatic way. In the paragraphs following we make some comparisons between the automatic complex and the sentiment considered from the dynamic point of view. (1). The Dynamic of the Complex on the Automatic as 

Compared to the Conscious Level.

Any group of impulses functioning on a comparatively automatic level is characterized by a considerable lack of restraint. The term "automatic"

Is an automatic complex probably is one of degree and more potent than a conscious one? should not be taken to exclude all consciousness. Even under conditions of relaxation, such as daydreaming or dreaming during sleep, the conscious impulse is restrained, at least in part, by respect for identity, reality, morality, etc. In other words the extent of conscious relaxation is never complete although we may not realize the fact that censorship is being applied. This normal control is indicated by the response of the muscles under such conditions as epilepsy and hypnotism. The muscles are capable of a power of rigidity and contraction which they do not have under conditions of normal cortical control. This indicates that the cerebral cortex normally is exerting inhibitory influence without our being aware of that fact. The automatic complex functions in a realm as free from cortical control as is any realm of acquired functioning; we prefer to express this fact in this way rather than to assume a near-conscious or unconscious realm of mind. The fact of the at least partial, functional detachment of the automatic complex from the conscious areas of the cerebral cortex means its separation from the guidance of the organized ideas. Such detachment from organized cognition does not imply that the impulse functions to no end but rather that the end of its

functioning is not understood by self. A person may be frightened by open spaces and experience a very real and definite fright which prompts self-protection without being conscious of the cause of the fear. In cases in which the cause of the impulse is not understood the effect of it is likely to be in the way of feeling and imagery; if one has forgotten the real cause he imagines a cause so as to make his action have a semblance of reasonableness. But due to the ease with which expression is found in imagination and emotion the automatic complex, which complex tends toward such expression, gives the impression of great potency. 

A life expression of phantasy may be found easier than a single facing of reality. Groves records the case of a man, "returning to his home at night, found all his family dead.

He entered the house a sane man. When he stepped over the threshold a few minutes later, he was hopelessly insane.

Confronted by an insupportable calamity he had found refuge in lasting fantasy". (59.232). The fact that living in a life of fantasy is easier than facing reality should warn us that we are likely to be deceived as to the actual power of the automatic complex. Let us consider this matter somewhat more carefully. 

(2). The Relation of the Number of Elements of the 

Complex to Its Dynamic Tendency. 

It should be kept in mind that the conscious complex or sentiment normally is composed of more elements than is 

How does complexity the automatic complex. This is tend to affect affect po- due primarily to the fact that there is a conscious realization of the tency? object of the sentiment. A great deal of the functioning of cognition is for the purpose of determining means for the realization of the end of the basic impulse of the sentiment; intelligence, as we have

Social Psychology said, also tends to choose between objects at times and to clarify the idea of an object or to interpret or idealize it. 

Also there are cases in which cognition is an effective releaser of energy; according to our basic conception of the source of energy, to release it is not essentially different from producing it because all energy comes from basic life processes. Take such a case as that of the polar party finding their last supply of food destroyed and consequently giving themselves up to despair. But now suppose that one of the party suggests the idea that there may be seals under the ice and that there are means at hand for securing them for food. Note how readily the suggestion releases renewed energy and makes it available for continued effort. 

So normally in connection with the conscious complex cognition constantly is finding attributes of the object of the sentiment which serve as effective stimuli to its impulses. 

The friend is idealized and hence qualities posited in his life which serve as stimuli to the impulse of friendship. 

Also the enemy is interpreted as being very hostile and deceptive and this interpretation stirs to action the impulses of rage and vengeance; the basic impulse of the sentiment of course regulates the interpretation but it is the interpretation which releases the energy. In either case the dynamic expression of the sentiment is made more potent by the interpretative tendencies of its cognitive powers. Also the conscious complex is more potent because of its tendency to incorporate a larger number of affectiveconative tendencies. This is true for two reasons: (1) more effective combinations are possible among a larger number of elements; (2) a more potent release of energy is possible in the case of the sentiment because its organization of a larger number of elements restrains the natural check and balance which obtains among unorganized impulses.

(3). The Bearing of Conscious Purpose upon the Dynamic Possibilities of the Complex. 

How does awareness of purpose affect power of impulse?

It is evident that an act may be purposeful without any conscious recognition of the fact on the part of the actor. 

An eel in starting its long journey across the Atlantic Ocean to its breeding place in the West Indies certainly is performing a purposeful act but just as certainly cannot be aware of the purpose of its act. Between clear recognition of ends and automatic functioning of reactions without consciousness of ends there exist all gradations of cognitive discernment of objectives. The hound on a trail may have some image or idea of the purpose of his action but such probably is not so clearly defined as that of a little child searching in a cupboard for cookies or candy. Also it is probable that no human understands in full the meaning of the impulses which spur him to action. With the human infant pain or discomfort produces crying without a clear recognition of the need. With fuller experience the discomfort of hunger is differentiated from cold and further the implications of hunger are defined as to the type of food desired. But whether the most completely satisfactory kind of food has been secured cannot be known; perhaps in the future types of food will be developed which satisfy hunger more completely. An automatic complex such as a phobia is purposeful in a crude sense. For instance, a general situation is feared and the fear, true to its nature, finds expression in some form of protective action; with the development of habit such protective action is likely to become standardized in type. Such expression is likely to be very primitive in its nature. Groves records that, "A young woman suffering from psycho-neurosis was greatly helped by studying her strange ceremonial observances from the viewpoint

Social Psychology of primitive society. She found herself repeating actions characteristic of primitive behavior. She also found that fear had much to do with her illness and was the force behind her strange conduct". (59:85). If purposes of action are not understood definitely the impulse is likely to express itself either in imagination or emotion or in some primitive and symbolic form of action. But since both such forms of expression call for less energy than the intelligent meeting of objective situations it follows that the clearer the recognition of ends the greater the demand made upon the dynamic of the complex. 

We conclude that a conscious complex is working toward an end that is more difficult of attainment than an automatic complex. This fact must be kept in mind if we are to equate potency on anything like an equal basis. Any 

What bearing has the conscious objective must take into type of objective on consideration reality, identity and the power needed? morality in some sense of the latter word. It is evident that these restrictions are felt to a much lesser degree, if at all, in the automatic complex. Myerson says, "Whether we accept or reject the Freudian philosophy, and whether we accept or reject the play of the subconscious, this can be assumed that every human being is a pot boiling with desires, passions, lusts, purposes, ideas, and emotions, some of which he clearly recognizes and clearly admits, and some of which he does not clearly recognize and would deny. 

These desires, passions, purposes, etc. are not in harmony one with another". (A125:66-67). In the expression of these conflicting elements in the dream the creative imagination is forced to disregard identity of person, continuity of time, reality of cosmic laws and largely demands of morality. If these are not disregarded there can be no expression of groups of unorganized or partially organized

impulses. The psychoanalysts assert that these objectionable impulses are camouflaged by a non-objectionable code language of symbols. Whether this is true or not, it remains true that expressions in the realm of imagery are of a sort entirely different from motor expressions intended to meet objective reality in an intelligent way. The difficulty of attaining satisfaction on the part of the conscious impulse is increased further by the demand of the conscious expression for sublimation. Consciousness demands that the self care not merely for the bodily and immediate interests of the friend but as well for his permanent and psychic interests; and, in case of conflict, for the latter rather than the former. The carrying out of the impulse of the sentiment is much more difficult than the expression of the automatic complex and hence the power back of it must be greater to attain its ends. It follows that we are dealing with a more potent impulse in the case of the sentiment.

CHAPTER V The  on the Part of Normal Human Beings. 

In this chapter we try to determine: How does the sentiment of the young child differ from that of the adult? How does a sentiment begin? What happens to the emotions and impulses of a sentiment as it grows? What development in knowledge is needed to cause the sentiment to grow properly? What development in self-control is needed during the growth of the sentiment?

I. Contrasting Attitudes of Childhood and Maturity 

Involving Sentiments. 

The sentiments of infant and adult are contrasted: (1) as to the nature of their object and the continued relation of self to it; (2) as to the significance of the sentiment for the life of the individual. 

1. The Tendency of the Concrete Personal Friendship to Persist.

We are coming to recognize that the genetic stages of human ontogeny are not nearly so distinct as was for a time believed. The child in the first grade can perceive, What is the nature of imagine, abstract, reason, and perthe child's sentiments? form practically all of the mental processes on material within its own experience. But in the realm of sentiments there are certain distinctions from maturity which are real and which deserve our attention. In the very nature of the case the 143

Social Psychology sentiment of the child must have distinctive marks. The young child can have no abstract sentiments because of lack of abstract ideas. Thus we can expect to find no marked love for God, country, church, school or home in the abstract. There may be, however, concrete substitutes for all of these. Also inadequate cognition prevents a distinct individualizing of the object. The kindergarten child is drawn to playmates of his own age but is not likely to be distinctly attached to a particular individual; adjustments can be made even to another "mother" very readily in early childhood. Because of this attachment to type rather than to individual there is little recognition of the value of the individual and little determination to retain a particular person as a friend. There is a marked inclination to overlook differences of sex or race and to express only the more basic impulses; the tendency to run and play together is much more potent than the remembrance of any recent quarrel or supposed injustice. Also because the definite purposes of life are not as yet clearly established there is likely not to be a definite selection and maintenance of a particular friend or enemy. Thus we can expect the friendships of early childhood to be temporary and adjusted to types rather than to individuals; or perhaps we should say to any individual of a type, there is no conscious recognition of the type as such. 

2. The Significance of Concrete Friendships Regardless of their Persistence.

Because of the changing object of the child's attachment we are not to conclude that the friendship is not real or important. Attachment to a particular object seems to the adult to be the central core of a friendship. Because of this the adult is apt to call a friendship fickle that does

not maintain a constant attachment to a particular object. 

But this is only another example of the teacher's fallacy; 

Why is a child's i.e., the tendency to suppose that others have the same mental refriendship important? actions as self. We must recognize that there is distinct value in a friendship apart from the value of a friend. The tenderness of the child is of the same sort as that of the adult and is felt deeply. Because it may be directed only for a short time toward one individual is no reason for supposing its importance to be less. 

The young child is not as likely to understand the deepest needs of his friend but his friendly impulses are just as deep and true. Only to an extent but slightly different does the adult understand the deepest needs of his friend. But the child's friendship is important, more because of its subjective, than because of its objective results. It is important in the same sense that the blade is important to the mature ear-without the blade there would be no ear. The impulses and emotions basic to the sentiment need opportunity for expression or the sentiment will not develop properly; probably one of the best preparatory influences gained from playing with dolls is this emotional preparation. Whatever can be said in favor of the value of the expression of the impulses basic to right types of regard and friendship also can be said against the expression of such impulses as tend to develop hatreds; probably race, national and various forms of group and party hatreds are started toward development in the life of the individual even before he enters the kindergarten.

II. The Beginnings of a Sentiment. 

We consider beginnings of sentiments here: (1) as to the impulses and emotions involved in them: (2) as to the forms of attention and cognition displayed.

Social Psychology 

1. The Beginning of the Sentiment in the Way of Impulse and Emotion.

McDougall says, "Each sentiment has a life history, like every other vital organization. It is gradually built up, increasing in complexity and strength, and may continue to grow indefinitely, or may enter into a period of decline, and may decay slowly or rapidly, partially or completely". (90:168).,To the study of beginnings and growth of the normal sentiment we give the remaining portion of this chapter.

Let us think first of the emotion of tenderness with its impulse to share. Authors differ considerably as to their 

Are altruistic tenden- conception of the functions percies basically racial? formed by heredity and by environment in the determination of life traits. We are not to think of environment as introducing new determiners but only as altering the expression of basic racial tendencies. Some writers emphasize the possibility that basic impulses are altruistic as well as egoistic. Holmes says, "this intense egoism is paralleled and often opposed by a profound altruism". (70:250). Thilly says, "As a matter of fact every human being is both egoistic or selfish, and altruistic and unselfish". (129:266).

Ribot concludes that, "what we find at the base of domestic aggregates is tender emotion, the genesis of altruism, but restricted to a closed group, without expansive force or elasticity". (112:281). Thomas also says that, "Morality, sympathy and altruism are of tribal origin, and have their roots in (1) the love of offspring, (2) the sensitivity connected with courtship, and (3) the comradeship which arises among men in prosecuting vital interests in common". (130:120). Any careful observer will recognize that the impulse to share is clearly evident at times in the conduct of the young child; this needs no more explanation than any

other basic impulse. If we are to be scientific we must take what we find in child expression and not assume a dominance of racial egoism rather than racial altruism. 

The roots of whatever altruism the race needs we may expect to find among the innate tendencies of the child. The fact of group life at all is dependent upon sympathetic responsiveness and individual tendencies towards co-operation and adjustment. The assumption of a gregarious or grouping impulse lays the basis for active sympathy, individual alignments for pleasure and profit, and on a higher level for real friendship. These tendencies are most commonly developed on the part of the child subsequent to their expression by the parent. Such imitation is not exact but sufficient to arouse the tender emotion and impulse to share on the part of the child. It is not necessary to assume that the impulse to give implies any idea of sacrifice on the part of the child who is the giver; if later the child desires the object he may grab it again after having given it. This means only that the impulse to give, like that to grab, is innate and since cognition is not innate, the motivation of the impulse and its intelligent direction must wait for developing understanding. But we should be clear as to the fact that friendship has a racial basis in the primary emotion of tenderness with its accompanying impulse to share. 

Tendencies making for the development of sentiments are connected with the primary emotions of wonder, anger, What primary emo and disgust. Disgust, with its imtions and impulses are pulse to push aside and anger, with its impulse to strike or oppose, basic to sentiments? have a place basic to the beginnings of hatred. In much the same way the tendency to submit is basic to admiration and regard. The emotion of wonder with its expression in curiosity is central to the beginning

Social Psychology of any sentiment. It is obvious that a child never would develop a permanent attitude toward an object without paying attention to that object. At first attention is likely to be of the objective or sensory type-it is caught through one of the senses by a stimulus produced by an attribute of the object. The child attends to that which makes the most pronounced sensory impression; this may be the new, the bright, the moving, the loud, etc. Surprise seems a natural introductory step to wonder and admiration. Surprise we believe to be only a heightened nervous condition due to automatic reactions to a sudden stimulus. But surprise is the natural signal for attention. The child's world is made up of changing situations leading to many and varied surprises. Closely following the giving of attention to an object it is natural for the child to manipulate the object so as to get from it the maximum of sensory impressions. Such manipulation is likely to lead to feelings of attraction or repulsion and to perceptions as to the characteristics of the object if not of the essential nature of the object itself. In the case of animate objects such perceptual acquaintance is likely to be followed by play, pain and injury leading to avoidance and antipathy, or mutual pleasure and enjoyment leading to continued association and friendship. 

2. The Beginning of the Sentiment in the Way of Attention and Perceptual Cognition. 

We have been considering mainly the affective and conative beginnings of the sentiment. We turn now to the considerations of beginnings along lines of attention and cognition.

We have noted the connection of attention with the sensory impressions and the impulses involved in surprise and curiosity. We desire to indicate some of the forms of attention and what they have to do with the beginnings of

beginnings of a sentiment? sentiments. Attention is essentially the focusing of mind upon a limited area of experience; this may be upon the sense impression of an object or the image or idea of a subject. We consider it best to 

How does sensory attention relate to the classify forms of attention in accord with the agencies which maintain the focus of mind; no teacher will regret the omission of the customary negative syllables ("in" and "non") associated with the word "voluntary". The most primitive of the types of attention is the objective or sensory. In this case the focus of mind is maintained by the force of a stimulus acting through one or more of the sense channels. Attention strictly of this type is maintained only so long as a particular stimulus is able to prevail over all comers. 

Sensory attention begins to relate the child to the objective world and to build up the more stable attitudes toward the attributes of objects. But it is doubtful whether there are definite sensory stimuli which arouse human innate emotions; the condition of plasticity or loose-linkage seems to prevent this. Aside from actually beginning to fall, Watson mentions only sudden noises as causes of fear on the part of human infants. (137:199-200). It does not take a very wide experience with human infants to notice that sudden noises are not at all sure to produce fear on the part of all of them; to conclude that this is a normal stimulus for fear on the part of all infants would seem to require much more evidence than Watson presents. Starting to fall is the beginning of actual danger and not merely a sensation indicating danger. Starting to fall seems a stimulus of a very different sort from the ordinary sensoryparceptual type. But it is quite possible that there are certain preferred inlets of attraction and repulsion such as attraction to one's own species, age, or sex. Certainly

Social Psychology there are attributes of objects which catch and hold sensory attention. Also the child is attracted by the presence and activity of other children. Naturally the attraction leads to some form of play and other forms of association leading in the direction of friendship. The arousal of aversions, repulsion and disgust, which constitute a basis for hatred, comes somewhat later; a scratching noise, slimy or wet objects, a mud puddle or even snakes and worms do not arouse aversions very readily on the part of the small child. But later when these tendencies do develop, it is through the channels of sensory attention.

Even among animals lower than humans there develop factors for the control of attention from within the organism. The impulse of hunger, anger or sex may direct the 

What bearing has attention of the animal in spite of emotional attention opposing sensory stimuli. Attention directed and held by any such upon the beginning factor from within the organism we of a sentiment? are calling emotional or instinctive attention. The extreme helplessness of the human infant makes him dependent upon his elders to a marked degree. 

Thus the impulse to eat, to play, to manipulate, etc., come to find satisfaction or inhibition through contacts with human beings. The impulse to play which develops quite early in the life causes the child to attend to other children as possible playmates. Little discrimination is likely to be used in the selection of a playmate as the demands of this impulse can be satisfied without such special attention. The denial of the satisfaction of any such basic impulse is likely to produce anger and hence make a beginning of a form of hatred; the reader is not to suppose that a sentiment develops from a few expressions of an emotion, but only that this is the way a beginning is made.

On account of this lack of discrimination attachments and repulsions are likely at first to be for types rather than for specific individuals.

The distinctively human form of attention is voluntary. 

The controlling element in this case is the organized self with its power to hold attention on ideas relating to selected ends. The exercise of voluntary attention presupposes a 

What does voluntary conception of an end or purpose, attention contribute to a developing senti. ment? the unification of self and the power of self-control over basic impulses. The same lack which makes voluntary attention practically absent in early child life accounts for the lack of discrimination in the selection of objects for the sentiments and thus for the absence of tendencies to promote or retard the welfare of particular individuals. The careful selection of a friend presupposes experience with different types, an ability to construct and compare ideas relating to each type, and a scale of values, in accord with which a particular type is to be called better or best. Why such an accomplishment is impossible for early childhood can be understood readily. It is natural at first to make discriminations on the basis of the sensory attributes of the object. 

Such discrimination notes color of hair, form of body, height, etc. Such attributes are to be found on the part of different objects of the same general type and hence need not cause attachment to a particular individual of that type. It is only when the sensory and instinctive stimuli are put aside resolutely and the seeker determines to find the real object back of its attributes that individual and distinctive characteristics are found. The most completely formed sentiment must wait for the development of voluntary attention; the sentiment never can be stable until its real object is defined and understood and that which is en-

Social Psychology during becomes its object. One cannot serve the real needs of another until his real needs are comprehended; a dog may be a loyal slave to a particular master but cannot understand the real need of the master well enough to serve his highest interests. A dog might submit to a cruel master but could not teach the master the harm which the expression of cruelty does to the one who expresses it; such service can be rendered only by the intelligent and discriminating friend. The conclusion is that the development of the power of voluntary attention is a prerequisite to the attainment of the highest form of a sentiment. 

III. Stages in the Growth of a Developing Sentiment. 

We consider here the growing sentiment: (1) as to the content of its emotional and dynamic elements; (2) as to the increasing clearness of the intelligence back of it; (3) as to the effectiveness of the self-assertion involved in it. 

1. The Growth of the Sentiment in Complexity of Emotional Units. 

We have spoken of the possibility of considering either the emotion or the instinct the constituent unit of the senas 

Why think of the timent. Speaking of the greater and lesser systems involved in primary emotion character Shand says, "Now the basic unit of the sentiment? among these lesser systems that are, or may be, organized in greater, are the primary emotions with their connected instincts". (121:27). We believe there are several reasons why it is better to speak of the primary emotion as the unit of the sentiment. (1). The emotion is the more definite conscious element and hence has taken to itself the more definite terminology. Names, which are understood the world

around, have been applied to the more clear-cut of the emotions. The instincts are classified so variously and are so adaptable in both their afferent and efferent parts that reference by name to an instinct does not arouse the same clear meaning as reference to an emotion. (2). Of the whole stimulus-response tendency the emotion is by far the most stable element. It is preceded by various organic and kinaesthetic sensations and preceded or accompanied by different images and ideas but is in itself a conscious affective element of experience with a definitely recognizable quality. (3). The primary emotions are common to every normal human being and differ only quantitatively among different individuals. Impulses which probably are more basic than emotions are so little conscious that they have received few definite names; for instance, the impulse to play is capable of such varied interpretation as to call forth a series of some seven theories as to its meaning-see text page 94. For these reasons we think it best to speak of the primary emotions as the unitary elements of the sentiments. Any differentiation of parts for purposes of discussion and explanation has, of course, nothing to do with the united functioning of the whole. 

The emotion of wonder performs an initial function to practically any type of sentiment. It is plain that before 

What place does wonan individual can develop a fixed der have in a develop- attitude toward an object that his ing sentiment? attention must be directed in a rather definite and continuous way to that object. Sensory attention-attention dominated by the quality of an object through a sense channel-is not sufficiently lasting to build up such a relationship. Wonder or curiosity is basic to that type of interest which holds the attention on an object until the object as such becomes definitely distinguished; the object itself must be distinguished from the attributes which it may have in com-

Social Psychology mon with many other objects of its class. Continued attention to an object may develop admiration, tenderness, repulsion or disgust-depending on the nature of the object —and thus be introductory to any of the three major types of sentiments-regard, love or hate. Thus, wonder performs a very definite function in the initiation of practically any sentiment.

Tenderness has a relationship to the sentiment of love or friendship which it does not have to regard or hate; its impulse is contrary to the general attitude of hatred and interferes in a way with negative self-feeling which has such a dominant place in regard. But the emotion of tenderness has a major place in the sentiments of the general type of love and friendship. In these sentiments tenderness gives the tone of its impulse to the sentiment as such and thus What is the function comes to exercise a dominating of tenderness in the control over the other emotional sentiment of friend. elements involved in the sentiment. ship or love?

We are not thinking of the development and assertion of influence of any emotion apart from the intellectual factors of mind. Probably it requires an element of preception to develop any emotion. It is to be noted that James emphasizes the word "perception" in the following quotation. He says, "My thesis-is that the bodily changes follow directly the PERCEPTION of the exciting fact, and that our feeling of the same changes as they occur is the emotion”. (A73:13). We assume this emphasis to imply that these bodily changes do not take place without some understanding of the nature of the object. But such an emotion as wonder has need of a less fully developed perception than has tenderness. Mere sensory elements are sufficient to arouse surprise and wonder follows readily after a recognition that the object is new and strange. But for the de-

velopment of tenderness the object must be construed as worthy of benefits given or the cause of benefits received by the observer. With a favorable object the sequence of development is likely to be: sensory attention on an attribute, interest in the object, recognition of it as a giver or receiver of benefits, the emotion of tenderness felt toward it; such development is likely to proceed automatically and thus the subject may not be conscious of each advancing stage.

Once tenderness has come to have a place in the growing complex it is bound to exert a definite influence. Tender- 

How does tenderness ness felt toward an object means influence the growing that the self is beginning to approsentiment? priate that object. And as soon as 

I begin to appropriate as mine a tree, mountain, person, philosophy, I begin to feel elation at what I consider its prosperity and subjection or depression at what seems to me its injury or lack of success. 

Also if an object which in any sense has become mine is in danger I readily feel fear for its safety. Probably the development of fear is later than that of elation and subjection, as it calls for a more complete understanding of the nature of the object and of its relation to the environment. The stimulus for anger is the blocking of another impulse and hence we may expect altruistic anger on behalf of the object of a sentiment built around tenderness only subsequent to the development of the other contained emotions. Fear for the welfare of the object probably precedes anger toward the cause which endangers the welfare of the object. 

We have just been speaking of the dominating influence of tenderness over the emotions contained in the sentiment of love or friendship. Disgust or anger exercises a kindred office in the case of hatred and subjection in the case of the sentiment of regard or respect. The impulse of one of

Social Psychology What emotions domi. 

nate the sentiments of regard and hate? these basic emotions comes to dominate the sentiment and to direct the attitude of the whole complex. Thus in hatred 

I come to feel elation at the success of the enemies of the object and subjection in the sense of depression at the success of the object. Also in hate I fear the object itself and my anger is apt to be aroused by this object. A kindred effect takes place in the sentiment of regard or respect but in a much less noticeable way; the emotions involved are much less violent in their expression. I feel somewhat elated when another admires the object of my regard. I feel some fear that the object which I admire may suffer harm and some anger against the one threatening such harm. But due to the fact of my admiration of the object, rather than its definite appropriation, I am not likely to be greatly aroused emotionally by either the furtherance or the inhibition of its welfare. into a sentiment?

In addition to the primary emotions mentioned there are likely to develop certain secondary emotions within the system of the sentiments; we think of the derived emotions as being functionally rather than structurally related to the sentiments. (See chart on page 113). Due to the influence of How do secondary tenderness within the sentiment an emotions become built object which functioning without the sentiment would produce disgust, within the sentiment tends to produce reproach. In such sentiment the object has become so idealized and so definitely associated with self that the possibility of its being responsible for a disgusting act practically is eliminated. But if the sentiment be hatred, that which without the sentiment would produce disgust or anger, functioning as an object of the sentiment, tends to produce malice or vengeance. In love tenderness makes it easier to develop the secondary emotion of admiration

and under certain conditions reverence; reverence implies that the object of wonder and tenderness suggests also the necessity of subjection and fear. The object of fear within the system of hatred is apt to become also the object of such emotions as jealousy, vengeance and malice; the recognition that there cannot be an open meeting of the object encourages opposition by deceptive methods, by which it is hoped the object may be counteracted. Awe as an element in love is akin to that of reverence only omitting the element of tenderness. Jealousy also may come to be associated with love and friendship under certain conditions. 

The basic condition for its development is a single prize for two rivals. But it is not likely to develop in such case unless one rival acknowledges himself inferior to the other or assumes that the person loved is granting undue advantages to the rival. But real love carries with it confidence in its object and hence there should be no assumption of unfair motives. But if the friendship is valued on the basis of pleasure, economic or social gain, or if one rival is distinctly lacking in self-respect, jealousy is likely to develop. Further, if the rival is hated the conditions for the development of jealousy are made more favorable. The conclusion is that secondary emotions come into the system of the sentiment subsequent to the primary emotions and develop in accord with certain life characteristics and environmental conditions. Any particular secondary emotion is not entirely certain to have a place in a sentiment of a particular type. As suggested above, the derived emotions are not organized structurally but come to have only a functional connection with the system of the sentiment. 

2. The Growth of the Sentiment in Cognitive Clearness. 

We consider cognitive development along two lines: (1) as to the nature of the object; (2) as to methods for pro-

Social Psychology moting the welfare of the object. 

How develop a knowl. edge of the object it. self?

We have suggested that the first attention given to an object is apt to be to its attributes and not to its basic qualities. Attachment at first is apt to be to a type such as to any boy or girl of a particular genetic period. Only certain of the attributes of the object are recognized and the nature of the object to which these are attributes is not as yet conceived clearly. It is possible that a very unattractive person may have attractive hair or physique, or any particular attribute may be undesirable and the object to which it belongs desirable. But as the attachment to the object becomes more fixed an investigation as to the nature of the object itself develops. Growth along this line means the differentiation of that which is central from that which is accidental. The friend comes to be valued for certain characteristics which are not to be found on the part of any other representative of his class. Love and hate are opposites as to their idealization tendencies; the blindness of love is optimistic while that of hate is pessimistic. The increasing insight of a friend assumes a more and more idealistic object, while the increasing insight of an enemy thinks of the object as being more of a disgusting and dangerous nature. The object which becomes known is the ideal rather than the real object; or if we choose to identify the ideal and the real we may say, the object as we believe it will become, rather than it at present is. 

Another line of cognitive growth promoted by the developing sentiment has to do with insight into methods of furthering or impeding the welfare of the object of the sentiment. In accord with the change of interest from the attributes to the object itself there is need for a change of methods for promoting the impulse of the sentiment. The

How develop increased knowledge as to methods of further. ing the impulse of the sentiment? problem of either furthering or inhibiting the welfare of the object or of submitting to its standards becomes a more difficult problem for cognition. This is true because the individual now is dealing not with shifting attributes but with more deep-seated actualities. We illustrate more in the realm of friendship than in that of regard or hatred; illustrations will come to the mind of the reader readily in either of these realms. 

The ministrations of the child to his friend is likely to be in the way of food, candy, toys, or such articles as will satisfy a temporary or immediate desire; in much the same way an adult in dealing with a new acquaintance must deal with his temporary needs because his more permanent ones are not known. But as an understanding of the more basic nature of the friend develops there develops also a type of ministration calculated to satisfy the needs of that nature. Our interpretation of the desires of our friends is always idealistic. If I call the attention of my friend to a book, picture, drama, etc., it is always with the implication that he is interested in the very best along that line. 

There may be a type of realist who is ready to say that I do not understand the real desires of my friend. But as a friend I do not profess to minister to his present desires but to those desires in their most sublimated form of expression. Ministration to possible worthy desires is judged better than feeding of raw meat to animals to the end of making them more savage and bestial than before. It is a strange justification on the part of the press for the broadcasting of accounts of crime and filth, to say that the public desire such; we never go on the assumption that a friend desires that which is not worthy of him. So with the development of the sentiment there comes increasingly a

Social Psychology realization of how to further its deep-seated and actual impulse.

The above line of thinking suggests a somewhat different function for developing cognition in relation to a sentiment. 

Interpretation of the nature of the object and idealization of the desires of the personal object may at least appear to give the sentiment a somewhat different turn.

Should developing understanding modify the impulse of the sentiment?

Mc- 

Dougall insists (90:45) that it is the single duty of cognition to adjust better means to the end of furthering the objectives of the impulses. But since the desires of a human being are capable of satisfaction on so many different levels, we conclude that in addition to promoting such satisfaction, we must also choose the level of its expression. 

One of the most important words that the dynamic psychology is calling to our attention is the word sublimation. 

My friend is in need of nourishment. As a friend it is my duty to help devise means to secure such nourishment. Do 

I fulfill that duty by seeing to it that he has satisfactory food and drink for the body? Must I not assume that he needs intellectual and social stimulus as well? Or perhaps he has acquired a thirst for alcohol. Am I as a friend to use my cognition only in the devising of means of supplying the demands of that thirst? It is evident that I must exercise my cognition to determine what desires should be satisfied as well as to devise suitable means for their satisfaction. But in fact such understanding of norms and values comes so to modify the direction of my friendship as to cause it to have a tendency to further only worthy desires and to deny those which are judged unworthy. In this sense the cognition directs the impulse of the sentiment.

3.

The Growth of the Sentiment in Stability of Conative 

Alignment. 

We consider conative growth as to: (1) loyalty to the higher needs of the object of the sentiment; (2) loyalty to the sentiment itself. It is more convenient and valuable to illustrate along lines of friendship rather than in reference to the sentiments of regard or hatred. 

We have indicated that friendship at its best lends its force to the promotion of the highest and best in the life of 

What general prin- the friend. Friendly loyalty must ciples govern friendly loyalty? utilize volition for the promotion of the best interests of the friend.

We consider here conditions favorable to volition. It is obvious that volition needs cognition quite as much as impulse and feeling need it. Before we can will the best for a friend we must understand his real needs. Or more concretely, before I can determine to promote a particular need for a friend I must have a basic impulse prompting me to that end. For volition, as Mc- 

Dougall suggests, is only a more highly organized form of conation. He says, "We may, then, define volition as the supporting or re-enforcing of a desire or conation by the cooperation of an impulse excited within the system of the self-regarding sentiment". (90:255). This implies that there is no power to self-assertion unless the self has organized certain of the primitive dynamic elements within its system and thus exercises control over their expression. 

It follows then that while we may cultivate and promote a certain instinctive attachment by keeping interest and attention upon it we cannot will a new interest which we do not have instinctively. Does it follow that friendships are determined by chance happenings of individuals whose instinctive tendencies draw them together? It is rather

Social Psychology clear that we cannot become definitely attached to that which, in its manifestations and in itself, is repulsive to us. 

So the only possibility of loving a person, who now is acting as an enemy to the good, is by finding a phase of his personality which has in it elements of the beautiful and good. 

So the task involving the voluntary assertion of self comes to be that of searching for elements in the character of others, which elements or potential elements, are capable of becoming the objects of really vital and assertive love. 

Volition can be based upon an intelligent understanding of what ought to be but cannot disregard the arousal of basic impulses from which it is to draw its power. 

There is something of a natural order of development in the way by which a permanent loyalty is built up. If two 

What genetic steps persons are drawn to each other lead to a permanent at first sight as friends it seems loyalty to a a friend? evident that they must have displayed "signs of loveliness" to each other. Such a ready display by both of mutually attractive qualities must be comparatively rare. If the attractive qualities displayed by both are real qualities of self then the development of a friendship between them should be rapid and easy, unless contradictory attributes appear later. A second possibility is that the individuals may be attracted to each other by qualities displayed only in certain moods or situations; individuals may enjoy a fishing trip, a musical entertainment or the discussion of politics together. Friendships based upon such occasional display of attractive qualities are not likely to be stable because the particular qualities are not likely to be displayed with sufficient frequency. A more permanent alignment is promised when the developing friends attain and maintain each for the other a mental picture of favorable characteristics. But a more hopeful situation exists when each be-

gins to look deeper than mood for qualities of life which he believes to exist in the more submerged but permanent self of the other. The lover gets a bare suggestion of desirable qualities but completes the suggestion by the exercise of his own creative imagination. This functioning of creative imagination constitutes the basis for the assertion that a friend is an ideal. The real qualities to which the lover develops his loyalty are abstractions which he has developed on the basis of suggestions coming from the behavior of the beloved. Such imagined qualities may be called real in two senses: (1) they represent the real object of loyalty and the self becomes deeply and permanently attached to them; (2) they may be taken as ideals by the friend and hence assert a very real and definite influence over his acquired characters. We become attached to fictitious as well as to historical characters; in fact, it is often suggested that the historian so interprets his characters as to make that which was historical really fictitious. So our most basic loyalties often are maintained in favor of characters in the creation of which we ourselves have had a prominent part. The genetic steps of a developing alignment consists in the increasing definiteness of the interpretation of the friend and in the firmness of the loyalty vowed to such object. 

It is necessary to keep in mind the distinction between loyalty to a friend and to a friendship. There is likely to be a definite interest in a particular friend for a consider- How build up loyalty able time before there develops to the sentiment it. pride in the friendship formed. 

The alignment has to be formed rather definitely before it comes to be recognized as a permanent possession. But the relationship is not as definite as it should be until there is formed a definite loyalty to the sentiment itself as well as self?

Social Psychology to its object. The first sign of loyalty to the sentiment is pride shown in the continuance of it. No self-respecting person likes to acknowledge that he has made a serious mistake in the formation of a friendly alignment; such acknowledgement depreciates his evaluation of self. But if a real friendship is in actuality developing, soon the alignment comes to be cherished for its own sake. At first there may be a marked expectancy of reciprocity on the part of the friend. But with the deepening of the attachment to the sentiment comes the willingness to go the second mile in service and sacrifice for the sake of the maintenance of the sentiment. Basic justice now is done to the friend regardless of reciprocity. Then there develops a willingness and even joy in the sacrifice of self necessary to maintain the sentiment. With the development of this step it can be concluded that a permanent loyalty has been established toward the sentiment. The expression of this loyalty may still be somewhat erratic; it may be somewhat mingled with expressions of disloyalty. Or certain outstanding expressions of loyalty may be maintained without advances being made. For instance my loyalty to my party may mean that I vote for it and say nothing against it and may not mean that I advocate it enthusiastically and strive to convert others to it. Or I may express a zealous loyalty at election time and almost forget my attachment at other times. But when loyalty is developed fully it is steadfast regardless of reciprocity, moods, outside interests or any other disturbing factor. The two factors which should hold the friend steady are: (1) his permanent determination to promote the well-being of the object of his friendship; (2) his deep-seated loyalty to the friendship itself.
