CHAPTER VI The Nurture of the Sentiment: Influences Tending to Modify the Development of the Sentiment. 

The reader of this chapter should keep in mind such questions as the following: What is original human nature like? How rigid and set are the elements that comprise it?

In what way or ways should original nature be modified? 

What environmental factors are potent toward such modification? Just what types of sentiments should we seek to develop?

I. The Importance of the Social Direction of Human Innate Tendencies. 

We consider here: (1) the nature of human innate tendencies; (2) their modification by individual reaction to environmental forces. 

1. The Nature of the Original Tendencies of the Human Being. 

Nurture in the case of the human being is important because of the nature of his innate tendencies. We note first the possible types of innate tendencies and then the types most prevalent on the part of the human being. 

(1). The Elements of Original Nature Commonly Recognized. 

At present authors seem fairly well agreed as to what names should be applied to the types of animal innate 169

Social Psychology cies? tendencies. Starch says, "The equipment with which human beings start in life may be divided into three types of inherited responses and abilities: Reflexes, instincts, and (B126:9). Thorn 

What are the types of capacities". animal innate tenden- dike says, "Three terms, reflexes, instincts and inborn capacities, divide the work of naming these unlearned tendencies". (A131:4). Kirkpatrick says that in the case of the higher animals, "that react in response to suggestive stimuli there is generally a clear distinction between psysiological processes going on within the organism and behavior in relation to the stimulating environment". (C80:92). Also Norsworthy and Whitley add to the usual list of reflexes, instincts and capacities, "automatic or physiological actions". They explain that, "automatic actions are such as those controlling the heart beats, digestive and intestinal movements:- -". (97:22). For our purposes we consider the physiological type or reaction a visceral or internal reflex or chain of reflexes. This leaves just the three terms: "reflex", "instinct" and "capacity". The reflex is an innately determined automatic response of a part of the organism such as the blink of the eye, the knee jerk, cough, sneeze, etc. It is best considered a unit act or the act of a comparatively small group of muscles; swallowing and digesting are best termed chains of reflexes. Pillsbury points out that the term instinct is being used in two senses: "The first is used to designate a more complicated reflex", and the second, "is used to designate all acts whose conditions are inherited". (A104:22023). This difference may be stated otherwise by saying that in the first usage both the end of action and the means for attaining that end are assumed to be determined innately, while in the second usage only the desire for the end is supposed to be hereditary, while the means for its attain-

Nurture of the Sentiment 171 ment is acquired by the individual. Starch says, “Capacities are distinguished from reflexes and instincts in being general mental abilities rather than specific motor responses and in referring primarily to the native mental equipment, such as the powers of sensation, perception, retention, attention, imagination, and all the varied complex psychic processes". (B126:10). Physical powers are just as much capacities as mental powers; the normal human infant has the capacity to swim just as much as the capacity to imagine. The capacity is not a present ability to react but rather the promise of an ability to be attained somewhere along the line of normal growth. Thus the normal human infant has the capacity to jump, think, run, imagine, etc.

He, as an infant, cannot do any of these things but if actually he is a normal human infant somewhere in the course of his development he will learn to do all of them with some degree of proficiency. 

(2). The Major Place of Capacities or Potentialities in the Original Nature of the Human Being. 

The human being has a full animal equipment of physiological and external reflexes. For the most part the external reflexes are essential to the successful functioning of What type of innate the sense end-organs and other ortendency predomi- gans externally located, while the visceral reflexes and chains of renates with humans? flexes promote the relation of the vital organs to each other and the life of the organism itself. If we use the term instinct in the sense of rather vague innate impulses or promptings toward ends, the actual attainment of which must be learned, we can say truly that the human has more instincts than any other animal. But if we use the word instinct to indicate a chain of sensori-motor reflexes which relate the whole organism

Social Psychology to its environment in some purposeful way, then we must say that human beings have very few instincts. Aside from swallowing, the vital processes and the tendency to cry, the human infant is entirely too helpless to react as a whole to its environment in any way calculated to serve the interests of its organism. In humans both stimulus and response tendencies are highly adaptive and vary greatly with different individuals. The reader should keep in mind that an acquired tendency is only a modified innate tendency and that in any case the tendency may be acquired to a greater or less degree; it is evident that there is more acquiring involved in learning to talk than in learning to laugh. It is the type of human tendency which we are calling capacity that is so prominent on the part of human beings. The normal human infant as such is the most helpless of all young animals but at the same time gives promise of attainments higher than those of any other of the young. Because the human is not bound to definite reactions by heredity he is free to learn to adapt himself to changing environs even to the extent of adjusting his desires to new situations. The fact that the human has such a multitude of unsatisfied desires, or we might say the fact that he can find satisfaction in the attainment of such varied objectives, emphasizes the factor of capacity in human original nature.

We note a few points further in order to clarify the relation between innate and acquired determiners. Many 

How are human capacities determined? writers speak of traits as either innate or acquired; the implication is that a tendency is one or the other and that the distinction is clear cut. We incline to the view that tendencies are acquired to a greater or less degree; a child may take a few tottering steps at the first attempt but this certainly it not walking in the usual sense of that word.

Nurture of the Sentiment 173 The adult human that could take only a few tottering steps on a smooth floor would be said to lack much toward the ability to walk. We have mentioned the forms of innate determiners: they are first cellular and later become neural or glandular. In most cases only the central portion of the neural pattern is fully closed which leaves the adaptable sensori-perceptual and motor parts to be completed as to neural arcs; it is quite evident that the number of arcs needing coordinating may in any case be more or less. We are coming to recognize that both heredity and environment exert influence toward the development of human traits. 

Waddle says, "The best environment cannot add to hereditary potentialities nor develop capacities that do not exist". (B135:82). The question is no longer heredity or environment but to what extent heredity and what extent environment. Various attempts have been made to determine the relative importance of heredity and environment as casual factors in the development of individual traits. The evidence thus secured seems susceptible of differing interpretations but a frequent conclusion is to say that heredity is responsible to the extent of two-thirds or three-fourths of the total. With certain exceptions Starch concludes that, "the ultimate achievement of any given individual is due to his original ability, probably to the extent of 60 to 90%, and to actual differences in opportunity or external circumstances only to the extent of 10 to 40%". (B126:94). But all seem to concede that innate potentialities are much superior to actual attainments. Starch says, "hardly one person in a thousand makes all the absolute gain possible for him even in a single capacity". (B126:96). The upper limit of an individual's attainment in any particular is set by nature but at a very generous level. Absolute levels of social life are determined very largely by environment; the relative superiority of one individual to his fellows of

Social Psychology a certain group is likely to point to hereditary endowment. 

What the human race at the full limit of its evolution can come to be no one has even dared to suggest. So the work of education and human culture can go forward with the full assurance that the past has not enslaved the present and that the future man will be limited much more by his vision, zeal and environment than by lack of material out of which to build splendid manhood. 

2. The Possibility of Modifying the Functioning of Hu man Innate Tendencies by Environmental Influence. 

We have indicated above that human heredity does not present educators with an unalterable situation. There still remains the question as to the need of altering the direction of native tendencies. 

(1). The Environmental Direction of Capacities into Functional Actualities. 

On Historically there has been much discussion as to whether nature is right and to what extent conscious direction of Why seek to direct native impulses is desirable. the whole the human race seems to human capacities? be coming to the conclusion that it can place less trust in racial determinism and more dependence on human nurture as a source of human health, happiness and advancement. This does not imply that we should disregard the potency of native impulses but rather that we should give conscious aid to those impulses in finding their highest expression in a particular environment. 

Norsworthy and Whitley apply the adjectives, mechanical, constant, delayed, transitory and crude to original responses. (97:23-29). Of these adjectives the term "crude" is deserving of most attention. Human original

Nurture of the Sentiment 175 tendencies are crude in the same sense that the tree in the forest or the ore in the mine is crude: the tendencies are capable of splendid development but in their present form not suited to the highest needs of human life. The need is not so much that any of the basic tendencies be destroyed or permanently inhibited but that they be so organized and directed as to promote the best interests of the life of any generation. The sentiment is one of the main organizations which directs this crude energy with constancy to an end which is more or less consciously selected and at least intended to promote human welfare. The more conscious attention given to the building of a sentiment the more it is likely that the end which it promotes will be well selected and the forces so organized as really to achieve that end. 

Sentiments will develop whether nurtured or not but if not nurtured their objects will not be selected to advantage; also such sentiments may be of a wrong type, only partially built or so lack organization as radically to interfere one with another. Major premises of thought may be so little considered as to permit racial prejudice, partyism, or pessimism on the part of people otherwise right-minded. In fact it is not hard to discover all kinds of contradictions in the feeling, thinking and acting of practically any human being. Much of this confusion, friction and misdirected energy can be remedied by the sublimation of innate capacities.

II. The Modification of Human Innate Tendencies Needed for the Development of Desirable Sentiments. 

Toward the development of desirable social sentiments we consider: (1) the development of major thought premises; (2) the development of optimistic feeling attitudes; (3) the development of stable attitudes of decision and loyalty.

Social Psychology 1. The Encouragement of Major Thought Premises as an Influence toward the Development of Social Sentiments. 

By a major thought premise we mean a comparatively stable idea which acts as a major premise for various lines of thinking. For example, the assumption that the racial tendencies of human nature are essentially good has a persistent and powerful influence over one's thinking con- 

How are major thought premises developed? cerning social problems. Such an assumption is sure to influence value judgments and hence make it easier to develop various lines of social adjustments. Religion is a fruitful source of influence on major thought premises; religion frequently supplies one of the elements of background for thought. The Christian assertion that God is good and that he has made man in his own image is bound to have an influence on Christian thought concerning the potential goodness of man. Belief in the goodness of human nature is certain to influence belief in the favorable outcome of well directed effort. Once assume such a background of belief and it follows that the development of friendly relations between different races, nations, occupations, etc., must become easier. Various forms of social influence make for or against friendly thought premises. The prevailing philosophy of his time is likely to influence the thought premises of an individual. The individual may not be much of a philosopher himself but is likely to be influenced by the prevailing drift of philosophical thinking of his time; a materialistic, indealistic, ascetic, or cynical trend of philosophy will give a characteristic set to his thinking. Also the main trend of national or social life is a potent factor. In a time of national disaster due to war, pestilence, famine or disease, it is easy to develop pessi-

Nurture of the Sentiment 177 mistic trends of thought regarding all people. But what is called "social heredity" perhaps is the most dominant factor; this term refers to the social influence brought to bear on the life of the infant. The attitude of the parents taken towards foreigners, different races, occupations, etc., is certain to give a definite set to the background of the thought of the young child. Race prejudice may be formed so early in the life and become so deep-seated as to seem to be a part of the physical heredity; the child thinks of his attitude as being a phase of his own nature rather than a product of environmental influence. The example set by the parents in treating the garbage man in a way entirely different from the way they treat the physician or minister, leaves a lasting imprint on the major thought premises of the child. 

2. The Development of Optimistic Feeling Attitudes. 

Optimistic feeling attitudes constitute a practical necessity for the development of numerous and varied friendships. The attitude of pessimism, involving as it does a 

How develop optimist- deep-seated distrust of human naic feeling attitudes? ture, lays the basis for hatred rather than for love. This is true because friendship demands the idealization of the object and this is possible only on the basis of optimism. Further, the development of many friendships demands the assumption of hidden qualities which are good; optimistic feeling attitudes are practically essential for such assumptions. 

The factors of life most essential for the development of an optimistic attitude are happiness, health and a measure of success along the lines of one's major interests. The casual relationship between any two of these three terms is mutual. Thus it happens that the development of any one of them helps in the development of all of the others. Hap-

Social Psychology piness is connected with success as it seems to be based upon a reasonable furthering of the interests which the individual considers major. For those of little creative imagination, the success must be manifest but for those who have deep confidence in self, belief in future success may act as a substitute for actual present success. There is little doubt that vigorous health makes for enthusiasm, optimism and success. We need to exercise caution in assuming that optimism and happiness are not possible without health. But it is true that in the main weakness developed by poor health lowers self-confidence and hence the likelihood of success and happiness. Mental hygiene is receiving and is deserving of consideration as well as physical hygiene. Such conditions as the various forms of psychoses, phobias, hysterias or worries certainly are influences making against health, happiness and optimism.

Any condition of child labor (too long hours or work too heavy for the worker) involving the omission of wholesome play life and happiness lays the foundation for cynicism and pessimism. Any influence that promotes happiness, health or a reasonable measure of success is on the side of the forces which develop optimistic feeling attitudes. 

3. The Development of Stable Conative Attitudes Pro ductive of Friendly Sentiments.

We have noted that a friendship has not come to its best until there is back of it a firm determination to be loyal How do value judg. both to the sentiment and to its ments affect friendly object. Here we consider certain loyalties? basic attitudes which have an influence on such loyalties. One's conviction as to the worth of a friend or friends in general is likely to influence greatly his status of loyalty. If one is convinced that the development of his life depends vi-

Nurture of the Sentiment 179 tally upon the character of his friends, he is likely to strive earnestly to develop and maintain desirable friendships. 

Or one may assume that friendship is a necessary prerequisite to breadth of view, knowledge of human relations or practical success in life. Such assumptions when held definitely are practically certain to register their influence in the way of a determination to develop and maintain desirable forms of friendship. As we indicated above such value judgments are secured from the general trend of religion and philosophy or the main emphasis of the public press of the day.

We are coming to realize more and more that one of the most powerful of the factors in human life is self-assertion. 

As this force is harnessed to the promotion of friendly What connection has loyalty we have a basis of assurself-assertion with ance for the steady continuance of friendly loyalty? the friendships involved. While it is true that friendship at its best is based upon mutual attachment and loyalty, nevertheless, there is much that one of the friends can do to maintain the relationship. If my friend becomes indifferent or hostile to the continuance of our friendship, it is likely to indicate that he believes that there is something wrong with my character or attitudes. Or if my friend fails to measure up to my expectancy I cannot escape blame for the failure. It may be due to my failure to idealize his life as formerly I was able to do. It may be due to my failure to stimulate the better tendencies of his life and hence to keep him growing toward his best. Hence failure of a friendship always is a reflection upon both parties involved in it. Or if the friendship is prosperous I have reason for taking pride in its prosperity. It is not to be assumed that its prosperity is due merely to the chance meeting of two mutually attractive personalities. An at

Social Psychology tractive personality is an achievement due to right basic attitudes and to repeated reactions of a right sort. Further a personality does not remain attractive unless it is undergoing a process of growth towards the idealistic. For instance, the natural attractiveness to the vigorous life of adolescence should be replaced by an achieved attractiveness of mind and spirit in middle and later life. If such cannot be attained there is little cause for individual satisfaction as the previous attractiveness was racial rather than individual; the attraction was due to natural processes of growth rather than to individual achievements. So if my friendship fails to prosper as it should I am right to conclude that there is need for more effort on my part. My self-assertion must be expressed wisely but is a factor of no little importance in the holding of a friend and the maintaining of a friendship. 

But my estimate of the value of friends and friendships does not depend entirely upon my own initiative and logical judgment. My judgment to a much greater extent than 

I am ready to acknowledge is a reflection of the prevailing social estimate of my time. During a period in which the 

How does society prevailing philosophy is materialencourage individual istic, cynical or ascetic the individual receives little encouragement friendly loyalty? towards a high evaluation of friendship. But if the philosophy of the time is social and idealistic and education places a marked emphasis on social contacts and breadth of view it becomes quite easy for the individual to place a high evaluation on friendship. Any social recognition of a particularly attractive friendship tends to give prestige and standing to this form of relationship. Literature may do this by emphasizing the great friendships of history or by creating such friendships in fiction. There may develop a social custom of celebrating

Nurture of the Sentiment 181 wedding anniversaries, especially in cases where great friendships have been maintained for long periods of time. 

Also the frequency of divorce or the social emphasis on its seriousness impresses the individual for or against the worth of true friendship. So it happens that the amount of individual pride in maintaining a stable idealistic friendship is determined largely by social emphasis and evaluation. 

Philosophy and religion tend to evaluate friendship and to pass such evaluation to the individual through tradition. 

But before the actual fruition in friendship is achieved, the individual must accept such evaluation and place the power of his self-assertion back of a steady effort to achieve worthy friendship. Conation on its highest level functioning as volition cannot be disregarded as a factor in the production of friendships.

III. The Actual Building of Particular Forms of Friendly 

Sentiments.

The suggestions given here as to the nurture of social sentiments are introductory and intended mainly to raise certain questions which will be given fuller treatment in later chapters. The types of relations considered are: (1) individual to individual; (2) individual to social group or social ideal; (3) social group to social group. 

1. The Nurture of Sublimated Forms of Individual 

Friendships.

In its most usual sense friendship involves a relationship between two individuals. It is obvious that we desire to encourage the more worthy and sublimated forms of friendship; it goes without saying, that it is regard and friendship, rather than hatred, that should be cultivated by social influences. Our concern here is to indicate certain of the

Social Psychology problems involved and to determine whether these can be developed toward a solution by bringing social influence to bear. Questions relating to the range of individual friendships involve: (1) the question of whether friendship is possible between any two human beings; (2) the question of how many friends one individual can have at one time. 

The first question may be considered from the psychological angle and might be stated: Is a stable friendly adjustment between any two human beings possible? Or from the social point of view it might be stated: To what extent do the mores or social approval permit the consumation of a friendship between any two human beings? The question concerning the possible number of friends at one time involves: (1) consideration of the exclusive nature of the inter-sex friendship; (2) consideration of the time involved in the maintenance of a friendship; (3) thought as to the actual possibility of sharing the life with a great many different individuals. It is rather plain that environmental influence has a bearing on the determination of the range of personal friendships. The set of public opinion may open or close certain lines of friendship. Custom or even law may confine friendship to certain group limits, such as the limits of racial or religious groups. Marriage laws may restrict to a very great or a very slight extent. National wars, racial hatreds, family feuds, business antagonisms, may make the development of certain friendships practically impossible. 

Social customs may restrict the broader friendships of married partners very much or very little. Conditions favoring education or travel may make possible new lines of individual friendships. The conclusion follows that environmental pressure has much to do with the determination of the range of individual personal friendships. 

What range of individual friendships can be nurtured?

Nurture of the Sentiment 183 ing for personal friendships?

Various questions have been raised as to the conditions favorable to the development and maintenace of a high type of personal friendship. Some of these questions are: 

How does environ. (1) To what extent should the ment influence favor. object of a friendship be idealable conditions mak. ized?; (2) In what ways should the friends be alike and in what ways different?; (3) Is it essential or desirable that each be definitely and positively attached to the other?; (4) To what extent should the formation of the friendship be free entirely from external domination or constraint?; (5) What type of personal quality makes for continued attractiveness? Education and social pressure have much to do with the development of the conditions entering into these problems. 

The social trend of idealism or materialistic realism has much to do with the tendency of the individual to idealize the object of his friendship. Especially in inter-sex friendships public opinion is a major influence upon the development of a positive and definite expression of feminine friendship; the expression of coyness and passivity is a reflection of the fashion of the day. Social custom determines to a great extent the possibility of a friendship being a free expression of the parties involved. Individual qualities are to some extent hereditary but the particular qualities which are displayed reflect the social emphasis of the time. It is evident that environmental influence can be planned so as to make for or against any one of these conditions bearing on the success of a personal friendship. 

Also there are certain moral or philosophical principles that have a bearing on the development of personal friendships. Certain of the outstanding questions involved here are: (1) Must morality always dominate friendly relations?; (2) To what extent should the friend have confidence in his feeling or intuitive judgments?; (3) How defi-

Social Psychology nitely must friendship be based on a general optimistic philosophy?; (4) Is an idealistic philosophy an essential

In ciples basic to devel the case of the great majority of oping friendship? human beings such moral and philosophical attitudes are reflections of the general social emphasis. The number of individuals who work out and apply consciously the principles of a philosophy are few. Respect for morality, like respect for God, is a sort of automatic major premise of thought which comes to the individual from the emphasis of the social group. On account of the developing sphere of influence of women there is more of a tendency to recognize the place of intuitive judgment, especially in the aesthetic and personal realms. It will be conceded readily that whenever the great philosophical leaders of a country or age have been idealistic, such idealism is reflected in the automatic assumptions of the mass of the people. In so far as such principles as are being considered in this paragraph are influential in the development of desirable personal friendships we have a situation amenable to educational influences. 

2. The Building of Sublimated Forms of Friendship on the Part of an Individual for an Institution or Ideal. 

We are considering the development of friendly relations here as to: (1) the relation of the individual to the small social group; (2) the relation of the individual to the large social group; (3) the relation of the individual to the broader religious or cosmic order. 

The individual develops attachments to various small social groups; he is the member of a home, of a fraternal group, an athletic team, a corporation board,

Nurture of the Sentiment 185 a cabinet, etc. The main problems here are: (1)

What special attention should be given to the key individuals of the small group?; (2) To what extent should their friendship be developed before 

What influence has society on individualsmall-group relations? the formation of the group?; (3) 

How can friendship be maintained under the formal conditions of group relations?; (4) What special conditions are involved in small group relations? Whether a small group relation should attempt to maintain friendship as its basis is determined by social emphasis. Marriage or business relations may be divorced entirely from friendship if the social mores so decree. Social custom or tradition determines whether the factor of friendship be considered in the appointment of a cabinet or special board of directors. Most of the social influences which affect the friendships between detached individuals also effect the relation of the individual to the small social group and to the members within that group. 

The relation of the individual to the larger social group is likely to take the form of submission and loyalty. Some of the problems involved in the relation of the individual What social influences to the larger social group are: (1) 

Can the individual himself deteraffect individual-group mine his group alignments?; (2) loyalties? 

How can he settle conflicts of group loyalties?; (3) How distinguish between the essential demands of the group and the leadership of particular individuals?; (4) Are groups justified on a purely social basis or must they perform definite service for the whole of society? How free an individual is to think for himself and to form his group alignments depends almost entirely upon his degree of education and freedom from social dominance; if he is held in any form of social slavery there is no possibility of his making decisions for himself. But if he is

Social Psychology free to decide, the trend of his values still is determined largely by the social emphasis along that line; one favors his nation above humanity if nationalism has been preached to him all his life. During the process of his education the individual may be encouraged to think and discriminate for himself or he may be led to assume that certain questions are not open for individual thought. Whether he obeys blindly or out of intelligent loyalty depends largely upon the type of emphasis given by his teachers. Again it is plain that much can be done through the organization of environmental forces towards getting the individual properly related to his social groupings. ments?

The most abstract relationship which the individual sustains is to the cosmic or religious realm. We are consid- 

What social influences ering this relation because of its affect religious senti- evident social bearing. Certain of the problems involved in this relationship are: (1) What special emotional content is normal to the religious sentiment?; (2) What objects of religious sentiments are common but undesirable?; (3) What knowledge is needed for the development of the best types of religious sentiments?; (4) What changes should take place in the religious sentiments of the growing child? We consider here the most abstract of friendly alignments. This fact points to the increased need for social guidance. The objects of his religious worship are for the most part determined for the individual by social custom. But if he is to exercise a part in such determination he must be educated to that end. It is rather evident that the condition of complexity and difficulty in this situation calls for the best social guidance we have. 

Even the elements involved in the religious sentiment are of a subsequent and derived type and for this reason call

Nurture of the Sentiment 187 for more social guidance than in the case of the more primitive personal relations. 

3. The Promotion of Friendly Alignments Between 

Groups. 

How promote friendly group relationships?

Another form of attachment akin to that of a sentiment is that of one social group for another. It is of distinct interest to the social order to build up desirable group relations. Some of the main problems involved here are: (1) Is group mind of such a nature as to permit of the establishment of sentiments between groups?; (2) How do permanent group relations differ from sentiments involving individuals?; (3) Must we assume group relations to exist on a crude or primitive level?; (4) What practical forces are available for the promotion of desirable group adjustments? The social direction of group relations evidently presents a difficult task. The increased difficulty should be taken to suggest more careful study and planning rather than an attitude of indifference to the problem. As regards the main problem we are considering, it is evident that group relations are constituted of social attitudes. 

Such an attitude as that of hatred between two races or nations may be inherited by a particular generation but such inheritance is social and not physical. This suggests merely that care be asserted to the educating of the coming generation during the period of its infancy and childhood. 

The whole possibility of building a civilization depends upon the power of the social order to adjust its major group relations. In this chapter we have contended merely that the various forms of friendship can be nurtured and furthered by the direction of social forces. In certain subsequent chapters the more detailed questions will be given attention.

CHAPTER VII The  of a Sentiment: The 

Possible and Desirable Alteration of the Sentiment Once it has Been Established. 

Many sentiments become established without much thought being given to their direction. Do we expect such sentiments to remain fixed through life? Does a sentiment in its very nature permit of readjustments? Does the age of the individual possessing the sentiment affect its stability? Are there conditions demanding readjustments of sentiments? Should one arbitrarily set about to readjust a sentiment? How can one determine whether a particular alignment is desirable or not? Such questions as these are discussed in the chapter following. 

I. The Expectancy that the Normal Sentiment will 

Maintain Its Stability. 

Under this heading we consider the stability of the sentiment: (1) as it is normal to the child and adolescent; (2) as we should expect to find it in the case of the mature human being. 

1. The Extent to which the Sentiment Maintains Its 

Stability During the Changes of Developing Life. 

It is easy to overestimate the permanency of the sentiment as to both its organization and its fixation on its object. We note some observations and testimonies as to friendship which in this case is taken as typical of other sentiments. Black says, "Most men make friends easily 191

Social Psychology 

To what extent are childhood sentiments lasting? enough; few keep them". (17:45). Aristotle asserts that the stability of friendship is determined largely by the character of the friends, involving the nature of their motives. He says, "The bad-have no principle of stability: in fact, they do not even continue like themselves: only they come to be friends for a short time from taking delight in one another's wickedness. Those connected by motives of profit, or pleasure, hold together somewhat longer: so long, that is to say, as they can give pleasure or profit mutually". (4:196). Cicero stresses the necessity of morality and likeness of thinking on the part of friends. He says, "True friendship, indeed, is absolutely inconsistent with every species of artifice and duplicity; and it is equally impossible it should be maintained between persons whose dispositions and general modes of thinking do not perfectly accord". (A27:199). If friendship demands so much in the way of character, motive and conformity of trends of thought, it must seem doubtful whether it can be attained in a lasting form between young children. The character of the young child is not established firmly. His motives and moods are temporary and fluctuating. But even a mood on the part of a child carries on a fight for a continued life. Who has not seen a little child resist being comforted? The mood in the background of anger or self-pity has begun in a way to take root and if given further stimulation will become a relatively permanent attitude. But it is evident that the friendship between two children cannot be based upon a permanent set of character. It lacks the basis of intelligence and self-control which the Roman Stoics prized so much in their wise and good man. There is, of course, a true beginning of tenderness which, with proper direction, stimulation and time for growth, will develop into a stable

type of friendship. But in its early form of feeling it is fleeting. To the little child each day brings its changes in all relations of life. So in the case of children we should not expect an undue amount of grief over the substitution of one playmate or friend for another. This is true because the sentiment has not yet matured nor has its object become to any marked degree individualized. lescent sentiments?

With the coming of adolescence marked changes develop in social outlook and thus in friendly attitudes. We quote some judgments as to the nature of adolescents and the status of their attachments. Lowry and Lambert say, "This is the hero worshiping period of a boy's life". 

How stable are ado- (B86:16). Galloway says it is a time for, "The rediscovery of the other sex, and a great increase in the interest of boys and girls in each other". (A50:211).

Further he says it is a period for, "The rise of sentiments of altruism and chivalry. These are apparently quite normal elements in adolescent development, if conditions are wholesome and favoring". (A50:212). More stability is found here than in the case of childhood because the adolescent, due to the growth of understanding and consciousness of self, develops a sense of responsibility. Hocking says, "The conceit of opinion in the adolescent is not empty; it is based on a readiness to assume responsibility, and on an actual assumption of responsibility in the work of mental worldbuilding if not of physical world-building. He appreciates for the first time that he has his own life to lead; he finds himself morally alone; he can no longer endure to see things through the eyes of others". (A67:247). This acceptance of responsibility is the basis for the development of friendly loyalty. In early adolescence the intersex attraction is for the group rather than the individual. Because of this a particular friendship of this type is likely to be fleeting.

Social Psychology 

So it is usually only with the beginning of the post-adolescent period that the attachment becomes more definite and hence centered about a particular individual. It is believed by the Freudians that the pattern for the intersex friendship may be developed quite early in life. The boy is apt to take such a pattern from his mother and the girl from her father. Freud says, "Psychoanalysis, which illumes the depth of mental life, has no difficulty in showing that the sexual ties of the earliest years of childhood also persist, though repressed and unconscious". (B49:117).

It is during the period of adolescence or post-adolescence that such a pattern becomes attached to an individual of the opposite sex. Thus adolescence seems the normal period for the development of monogamous friendships. 

Maturity probably has more stability but less adaptability. 

In maturity habits and attitudes become rigid and thus make it very difficult to adjust the life to the friend. Hocking says, "To be mature is to see the pleasure of life in the setting of its labors; to be adolescent is to have sufficient vigor to welcome it all". (A67:297). Stability of friendship needs not only stability of life. It needs also idealism, vigor and confidence. So the best period for the forming of lasting friendships is after the life has gained sufficient understanding and responsibility but before its habits have established such rigidity as to make adaptability difficult. 

Probably the friendships which give the most promise of stability are formed during the period of post-adolescence. 

This holds not alone for the intersex friendship which develops the foundation for monogamous marriage but also for friendships between members of the same sex. 

2. The Normal Stability of the Sentiments of the Mature 

Human Being.

Apart from the consideration of special genetic periods

let us now consider the problem of maintaining a stability of relationship between two living and hence changing individuals. A stability of relation between two moving ob- 

Must adjustments be jects obviously must be brought about by coordinated lines of made in sentiments? movement. Thus if a friendship is to persist there must be coordinated lines of growth in the lives of the friends. Friends that cherish good will and feel tenderness for each other for a time may come to a testing time beyond the power of one or both of them. Cicero says, "the deserting of a friend in his distress, and the neglecting of him in one's own prosperity, are the two tests which discover the weakness and instability of most connections of this nature". (A27:199). Fully developed friendship ought to be able to meet any such test. Trumbull says, "A change in circumstance, that separates those who rejoice in the joy of inspiring intercourse, does not change the character or the affection of him who is a true friend". (B133:60). But the true friend must realize that mere persistence is not sufficient to meet the demands of a developing friendship. The changes in a life may not be in its economic status but in its moral character. King says, "If you would not cut yourself off from later service of your friend, you must grow with his growth". (79:42).

But also you may have to choose between growing into degeneracy of character and giving up a friend. But even if both grow along moral lines adjustments between them as friends are essential. These necessary adjustments apply to all phases of the relationship. New interpretations of the object of the sentiment must be formed. New reasons for the high evaluation of the sentiment must be developed. New vows of allegiance to friend and friendship must be registered by both parties. It has been well said that the man who repeats his goodness of yesterday is not a

Social Psychology good but a bad man. So the friend who does not renew his friendship with every step of his intellectual and moral growth was rather than is a friend. To all external appearances the friendship may seem constant and unchanging. But if it a friendship between two growing individuals we must assume that the growth of both parties to the friendship is proceeding along parallel lines. 

We now consider a little more definitely just what adjustments a continuing sentiment demands. If the adjustment is to remain stable account must be taken of all 

What changes must occur in a developing friendship? elements in the growing life of the individuals involved. All forms of education mean change on the part of the one being educated. Intellectual education means change in all forms of cognitive judgment. Value judgments must have an important bearing on the interpretation of the object of the friendship. For Aristotle says, "each individual feels Friendship not for what is but for that which conveys to his mind the impression of being good to himself". (4:184).

But the radical changes which education brings in value judgments must change not only the interpretation as to whether the individual fulfills the ideal of friendship but also the valuation placed on individual friendships. Further, moral education implies changes in all forms of intuitive or emotional judgments. Moral habits of emotion come to be established in different levels of excellence; one may come to appreciate what is worthy in music and still enjoy what is vulgar in the way of a story involving sex. 

Perhaps without realizing that a change is taking place the individual comes to be repelled by that to which formerly he was attracted; the attitude which the friend takes toward party politics may come to be very distasteful. It is this type of feeling judgment that is potent enough to cause an individual to contract a life attachment in friendship or

to break such an attachment. Aristotle in speaking of the transient nature of the friendship based upon pleasure or utility says, "Now it is the nature of utility not to be permanent but constantly varying; so, of course, when the motive which made them friends is vanished, the Friendship likewise dissolves; since it existed only relatively to those circumstances". (4:186). But such changes in motive and evaluation seem normal to growth and education. 

It follows that if two individuals are to remain friends their basic impulses and desires must be moralized along parallel or converging lines. We should come to expect all these constant adjustments on the part of those who continue to be friends. Especially is this true if it is an intersex friendship and all the changes incident to sex development and adjustment are involved. 

What changes in sentiments are demanded by objective circumstances?

Besides the adjustments demanded by growth and education shifting environmental factors call for adjustments if sentiments are to remain stable. Without consideration of the environmental relations many thoughtless individuals expect the attitudes normal to courtship to maintain during marriage; we might as well expect the attitudes of the playground to maintain in the classroom or those of the golf links in the business relations. Such expectancy is as foolish as it is false. It is the glory of human personality that it responds differently to different situations. Darwin's dogs acted towards him after he had been gone three years just as they did in cases when he had been gone a few hours. Had his neighbors and friends done the same it would have indicated a level of intelligence similar to that of the dogs. The responsibilities and close contacts of home life are distinctly different from those of courtship. The factor of sex relations alone is sufficient to ac-

Social Psychology count for a different tone of actions and reactions. With the sex adjustment of marriage and the mutual responsibility for the support of the home and its members we should not expect the same emotional expressions of friendship as are normal to courtship. Such expectancy—and it applies also to all cases involving marked changes of circumstances should be understood, because otherwise it may seem to one of the parties to the friendship that the other has lost his love. Perhaps the most desirable expression of friendship at any time is that of quiet loyalty and good will with a moderate expression of emotion. Miss 

Colcord speaks of the surprise of many at the extent "of marital discord centering about money affairs". (28:194).

Alice Ames Winter in speaking of family troubles that were serious enough to be brought into court says, "About half of the hatreds started-not matter where they ended-in quarrels about money: how the family funds should be spent, and who should spend them". (AA82: XLII:35).

This is related to the fact that money has so much to do with self-respect and self-assertion. There can be little question that economic conditions bear definitely upon relations involving friendships. If my friend acquires wealth

I need not give him up but my friendly attitude towards him is likely to take a somewhat different turn. If he loses wealth suddenly my expression of friendship will be altered thereby. It is evident that environmental relationships must be taken into consideration and that we must so plan our friendships as to preserve them under varied conditions. We must be more concerned to preserve the friendship itself than to maintain the particular expression which it has taken; the sentiment itself-which is the whole-always must be considered of more value than any of its emotional parts. The main point to keep in mind is that the sentiment which remains stable through the years

must involve constant and varied adjustments.

II. The Conscious Disorganization and Rebuilding of the 

Sentiment.

We consider here: (1) the modifications calling for conscious adjustment of an individual friendship; (2) Conditions under which an individual should readjust his group or institutional loyalties. 

1. The Modification of Personal Attributes Needed to Maintain a Friendly Personal Adjustment. 

friend become increasingly intelligent?

A friendship maintained between two growing individuals calls for wise guidance. With the fuller development and assertion of impulses and emotions there must be an Why must a steadfast increasing development of cognition or the friendship is likely to be disrupted. The individual must come to realize, for instance, that we cannot always expect our friends to share our moods. 

Sympathy in this sense may become grossly selfish and tyrannical. The little child acts as though he expects others to be in the same mood as he or readily to catch his mood. 

But such expectancy should not continue even into adolescense. The basic trend of the friendship is to promote the best interests of the friend. Intellectual growth should involve a clear understanding of what constitutes the real interests of another. Miss Condé says, "One of the commonest ways in which girls fail one another in their friendships is through a lack of fine feeling in guarding what their friends have entrusted to them". (29:50). We rather expect the little child to tell all the family secrets to the neighbors. In such case we blame the parents for saying and thinking things about their neighbors which they

Social Psychology should not say or think. But as the child grows older we expect him to accept the responsibility incident to his friendship. He must understand the conditions of friendly loyalty and not hurt a friend for the sake of giving some spicy "inside information". Maturation of intelligence should mean that we come to understand better what favors it is reasonable to request of a friend. Miss Condé quotes the remonstrance of a fine Quaker girl who says, "Yes, I know thee loves me, but the trouble is, thee strangles me with thy love". (29:49). Such love is not false but is crude and primitive. It needs the guidance of a more intelligent insight. It is a fairly accurate rule to assume that in matters involving give and take, friendship must abide by the ordinary rule of exchange: full value must be given for that which is received. In friendship the benefits given and received may not be at all similar in kind, but such is not implied in any form of exchange. Neither is it likely that the benefits can be reduced to an equality of financial value; the exchange must be on the basis of individual valuation of benefits received. If someone buys an automobile in order to take you for a ride in it you retain your self-respect only by assuming that the mental and moral stimulus of your company is of more value to him than the cost of the car. Requests on the basis of friendship alone should be rare indeed.

With the continued growth of a personal friendship there should be the expectancy of a new and fuller mani- Why does a growing festation of self-control on the part friendship demand a new measure of selfcontrol? of the friends. The more violent expressions of the feelings of tenderness and elation should become less frequent and a more quiet good will should tend to replace them. Strong emotion always is an indication of a lack of stability and should

always be assumed to mark a temporary stage of individual growth. Miss Condé says, "These frantic friendships usually are found between an older and younger girl, or a stronger and weaker personality. As time goes, the unmistakable earmarks of unwholesomeness appear. One becomes the satellite of the other". (29:54). It is probable that the Roman Stoic went too far in suppressing the emotional-intuitive expressions of life. But a wholesome measure of self-control is a desirable element in all friendly relations. Strong emotion is likely to be followed by a lapse into emotion of a different sort which becomes dangerous to the friendship; little children who become too hilarious in their play next are apt to be crying or fighting. 

As the friendship grows older its members should not be expecting each other to do some unusual thing as an expression of friendship. A quiet attitude of helpfulness is just as likely to imply that sacrifice will be made when conditions demand it. The one who has been a friend for a considerable time should be quieter in mood and more efficient in service. Or thinking from the point of view of the developing life it should be observed that early adolescence is the stress and strain period and ordinarily we should expect the moods to become more quiet and less changeable with the advent of middle life. The mature man is likely to feel a lack of excess energy in comparison with the vigor and enthusiasm of the youth. There is need for the coordination of effort here. The barter of quiet efficiency for enthusiasm is no robbery but may supply a mutual need. 

We are not asserting that the friendly relation is better when it is based upon more quietness of emotion and more assertion of will. We are saying that we should expect its early stages to be more emotional and enthusiastic and its later stages to be more quiet and efficient. This applies not only to the case where the individuals start as adolescents

Social Psychology and reach middle life but also to the older friendship regardless of the age of the friends. The mature friendship should be marked by an intelligent and efficient self-control. 

The friend must understand that emotional enthusiasm is only one of the varied expressions of the central impulse of a sentiment; the more mature form of that expression is intelligent volition.

When two friends are undergoing a process of education their friendship must be maintained by a process of disorganization and rebuilding. Or if one of the individuals 

Why consciously adjust the lives of friends to each other? fails to grow the friendship will have to undergo a radical readjustment of type. The college student who, after four years of intensive education away from home, goes back to old friends must expect to make readjustments of friendships. Many of the former friends may have grown much along right lines; we are not to assume that the study of books in schools is essential to education and growth. But the lines of growth in the cases of the returning graduate and his former friend are likely to be very different. There may be more in common between him and his old friend than the graduate at first realizes. There may be basic good will, respect for morality, common interests in national and religious issues. But close personal friendship tends to demand even more in common than this. 

It is nourished and fed to a great extent by that which is individual and personal. There is need for an extensive stock of personal experiences, the desire to discuss which, tends to bring and hold the individuals together. The best hope of a continued friendship rests upon the conscious adjustment of the two lives into parallel lines of growth. 

Aristotle in speaking of differences between friends says, "Of course, in such cases it is out of the question to attempt

to define up to what point they may continue friends: for you may remove many points of agreement and the friendship last nevertheless;-". (4:194). But although it may be said that the friendship lasts it is not of the same vigorous and close type. We should welcome, rather than fear, growth and change on the part of our friends; it is easier to give conscious adjustment to two growing lives than to one stagnant and one growing or to two stagnant lives. It is true, in a sense, that the parent loses the babe as it becomes a child and the child as he becomes an adolescent. But in such cases the loss of the bud brings forth the flower; the wheat brings forth its thirty and sixty fold only when the old grain dies. Friends are lost to each other if the growth of either stops. They may be friends always if they make a conscious effort to keep their lives adjusted along parallel lines. 

2. Conditions Under which Individual or Group Friend ships Should Be Adjusted.

In this connection we consider: (1) how disorganization is related essentially with building better sentiments; (2) what conditions demand conscious readjustment of individual friendships; (3) what special conditions call for readjustment between individuals and groups; (4) values to be looked for in friendship as a basis for their readjustment. 

It is one thing to approve natural processes and quite another to strive to direct them as to their basic channels. 

Should we disorgan. ize friendships voluntarily?

It is a wise provision that has put the most important processes of nature beyond the control of consciousness. We do not control digestion, absorption and cell building voluntarily. The direction of the growth of the different bodily parts is beyond our immediate voluntary control. For the most part

Social Psychology the whole race is protected against the characteristics which any generation acquires. In matters of the most vital concern the individual should hesitate to match his judgment against the prevailing tendencies of nature. In many cases we hesitate to tear down because we cannot construct in a better way. So in friendship it is best to lay our emphasis mainly on the constructive side. This in a way must involve disorganization but the break between the disorganizing and the constructing need not be pronounced. Before destroying the bud completely nature gives some evidence of the developing blossom. Before eliminating the child she shows some signs of promise of the adolescent; 

Woodrow Wilson is quoted as saying that a sophomore is an individual in which the sap is beginning to rise but has not yet reached the head. Construction should be the reason for destruction. But certain obligations rest upon intelligent human nature which have a bearing on the conscious disorganization of friendships. There are times when the demands of a higher loyalty require the giving up of a lesser alignment. Loyalty to character and morality must take precedence to individual attachments. Cicero says, "I lay it down, then, as a rule without exception, 'that no degree of friendship can neither justify or excuse the commission of a criminal action'". (A27:187). For our purposes a "criminal action" must involve anything that injures either the character of self or friend or opposes the higher social values. Certainly we should view with deep personal sorrow the spectacle of a friend starting down the primitive or immoral way of life. We owe much to the old friendship. But we owe more to morality and right citizenship. 

The real object of our loyalty is the potential good of the individual and hence we must oppose vigorously the potential evil in him. My friend must understand clearly that if we are to go together it must be along the higher way of

the moral life. It is only thus that, in reality, I can be loyal to his better self.

The tendencies of life which call for the voluntary readjustments of sentiments deserve more detailed examina- 

What reasons justify disorganizing friendships? tion. We take it as axiomatic that a friendship never should be broken hastily. Hold on to the friend so long as there is a real chance of saving him for the better way of life; he is your "other self" and you have a special responsibility for him. But because of this responsibility you must insist upon certain things. 

The friend must grow along worthy lines. The individual who moralizes or sublimates his impulses or tendencies so as to make them social (see upper division of our chart on human impulses opposite page 118) must prevent their expression on primitive levels. One will never hunger and thirst for righteousness if he hungers for gossip and scandal and thirsts for alcohol. The primitive outlet first must be blocked before the energy rises to higher levels. Crude tastes in recreation, pleasure, sport, drama, literature, must be reckoned as direct enemies to continued friendship. 

Further there is grave danger to friendship of primitive tendencies of thought. Science cannot be a true yokefellow with superstition. Since the partisan and primitive in any form of party alignment develops disgust it will tend inevitably to break up friendships. You may remain a friend to what the individual was, and what you hope him still to be, but you dare not ally yourself to what he now is. A wide range of differences in thought and attitude on practically every question in life is permissible and at times desirable. After the basic requirements of likeness is fulfilled then the wider the range of differences the better; self and friend together constitute a better unit than either self and friend separately. We are insisting mainly that certain

Social Psychology tendencies or attitudes will of themselves ruin the friendship and on this account the one who values both friend and friendship must give solemn warning that such tendencies or attitudes must be avoided. Only growth in worthy interests and in stable moral values gives promise of continued friendship; both friends must grow together along these lines or else cease to be friends. 

Also certain of the alignments which the individual has with groups may demand readjustment. 

Parties may change their principles and the individual maintain his. 

One source of authority over the individual may conflict with another; morality with the church, the church with the 

Why readjust individual - group alignments? state, the teacher with the parent, religion with business. In such cases the individual is called upon to make decisions and readjustment of his loyalties. Small group alignments involve many such conflicts and special problems. Men always have been attracted to the possibilities involved in the development of friendships within small groups. They have established monasteries for the culture of spiritual life. They have established numerous fraternal orders within the school or general social order. They have sought detached communities for selected groups. At least in theory these small groups need not be considered in opposition to the coordination of the larger social order. In fact Royce asserts that the broader loyalties will be improved only through "a new and wiser provincialism". He says, "I mean the sort of provincialism which makes people want to idealize, to adorn, to ennoble, to educate, their own province; to hold sacred its tradition, to honor its worthy dead, to support and multiply its public possessions". (A116:245-6). But instead of developing such fruitage the special group at times, in their zeal for the province, may neglect the wider

interests of the nation or larger social group. In such group relations it is easy to further the temporary advantage and forget the permanent. For instance, how commonly do we protect our children and brothers from due processes of law! Here is the case of a friend and brother who has offended against school or civic law. Shall I as his friend and brother bring him before the proper authorities for punishment or correction? Dare I as his friend and brother do otherwise? Can I shield him from public punishment and personal disgrace and through the creative influence of my friendship bring his character to the place where he will not offend again? To undertake this task is indeed to accept a grave responsibility. I should be willing to suffer in the place of my friend but I must be sure that my suffering does not weaken my own character and also lessen the moral responsibility of my friend. As a friend, I may stimulate him to make a firm stand against wrong, but I am his enemy and not his friend if I make the doing of wrong easier for him. Friendship at times involves a type of responsibility which may result in the loss of a personal friend or of a group alignment. Lyman Abbott said that we have a duty at times to punish. Hocking says, "Punishment, I repeat, is an expression of social hope-the hope of remaking or saving the man, by revealing to him in the language of deeds the meaning of his own deed”. (A67:- 

258). It should be said that our duty consists in the remaking, not in the punishing; the character must become right whether through punishment or without it. Punishment may or may not be interpreted rightly by the offend- Harsh or hasty punishment may harden rather than bring repentance. Probably it is a right impulse on the part of the friend to desire to suffer with the offender. By so doing he makes it plain that he does not desire to punish and that really he is interested in bringing the friend to hist

Social Psychology better self. But certain conclusions are plain. There must be no trifling with that which is wrong, either by individuals, groups or institutions. The individual must break an alignment rather than become a partner to that which is definitely wrong; the individual by the exercise of his best judgment must decide in a doubtful case whether an issue is right or wrong. Also there must be no self-deception or side stepping of reality or actual facts. If my party is at present in the wrong I must not call that wrong by a right name; to further the present attitude of a government when that government is plainly in the wrong is to act as a traitor to the permanent good of the country. The wrong must be righted and the institution must become rightminded if it claims my allegiance. Any institution that is fundamentally right, will repudiate a temporary form of wrong leadership and honor those members who has persisted quietly in the furtherance of its basic right tendencies. 

The general principles of morality must apply to the relationships of the individual to either the small or large group. 

He must consciously alter his adjustments until he comes as near as possible to having his alignments just and right. 

This evident duty calls for a somewhat constant readjustment of his group attachments and sentiments. 

In order to reduce this problem of readjusting sentiments more to a form of principles, it is necessary to consider just what values one should expect to receive from friend- 

What values should a ships. We quote from certain authorities suggesting first certain of friendship develop? the more general values involved in friendships. Aristotle speaks of friendship as “a thing most necessary for life, since no one would choose to live without friends though he should have all the other good things in the world:-". He says it "helps the young to keep from error", the old in respect to their weaknesses and

that it acts as "the bond of Social Communities". He mentions that "some go so far as to hold, that 'good man' and 'friend' are terms synonymous". (4:182-83). Cicero says that friendship adds to the relish of prosperity and divides the load of misfortune. (A27:178). Thoreau says, "in proportion to our truthfulness and confidence in one another, our lives are divine and miraculous, and answer to our ideal". (131:13). Hugh Black and Dean Brown both speak of the cooperative value of friendships. Black says, "Two together mean more than two added singly; for a new element is introduced which increases the power of each individually". (17:67). Brown says, "By the association of effort, individual energy is multiplied in a kind of geometrical ratio. 'One of you shall chase a thousand, and two shall put', not two thousand as we might naturally suppose but, 'ten thousand to flight'". (22:146). Seneca says, "Of all felicities, the most charming is that of a firm and gentle friendship. It sweetens all our cares, dispels our sorrows, and counsels us in all extremities". (A120:184).

MacCunn says the best aspect of friendship is our belief in what our friends expect of us. "For it is then that they become to us, not of their own choice but of ours, a kind of second conscience, in whose presence our weaknesses and back-slidings become 'that worst kind of sacrilege that tears down the invisible altar of trust' ". (88:115). Jane

Adams says a girl cannot be rescued unless brought back into a life of normal human fellowship. (1:178). Miss

Condé says that the very fact of friendship inspires one "with the power of sacrifice and unselfishness". (29:91).

It is evident that these interpreters of the values of friendship associate it closely with human happiness of the highest type, moral power and idealism. Even those who stress its potency to satisfy the intellectual and emotional cravings imply that thereby it betters and uplifts the life.

Social Psychology 

Friendship needs readjustment if it leads to certain rather definite types of character fruitage. Certainly the friendship should result in a positive stimulation of idealism on the part of both friends; this implies also that the institution What special character fruitage should friendship develop? make it easier for the individual to become idealistic and that the individual further the idealistic tendencies of the institutions to which he is attached. There should be a positive strengthening of intuitive trust in the rightness of worthy conceptions of God and man. Friendship that demands immediate contacts and rewards is very primitive and should grow speedily toward better things. Emerson says, "Leave this touching and clawing, Let him be to me a spirit.

A Message, a thought, a sincerity, a glance from him, I want, but not news nor pottage. I can get politics and chat, and neighborly conveniences, from cheaper companions". (41:121). Friendship should preserve and strengthen the original idealism of the child or the primary idealism of the institution. It is normal for young life to have great enthusiasm, optimism and trust in personal or spiritual forces; a new institutional movement of any kind has kindred characteristics. Friendship should do much toward keeping this attitude alive and toward counteracting the deadly materialism which comes from dependence upon physical and economic forces. Let one friendship prove worthy in every respect and trust in both God and man is likely to be strengthened greatly. Optimism and confidence should lead to the development of moral habits of thought and emotion. We get many of our major thought premises from our friends. Whether these are good or bad depends upon whether the habitual thinking of the friend is high or low. 

In a similar way our habitual types of enjoyment come to us from the type thing enjoyed by our friends. In order

to further right types of enjoyment we should associate with friends who enjoy right types of music, literature, recreation, etc. Further, our friendly relations have much to do with our habitual decisions and lines of conduct. Our worst enemies may be those who seek to pamper and indulge us. How many right-minded parents take pride in spoiling their children! We are coming to see that it is no light thing to spoil the character of another. It is better to sacrifice the right hand, right eye or even the physical life itself than to spoil the character. We must cause our friendships to take such adjustments as to further everything that is right and good in the character of both friend and self.

So we conclude that loyalty to moral idealism must act as a guiding principle in the forming of new friendships and How should our ideals in the reforming of old ones; this guide our sentiments? applies to group alignments or attachments as well as to individual friendships. Furtherance of some phase or implication of our supreme ideal is the prime justification for the formation or continuance of a friendship. The incarnation of the ideal should be furthered in the lives of both friends and in the steady assertion of the institution, whereever an institution is involved. The friendship which does most for self is likely also to do most for friend. If love is sublimated sufficiently we may say either, Love your neighbor as yourself or Love yourself as your neighbor; under such condition the two expessions mean the same. As we indicated on our chart of human impulses, the sublimation of egoism leads to altruism. Justice and good will rendered to others is a prerequisite to the harmonizing of the impulses of self, which harmonizing is an essential to individual happiness. Joseph Butler says, "there is a natural principle of benevolence in man; which is in

Social Psychology some degree to society, what self-love is to the individual." (A110:370). Worthy self-love and true benevolence each will be furthered if the friendship is dominated by the principles of the ideal. Also the friendship that is not so dominated is a menace to the character both of self and of friend. The friend is injured by encouragement to go the low way of life and self injured by adjustment to the more primitive tendencies of the friend. But there is much justice in the exhortation that we give a young life time to catch up with its ideals. In such a life there can be real idealism which is discernible only to the intuitive judgment of the friend. Friendship should imply patience involving a willingness to water, cultivate and wait for the fruitage in life which is slow in appearing. But also it should imply courage and decisiveness, involving power to terminate a pleasant relationship, whenever it is evident that such relationship is anti-idealistic. We must not be lured by friendship back into the primitive cave of ease and indulgence once we have escaped and been able to look squarely at the sun of idealism.
