CHAPTER VIII 

The Relation of the Sentiment to the Stability of 

Social Institutions. 

In this chapter we begin the discussion of applications of social psychology to institutional life. This chapter considers questions such as: May there be a form of undesirable stability on the part of an institution? What type of institutional stability is desirable? What bearing do the different types of sentiments have on institutional stability? 

What effects should the love of an institution have on the life of an individual? What benefits should the institution receive from the friendship of the individual? 

I. The Social Status of Institutional Stability. 

In this connection we consider: (1) conditions of institutional stability which seem undesirable; (2) desirable conditions of institutional stability. 

1. Undesirable Conditions of Institutional Stability. 

An institution may be stable in that it clings blindly and persistently to all its present forms which are based upon customs from the past. In the most extreme cases of blind 

What is blind conservatism? conservatism there is little discrimination between that which is major and that which is minor. 

Every tradition and custom as it comes from the past is hallowed and its maintenance is considered a part of loyalty to the institution. In order to make sure that the exact conditions of the past are maintained often the tendency is to stress the external form rather than the vital principle. 215

Social Psychology 

Loyalty to the fathers as a form of ancestor worship is likely to retain the same tool or form under conditions which are not at all suited to its use. Harvesting with a cradle was at one time a progressive step in agriculture and those who used it were the progressives of the community; the cradle was greatly in advance of the old sickle which preceded it. But to retain the use of the cradle in a day of binders and headers is to be loyal only to the forms and practices of the fathers and to be very disloyal to their ideals and attitudes. The same is true of a civic, educational or religious organization that maintains the words and forms of its leader and organizer but neglects to attain his ideals and his tendency to use the best which the present affords to serve the highest interests of the future. Any type of party-philosophical, medical, athletic, social, etc. -may keep its party cries but concentrate its attention on problems of the past rather than those of the present looking toward the future. So the blind conservative presents only the external semblance of his institution and is likely to sacrifice its basic aims, general attitudes and broad principles. An organization founded to promote civic liberty may come to favor certain methods of militaristic oppression. A church founded to promote organic unity among the churches may come to be extremely sectarian and bigoted. Such contradictions come about by holding blindly to traditional forms and means rather than basic principles and ideals. 

Why is external coercion undesirable?

A semblance of institutional stability may be preserved by external coercion. A nation or group of small nations may be held together for a time by an external autocratic government. Educational or religious institutions may for a time coerce their members into a semblance of unity. The members of a family may be kept

Sentiment and Stability 217 together because through the agency of the home they secure the necessities of life they could not secure otherwise. Individuals in a civic party may be constrained to maintain the externals of the party organization by the threat of party revelations or the promise of party positions. Even bandits may maintain for a time the appearance of external unity due to a common fear of social punishment. Stability maintained by all such forms of external coercion is undesirable. It is undesirable because it gives no promise of continued stability along lines of healthy growth. In fact there is no real foundation for stability and there is danger at any time of the collapse of the institution. Stability will be maintained only so long as conditions warrant submission to the external authority. The stability of such an institution is endangered by public education or by the organization of other institutions offering more freedom to the individual. 

May institutional stability disregard environmental change?

There is, at times, an attempt to maintain the stability of an organization after there ceases to be a demand for it; there was a definite demand for it at the time it was founded but social conditions have so changed as to make no demand for this institution at present. Institutions refuse to die after their particular social service has been accomplished. Religious denominations carry the controversies of one generation or country into the life of a generation or people to whom they have no meaning; there was a recent report of the establishment in China of an American Dutch Reformed Church. 

Institutions find it difficult to adjust to scientific discoveries and inventions which change social demands so radically. We continue to fear race suicide and deplore small families when in fact there is a distressing condition of unemployment and overpopulation. We continue to advocate both farm relief and great national projects for the

Social Psychology opening of new areas of farm land through irrigation. We rejoice at the invention of new machinery and deplore the unemployment produced by it. Even a war is fought to end war and great military establishments are maintained in order to maintain peace. Religious authorities have fought against the discovered facts that the world moves, is spherical in shape and is not the center of the solar system. There is a set to institutional life which at times carries it much beyond the completion of the particular task for which it was organized. Such form of institutional stability must be deplored. 

2. Desirable Conditions of Institutional Stability. 

But in addition to a mere semblance of stability and a condition of undesirable stability there may be on the part of an institution a real and a desirable stability. We consider what connection that stability has with institutional progress and with individual freedom. 

(1). The Correlation of the Stability with the Growth of an Institution.

Should institutional stability permit of growth?

The first and primary condition of healthy and continued life on the part of an institution is that its stability permits of growth and progress towards its ideals. Progress and stability must not be reckoned as contrasting terms. Stable progress should be considered the most desirable condition of institutional stability. In a very true and real sense the letter and the form are the enemies of the spirit and the ideals of the institution. To fix attention on the externals with great concern to maintain them as they are, is the same as to take attention from the vital principles; attention can be fixed

Sentiment and Stability 219 upon only one object at a time. But if attention is upon the aim or ideal, then it is evident that such ideal can be furthered through a series of changing forms without loss of institutional stability. The law of inertia applies with equal force to moving as to stationary objects. In certain instances the moving object may be much more stable than the stationary one; the spinning earth is much more stable than the "eternal hills", with their tendency to develop erosion. The institution which is concerned mainly in the application of its basic principles to the environmental needs of its time, is the institution most certain to be stable. 

So we should think of growth toward an ideal as one of the necessary conditions for stability on the part of an institution.

It is highly important that the supporters of an institution be motivated by the desire to promote the social service intended by the basic principles of the organization. Its constituency must support the main purpose of the institu- 

What is the intellition above any name or form it gent support of an may take at any particular time institution? or place. There can be no objection to a party loyalty if the loyalty is directed to the support of the right interests. But the feeling loyalty that supports a local church to the detriment of a denomination of a denomination to the detriment of the Kingdom of God, is a feeling loyalty that needs enlightenment. Those who insist on injuring the religious life of communities by crowding too many churches into them need to note what the real seer says: "I saw no Sanctuary in the City, for the Lord God, the Ruler of all, is its Sanctuary, and so is the Lamb". (Rev. 21:22). The church which is intended to bring men to God is forgetful of its purpose when it tries to maintain its life under conditions which demand its elimination in order to further its real

Social Psychology purpose. What is true of a church is, of course, true of a school of medicine, a type of educational institution, a civic party, a form of taxation or economic control, etc. The basic principles of the institution must have the intelligent support of its members. If the institution is stable no large element in its constituency is in favor of its disruption; no major revolution exists in the state and no considerable number of the supporters of the school desire that it be disbanded. Besides this every member of the constituency assumes the perpetuity of the institution; no question is being raised as to its continuance, as such continuance is assumed by all. Further, it is desirable that the institution be recognized by other institutions of its kind; as a state it should have a recognized place among states and as a business organization or other institution it should receive recognition by institutions of its own type. We repeat that such support must be of the stable principles of the institution rather than of any particular group of its leaders, or any particular form that it may take at any one time. 

(2). The Correlation of Individual Liberty with Institutional Dominance. 

It may be profitable at this point to give some attention to the old question of the relation of individuality to institutional authority and stability. It has seemed to many 

May one be individ. that the alternative is either the ualistic and loyal to supremacy of the individual or that of the institution. Eddy says, "On an institution? the one hand, if the state has all power it becomes an insufferable tyranny, as in Russia, both under the czar and under military communism. On the other hand, if the individual has all power and the state has none, a yet more dangerous anarchy is introduced and chaos results". (B26:XLI:1202). Eddy states, however, that

Sentiment and Stability 221 there must be respect shown for individual conscience in such cases as those in which the thought of the individual seems to be clear and his convictions pronounced. He says further, "It is against the background of nineteen centuries of Christian ideals of freedom, of a thousand years of struggle in Anglo-Saxon countries for religious and political liberty, of three centuries of American traditions that the right of liberty of conscience on moral issues in peace or in war has been forever defined". (B26:XLI:1203). But to support the necessity of an absolute assertion on the part of either individual or institution is to assume the falsity of the democratic principle of government. If the free assertion of conscience and individual thought will overthrow government and produce chaos, then it follows that general education and encouragement of individuality of thought and decision are dangerous tendencies. Rather, we must assume the obligation of each individual to further and support the best interests of his institutions with the full confidence that we can trust the correctness of his individual thought and the social rightness of his individual decisions. Confidence in government by all the people assumes that on the basis of free conscience, involving individual thought and decision the state or other institution will not suffer but that its best interests will be furthered thereby. As indicated on our chart of human impulses, egoism at its best supports intelligent altruism. It is possible that majority decision on the part of free citizens may for a time lead to wrong decisions. But after the vigorous protest and perhaps martyrdom of certain leaders or even of a persistent minority, we must assume-if we are to maintain faith in democratic government-that the majority opinion will come to be that which will further the best interests both of the individual and of the principles of the institution. The offering of the individual life as a pro-

Social Psychology test against some system of thought or tendency of government is bound to secure a hearing and our confidence is that once the question has been squarely faced by the majority that the final decision will be a right decision. There is no essential reason why the individual cannot be a conscientious, intelligent and loyal supporter of his institutions. 

But the individual needs principles of guidance for his thinking and for the expression of his individuality. Mere revolt against authority is no index of a desirable ex- How should individ. pression of individuality. It takes as much individuality to appreuality be guided toward institutional supciate and accept leadership in an port? intelligent fashion as to make an intelligent protest against such leadership. The thoughtful individual always will recognize the necessity of supporting a delegated leader just so far as his thinking and conscience permits. The present administration holds the responsibility of government and as governmental leadership is imperative, should receive support from the individual to the extent that such can be given honestly and with no violation of conscience. It is to be kept in mind that individual conscience is not infallible and must always be open to enlightenment; this does not imply, however, that one is justified at any time in going counter to the dictates of his own clear conscience and sober judgment. Further, the individual must guard against narrow, temporary loyalties and especially against the substitution of means for ends. A temporary victory gained for the party by dishonest means constitutes disloyalty to the basic principles of the party. A harsh expression of discipline in school life may bring quiet and external order for the time but may injure the cooperation between pupil and teacher which is so essential to school progress. The clerk who succeeds in selling imperfect goods

Sentiment and Stability 223 thereby injures to some extent the reputation of his company. The individual always must keep in mind the broader interests, the basic principles and the idealistic tendencies of the institution to which he is loyal. The furtherance of a previously successful means never should be thought of as a duty, unless it at present also is judged the best means for advancing a worthy end. 

II. The Relation of the Types of the Sentiment to Insti tutional Stability.

Here we consider: (1) types of sentiments and their effects on desirable forms of institutional stability; (2) the effect of the loyal support of an institution on the one who supports it and on the institution. 

1. Individual Hatred as a Factor in Institutional 

Stability.

Since the sentiment is an organization of the more basic and potent of the innate tendencies it seems obvious that its support of an institution must make for its stability. 

In this connection we are consider- 

Does hatred of others unify an institution? ing the possibilities involved in the different types of sentiments. Enmity between institutions has at times seemed to be a force tending to unify the institutions involved. A nation at war, so far as external appearances go, gives, the manifestation of marked national unity. Any civic or social party under the stress of attack by another party is likely to suppress for the time being its internal factions and display an external semblance of unity. But it is rather obvious that such appearance of unity is superficial and gives no promise of possessing lasting qualities. It belongs to the type of undesirable, rather than that of desirable, forms of unity.

Social Psychology 

The individual who buys Liberty bonds under the pressure of public opinion during war does not for that reason form a more intelligent and lasting attachment to his country. 

Nor does the man whose loyalty to his business company consists in discrediting their opponents give any great promise of being a useful member to the firm. The man who learns to hate outside his party will the more readily learn to hate different divisions within his own party. 

Fighting against an enemy may lead readily to fighting within the constituency; how often in history have a group of associated and victorious generals fought for the division of the spoils among themselves! The parties, factions, divisions and strife, which everywhere have followed the recent great war must be obvious to any careful observer. The semblance of unity within a group, produced by the hatred of its members for another group, is superficial and external because it depends upon external situations which are not likely to persist. In cases where the unity depends upon this factor only, there are no positive internal attachments and hence we are not justified in placing dependence upon hatred of others as a means of producing desirable institutional stability. 

2. The Sentiment of Regard for an Institution Consid ered as a Factor in the Development of Institutional Stability.

An individual may attach himself to an institution by developing a high regard or respect for it; thus he comes to have a pride in the prosperity of an institution which in a sense is his own. The institution may be thought of as an agency for self-expression and individual joy and elation may be felt at its success and similar depression and sorrow at its failure. But if the institution has this basis of support only, its stability will be insecure. The individual

Sentiment and Stability 225 who uses an institution as a medium for the expression of self is too selfish in a narrow sense to be considered loyal to the institution. In reality the interests of the institution 

Does my regard for an institution further its stability? are subordinate to those of self. A demand is made upon the institution to honour self rather than a pledge of self to the support of the institution. But if an institution is to maintain its stability and healthy growth it must be judged worthy of a loyalty which takes precedence to that given to any individual including self. The whole must be considered larger and more important than any of its parts. In like manner the interests of the whole institution must take precedence to any division of it or to any individual considered as one of its members. This does not imply that the conscience of the individual must be subordinate to institutional machinery; we are saying rather that the interests of the one individual cannot take precedence to the interests of many. The individual who thinks of the institution as merely a means for self-expression and self-gratification in times of stress and strain is apt to desert the organization in order to preserve his own interests or reputation. Certainly it is desirable that an institution be of such a nature as to justify the individual in feeling pride in the fact that it is his institution. But pride by itself tends to be a hard, cold, selfish attitude which does not constitute a sufficient guarantee of loyalty to interests, which are not immediate and personal, to justify confidence that it will be a major factor in the production of institutional stability. 

3. Love for an Institution Considered as a Factor Mak ing for Its Stability. 

Here we consider: (1) some of the psychological implica-

Social Psychology tions of the love of an individual for an institution; (2) some institutional benefits growing out of such friendly loyalty. 

(1). Certain Psychological Implications of the Love of an Individual for an Institution.

We consider first something of the intellectual implications of the love of an individual for an institution. We are using the word institution in a very wide sense and How does my love for thinking of any possible party relaan institution affect tionship as being involved in it. We are not considering at this place my thought of it? whether the changes in the thought of the individual must precede or whether they may follow the friendly attachment to the institution. Regardless of this temporal order of development it is evident that before there exists on the part of the individual a stable friendship for an institution that organization must be highly valued by him. If he possesses a friendly loyalty for the institution he believes sincerely that there are great possibilities involved in it. He may consider the institution at present faulty and inefficient but if he really loves it, he must consider its potentialities worthy and great; love and a conception of immortality are closely akin. My love for an institution carries with it a confidential belief in its perpetuity. Such belief permits, and in fact insists, that the institution undergo such changes as make for the idealization of its status. Such favorable major premises constitute an automatic background element in the thought of anyone who loves an institution. 

Further, the love of an individual for an institution involves the pledge of the sacrificial giving of self to the furtherance of the interests of the institution. This subordination of self is in behalf of the ideal institution but pos-

Sentiment and Stability 227 

What pledge of self does love for an institution imply? sibly not in behalf of the present leadership of the institution. A present administration may make demands upon the individual which he is not able to concede. One may be convinced that war as such is highly destructive of the interests of any nation that engages in it or he may be persuaded that this particular war is in no sense justified. Thus to refuse the lead of a present administration to bear arms is in no sense to revolt against government or to violate loyalty to a nation as such. Practically every contract to submit to authority involves reasonable exceptions. I take directions from the head of my business firm but not such as involve dishonest practices. A child obeys his parents but not in disregard of his health or the health of others. An athlete obeys his coach but not to the permanent injury or death of an opposing player. Even a hypnotized person refuses to strike a person with a steel sword although he will strike with a paper sword. Such exceptions are expected of obedient subordinates. The real institution will not profit by the degradation of its members, nor is it likely to make demands upon the individual contrary to his highest social interests. Demands may be made for expression of effort and for physical and economic sacrifice but not for violation of conscience or degradation of soul life. Intelligent altruism does not conflict with the real spiritual interests of the individual.

Friendly attachment to an institution calls for the expression of many of the same emotions involved in a friendship between individuals. It is quite possible and also natural for the individual to feel tenderness for the ideal institution. Conscience may be aroused in relation to the institution as well as in response to the mistreatment of an individual. The disloyalty of self to the better interests

Social Psychology 

What emotions are involved in an individ. ual's love for an institution? con- of the institution is condemned by conscience. The main danger in this connection is that conscience become subordinated to the mere name, external form or present administration, of the institution rather than to its more ideal and and permanent interests; science, in the case of the ordinary individual, is likely to condemn the failure to vote for the traditional party regardless of whether that party at this election stands for its traditional principles or not. Also such common emotions as fear, anger and elation may be aroused readily in relation to an institution. 

There may be fear that the institution be injured in some way or that it become degraded or formalistic. Anger may be aroused by the expression of opposition to the essential interests of the institution or by the tendency towards degeneracy of its leaders or elements of its constituency. In like manner elation will be aroused at the real prosperity of the idealistic interests of the institution, and subjection, sorrow or chagrin, at its defeat or humiliation. 

Also an individual's love for an institution must have a definite effect on his expressional life; it will cause him ha- 

How does my love for bitually to give expressions of conan institution affect fidence in, and good will towards, it. The expressions in words and my decisions and acdeeds should be on the basis of tions? motives of good will towards the aims of the organization. Verbally, and in action, he should express confidence in the growth and attainments of the institution. This might involve the investment in the bonds of a business concern, the sending of a child to an educational institution or the trusting of a particular school of medicine for the cure of a disease. His expressional attitude should include decisions looking toward future ac-

Sentiment and Stability 229 tions as well as present expressions. His ballot should be cast with regularity on the side of the better interests of the institution; this at times is likely to involve decisions which to the regular party man seem disloyal. His financial support should be regular and hearty in behalf of all tendencies making for the advancement and idealization of the institution. 

(2). Certain Institutional Benefits Furthered by the Love of the Individual. 

Love is essentially a protective tendency. The love of its members should guard an institution from degeneracy, just as love guards the health, happiness and other interests of the individual. There are dan- 

How should love progers of degeneration on the part of tect an institution? an institution which are subtle and not recognized readily by those who think in terms of the present or the part in distinction to the whole. An institution may lower its standards in order to secure an increase in its membership. Thus members are likely to be admitted, the influence of whose lives makes for the lowering of institutional standards. In various ways there may develop the attempt to secure ready and hence superficial results. In the home the young child may be silenced and suppressed by harsh methods to the end that temporary relief be secured from a troublesome situation. But such harshness and the feeling of resentment which follows it is likely to spoil the fine spirit which should characterize the home. Especially is it important to protect the institution from the intense but narrow loyalty of its members. 

Such loyalty as tends to promote the idea that the poorest Republican is better than the best Democrat soon comes to believe that the interests of the Republican party are superior to those of the nation. Many have such an intense

Social Psychology spirit of nationalism as to be entirely blinded to the broader interests of justice; actions which if performed by a foreign country would be resented bitterly are either, not noted at all, or condoned, if performed by one's own country. An intense loyalty to a particular name, form, ceremony or temporary status, should be taken as an indication of degeneracy. In this sense especially is it true that the form is the enemy of the spirit. Hence it follows that to protect the real spirit, attitude and ideals of an institution we must guard against a marked pledging of loyalty to its forms. Crude expressions of love may injure an institution greatly. Intelligent expressions of love may protect and promote the best interests of the institution. 

The effect of love, at its best is to promote stable and steady growth on the part of the institution. The primitive loyalty which seeks to advance the status of an or- How should love in. ganization at the expense of other fluence institutional organizations of kindred type, is growth? tending to promote apparent but not real growth. The nation which expands by stealing its neighbor's territory is sowing seeds which some day will bring a harvest of destructive warfare. 

The church which increases its membership at the expense of a neighboring church has apparent growth but by doing so, drives further into the future that brotherhood and cooperation of Christian people which is the main objective of the Kingdom of God. The business concern whose prosperity means the detriment of another firm of similar interests is not making stable progress. We must not forget that the part never can prosper to the detriment of the whole. If neighboring nations are weak and poor there can be no profitable trade relations with them. No one college prospers permanently unless all colleges prosper. In his temptation Jesus decided against the use of those dra-

Sentiment and Stability 231 matic methods which would secure the rapid but superficial growth of his movement. He thought it best to give his life to the sowing of seed and the laying of foundations which would insure a more stable and permanent growth of his Kingdom. Love for the practical application of high idealism does not become popular readily with large numbers of people. It is easy to create thirst for alcohol but a similar definite and potent thirst for righteousness is a matter of slow growth and probably attainable at this stage of man's development only by a limited number of people. 

Such "direct methods" as promise a ready attainment of idealistic aims should be looked upon with suspicion or at least scrutinized with extreme care by those who love and cherish the best interests of their institution. Probably there is no short cut by which the fullest amount of liberty and security for universal democracy can be attained. Nor can stable spirituality be implanted in human lives by any other means than by slow growth and development. So the real friend of an institution should be satisfied with steady but slow growth toward its supreme end. 

What and progress stability should loy. alty promote?

The friend of the institution should insist that it face forward and advance toward the realization of its aims. 

The thought of stability should not be detached from that of 

Life means moveprogress. ment and unless such movement be in the way of progress it will of necessity take the course of disintegration or degeneracy. The youth of the coming generation will not ally itself with a stagnant organization. However the demand for progress should not be interpreted in a narrow way. Progress may be of various sorts. There may be such progress as is evident to all in the way of growth in size or increased effectiveness of the institution. 

Also the institution may grow by becoming more effectively

Social Psychology organized or by educating a larger percentage of its constituency in its more basic principles. Or the institution may give evidence of its growth by its increased effectiveness in arousing its basic ideals in the minds of the general public. Such progress in the end may make the institution unnecessary to the general social life. The organization which has as its purpose the freeing of a certain group of slaves should be satisfied to reorganize and state a new aim when its particular object has been attained. The fact that organizations strive to live after their avowed aims have been realized shows that the attachment may be to the form and name of the organization rather than to its basic principles and aims. In brief we may say that progress and stability should be mutually casual. Progress should be of such a sort as not to disturb stability but rather to further it. Stability should find expression in progress and also should find in such advancement the guarantee of its continuance. This means that stability must be of such a sort as to permit progress. Now the only stability that will permit of progress is the stability of spirit and idealistic aim rather than the stability of external form. Hence progress of the type desired must be in the directing or realizing of the ideal, rather than of externalizing it or selecting a certain genetic stage of its embodiment as a substitute for it. It must be the principles of home life and friendly relationship which remain stable, rather than any form which these principles appear to take in a particular generation or historical period. Forms of all types and varieties are, and must be, subject to change but back of the form may remain the vital principle which had its earlier embodiment in this particular form. Thus real progress must mean the choice of those forms of expression which adjust in a better way the vital principle of the institution to the environmental need of the time.
