9. "And there is unity where there is community of pleasures and pains-where all the citizens are glad or grieved on the same occasions of joy or sorrow?" (A106:194). Can such a condition be realized by planning for it? Would it be desirable? 

10. "The citizen of the United States need not be blind to the crudities, the blunders, and the shortcomings of his nation. Criticism it not tabooed by patriotism". (C92:76). Do you agree? Is this true of all citizens regardless of age or time or residence in the United States? 

11. The demand for Christian unity is imperative". (B22: 

159). Is progress being made toward Christian unity? 

What special forces are promoting it? Should the churches come to think of the more narrow attitudes of denominationalism as sin?

CHAPTER IX A Conception of  of Sentiments: 

A Basic Social Distinction as Regards Types of Sentiments. 

In this chapter we propose a single, but rather fundamental, thesis namely: that love is good and hate is evil. 

Is love good as to its basic motives? Is it good as to its character and social effects? In like manner is hate evil as to character and social effects? Or is hate good when it is directed against evil? Is love evil when its object is evil? Such problems as these center about the central thesis of the chapter. 

I. A Moral Distinction Based upon the Nature of the Central Impulse of the Sentiment. 

We need to give careful consideration to the moral status of the types of sentiments in order to determine which should be nurtured. This is especially pertinent as we begin the consideration of the more detailed social application of the sentiments. We consider here: (1) the type of sentiment which favors social stability and progress; (2) the type which hinders or impedes social unity and accord. 

1. The Types of Sentiments which Promote the Social 

Order. 

(1). The Consideration of Extreme Types of Sentiments for the Purpose of Making a Basic Distinction. 

For the purposes of clarifying the discussion we confine our attention in this chapter to the consideration of the sentiments of love and hate. As our chart (see page 113) in- 235

Social Psychology dicates we are considering regard or respect as being a much milder form of sentiment than love and hate; this sentiment does not have in it the powerful drives of tenderness and disgust. For the most part its 

How does regard relate to love and hate? influence is on the side of desirable social relations. If all individuals had respect for the personality of all others the basis for much of the evil in social relations would be removed; this would be true especially if all institutions held each other in respect. But whatever can be said for regard as a social adjustor can be said with much more conviction for love and friendship. So for purposes of making a basic distinction we need not mention regard further. 

(2). The Tendency of the Impulse of Love or Friend ship to Promote Social Accord.

In the first place we may say that the impulse in love and friendship is social in that it promotes social good will; we are thinking of the social good as being the determiner 

Is the motive of love social? of that which is called "moral". In all cases in which this sentiment is involved the lover cherishes good will toward the beloved. The famous statements of Plato and of the Apostle Paul concerning the basic aim of love seem on first reading to be somewhat in disagreement. 

Plato suggests that loves desires "The possession of the good" and thereby gains "happiness". Paul says that love is not "self-assertive" and does not tend to "aggrandize herself". (Weymouth translation). But the gaining of happiness would seem to involve assertion and the possession of the good a form of aggrandizement. But the possession of the good is for the good of the object as well as for the good of self. By this type of assertion and protective care the good and happiness of both self and object is furthered.

Plato does not say that love seeks happiness but rather gains it by possessing and furthering the good. The basic trend in love is the desire for the good of its object. Such a good motive may, of course, express itself in a weak and foolish fashion. But regardless of this we are safe in saying that the motive of love or friendship is good. 

Does love promote social cooperation? 

The social nature of love or friendship is seen further in the fact that such sentiment tends to encourage and to maintain cooperative social relations. At times the expression of good will may be so misguided as to do actual social harm. But such cannot be taken as its normal or usual expression. Also it is possible that a Platonic love may be developed to the end that possession of the ideal may be substituted for cooperative activity with the object of the sentiment. But for the most part such idealistic friendships seem to develop after close contacts rather than before; Havelock Ellis insists that the 

Platonic must be reached through the erotic. Also the motives of the beloved are deemed friendly and the potentialities of the beloved are considered promising. All this encourages cooperative relations between those who are friends to any degree. Also there is a tendency to maintain such cooperative attitude. In a sense somewhat different from that meant by Paul, "Love never fails"; Paul seems to refer to the stable place which love must continue to have in a life. But love never completely fails to win a social victory. It has won an ideal victory already by its very development. It has wonderful powers to recreate its object. It has sincere trust that its object will reciprocate faithfully. This feeling of power and trust gives it wonderful endurance even when not reciprocated. Such an enduring attitude seldom fails to bring about a measure of cooperation between self and the object of the sentiment.

Social Psychology 

Love does not fail to maintain its motive and the maintenance of such motive does not fail completely to bring a measure of permanent social accord. (3). The Social Direction by the Sentiment of the 

Dynamic of the Emotional Units of Love or 

Friendship. 

When we spoke of the genesis of the sentiment we noted that its emotional units are not assertive to the same degree; this we attempt to show graphically (see page 113) by 

How does tenderness the different length of lines connectin love influence ela- ing the sentiment with the primary tion and subjection? emotions. The impulse of tenderness is sufficiently dominant to give its motivation to the sentiment and hence to the other contained emotional elements. We think of the impulse of tenderness as primarily the parental and especially the maternal impulse. Norsworthy and Whitley speak of it as "that desire to shelter that makes for the warmth and intimacy of home relations". (97:95). McDougall says, "Its impulse is primarily to afford physical protection to the child, especially by throwing the arms about it;-". (90:74). Like any other native impulse it must be moralized or sublimated to perform its work in a way meeting social approval; but this is no justification for the assertion of Bernard that, "It is an acquired emotional attitude". (15:335). Its sublimation is along the basic lines of its innate tendencies; if this is all that "acquired" means then we may say that all human impulses are acquired. As soon as an understanding of the object of tenderness is attained, joy is felt in its supposed prosperity and depression and sorrow on the occasion of its injury or defeat. 

This extension of self-interests to the interests of another individual or institution give to joy, elation, subjection or

sorrow a distinctly social turn. The influence of the sentiment over these emotional units is a part of the evidence for the assertion that the sentiment itself deserves to be called social or moral.

The important impulse of curiosity with its emotion of wonder come to be influenced in a definite way by the basic impulse of the sentiment of love or friendship. In its 

How does love influ- original state as a tendency, curiosity is not social. Shand says it ence wonder? is "a single impulse to know, instinctively governing and sustaining the attention, and evoking those bodily movements which will enable us to gain a fuller acquaintance with its object". (121:438).

McDougall speaks a little more definitely and says, "its impulse is to approach and to examine more closely the object that excites it". (90:60). If you will consider what happens in the small community at times in the way of talebearing and slander, made possible by curiosity about the details of a neighbor's affairs, you will realize how antisocial and destructive curiosity can be. But curiosity under the influence of love takes a social trend. The attention becomes instinctive or emotional and the tendency of the impulse is not merely to know in general but to discover what is good about the object. Creative imagination is aroused which leads to optimistic idealization of the object of curiosity. Thus a natural tendency to discover, comes to be an active tendency to discover that which is right and good about the object. This socialization of wonder and curiosity is due to the influence of tenderness which has come to function as the major impulse of the sentiment of love. 

Also love or friendship comes to have a very marked influence over the very powerful protective impulse of fear which is embodied within its system. That love has a dis-

Social Psychology ence its contained element of fear? tinct influence on fear is recognized commonly. The beloved and loving apostle writes, "Love has in it no element of fear; but perfect love drives away fear, because fear

How does love influ. involves pain, and if a man gives way to fear there is something imperfect in his love". (I John 4:18).

We interpret this to mean that the sentiment of love causes the lover to cease to fear the beloved; it cannot be taken to imply that he ceases to fear for the welfare of the beloved. The protective impulse of love produces this effect. If one is to protect and appropriate an object, he must so relate that object to self as to cease to fear it; this may be expressed by saying that the self is so enlarged as to make the object a part of self. The protective tendency of primitive love may be so shortsighted and misguided as actually to do harm rather than good. But with fully developed love the element of protection is extended to the real values of the object and comes to be intelligent in its expression. Thus a social influence is developed in the direction of social cooperation. The defense tendency by itself leads readily to suspicion, preparation against attack and hence to social disruption. 

Fear needs to work within the system of love in order to function to the good of the social order. 

Lastly let us consider the influence of the sentiment of love over the assertive impulse of anger. Stratton says, "Anger is an achievement in mental progress.- -The better kinds of animal life depend upon its powerful aid”. (128:35). The arousal of anger 

How does love influence anger within its depends upon the partial blocking of the expression of other native system? impulses. McDougall says, "The condition of its excitement is rather any opposition to the free exercise of any impulse, any obstruction to the activity

to which the creature is impelled by any one of the other instincts". (90:62). No question need be raised as to the benefit of this reenforcing tendency toward the accomplishment of our aims and purposes. But our praise must be reserved for sublimated and not given to primitive anger. 

Note Seneca's introductory sentence to his discussion of anger. "We are here to encounter the most outrageous, brutal, dangerous and intractable of all passions; the most loathsome and unmannerly; nay, the most ridiculous too; and the subduing of this monster will do a great deal toward the establishment of human peace". (A120:225). Of course, the plan of the Stoic is to subject anger to rigid selfcontrol. There can be no criticism of self-control in so far as it promotes the social good. But a more excellent way of dealing with anger has been disclosed to us since the days of the Roman Stoics. Voluntary assertion in the way of self-control is very uncertain in its outcome and very expensive in its use of energy. But rightly socialized or sublimated anger eliminates the necessity of self-control. In a sentiment such as friendship anger fights not against an object but in its behalf. If felt against the object it takes the form of reproach which is temporary and not destructive of social relations; it is not directed against the ideal object but against a temporary and false manifestation of the object. Anger comes to be aroused by any interference with the expressional tendencies of the object of love. 

Its impulse is given to make possible the realization of these tendencies. Its development within the system of love restrains anger from making associations with disgust and thus prevents the development of the antisocial tendencies of vengeance and malice. Whether the potency of anger in reality promotes the highest expressional tendencies of the object of love, depends on whether correct value judgments are held by the self as to which of the expression-

Social Psychology al tendencies of the object are of most worth. But regardless of certain blunders of judgment it remains true that the incorporation of anger within the system of love inhibits its antisocial tendencies and promotes its social potentialities; the friend comes not to fight against, but for, and in association with, the object of the sentiment. 

2. The Type of Sentiment The Social Impulse of which Tends to Disrupt the Social Order. 

We note here: (1) the social destructiveness of the impulse of hatred; (2) the tendency of hatred to give a wrong turn to the emotional units within its system. 

(1). The Antisocial Nature of the Motive of Hatred. 

The basic impulse in the sentiment of hatred develops opposition to the object of the sentiment. Darwin says, "Few individuals, can long reflect about a hated person, What is the nature of without feeling and exhibiting the motive of hatred? signs of indignation and rage". (31:237). Bogardus says, "Hate is an organization of emotional energy against a person or a group believed to be hostile". (A18:17). Shand speaks more definitely of this opposition when he says, "Destruction then becomes the prominent end of hatred". (121:58). It is evident that these writers believe that the motive of the sentiment of hatred is antisocial in so far as the relation of the self to the object of hatred is concerned. It is quite possible that the question of Shylock, "Hates any man the thing he would not kill?" can be answered rightfully at times in the affirmative. In certain cases hatred does not seek to destroy its object but only to impede its welfare; only when it has developed within its complex the secondary emotions of vengeance and malice does it work toward

Is hatred basically antisocial? the total destruction of its object. If the object is a person the impulse tends to produce annoyance, injury or death. If the object is an institution the impulse is to discredit it and to hinder its prosperity; to hate militarism is to oppose it and to point out its wrong motives and results. 

Hatred seems in its very nature to be basically antisocial. 

It develops cynical and materialistic assumptions concerning the object hated. Hocking in speaking of enmity says, "Its sojourn in the system is like that of a disease which gradually summons out the forces of immunity and rejection". (67:54). There is a tendency for the system to become a unity in its expressional nature. 

During the war we were tempted to believe that we could both love and slay our enemies; but our basic nature revolts against such contradictions. Will Irwin points out that the generous kind impulses of men had to be drilled out of them before they became effective soldiers. He says, "Bayonet practice, the jabbing of a dummy German in vital points of the enemy, was a most effective piece of 'psychological preparation', the sergeants in charge of this game invented a kind of hymn of hate, a familiar combination of American oaths chanted to the swing of the rifle. It went like this: 'God (present point) 'damn' (swing back) 'you' (thrust)". (87:57). We cannot hate without assuming that the motives of the enemy are wrong and selfish. 

In speaking of religious antagonisms Jefferson says, "Some people are crazy about the Roman Catholic Church. They use a muck rake, and rake together all the smut and filth which they find in the alleys of the Roman Catholic world. 

God punishes them for doing that. Such people are thrown into panic. They become victims of an hysteria which is dangerous and pitiable". (75:173). This is just one example of the social dissension which is caused by cherish-

Social Psychology ing sentiments of hatred. The tendency of suspicion and antagonism soon spreads beyond the immediate enemy; this leads readily to a distrust of the children, employes and friends of the enemy. Thus considerable groups are likely to be brought into hostile relations. Wrong motives are assumed on both sides, secret alignments are developed, and thus a vicious circle is started which makes it very hard to restore social relationships. The person or institution hated is deemed bad beyond repair and hence there is little likelihood of the revelation of the better self on either side such as would tend to make the restoration of cooperative relationships possible. (2). The Tendency of the Sentiment of Hatred to Give an Antisocial Turn to the Impulses of Its Contained Emotional Units.

The sentiment of hatred gets its original motavation (see synthetic chart on page 113) from the impulse of disgust or repulsion. Since this impulse comes to be rather central to the sentiment of hatred, it 

What is the basic impulse of disgust? is important that we understand its nature rather definitely. Quoting from Richet, Ribot says, "Disgust is connected with conservation; it is 'an instinctive feeling of protection"". (112:204). McDougall says, "The impulse differs from that of fear in that, while the latter prompts the bodily retreat from its object, the former prompts to actions that remove or reject the offending object". (90:58). Darwin relates it primarily to the sense of taste and to new experiences. He says, "The term 'disgust', in its simplest sense, means something offensive to the taste. It is curious how readily this feeling is excited by anything unusual in the appearance, odour, or nature of our food". (31:256). Such a protective impulse is deserving only of praise. This is

true especially of the functioning of disgust in the aesthetic and moral realm. Disgust at the uncouth and immoral is a potent protector against the tendency to become accustomed to such undesirable expressions; without disgust we would be slaves to sensuality and to all forms of the customary. So it is to be understood that condemnation of hatred is not to be taken as condemnation of disgust in its primary form. But the motivation given to hatred by disgust becomes a sinister influence over the other emotional elements involved in that sentiment. 

We note first the influence of the impulse given to hatred by disgust over the tendency of curiosity or wonder; we think of wonder as referring more to the emotion and curiosity more to the impulse to investigate. Shand suggests opposition between curiosity and disgust. He says, “Disgust tends to exclude curiosity about its object, and all further knowledge of it; and curiosity, likewise, tends to repress disgust at its object". (121:378). He says further that disgust tends to repress pity and sorrow on behalf of the object and that these also react against disgust. Shand makes a distinction between disgust and repugnance. He says of repugnance, "At the conceptual level of intelligence repugnance tends to attribute bad qualities to its object, and to deny or lose sight of the good ones, substituting negative for positive values". (121:403). We are using the term "disgust" in a broad 

How does hatred influence wonder? enough sense to include repugnance. What Shand says about repugnance at the conceptual level may be spoken of as one of the most prominent tendencies of hatred: it tends to note the bad and overlook the good qualities of its object. 

Thus an openminded investigation of the object hated is made impossible. Interest is still keen in the hated object but cynical and pessimistic assumptions about it make a

Social Psychology fair investigation impossible. Thus attention is kept on the weak points of the object, neutral acts are interpreted as though they were hostile, hostile acts are magnified and kept in memory while friendly acts are either not noted or, if observed, are soon forgotten or a sinister motive is assigned for them. So it appears that the developing system of hatred gives wonder and curiosity an antisocial turn. hatred make anger within it? over It is recognized generally that anger has a prominent place in the sentiment of hatred. Shand even finds it necessary to insist that anger and hatred are different. He 

What change does says, "Hate is not anger; and anger is not hate, though it may develop into hate.-Hate has, indeed, its bursts of anger; but resists them unless the occasion is suitable". (121:58).

Anger by itself we believe tends only to remove difficulties which are blocking the attainment of desire. But anger coupled with disgust (with perhaps a mingling of elation) makes a dangerous combination. It is within the system of hatred that we find this dangerous combination. Disgust lowers the appreciation of the worth of the object of anger and causes us to believe that its destruction is appropriate; it is easy to believe that any disgusting object is better destroyed. With a low evaluation of the object and backed by the persistent influence of the organization of the sentiment it is easy for anger to develop into vengeance and malice. Thus self-control is made much more difficult and the worst forms of antisocial influence are started. Because of the strength of the united impulses of disgust and anger the most serious antisocial effects are to be anticipated. For these reasons we say that hate gives to anger an antisocial turn.

Within the system of hatred the protective impulse involved in fear undergoes important modification. Shand

recognizes that hate exerts a directive influence over fear. 

He says, "The fears of hate are directed to the end of preventing the escape of the hated object from injury or destruction, and are excited by those events that seem to favor this escape". (121:213). The disgust impulse functioning as the impulse of hatred causes the feared object to be conceived as repulsive as well as dangerous. A human is reduced to the status of a "dog" by disgust and hence is 

What is the influence of hatred on its contained fear? more greatly feared because he is believed to have no more conscience. or idealistic restraint than a dog; note the influence of disgust in such terms as "Dago", "Yap", "Hun", "Nigger", etc. Most terrible practices are attributed to hated races and institutions; the early Christians were thought by the Romans not only to be traitors and infidels but also to be guilty of infanticide and cannibalism. This influence of disgust destroys confidence in original nature and produces a distinctly pessimistic outlook. Further, the disgust impulse in hatred prevents such combinations of fear as develop admiration, awe and reverence. This is because these emotions involve submission and tenderness (in reverence). There can be no tendency to submit to a disgusting object because such cannot be conceived as being superior to self. So it follows that the coupling of fear with disgust in hatred is likely to develop distinctly antisocial tendencies and relationships. 

The rejecting and discrediting tendency of hatred is likely also to assert an undesirable influence over such emotion as joy or elation. Such ex- 

How is joy or elation influenced by hatred? pressions as joy and elation seem social in their very nature; the loving father says, "We are bound to make merry and rejoice, for this brother of yours was dead and has come back

Social Psychology to life, he was lost and has been found". (Luke 15:32).

But it is because of his loving attitude that the father feels the necessity of social rejoicing under such conditions. The elder brother-whose mind is fixed on the disgusting ideas of swine and harlot "was angry and would not go in❞ to the place of rejoicing. The splendid social tendency of elation is apt to be turned into the false "sweetness" of revenge by the accompanying presence of disgust and repugnance in the system of hatred. The spirit of wholesome rivalry and sportmanship is spoiled because there is not sufficient respect for the life and character of the opponent; real sportsmanship must be between moral equals. It is easy to believe that a repulsive opponent is guilty of all forms of deceit and false play; in Jesus' story of the lost boy, the elder brother simply assumes contact of the younger with the harlot. Our rejoicing over the discomfiture of personal enemy is on a social level different from that of our joy in welcoming a returning lost brother. The one is the joy of hatred, the other is the joy of love; the first is antisocial and the second social. So it happens that the sentiment of hatred, colored as it is by the impulses of repulsion and disgust, causes elation and joy to degenerate into antisocial tendencies.

But perhaps we see the most destructive influence of hatred when we note the direction it gives to the impulse of subjection. The tendency to submit is one of the most 

How does hatred in- distinctively social tendencies in human life. Without submission fluence subjection? to the mores, taboo, tradition, law, superior excellence or skill, there would result an anarchy of individualism with no possibility of social life. Submission to social pressure and to that which we admire in individual life is a prime requisite for group activity and indeed for group life. Also submission to our highest ideas

of the superhuman constitutes a source for inspiration and strength for individual life. But in order for submission to function to the good of self and society it must be stimulated by that which is truly worthy and good. The cringing slave, the pessimist, the fatalist and the cynic submit (often under protest) but to the injury of self and of society. In like manner the lazy or careless man is submissive, but to the moral injury of all concerned. Whatever gives a lower conception of self makes for a wrong type of submission; here is a case in which the fraction must be increased by raising the numerator and not by lowering the denominator. (59:161). The very tendency to hate, with all its materialistic conceptions and wrong motives, does in fact tend to produce a lowered self-respect. The assumption of evil motives on the part of others is usually only a reflex of their recognition in self. The maintaining of such motives and attitudes as are condemned by our better nature makes for a lack of confidence in self and hence for low aim in life and crude sensuous ways of seeking life's pleasures; sensuality is a mark of a degenerate conception of self. Further, such a person is likely to resort to conceited boasting in order to bolster up his selfrespect; he believes he cannot do it in actuality and hence expresses it in imagination and words. The man who has little respect for self is likely to be the libertine, the hobo, the loafer, the anarchist, the cynic, the pessimist; a high and worthy conception of self makes most of such attitudes impossible. The man with little self-respect is likely to grab at some immediate gain in life or to become a tyrant in a small way over those weaker than himself; the crime for the sake of notoriety indicates a crude way of attempting to advance self-respect. (A125:38-43). Hatred functions to the breakdown of self-respect by introducing into life motives and tendencies which are despised by the better

Social Psychology nature of the individual. The submission of the self to the toleration of hatred is the beginning of all its wrong forms of submission. Booker T. Washington expressed the idea that the greatest injury possible to character is to tolerate the hatred of another. This kind of self-subjection makes easier all other antisocial kinds of submission. 

II. A Social Distinction Between Types of Sentiments Based upon their Character and Institutional Effects. 

Thus far in this chapter we have said that love or friendship is social in its organization tendencies, in its motives and in general in its social effects; also it has been pointed out that hatred is the direct opposite of love in these regards. We compare these types of sentiments further: (1) as to individual character effects; (2) as to social benefits or injuries. 

1. Individual Character Benefits Resulting from the Nurture of Friendship within the Individual Life. 

Here we consider character benefits: (1) as to the unity of moral character; (2) as to the stability of moral character. its character?

Ribot says, "In order to constitute a character, two conditions are necessary and sufficient; unity and stability". (112:384). The effect of love or friendship upon the unity

How does love in the and stability of character is an imlife effect the unity of portant test of its essential social nature. Let us note first the effect of this type of sentiment upon the unity of moral character. It is recognized commonly that the racial impulses do not of themselves harmonize; without mental (cerebral) control the life is that of insanity and anarchy. It is the organizing power of the sentiment that brings unity into the life of the individual. Further,

the different sentiments must undergo organization. Mc- 

Dougall says, "One essential condition of strong character seems to be the organization of the sentiments in some harmonious system or hierarchy. The most usual or readiest way in which such systematization of the sentiments can be brought about, is the predominance of some one sentiment, that in all circumstances is capable of supplying a dominant motive, that directs all conduct toward the realization of one end to which all other ends are subordinated". (90:265).

We believe that the real "loyalty to loyalty" is involved in the supreme love of the highest good. Shand points out that, "It is a defect of Love that it cannot acquire justice, unless it can first love all men equally or according to their deserts;-". (121:109). But the one who has a supreme love for the highest good does get this harmony and balance; it is only the one who loves the lesser good in a supreme way who spoils the balance of justice. Subordinate to this supreme love of the good, beautiful and true, there will develop friendly attachments to lesser ends to the extent that each has its place in the scheme of value judgments. 

Within each of these orders of sentiments will be found the primary impulses and primary and secondary emotions, thus completing the hierarchical system and insuring the balance of justice. Just as in the cognitive realm there must be an ultimate and various proximate ideals, so in the affective-conative realm of mind there must be a dominant and various subordinant sentiments; the more valuable objects of attachment must take precedence to the less valuable. When this is accomplished by a hierarchy of sentiments we have the achievement of a unified character; if the supreme ideal is moral we have a moral character. This achievement of an automatic and stable character unity seems possible only with proper development and adjustment to each other of various sentiments of friendship. 

Ribot says, "Stability is merely unity continued in time.

Social Psychology 

Cer- If it does not last, this cohesion of the desires is of no value for the determination of character". (112:385). Since this

How do organized is true we should note how a hierfriendly sentiments in- archy of friendly sentiments makes fluence stability of for stability of character. One of character? the prime results of habit is to furnish stability by making reactions automatic; in so far as a reaction is voluntary it is uncertain but in so far as it is automatic it is constant. tainly a supreme love of the highest good (perfection, happiness, social welfare) causes the major premises of thought to become automatic. For instance, one assumes constantly that God is good, man is good, democracy will succeed, cooperation between nations, industries and races can be attained. These assumptions are maintained constantly because they are implications of the supreme ideal. Further, the supreme love of the good tends to stabilize and make automatic the functioning of the major principles of decision. For example, the love of the good carries with it a loyal determination to be honest. This loyalty to the general principle of honesty comes to involve automatic decisions favoring honesty along all lines; honest statements are made, time is employed honestly, borrowed articles are returned promptly and personally, and in all particular ways the principle of honesty is manifest in automatic expressions. Also if there is a real domination of the supreme love of the good, tendencies of desire become stabilized by becoming automatic. We desire, and hence enjoy, that which we truly and deeply love. The one who truly loves the good and beautiful comes to enjoy the expression, in concrete ways, of that which is good and beautiful; this should involve literature, drama, hygiene, recreation, humor, etc. As Shand says, "The qualities that a sentiment acquires for its own needs in becoming fixed, tend to qualify the character as a whole". (121:121). It

is true not only that we become attached to the new type but also that we come to be repelled by its opposite type; love for classical music means disgust for "jazz", love for Shakespeare means disgust for the crude in drama. The control of the sentiment tends to stabilize and to make automatic such desires. Finally habits of thought, feeling and decision, make themselves manifest in habits of conduct. If one's habits of dress, eating, recreation, social contact, etc., are not expressions of his basic thought and decisions, then the life is formal and destitute of character. 

But such casual relations will exist between thought, feeling, decision, and action if a real hierarchy of sentiments has been formed consciously. So we conclude that a closely organized and consciously formed system of right sentiments of love and friendship will make for stable moral character. 

2. Some Social Benefits Resulting from the Develop ment by the Individual of Sentiments of Love and Friendship. 

We consider here such social benefits as (1) elimination of hostile expressions; (2) furtherance of social duties; (3) production of group unity. 

Love asserts a real force toward the removal of attitudes unfavorable to the unity of the social order. Real friendship between nations would eliminate much of the 

What antisocial attitudes does love tend to eliminate? present fear and distrust which exists among them. In industrial conflicts assumptions of wrong motives on either side, which assumptions are so hurtful to peace and cooperation, would be eliminated. Misleading reports of racial differences, which are at present believed and which do so much to promote racial prejudice, would not, if real

Social Psychology friendship existed, be believed; if we were true friends as races we would not think or speak of an "unspeakable Turk" or a "yellow peril". Many of the attitudes of distrust which make for friction in school life would not exist if a condition of friendship existed between pupils and the teachers and administrators of schools. On the whole, true friendship would remove much of suspicion, distrust and assumptions of wrong motives in community, city, state, nation and world.

Also friendship makes possible, and in fact easy, the discharge of social obligations, the meeting of which otherwise would be distinctly unpleasant. Love finds delight in 

How does love make doing hard tasks for the beloved; duty easy? novelists compete with each other in setting hard tasks and in showing how readily the lover masters them. Loves' blindness and confidence makes the doing of these tasks not a hardship but a joy. Can you think of the Apostle Paul enduring the hardships of his ministry and performing the tasks of social service which he accepted without the sustaining sentiment of love? Or consider the very difficult task of adjusting the parties of marriage to each other; to adjust two mature and free individuals, who may have had very little acquaintance previous to their courtship period, so that the intimate relations of marriage can be met successfully, is a very great task indeed; the wonder is not that it often fails but that without special thought and effort it ever succeeds. Of course, we must assume that the love is of a fully developed and sublimated order and directed to an idealized object. Crude attachment for pleasure or profit cannot produce such results. Nor can the love of ease or of self in a narrow sense produce good results. 

But with real friendship binding individuals and groups together all things are possible. Wonderful patience with continued annoyances is a normal result. The perform-

ance of hard labor, the sacrifice of property or of health, the endurance of peculiar privations, for the sake of the beloved occur too frequently to make them events of news. 

But it is this particular type of sentiment, known as love or friendship, and it alone, that makes such results at all possible.

How do friendly attitudes unite groups?

Many of the most difficult social problems are artificial problems. The problem is not a normal or inevitable result of objective situations but is produced by subjective attitudes peculiar to certain groups; we could have an Italian and an Austrian problem as well as a Negro and a Japanese problem were we to develop the same racial attitudes in the first instances. In the nature of the case it is evident that nations can cooperate as well as compete and wage war; with friendly attitudes cooperation is just as natural as competition and warfare with hostile attitudes. Change the way of thinking and feeling between capital and labor, and industrial cooperation between managers and hand workers will seem the only natural and sensible procedure. Emphasize the love of God and the love of man as the great principles of religion, and denominational friction and strife must give way to sympathetic understanding and cooperation. Let races recognize their definite dependence upon and need for each other, and racial adjustment is the normal development. In like manner, friendship holds the fairy wand which will unite families, parties, communities, varied social groups, in real cooperative social endeavor. Friendship will blind the races to their differences and defects and thus give that degree of harmony and peace necessary for a permanent world civilization. But there are those who are ready to say that peace and social cooperation are attainable more readily than friendship. It is quite possible that equitable adjustments should be made as a preliminary step to the be-

Social Psychology ginnings of friendship. Such discussion has to do with means and not ends-we desire friendly cooperation secured either through a preliminary friendly attitude or an introductory attempt at cooperation. But our thinking world needs to realize clearly that there can be no permanent peace and cooperation among social groups without fully developed sentiments of friendship on the part of each party for every other. And further, we should keep in mind that there are ways of encouraging friendships without regard to the exercise of varied forms of social pressure making for unity and cooperation between groups. Let us not assume that administrative coordination of groups can be a substitute for the binding ties of sentiments. To build and preserve our world civilization every promising agency should be used to the full. 

3. Some Injurious Effects on Individual Character Pro duced by Harboring the Sentiment of Hatred. 

Let us turn our thought to certain dangers and injuries produced by the development of sentiments of hatred. We consider under this heading some of the undesirable influences of hatred upon the character of the individual harboring it. ary emotions of hatred effect character unity?

The normal tendency of hatred in the life is to endanger the unity of character by organizing secondary emotions in opposition to the better motives of the self. Envy, How do the second- malice and vengeance seem associated essentially with hatred, and jealousy at its worst is so connected; see chart page 113. think it well to give a group of quotations from scattered authors showing something of the evil effects of these secondary emotions on the life of the individual. Shand concludes that, "Hate,-tends to destroy all virtues, ideals and duties that restrain it from its ends". We

(121:119). Also it organizes secondary emotions suited primarily to the promotion of its ends. Practically all writers agree that the secondary emotions of hatred are undesirable from the social angle. Stratton says that jealousy was a source of monotheism and also that it defends individuality against the threatening tendency of love toward fusion of lover and beloved. (128:212ff). We do not question the assertion that these emotions are derived from right basic attitudes or that some of them may have served some useful purpose in the long reach of human evolution. We are saying rather that organized in the system of hatred and, functioning under present conditions of human life, they are antisocial and especially they are injurious to character unity. This antisocial tendency is evident in the quotations following. Xenophon records that Socrates said that those only "were envious who were annoyed at the good success of their friends". (A110:5). 

Bogardus says, "As a rule jealousy narrows personality, lowers one's social standing and cuts down one's usefulness". He says of revenge that, "It demands at least 'an eye for an eye', and-is likely to overrun its goal and to exact a double portion of atonement". (A18:17).

Groves says, Jealousy is a characteristic, but mean reaction—”. (59:114). Angell says, "Envy is generally applied to our covetousness of the prosperity and possessions of others.-Jealousy we commonly apply to a similar feeling towards persons who are our supposed rivals, whether actually successful or simply feared". (3:357). Murray says, "The malevolent affections of envy and jealousy are effects of failure in a competition; but success often brings with it a kind of malice as well,-the malicious pleasure of feeling our success enhanced by relief against the failure of a beaten rival". (96:459). The main point of all this, for our present consideration, is that these secondary emotions of the sentiment of hatred are

Social Psychology considered by the right-minded self as unworthy of it. This is indicated clearly by the fact that no self-respecting person will acknowledge that he is possessed by such emotions as envy, jealousy or malice; these emotions indicate defeat or fear of defeat on the part of self in its social relations and hence are spurned by self. But since such emotions are associated normally with hatred there is aroused an antagonism within the self which threatens the unity of character. The individual can secure peace and strength only by the organization of character on the basis of such an antisocial impulse as vengeance or by the total condemnation and elimination of all such impulses from the life. 

Such elimination is needed to permit the life to organize about the higher demands of conscience and thus to preserve and promote the unity of character. 

The most disastrous conflicts are apt to develop between these secondary emotions of hatred and the normal tend- How do the secondary emotions of hatred effect character stability? ency of self-assertion. Not only do we refuse to acknowledge these emotions to others but we take means to deceive ourselves as to their real nature. The impulsion of thought which is termed rationalization inclines to utilize cognitive ability to the end of making that seem good which is known to be evil. The individual motivated by revenge rationalizes to the end of making himself believe that his action is justified. Or phantasy is a more desperate attempt to escape from a disagreeable reality which is opposed by our better nature. Phantasy consists in such readjustment of environment in imagination as to permit the individual to seem to escape the actual conditions of life. 

For example assume that I am harboring a malicious motive. This fact is condemned not only by others but by myself. But if I can assume that all people are malicious nd that my particular circumstances justify my reaction,

I can, in a measure, make peace with my conscience. 

Hence through phantasy I so readjust the social order as to bring about this result. Where such justification seems impossible, repression of the condemned impulse is resorted to. This tends to produce the pathological complex and further to subject the character to a strain making for its disruption. Some of the effects of the conflict of self-assertion with the motives of the secondary emotions of hatred are pathological complexes, multiple selves, hesitancy and weakness or recklessness with accompanying repentance and remorse. The life is kept agitated by this struggle against the antisocial tendencies which hatred brings into it. The sum total of the tendencies represented by conscience, idealism and all forms of clear conscious motivation is at marked variance with the type of impulse represented by malice, enmity, jealousy and revenge. The strong tendency of life to harmonize its elements keeps this strife in constant agitation. If the conflict is not conscious it is apt to express itself in restlessness, nervousness, doubt, fear, varied tempers, etc. There is lacking that stability of character which gives poise, assurance and strength of life. 

Speaking from the angle of character stability, we may conclude that the presence of hatred in the life is very undesirable. 

4. Some Social Effects of the Dominance of the Senti ment of Hatred in the Lives of Individuals.

We consider here: (1) some assumed social benefits of hatred; (2) some injurious social effects of hatred. 

(1). Some Assumed Social Benefits Produced by the 

Sentiment of Hatred.

One of the assumed benefits of hatred is that it unifies the group that hates; note the unification of the spirit of

Social Psychology 

Does hates? the nation during the time of war. At the beginning of our 

Civil War it was advocated that we declare war against both England and France in order to unify the United group hatred States. But such unification of unify the group that any group is bound to be superficial, temporary and socially dangerous. It never has right motives back of it and persists only so long as the external danger lasts. After the external pressure has been removed there is more likely to be group disruption than before the pressure is applied. The spirit of hatred persists in the life and, after the conquering of one enemy, turns its motivation against another. A type of superficial and deceptive unification may be built up by group hatred but its total effect is to injure and not to help group unity. 

Some assume that hatred of evil is a natural correlate of love of the good. Eucken says, "As Plato and Aristotle urged long ago, the chief thing in education is that the Is hatred of evil de- young soul should learn with all its sirable? might to love good and to hate evil". (A117:5). We are inclined to conclude that hatred of evil is not a necessary and normal correlate to love of good. Certainly we desire an organization of all the forces of life against environmental tendencies that are evil; to support the good and to oppose the evil are in fact supplementary tendencies. But to oppose the evil is not the same as to hate the evil; hatred weakens the life and prevents the unified opposition of evil. 

Also it is not possible to hate evil in the abstract without hating it in the concrete. Hear again the inspired writer who in his own life knew so well the tendencies of love and hate. "If any one says that he loves God, while he hates his brother man, he is a liar; for he who does not love his brother man whom he has seen, cannot love God whom he has not seen". (I John 4:20). What is true of the senti-

ment of love, is true of the sentiment of hate, for they are alike in this respect. One does not hate evil without hating the devil, kaiser, pope or some other supposed embodiment of evil; hatred by nature is primitive and comes to demand a concrete object. It is practically sure to lead in the case of the average human being to the hatred of his fellow humans; and "Any one who professes to be in the light and yet hates his brother man is still in darkness". (I John 2:9). It is at times urged that hatred of evil is necessary in order to avoid alignment with it. But hatred of evil is much more certain to produce than avoid alignment with it. To avoid alignment with evil, primitive disgust produces a much more effective impulse than does hatred. One cannot promote the impulses of malice or vengeance without keeping close contact with their objects; the evil object must be kept in mind. It may be argued further that the hatred of evil is necessary in order to eliminate evil. But we reply that we do not bring the most effective opposition to bear against evil through the hatred of it. This is true for several reasons. As we have indicated, the individual who hates develops such a degree of internal tension and strife as to weaken the power of his assertion. Again, hatred is likely to cause the individual to misjudge the strength of the opposition; it despises and fails to recognize strength and hence to prepare to meet it. 

Further, the acknowledged bias of the hating individual discredits his leadership over openminded persons whom he might otherwise influence to oppose the hated object. 

Speaking basically, evil probably is not an entity that can be destroyed; it is a possible attribute of every life that has the power to become good. It cannot be overcome by developing further evil-the evil of hatred will not cure the evil of the world. Good is the final condition which must prevail over evil. God is love and love must prevail over both hatred and evil. We must not avoid evil but seek to

Social Psychology meet and overcome it with good. 

(2). Some Injurious Social Effects Resulting from the Development of Hatred on the Part of Individuals. 

It is evident that hatred promotes misunderstanding and distrust between groups and leads to open conflicts among nations. The whole status of world peace is endangered by national hatreds. Kirby Page concludes, "We cannot es- 

Does hatred endanger cape the fact that hatred, fear, huge peace between nations? armaments, desperate economic rivalry, coupled with the desire for revenge and restitution, are unstable foundations for an enduring peace". (A97:50). Hatred involves fear of its object and a spirit of revenge which makes easy the development of the vicious circle of military preparation, war, hatred and distrust, with further tendencies to militarism. 

Suspicion and hatred make for a readiness to believe all kinds of reports of the evil intentions of other nations, which fact in turn augments fear with its military preparation and retaliation. Whether originally hatred is the cause or the effect of war, soon it comes to be a prominent causal element in it.

It is fortunate that nations alone of all social groups wage war; if all forms of group conflicts resulted in group wars then, indeed, would our civilization be doomed. But 

Does hatred produce group antagonism and conflict may wrong relations in all produce very undesirable social effects without producing war. A types of groups? strike in the business world does not wound and kill large numbers of people as does war but its results on society are distinctly antisocial. This is only one example from the economic world of the disruption and disorder resulting from the distrust and antagonism of one group for another. Because of the near contact of capi-

tal and labor, it is less easy to cause wild propaganda to be believed, than is the case between nations and races that are separated more widely. Also the fact that the contending parties are within the confines and under the government of the state, prohibits much of the crude barbarism of war. Racial groups develop hatreds readily but ordinarily do not come to war unless the races also are organized as nations; at present loyalty to the national unit seems to be recognized as the most binding. But nations composed of different races come to war more readily because race hatred is so easy to develop. Religious groups, due to their hatred of other such groups, at time tend to destroy on the part of their members the very spirit which religion is intended to foster; some of the most degraded forms of belligerent action have developed from motives associated with religion. Do the records of war furnish anything more terrible than the doings of the Spanish Inquisition? Draper tells us, "that between 1481 and 1808 it had punished three hundred and forty thousand persons, and of these nearly thirty-two thousand had been burnt;- 

This disgrace of Christianity, and indeed of the human race, had different constitutions in different countries".-At times the confessional was used to secure evidence and "Summoned before the dread tribunal" the suspect "was simply informed that he lay under strong suspicions of heresy. No accuser was named; but the thumb-screw, the stretching-rope, the boot and wedge, or other enginery of torture, soon supplied the defect, and, innocent or guilty, he accused himself". (A35:207-08). If any one thinks such barbarity due to a particular racial tendency he should read at a time when his nerves are steady-the details of what actually happened in New England when witches were tortured to death. Ordinarily group hatreds in religion do not go so far as this but here is an indication of what is a possible result of antagonisms and misunder-

Social Psychology standings even within a religion of as lofty a spirit and motive as Christianity. Also local communities may develop hatreds and conflicts extending through generations to the disruption of the social good. Individual feuds may extend for a lifetime and develop into family feuds which last through generations. That group hatreds constitute a menace to the social order is evident.
