CHAPTER X The Building of  Between Individuals. 

This chapter considers friendships between individuals. 

Such problems as the ones following are considered. Should hatred ever exist between individuals? Is it possible to coordinate the social order through the friendships of individuals? How many friends can one person have at one time? Can any two human beings be friends? What is the real object of an individual friendship? How much alike should friends be? Must moral principles dominate a friendship? Must the friendship be valued as well as the friend?

I. The Desirability that Personal Sentiments Be Social 

Rather Than Antisocial. 

We consider here: (1) the evils of the antisocial type of personal sentiment; (2) the social values involved in friendly sentiments. 

1. The Place of Hatred in the Relationship of 

Individuals. 

We considered in the previous chapter certain of the arguments in favor of the hatred of evil. We concluded that the hatred of evil is bad for the character of the individual who hates, that it is not the best way to secure protection against evil and that it is not an efficient way to overcome evil. However sound the reader may consider these conclusions it is well within the bounds of truth to assert that hatred never should be directed against a person. 267

Social Psychology 

Should hatred ever be directed against a person?

There can be little question but that in this connection the higher law is to love even your enemies. It must be kept in mind that love furthers, rather than inhibits, the tendency to correct the faults of its object. Jesus denounced severely certain religious leaders of his time but at the same time he set his face steadfastly to go to Jerusalem to try to save them; he realized that his going to Jerusalem meant terrible suffering and death for him. McFayden points out 'that Jesus' denunciations ended with a sob". (92:186). He manifested at all times a basic respect and love for the personality of the one whose present attitude he condemned. The lost boy in his degradation and disappointment comes to himself and the self to which he comes is a good self. This hidden good self may not be discovered by any test at present available. The lover must create it in his own imagination on the basis of a very scanty material manifestation on the part of the beloved. But this is nothing new for friendship. The object of love normally is a creation made by the lover. If I love the well favored agreeable person who is kindly disposed toward me certainly I have more sensory material out of which to make the object of the sentiment. But supposing the person be acting in every way at present as an enemy acts, I can, nevertheless, find in him, if I dig deeply enough, sufficient material to act as a sensory basis for my ideal for him. In such case the task is difficult but love is strong and rejoices in hard tasks. 

Moreover imagination is essentially creative and can construct a beautiful object out of very scant material. To hate the basic self of an individual is to support pessimistic attitudes. The one who so hates, not only opposes that which exists, but assumes that all future expressions must be of the same nature as present ones. The latter assumption indicates pessimism. I may be right in oppos-

ing that which now exists but I should assume that possibly the future manifestations of the object will differ from its present manifestations; it is the confidence of love that the future manifestations can be made different. Optimism assumes that the potential self is good and that it can be brought to manifest that goodness by being stimulated by a right sort of environment. For these reasons hatred never should be directed against the basic self of another. 

2. The Desirability that the Social Sentiments of Regard and Friendship Dominate Human Relations. 

We consider here: (1) social values of the sentiment of regard; (2) social values of love or friendship between individuals.

It follows from the conclusions previously reached, that we must depend upon the more positive social sentiments of regard and friendship to build up cooperative social rela- What are the social tions. As we noted in a previous section, regard is a milder form of advantages of regard? social attitude than love or friendship. Our conception of the make-up of regard is indicated on the chart on page 113. The prevailing tendency of regard or respect seems to be a sort of passive enjoyment of the prosperity and welfare of the respected object. The element of subjection in regard because of its prominent place gives a passive turn to the whole attitude. But even passive good will is a safeguard againt the development of such emotions as envy, malice and vengeance. Admiration rules out envy because the superiority of the object is accepted and in fact welcomed and enjoyed. My regard for an object places it in a way among my cherished possessions and hence rules out any feeling of malice I otherwise might have for it. In like manner there can be no occasion for a feeling of vengeance as the attitude on the whole is one of well wishing which is the direct opposite of

Social Psychology the tendency of vengeance. So we think of the presence of the sentiment of regard as constituting a rather positive inhibiting tendency counter to the development of certain of the more antisocial motivations of life. 

What are the social limitations of regard?

While the sentiment of regard safeguards the life against the entrance into it of certain undesirable impulses, in itself it does not further a very positive form of social attachment. Regard is content with the Platonic type of social relationship. There is little insistence upon personal contact or upon the mutual give and take of close personal relationships. The person whom I hold in high regard serves for me as an ideal at least in certain particulars; the interest many times is in certain idealistic qualities rather than in the sum total of the individuality. 

This is due to a lack in the sentiment of the strong driving power of the impulse or impulses of sex and tenderness. 

There is a certain shy and passive appropriation of the object but no positive acceptance of responsibility for its welfare and development. There is likely then to be lacking a vigorous defense and positive stimulation of the character of another person held in respect. Let the character of the object of regard be discredited and we are merely sorry but do little more than refer regard to another object; the ideal is retained by being transferred to another individual. Our interest in the ideal does not carry with it a very positive interest in the welfare of a particular individual. Vigorous protest is likely to be offered only when we realize that in some way we suffer definite loss. 

But the transference of this aspect of our ideal from one particular embodiment to another does not imply any great loss. On this account we are not aroused emotionally and do not tend vigorously to defend the character of the admired person. 

Hence the question is raised as to whether regard or respect in itself is sufficient to hold together the discordant

Will the sentiment of regard achieve social unity? elements of the social order. We should pause for a moment to consider how many discordant elements are at work in society. Williams gives the major portion of his book on social psychology to the consideration of conflict in the economic, political, professional, family, cultural and educational relations. (141:65-382). Ellis says, "The world is cemented with blood and sweat; without pain and fortitude that is to say without struggle and conflict-there would have been no world at all". (39:67). Jefferson says, "There is a vast volume of discontent in this country (the United 

States) with things as they are ". (75:145). Speaking of industrial discontent Mains says, "It is a revolt of the common intelligence against all social and industrial injustice". (91:150). Differences center about race, nation, religion and all types of industrial relations and party alignments. The most intense and bitter animosities develop out of religious differences. Because religion is deemed to be of major importance any minor difference in that realm is judged to be of great importance; the fact that religion deals in a realm where so little is known and so much depends on faith also has a bearing on this emphasis. Because race comparison involves contrasts in dress, food, manners, etc., it is a potent factor in divisive tendencies. The struggle in industry is intense because it is for the necessities of life as well as position and leadership. We have but recently witnessed the terrible destructiveness of the conflicts between nations. It is evident that with such powerful forces working to disrupt and divide there is need of very potent binding powers to unite or we can have no cooperative social order. Such a task is too great for the mild attitude of the sentiment of regard. There is just one form of acquired tendency that is potent enough to meet this demand, and that is the sentiment of love or friendship.

Social Psychology 

All the great writers on love speak confidentially of its power to achieve its ends. It may fail at times to win a particular object but it never fails to register a desirable 

Can love overcome hatred? subjective influence. Nor is love likely to fail entirely in its effort to promote the best interests of its object. Intelligent love for another person usually succeeds in finding a way to further his interests. The very attitude of loyalty and concentrated devotion on the part of the lover calls out the better attitudes on the part of the beloved. In real love there is nothing of flattery and toadying which pauperizes or deceives its object. Although the lover may be ignorant in many ways, yet his very spirit of consecrated devotion is likely to lead him to find a way to further the real interests of the beloved. Even if in a particular case one fails to advance the interests of a friend there can be no failure in the way of improving the character of the one cherishing the friendship. My idealization of the life of another is a positive stimulation giving right direction to my own life. My confidence in the loyalty and virtue of another leads me to establish better premises of thought and decision for myself. The real altruism involved in the tenderness of love broadens the scope of all phases of the life of the lover. This is likely to make for openmindedness in a real sense as the points of view of others are hence attained more readily. One cannot see life from the point of view of even one other person without breaking away in part from narrow views of life. 

The dogmatism of the one who has developed the true attitude of love never can be as narrow as before. There is reason for thinking that love as a force can hold its own with hatred. We have pointed out certain of the weaknesses of hatred. Since the building of a better social order depends upon the victory of love over hate we quote somewhat further as to their relative strength; although it

is plain that in this field we cannot demonstrate a conclusion as in geometry. McFayden says, "If love is the great constructive force of life, and it is only while we are in the path of unselfish service that we are doing work that endures, hatred is the great disintergrating force. Watch a man who is allowing hatred to eat into his heart: how it blinds his judgment, leads him to ascribe to Beelzebub work done in the spirit of Jesus, corrodes his finer feelings, vivifies his baser instincts, turns his energies into false channels, and spreads the corrupting influence of his malevolence into the lives of others;-". (92:177-78). Hocking says, "It is not hard to see some at least of the reasons why enmity tends to undermine its own foundations. In the first place, of all sentiments, it requires the highest degree of inner tension. Two opposing attitudes are combined in it, the effort to bring the hated object forward into clear consciousness, and the effort to expel it from consciousness, because it is hated". (67:54). Thus hatred in the end fails because it is self-contradictory. The real self is opposed to the program of hatred and thus alters and restrains it. The blindness of hatred differs morally from the blindness of love. 

The blindness of love is that of idealism. The blindness of hatred is that of materialism and pessimism. Both work upon an undeveloped world but love constructs a desirable, and hate an undesirable, world. We are convinced that the forces of love are capable of matching the forces of hatred. Both sentiments use many of the same basic forces but hate has essential contradictions within it, while love enjoys the support of the better judgment, the conscience and the self.

But to say that love is equal in power to hatred is not the same as to say that it can bind together the social order in spite of all its divisive and discordant factors. Social friction and discord are produced not merely by hate but

Social Psychology 

Can love produce social cooperation? also by greed, narrow selfishness, partyism, ignorance, superstition, undesirable social traditions and institutions, and various other factors. But as we have indicated on our chart of human impulses life seems to operate on the basis of a check and balance system of impulses. 

Curiosity is offset by fear, lust and desire by repugnance and disgust, elation by subjection, etc. (See chart opposite page 118). Before the genetic development of self-control the only safeguard of the organism against excess consists in some such scheme of check and balance. But such a scheme will work only when there is a comparative equality of force in the opposing elements. If our line of reasoning is at all correct it must follow that we should expect to find social elements in strength proportionate to the antisocial. Especially should this be true in the case of such gregarious animals as man. And in fact we find various types of basic impulse working for social cooperation. Besides the basic impulse of gregariousness, there are passive and active sympathy, lust, rivalry and the impulses connected with the parental tendencies. It is a fact that in some way or another the social sentiment of love or friendship utilizes the potency involved in all these cooperative tendencies; this is done through its essential organization as a sentiment. It is on the basis of such evidence that we are led to conclude that love is able to construct a cooperative social order. Just as hatred magnifies racial and class prejudices and differences, so love minimizes such differences and magnifies basic likenesses and good points. We believe that in any actual show of strength, the social sentiment can hold its own with the antisocial. But it is likely that the social sentiment needs more cultivation than does the antisocial. Love tends toward idealism while hatred toward degeneracy and corruption; it is always easier to lead life along the more primi-

tive than along the more cultured way. The friendly tendencies of life can achieve social cooperation but must be consciously directed to that end with a constant expression of intelligence and determination.

II. Possibilities, Conditions and Principles Involved in the 

Building of Desirable Personal Friendships. 

Our problems here concern: (1) the range of individual friendships; (2) conditions favorable to the development of friendships between individuals; (3) principles governing the development of personal friendships. 

1. The Possible Range of the Individual's Friendships. 

Problems involving the range of friendships include: (1) the number of friendships possible at any one time; (2) the possibility of a friendship between any two normal human beings. 

(1). The Number of Friends Possible for the Individual at any One Time.

Aristotle in speaking of the number of friends possible says, "there is a limited number, which perhaps may be laid down to be the greatest number with whom it would be How friends possible to keep up intimacy; this many can I have at one being thought to be one of the greatest marks of Friendship, and it time? being quite obvious that it is not possible to be intimate with many, in other words to part one's self among many". (4:230). It is quite evident that it is impossible to have all things in common with each one of a large number of friends. Plutarch points out that, "the soul suitable for many friendships must be impressionable, and versatile, and pliant, and changeable. But

Social Psychology friendship requires a steady, constant and unchangeable character, a person that is uniform in his intimacy. And so a constant friend is a thing rare and hard to find". (107:153). Emerson also says that friendship at its best is demanding in the way of worthy character. He says, "For perfect friendship it may be said to require natures so rare and costly, so well tempered each, and so happily adapted that very seldom can its satisfaction be realized". (41:119). In this sense we seem to be speaking of friendship of a very high and exalted order. If we are to use the term in this lofty sense we must think of friendship as being an unusual and aristocratic relationship; history records only a few pairs of friends whose friendship was outstanding in the way of that of Jonathan and David. 

But certainly there are valued attachments and comradeships which are much more extended in range and which partake truly of the nature of friendship; any expression of human life which insists on the perfect must expect to be rare. Democracy implies that varied types of individuals can work together in a friendly way. Mrs. Spencer expresses the idea that even the intimate relations of marriage need not exclude the old friendships. She says, "The most a wife can accomplish by selfish denial to her husband of the right to keep his friends and enjoy the old as well as the new companionship is to make it impossible for him to enjoy his friends in her company". (126:126). The more we consider the friend as being of the nature of the ideal the more we see the possibility of the embodiment of that ideal in the lives of quite a variety of individuals. The 

Platonic conception of friendship does not make the same demand for exclusiveness or for personal intimacy as is assumed in friendship of the Jonathan-David type. The discussion thus far warrants the conclusion that the number of friends possible at any one time depends upon the conception of the nature of the object of friendship and upon

the amount of personal intimacy demanded by each of the friends. Both these points need more extended consideration. personal demand?

Let us for the time being assume with Aristotle that intimacy is the most definite mark of friendship. But still there remains the necessity of inquiring as to the type of 

What intimacy does intimacy demanded. We have infriendship timated already, and later will stress more definitely, the demand of friendship that each of the friends possess moral character. Plutarch says that "having many friends puts people apart and severs and disunites them, by transferring and shifting the tie of friendship too frequently, and does not admit of a mixture and welding of goodwill by the diffusing and compacting of intimacy". (107:149). This assertion seems to be based on the assumption not only that the friends are many but they are of different types morally. It is quite evident that the increase in the number of one's personal friends must decrease the possibility of intimate contact with any one of the number. According to the natural limitations of space and time one cannot associate intimately with a large number of personal friends. This way of thinking is based upon the supposition that the friend is primarily an individual rather than an ideal; we shall give a paragraph to this problem a little later. But if we are willing to decrease the emphasis upon the personal element in friendship and increase it on the ideal element, the conclusion becomes different. Let us suppose we are attached to the individual because of his personal suggestion of an ideal. In such assumption the individual will hold in relation to the ideal a rather secondary place. We call as our friends historical characters whom we did not know personally and who had no chance to know us. Even we feel all the characteristic attachments of friendship toward imaginary characters who

Social Psychology never lived as individuals at all. Now the more particular embodiments of the ideal the better; the ideal becomes the more clarified, the more it is exemplified in the lives of different individuals. Thus instead of a number of individuals who embody the same principles of moral character-drawing us away from the real focus of our attachment, rather they tend in the opposite direction and draw us toward it. For example the more individual examples of honesty we perceive the more we become attracted to this virtue and the more intimate becomes our friendship for this type. We arrive on the basis of such thinking at two conclusions: (1) that numbers of individuals of the same moral type who become my friends do not disperse but the rather concentrate my friendship; (2) the intimacy demanded by friendship is idealistic rather than individualistic. 

Certain special conditions restrict the range of friendship to narrow confines. Friendships of an intersex type offer special problems along this line. We quoted from Mrs. 

Are certain types of Spencer concerning the folly of a friendship exclusive? wife trying to separate her husband from his old friends. But on the next page she says, "The art of holding on to old associations and yet substituting, where substitution is wise or necessary, a new for an established relationship is a great art. 

In the case of the newly married whose friends have been in widely different circles, it is often an impossible one". (126:127). There is little question that marriage in its most civilized form must be monogamus; whether peculiar, primitive conditions call for different forms cannot be discussed here. Promiscuity in sex practices in the very nature of the case spoils friendship within the home. Even where polygamy or polyandry is sanctioned by religion and custom such practice seems to spoil friendship within the home; instances are reported in which the wife insisted on

her husband's taking another wife but such homes are not built upon friendship and the new wife may be wanted as a slave or a companion. Promiscuity is antihygienic and normally is highly repulsive at least to the better type of female. In marriage relations intersex friendships must involve loyalty in matters of sex. But such exclusiveness applies only to the sexual element of this type of friendship. There need be no more restrictions upon the psychic elements of intersex friendships than upon the intrasex type. If sex relations be moralized sufficiently there need be no more demand for exclusiveness; with the fuller development of civilization men and women mix more freely in varied relations. Necessary restrictions come about only when the sex impulse is not sublimated sufficiently. 

Another special case seeming to demand exclusiveness of relationships is the friendship involving a great number of common interests. If I demand of my friend that he share my intellectual interests, belong to my clubs and parties, enjoy my recreations and share my moods, I do indeed appropriate a large share of his life. Or if I wait until I find an individual who conforms in all these ways without change I may expect to find him rarely because individuality makes such conformity unlikely. Also the persistence of such conformity implies parallel or converging lines of growth. It is evident that if two individuals assume that their friendships means conformity in all these ways that they must expect to associate with each other a great deal and thus to have little opportunity for other lines of friendship. But such an alignment and blending of interests sacrifice much that a friendship ought to promote. A friend should be a real "other self" and not a mere echo of self.

He should supply the weak points of my character and I the weak points of his so that we two make a more perfect whole as regards character and life than is the case with either one of us. It is very much to

Social Psychology the interest of my character, that in addition to the essen-. tial moral likenesses of self and friend, there be as wide a range as possible of differences between us. This is brought about only when each of us asserts his individuality and has, apart from the other, a large number of different types of friends. We conclude that there are few cases which demand that friendship at its best be an exclusive relationship, most such cases are supposed and not real. (2). The Possibility of a Friendship Between any Two 

Normal Human Beings. be friends?

Another problem under the range of friendship concerns the possibility of my being a friend to and having as a friend any other normal human being. The Great Teacher 

Can two human beings has enjoined us to love our enemies, evidently with the assumption that such is possible. The greatest of the prophets also have conceived the possibility of friendships existing between peoples cherishing age old animosities. Isaiah says, "In that day shall there be a highway out of Egypt to Assyria, and the Assyrians shall come into Egypt, and the Egyptians into Assyria; and the Egyptians shall worship with the Assyrians. In that day shall Israel be the third with Egypt and Assyria, a blessing in the midst of the earth; for that Jehovah of hosts hath blessed them, saying, Blessed be Egypt my people, and Assyria the work of my hands, and Israel mine inheritance". (Is. 19:23-25).

It may seem to some that such expressions are only visionary and do not represent actualities in the way of human relations. But we find actual cases in which individuals develop a distinctly changed attitude. Bogardus gives the report of the changed attitude of a young woman who had developed hatred for foreigners. This young woman says, "Before I left college I was elected president of the Cosmopolitan Club, and since leaving I have helped to organ-

ize an inter-racial committee for the discussion of racial problems in our town on the basis of good will and cooperative activity". (A18:236). Cases of individuals who have made some attempt at friendship without succeeding are of course numerous, as our increasing divorce statistics indicate; it certainly cannot be asserted that the development of friendship is easy or that every honest attempt will succeed. There need be no assumption that the individual by the assertion of self comes to love that which is unlovely; let us assume for the time that it is impossible for one to love that which he believes to be thoroughly unlovely. We concede further that it is undesirable to love the unlovely because the lover becomes like the beloved and hence would become unlovely. Rather the assumption is that there are desirable and attractive traits hidden in the life of every normal human being. Man made in the image of God has not lost all the characteristics of his creator. These atttractive characteristics are merely hidden behind unattractive habits and attitudes. The friend who has confidence that they are there, usually has the power to cause them to blossom forth. 

At times there is the need of patience and perserverance in the quest but rarely is the quest hopeless. It is a rare individual who makes no response in kind to the smile of a friend; of course, he is not at all sure to respond to the smile of a high-powered salesman. It is the assumption of

Christianity that every prodigal son can be brought to himself and the self to which he comes will be a good self; this does not assume that any particular social influence has the power to make such a social reformation. Nor can we assume that there will develop a deep feeling of friendship between every two individuals whose characters possess moral worth. This person as an individual must appeal to my individual feeling nature before I can develop an enthusiastic feeling of friendship for him. I can recognize his good points, I can cooperate with him and wish him

Social Psychology well, but he, as an individual, must appeal to me as an individual if there develops friendship of a personal sort. 

So we conclude: (1) there can be mutual understanding and cooperation developed between any two normal human beings; (2) they can appreciate and encourage each other to develop the better qualities of their individuality; (3) it is not certain that they can become close personal friends unless in their moral development they make an individual appeal to each other. 

2. Conditions Favorable to the Development of the Finest Types of Individual Friendships. 

Under this heading we consider: (1) the nature of the object of an individual friendship; (2) desirable conditions of likeness and difference; (3) the necessity of mutual attachment; (4) the necessity that individuals be free to choose their friends. 

(1). The Development and Maintenance of an Idealized 

Object for the Friendship.

We have suggested already the problem involved in the determination of the object of a personal friendship. Many of the writers of novels, dramas and songs have taken it Is a friend an indi- for granted that the object of love vidual or an ideal? is an individual; the reader can illustrate this abundantly from his own memory. Starch speaks of one phase of this problem when he says, "Probably more fallacious psychology of sex has been spread abroad by novelists and journalists than has been disseminated on any psychological question of popular interest". (B126:63). This fallacious conception concerns frequently the nature of the object of friendship. We find every one of the classical writers on friendship insisting that morality must take precedence to in-

dividuality. Thoreau quotes Confucius as saying, "To contract ties of Friendship with any one is to contract 

Friendship with his virtue. There ought not to be any other motive in Friendship". (131:36). Other writers both ancient and modern stress the primacy of morality to individuality. Zeno says, "that among the wicked there is no such thing as friendship, and that no wicked man can have a friend". (A110:107). It appears from this that

Zeno thought of the morality, rather than the individuality, as the real object of the friendship. Hence the real object of attachment is some aspect of goodness, beauty or truth, as it finds concrete embodiment in an individual. 

But, of course, there must be something about the individual to suggest the potential embodiment of the virtue. We cling to the individual because we believe that he at least has the future possibility of embodying some virtue. As 

Plato suggests, the individual is two steps removed from the ideal, but there must be about him such qualities as will establish a concrete basis for the construction of an abstract idea of a virtue. So we conclude: (1) basically the ideal is the object of an individual friendship; (2) but the ideal to some extent must be embodied in the life of the individual.

Perhaps it is worth while to inquire somewhat further as to the relation of the individual friend to the ideal object of friendship. In most actual cases of friendship there 

Must the individual is loyalty maintained to an indifriend portray the vidual. In many instances the idealistic? friend is willing to make certain concessions even along moral lines for the sake of maintaining the friendship with the individual. Does this mean that he values the person in distinction to the ideal? It is more likely to mean, in so far as he makes any conscious interpretation of the feeling element of the friendship, that he thinks the individual has

Social Psychology as not yet completely embodied his idealistic possibilities. 

Frequently we say of the young person that we must give him time to catch up with his ideals. Many times the lover does not realize consciously what it is about the beloved that is attractive to him; this is not peculiar, many times we do not know what it is in appearance or tone of voice which causes us to recognize our friends. But in any case we must assume that there is about the individual a certain embodiment of virtue, or at least that the friend so believes. There is a suggestion of beauty in his voice, carriage or facial expression. There is a manifestation of honor or truthfulness in the way he deals with money, industry or time. Perhaps he displays a certain element of courage in connection with sickness, defeat, physical or moral dangers. These cues of virtue may be very meager or very primitive in their nature but we believe they must exist. The conscious attraction may be to the individual, as such, but the reason for such attraction probably is that the individual is supposed to embody a virtue. It seems almost impossible to think of friendship or love apart from the idea of goodness or beauty. It goes practically without saying that if the individual must portray the idealistic in order to initiate the friendship, he must continue to portray, and in fact must develop along idealistic lines, in order to maintain the friendship. 

(2). The Desirable Conditions of Likeness and Differ ence on the Part of Those Desiring to Become 

Personal Friends. 

There must be a certain degree of similarity between those who are attractive to each other. Griggs says, "To make a high friendship possible there must be a certain identity of personality, a common range of taste, interest and activity, to draw the two lives together in mutual un-

In what ways should friends be similar? derstanding". (58:21). Emerson says, "No two men but being left alone with each other enter into simpler relations. Yet it is affinity that determines which two shall converse. Unrelated men give little joy to each other; will never suspect the latent powers of each". (41:120). The following fine passage from Aristotle is worth the careful thought of anyone. "That then is perfect 

Friendship which subsists between those who are good and whose similarity consists in their goodness: for these men wish one another's good in similar ways; in so far as they are good (and good they are in themselves); and those are specially friends who wish good to their friends for their sakes, because they feel thus toward them on their own account and not as a mere matter of result; so the friendship between these men continues to subsist so long as they are good; and goodness, we know, has in it a principle of permanence". (4:186-87). So we conclude that friends should have similar idealistic aims and similar moral standards. Divergence between friends in the way of worthiness of either ends, or means for their attainment, is bound sooner or later to produce tendencies disruptive of their friendship. Besides such basic essentials, it is very helpful to friendship if both parties have certain basic interests in common along lines of industry, play or art. Also a variety of common experiences involving sorrow, joy, anxiety, hope, struggle, etc., is distinctly helpful. Further, friendship may be promoted by the recognition of a common task such as the rearing of a child, a business undertaking, planning an invention, creating a book, work of art, etc. Such common endeavor perhaps is not essential to friendship but is very helpful towards making it stable. 

Many questions come up as to the desirability of the friends having their religion, politics, recreations, lines of education, in common. Do such matters have 

Do such matters have a vital relation

Social Psychology to friendships? In certain cases they do and in others they do not. Even loyalty to political parties may disrupt a friendship if the friends, or one of them, think or thinks of such loyalty as having supreme value. So we conclude that there is need for unity in what the friends believe to be of supreme worth in life; just what items this involves depends on the value judgments of the individual. 

But it is evident that friendship is based upon differences as well as upon likenesses. Griggs says, "The third law of friendship is thus the law of unlikeness, or complementary relationships of personality". (58:23). Platonic Aristo- 

How should friends differ? phanes raises the question as to whether two friends desire to be fused and made into one and concludes that this is the meaning of their desire. He says further, "And the reason is that human nature was originally one and we were a whole, and the desire and pursuit of the whole is called love". (B106:318). This certainly is a very interesting suggestion-one life is not complete in itself but seeks a friend or "other self" to cause it to become complete. But if this is a true suggestion the friend must be different from self or the two will not fit together to make a complete whole; the left hand would not fit into the right if it were exactly like the right. If my friend supplements my weakness with his strength, the possession of him as a friend rounds out and completes my personality. I tend to admire that in another which constitutes a need of my own nature. Hence I seek as a friend one who supplements my own life. For this reason the strongly feminine is attractive to the strongly masculine and vice versa. The rigor and enthusiasm of youth and the poise and dignity of age are mutually admired. In like manner there is likely to be an attachment between the practical and theoretical, the introvert and the extrovert, the intellectual and emotional and other such supplementary pairs.

Desirable differences need not conflict in any way with desirable likenesses. Love seeks both the like and the unlike; the like in basic essentials, the unlike in desirable supplementary traits. A free, strong, individual feminine character, may contrast greatly with a masculine character to which the same adjectives apply. Each may be strong and independent in a different way. Each may have definite moral standards of his own and still each may supplement the other. It is such a situation of likeness and difference that gives greatest promise for the development of a friendship. 

(3). The Reciprocal Attachment of the Individuals In volved in the Personal Friendship.

Individual friendship at its best should be a positive reciprocal relationship. Anna Garlin Spencer says, "Among men the submergence of one personality in another, so that Why should friends although there are two people, be attached mutually? there is but one mind and one purpose, may be friendship, but it is not the equal comradeship which the healthy-minded seek". (126:125). Certainly it was to the friend who was also a distinct individual, to whom Socrates referred when he placed the following high evaluation on a friend: "Yes, by the Dog of Egypt, I should greatly prefer a real friend to all the gold of Darius, or even to Darius himself: I am such a lover of friends as that". (C106:62). A real friend can be no other than an individual who is loved for the worth of his personality and who loves the idealized personality of his friend in return. Friendships termed "mashes" or "crushes" or based upon desire for "social uplift", "under-current influence", economic gain, recreational diversion or any form of passing pleasure are doomed to fail to give the lasting values of real friendship. It

Social Psychology seems at times that a friendship is carried on largely by the initiative of one of its members; in the intersex friendship coyness may give the impression of passivity where such is not in actuality the case. Love is slow to give up its quest and may persist for a long time without much appearance of return in kind. Some individuals by their very temper respond to friendship rather than initiate it; there is a type of responsiveness which may be taken as activity rather than passivity. After taking such points into consideration it still remains true that for a fully developed and desirable friendship there should be active love on the part of both parties. One friend may become attached much sooner than the other but before the friendship is stable the attraction must be mutual. (4). The Freedom of the Individuals Involved in the 

Friendship from External Domination and Con straint.

We are insisting that the motive of love or friendship must be the promotion of the welfare, or what is believed The to be the welfare, of the object of the sentiment. 

In what sense should friendship be free? alignment formed for the sake of pleasure or profit is of a very primitive type; possibly it may develop in to a desirable type of friendship but to do so the motive must become more altruistic. To insure a right motivation in friendship the individuals involved in it must be free from external domination and constraint. There are various forms of constraint some of which may be brought to bear without the fact being detected very defiinitely. In the isolated community or small fraternal group there may develop a social pressure upon individuals to become friends. Friendships among earlier members of the families also may exert such influence. It is evident that

those who are rich or in official positions which enable them to grant favors may be sought as friends without the right motive back of the alignment. On the other hand the poverty or some other unfortunate circumstance about the life of an individual may tend to lessen his self-respect and thus cause him to welcome friendly alignments which in other circumstances he would not choose of his own free will. The pressure brought upon young women to enter marriage certainly is not as severe as formerly was the case; under primitive tribal conditions women could not live a life detached from the care of husband or father. But under many conditions considerable pressure still is brought to bear. Parental expectancy or tradition may favor marriage at a certain age. Families may bring pressure to bear for social or economic reasons. Inequality of the sexes may be brought about by such conditions as war and thus bring pressure to make an undesirable alignment. All such influences must be considered undesirable and avoided as far as possible. The range of desirable friends should be as large as possible, conditions of approach and relationships as free from external pressure as social safety permits, and stage by stage the development of the friendship as normal as possible. There need be no question of the right of society to restrict procreation and even the contraction of marriage when it seems obvious that the resultant conditions will be antisocial. But the burden of evidence should be upon the one who advocates the social restrictions rather than the individual freedom. We shall speak a little more definitely of this matter in connection with the development of the home. 

3. Principles Governing the Development of a Subli mated Type of Personal Friendship. 

The principles advocated here are: (1) a supreme evalua-

Social Psychology ion of the moral; (2) a measure of confidence in intuitive judgment; (3) a high value placed on friendship itself; (4) a basic idealistic philosophy. 

(1). The Valuation of the Moral or Idealistic Above the Personal or Individualistic. 

The insistence of both ancient and modern writers is that the individual cannot be a thoroughly bad man and at the same time be a friend. Cicero says, "I lay it down as a fundamental 

Must Friendship be based on morality? axiom that 'true 

Friendship can only subsist between those who are animated by the strictest principles of honor and virtue'". (A27:176).

Cicero seems justified in asserting that this represents the thought of all the great writers who preceded him. Our study of writings on friendship between Cicero's time and our own gives every warrant for maintaining this fundamental axiom. There may for a time be a seeming attractiveness about the nonmoral carefree individual. The company of such an individual aids one to relax and to enjoy the passing moment. There seems about him a breadth of openmindedness which impresses one as a wholesome contrast to the narrow dogmatism at times set forth in the name of morality. But such an impression is of the nature of an illusion. It promises much more than in reality it can fulfill. It is toward the beginning of life rather than toward its close that one is led astray by such impressions of value. There is an essential attractiveness about youth that is not at once destroyed by immoral tendencies. It may even appeal to our imagination to see an individual disregard the total wisdom of the race; certain great prophets and reformers have stood alone against a solid public opinion. But soon we come to realize that an immoral act is an unfriendly act. For the sake of our ideal,

our friendship, and our friend we must insist that he be moral. I cannot become a partner in the demoralization of the character of a friend. If he insists on going the antimoral way of life, as a friend, I must warn him that I cannot go with him along that way. The outcome of going the lower way of life is not only degradation but it is disappointment. It is only a matter of time until the one disloyal to the mores becomes disloyal to his friends. Honor among thieves is a misleading suggestion. Thieves steal from thieves as readily as from others; only external pressure holds them together for a time. Morality is an essential basis and prerequisite to friendship. The moral and idealistic must prevail if in conflict with the merely personal or temporary. (2). A Measure of Confidence in Intuitive as Well as 

Logical or Intellectual Judgment. 

The development of a worthy type of lasting personal friendship demands the assertion of all the powers of the individual. Certainly this must include the full measure of wisdom and balanced logical 

Why place some trust in intuitive judgment? judgment that the individual possesses. One does well to think carefully and to utilize all available evidence before accepting the responsibility of a close personal friendship; such is a responsibility not to be estimated lightly. But all evidence never is completely available, as much of the promise of friendship is a promise of what is yet to be. The insistence under all conditions upon logical and deliberate judgments is likely to be hurtful to the development and maintenance of friendship. A friendship is a great adventure of faith and one cannot expect to have a detailed chart of all the way that lies ahead. Some freedom must be given to the creative imagination and some trust to the

Social Psychology intuitive feeling. We spoke in Chapter II concerning the trustworthiness of intuitive judgments. Certainly they are much more trustworthy on the part of a balanced character whose impulses have not been permitted to run riot in the life. But in any case there must be some trust in the future, some willingness to adventure and some reliance upon the guidance of deep-seated intuitive promptings. 

(3). A High Evaluation Placed Upon the Sentiment of 

Friendship as Well as Upon the Particular Friend. as well as friends?

There is a real danger that the individual may realize the worth of his friend without realizing the value of the friendship itself. But a high valuation of friendship is funda- 

Why value friendship mental. Unless I recognize something of the values to be secured and given through friendship I am not at all likely to develop and promote it as I should. 

We need to realize this broadly. Rulers must recognize that if their government is to be secure their subjects must love as well as fear them. Capitalists and leaders of labor unions must recognize that it makes a tremendous difference whether their opponents and the general public hold them in high respect and think of them in a friendly way. 

Nations and races must realize that their basic security rests upon the friendly relations which they maintain with groups similar to themselves. Partners in the making of a home must realize that the success is their love for each other; this is true only to a somewhat less extent of business partners or any other pair of individuals holding close relations to each other. 

Teachers and pupils must realize that the success of education depends upon their friendly cooperation. In short there must be a general recognition of the value of friendship as such. The fact that certain friends are of more element most basic to their

value than others must in no sense get us to thinking that the value of friendship depends solely upon its object. We must recognize that the coordination and stability of the social order depends upon the binding ties of love and friendship. 

(4). The Necessity of a Measure of Idealistic 

Philosophy.

Lastly we mention the need that the individual friend have sufficient idealistic philosophy to believe that the cosmic order is such as to permit the individual to place a high 

What idealistic phil. valuation on friendship. Successful osophy does friend. friendship demands basic philosophical attitudes favorable to it. ship require?

The success of a friendship depends upon so many intangible factors that to insist upon proof of every assertion and an objective guarantee that every obligation will be fulfilled is to destroy friendship. There must be trust that the cosmic order is such as to make reliance upon friendship reasonable. Such reliance may be either theoretical and practical or merely practical. A man may call himself an athiest and in his everyday expression of life act as a devout coworker of an imminent God. Or he may profess to be a cold and hard militarist and conduct himself in relation to his family as one who trusts the spiritual forces of life. But if he is both a theoretical and practical pessimist and cynic he does not have the background for the development of friendship. 

Friendship involves the entire life and must assume a supporting philosophy.
