CHAPTER XI The Development of Social Sentiments in Connection with Small Groups: Special Attention is Given to the Home. 

In this chapter we consider the relation of the sentiment to the small social group. 

Please think these questions over before reading the chapter. What small groups are of special importance to society? What are the significant characteristics of a small social group? What type of sentiment creates and maintains such groups? Are there outstanding individuals in such groups whose friendship for each other has special significance for the group relations? 

How encourage such forms of friendship? How build the entire group together in friendship?

I. The Essentials of the Small Social Group. 

We consider: (1) the characterization of the small group; (2) a typical example of such group; (3) the extent of such social groups. 

1. The Characteristics of the Small Social Group. 

How distinguish a small social group?

We continue the study of the social applications of the sentiment by thinking of its relations to the small social group. We are thinking of a social group as small when it is of such size as to make individual friendships among all its members possible. They can come to know each other readily by name and understand something of the individual background of each. Also the group is of such size that its function can be understood readily by all its members; its function may extend into the 297

Social Psychology broader social order but is to a sufficient extent local, as to be comprehended by the most concrete minded of the group.

On this account it is easier to develop a loyalty to the group as such; it is quite evident that the concrete sentiment is much easier to develop than the abstract. 

2. The Home as a Typical Example of the Small Social Group. 

It is an interesting experiment in group thinking to try to work out a definition of a home. Some think of it as a place of refuge and retirement from the heavy labors of 

What is a home? life. Some think of it as the institution that raises and cares for little children. Some emphasize the idea of comradeship and companionship and think of the home essentially as a place for close individual friendships. Some incline to stress morality and think of the home as a leaven for the whole social order. We must remember that homes are constituted very differently. Not all of them involve marriage and children, although perhaps it is of this group that we think most frequently. Not all homes have even relatives living together and in many instances one person only resides permanently in the home. 

But in the latter case ordinarily it is open to a group of individuals who in a sense are members of it. For our purposes we are thinking of the home as a typical example of the small group relationship. The discussion of the chapter is made more concrete by the use of an example. 

The home is favored for such an example because: (1) it takes care of the immortality of the race; (2) it provides the very important social heredity for each generation; (3) it largely determines the set of the individual's race and group prejudices and party alignments; (4) it is a potent factor in determining the total moral tone of society. This

is not an exhaustive statement of its values but sufficient to justify the use of the home as one of the most important cases of the small group relationship. 

3. The Extent of Small Group Relationships within the Social Order. 

But we would have the reader think of the principles discussed in the chapter as having application to a considerable number of small social groups. We attempt to 

What small social groups exist? name only a sufficient number to stimulate thought along this line. 

Fraternal groups are somewhat akin to those of the home; these may be thought of as including local or national fraternities or sororities in colleges or universities and also the fraternal groups not connected with educational institutions. The small social club takes so many forms that we make no attempt to enumerate them. Groupings for religious purposes may be small as well as large. We may think of special religious ordersthose seeking to live on a higher level of religious life-or of the officers and leaders of religious groups. The small social group in the business world takes various forms. It may be a small group of partners, directors of a business concern or a superintendent and his immediate associates conducting a business operation. In the educational and professional world groupings are many and varied. A board of directors for a school or a faculty constitute important social groups of rather limited numbers; in the larger schools the faculties are likely to be broken up into departmental groups. Members of a dramatic, forensic or athletic team constitute small social groups. Various professional organizations offer small as well as large groups. 

An editor of a paper or magazine has his small group of associates. In fact the social order in all phases of its ex-

Social Psychology pression has these more or less permanent small social groups.

II. The Utility of the Main Types of Sentiments Towards Producing and Maintaining the Stability and Pro gress of the Small Social Group. 

Here we consider the three types of sentiments and their functions in regard to developing and maintaining the stability of small social groups. 

1. The Possible Use of Hatred in Developing and Main taining the Stability of the Small Social Group. 

Hatred exerts its force towards the maintenance of a group only as it constrains the members of the group to hold together against forces which tend to disrupt it. Mc- 

Does hatred ever tend Fayden says that, "The Chistian to preserve a small home is in danger from various sides today". (93:213). There group? is just the possibility that where such a group is in danger from external pressure that animosity toward another group may preserve their unity. 

Not only can a loyalty be developed for a group but an extreme antagonism can be developed against the breaking up of a group. Miss Colcord makes mention of "A woman who had left her home town and found clerical work in a strange city, in order not to be near her syphilitic husband from whom she had determined to separate,-". This woman is quoted as saying, "When you have been married to a man, you can't get over feeling that your place is with him". (28:14). Such natural aversion to severing the marriage relationship is increased by religious emphasis on the wrongness of divorce and further by the publicity given to divorce proceedings by the open court trial and by the public press. Such aversion to the breaking up of the

small group may come to approximate hatred and may serve as a force tending to hold the group to the external semblance of unity. This is more likely to take the form of hatred if it is an individual or group that is exerting the pressure towards disruption. For instance the government may consider it necessary to disband certain corporations or appropriate the property of certain religious organizations. The opposition to this external encroachment may develop something of the semblance of temporary unity within the group.

But to prevent the breaking apart of the group is in no sense the same as to preserve the friendly sentiments uniting it. Perhaps we are not to assume that every effort 

Can hatred preserve should be made to preserve group friendship within the unity. Dewey and Tufts seem to imply that in some instances the group? disruption of the home, rather than its continuance, may further its real interests. They point out that at times the home becomes, "a seeming fatal obstacle to the very purpose which it was destined to promote; unfaithfulness, cruelty, habitual intoxication, and other less coarse, but equally effective modes of behavior may be destructive of the common life and morally injurious to the children". (A34:604). Mrs. Spencer says, "The statistics of illegitimacy, of commercialized prostitution, of venereal disease, of infant mortality, of early death, or life-ling invalidism of wives and mothers, of marital unhappiness and parental neglect which are found in honest investigation in states and nations in which no divorce is allowed, do not lead to the belief that legal permanence of the marriage bond secures socially helpful family life". (126:41). Fear of public opinion against the breaking of the marriage tie cannot preserve within the home those relationships which give it the reputation of promoting life to its best. Fear, disgust, repulsion and

Social Psychology antipathy are not tendencies likely to promote within the homes such qualities as are typical of the best homes. We find here, as elsewhere, hatred is not a promoter of desirable social life. And what is true in a typical way of the home is true of any kindred group. Our dependence for the development of desirable unity must be upon the social type of sentiment. 

2. The Utility of the Sentiment of Respect or Regard Towards Preserving the Stability of the Small Social Group. 

Miss Colcord mentions the case of Mrs. Finnegan who "is found on the morning when her case against Finnegan is to come up in the domestic relations court, busily wash- 

Does regard preserve ing and ironing his other shirt in the values of the small order that he may make a proper group? appearance and not disgrace the family before the judge". (28:15).

It is evident in this case that respect demands that other members of the family-even though divorce is pendingalso give the appearance of respectibility. Carpenter says that the further improvement of conditions of married life depends among other things upon "the furtherance of the freedom and self-dependence of women". (26:106). If friendship is to be as stable and mutual as it should it is plain that it must exist between two free and self-respecting persons. This condition of freedom is very different from that which existed in the Roman family. Mrs. Bosanquet notes that under the patriarchal family system, "A woman when she married passed out of her original family into that of her husband and became subject to the power ruling therein;-". (19:12). This power of the father and husband was absolute and despotic. It is quite possible that a family be held together by the absolute rule of the

husband; this is true for much the same reason that a state can be governed by an absolute monarch. But such a situation can persist only up to a certain stage of human development. In the civilized order of things the absolute monarch in the state must give way to the rule of a larger number of citizens and the autocrat in the home to the cooperative control of the different members of the household. This process is furthered in any group by development of respect for personality. The development of respect for others leads to the division of authority of management because of the assumption that others are fitted for it. The development of self-respect makes for a wholesome demand for right and fair treatment and this in turn tends to lay a more stable foundation for group unity. Selfrespect always furthers the power of self-control and promotes the standards of morality which are basic to the continued life of the group. So we may think of the sentiment of regard or respect as operating in the direction, not only of preserving the unity, but also of preserving the values of small relations. 

3. The Tendency of Friendship or Love to Produce Satisfactory Relations within the Small Social Group. 

The close and continued intimacy of the home and its demands for intimate personal adjustments imposes a tremendous strain upon its unity. This is true especially in 

What basic place must the more democratic home which is love hold in the small based upon agreement among its members to cooperate. Those who group? are distressed about the frequent breaking up of the home in our day should take into consideration the fact that the democratic home attempts a much harder task than the autocratic one; but when success is achieved a much finer and higher result follows. Emer-

Social Psychology son says, "The higher the style we demand of friendship, of course the less easy to establish it with flesh and blood. 

We walk alone in the world. Friends such as we desire are dreams and fables". (41:123). Or we say, so long as friends are "dreams and fables" the task of friendship is not so demanding and difficult. It is easy for the novelist to assert that these devoted lovers lived happily ever afterwards. But as a matter of fact the real test of their friendship begins with the beginning of their close and varied relationships. Let one contemplate the variety and intimacy of adjustments called for in the marriage relationship and he will realize at once that the task of such adjustment is exceedingly difficult. In as far as other small groups approach this demand for constant and intimate adjustments, in so far is the maintenance of their unity difficult. The man who succeeds in maintaining his smile and pleasant tone of voice all day in his office may find it much more difficult during the hours of expected relaxation in the home; it is not infrequent that the putting on of our old clothes means the putting on of our primitive and crude manners. There is no tendency fitted to solve this difficult adjustment so well as the positive social sentiment of love. 

The positive sentiment of love or friendship alone has sufficient power to withstand the constant jolts of close contact, to maintain an optimistic blindness to faults, and to call forth that type of responsiveness from associates which makes for unified and happy relations. We shall give attention mainly to the discussion of the building up of this type of sentiment. 

III. The Positive Sentiment of Friendship Binding the 

Main Parties of the Small Social Group to Each Other.

We consider here: (1) the importance of developing and

maintaining satisfactory relations between the main parties to the group; (2) how to secure optimum conditions for developing such friendships; (3) the conditions under which the friendship should be matured; (4) the preserving of the matured friendship from disintegration. 

1. The Importance of Securing a Proper Relationship Between the Main Parties within the Small Group. 

The relationship of certain individuals within the small group often constitutes a key to the whole unity of the group. The development and maintenance of a real friendship between husband and wife is the main problem of friendly relations within the home. Let this relationship be what is should and we need to worry little about a 

Are certain friend. proper relationship between parents and children and ships within the group more significant? among the children of the family. What is true of the relation of the parents to each other may be true of certain of the officials of a business organization or outstanding members of a board or cabinet; possibly in every small group there are certain individuals the significance of whose relations to each other approximates that of parent to parent to some extent. The more outstanding or prominent members of the groupperhaps due to age, intelligence, office, wealth, beauty, skill, etc.-maintain the tone of the group life and also set an example for the imitation of the others. Even in large groups such as nations it has been deemed important to have kinship and close friendship relationships between rulers; note the tendency of royal families to intermarry. 

Such influence is much more outstanding in the small than in the large group. Two business partners by developing a fine relationship between themselves can establish a friendly tone within their whole organization. It is in con-

Social Psychology sideration of this type of effect that we give special attention to the friendly relations of the outstanding members of a group. 

2. The Desirability that the Conditions for Forming 

Friendships Between these Key Individuals Be 

Optimal.

We have said that the stability of small groups is determined for the most part by the friendship which holds their members together. Also that special concern must 

What conditions favor be given to the friendship estabsuch friendships? lished between the key individuals of the group. 

Since such friendship is a personal one the principles for developing personal friendship apply here. The attachment should be mutual and assertive on the part of each member involved. Once let one of the parties to friendship become a receiver and not a giver and the friendship has started on the path of degeneracy. Some marks of such degeneracy are that the friendship is looked to as a source of financial income, of social position, of immediate enjoyment, or some such material benefit. If the friendship be intersex the principle which Ellen Key attributes to George Sand is important: soul must not betray sense nor sense betray soul. (A78:23). 

There must be such a sex adjustment in marriage as to involve neither sensuality or frigidity. Coyness should be considered a positive factor making for a condition of intersex love in which the senses do not betray the soul. Each friend must have basic confidence in the other confidence in his motives and in his possibility for idealistic growth. 

It is important in these friendships that there be agreement as to the need of supreme loyalty to the great moral and spiritual principles of life. How far this need extend as to agreement in thinking and party alignment depends

entirely upon how much emphasis either of the friends places upon a particular type of thought or party alignment. 

No disagreement is insignificant if it is considered major by one of the parties; one home made an issue of whether the wife should fry the cakes while husband ate or whether she should fry them before and both eat together. Each friend must realize the significance of their friendship and be willing to make sacrifices to promote it. Close friendship is a grave responsibility and difficult task. If much depends upon the success of this friendship the parties to it need consider well the possibility of the adjustment of their lives to each other; it must be recognized that distinct differences of race, customary financial status, educational standards, lines of enjoyment, temperament, all make for a much more difficult task of adjustment. There is special need for an open-minded facing of differences, a willingness to adjust through the sacrifice of nonessentials, and especially a trust in the future and an optimistic assumption that adjustments can and will be made. 

3. The Desirability that the Friendship be Well Matured 

Before External Commitment to Group Relations. 

Judge Lindsey lays much emphasis upon the necessity of conditions of freedom for a proper development of a friendly intersex adjustment. This as a principle has a wider application to friendly adjustments than to that type of the intersex friendship that involves marriage. During 

Why is a free period the free period preceding the social favorable to friendly bonds of the group the conditions adjustment? are likely to be much more favorable for the development of a real friendship. The intimacy of close group relations, constrained by external bonds, is likely to bring a destructive realism into the friendship before it has had time to grow

Social Psychology into a satisfactory stage of idealism. Let us consider this matter especially as it refers to the home but keeping in mind the wider application of the principles involved. It seems to the writer that there is a measure of contradiction involved in the suggestion of a companionate marriage for the sake of granting freedom for the development of love.

The companionate, with its implication of conceptual control and divorce by agreement, in a way guarantees freedom but counteracts the real purpose of freedom. The main purpose of freedom is to give an opportunity for sex sublimation; this makes for creative idealism in the psychic and especially in the aesthetic realm. But if an impulse find expression on a low level there is little likelihood that it rise to a higher social or moral level. Thus if the creativeness of sex finds expression along the lines of physical reproduction it is not likely to create worthy ideals in the aesthetic and moral realms. Coyness has much better opportunity to fulfill its purpose under conditions of friendship than under those of marriage. Ellis suggests that the passivity of coyness is not real but apparent only. (B39:229). It is a positive factor encouraging the sublimation of the libido from the realm of sense to that of soul. But such a potent factor functions successfully only under conditions of freedom from marriage relations; it seems to us that Lindsey is right in stressing the freedom but wrong in assuming that it can be joined with marriage relations. Just as the expression of coyness makes for the idealization of the friend in the thought of the man so possibly the uncertainty and separation involved in the premarital friendship has the same effect on the woman; many individuals begin to let down on their idealism as soon as marriage is contracted. 

For these reasons it is well that the period of courtship rather than that of marriage be used to lay the idealistic foundations for the friendship which we expect to act as a binding tie throughout the entire period of the married life. 

The realism produced by the bonds of marriage are to some

extent produced by any such external bonds as hold together the members of a cabinet, business organization, congress, etc. The friendship is likely to be more idealisic and real if matured in the free period. 

What principles govern the length of the free period?

The length of the free period prior to the public committal to group loyalty is a matter of considerable concern. 

If certain stages of growth in the sentiment are to be accomplished during this free period then the proper length of such period is not a matter of minor importance. We give some quotations bearing on this matter in relation to marriage; the extent of the period need not differ here essentially from that of other close group bonds. Saleeby says, "The eugenist, I think, must welcome long engagements simply because,they tend by the mere operation of time, as we say, to enhance the importance of the psychical and to reduce the importance of the physical factors which determine sex attraction". (118:229). Cicero states that in the forming of a friendship, "we should never suffer affection to take root in our hearts before judgment has time to interpose". (A27:207). Gray says that, "Before a man and a woman get engaged they would do well to have some long talks together, and so to find out what their real interests are, and whether their general views and purposes in life are such as can possibly be harmonized". (55:49). Evidently it is more important that this be done before marriage than that it be done before engagement, although it is true that there should be certain basic understandings before marriage is contemplated. Galloway outlines certain kinds of information which should be secured during courtship in order to insure success and happiness in marriage. Each should be informed, "(1) Of the general male and female differences; (2) of the special qualities of the particular member of the other sex who may be under consideraton as

Social Psychology a mate; (3) in respect to the technique and methods of using personal and sexual differences and likenesses to produce happiness". (A50:230). It is obvious that such education as is here outlined is not going to be acquired in a very brief length of time. In general the length of the free period should be sufficient to permit the maturation and sublimation of the friendship. There is need not only of the development of knowledge but also of the organization of the emotional-instinctive elements of the complex itself and the set of self which we term loyalty to friend and friendship.

Let us attempt to state this matter a little more definitely. 

We have indicated that during the free or idealistic period there should develop the completion of the organization of What genesis of the the sentiment, the knowledge of friendship should take self concerning the object of the place during the free sentiment and proper relations of self to that object, and further a period? determination on part of self to further the best interests of the object and the friendship. 

This is but to say that the sentiment, as such, needs to mature before the objective constraints be brought to bear in order to compel the adjustment. The whole process of idealization is one which cannot take place quickly. Stable habits of thought need to be developed in a way favorable to the idealized object of the sentiment; the idealized object must be so definitely set in mind that the realistic object will not dominate over it during the period of forced close association. For his highly idealistic friendship Emerson always seems to assume the right to escape from the person whenever one so desires. If this were always possible then the maintenance of a friendship would be an easy task. 

But the real purpose of a friendship is to so leaven the actualities of life as to make them endurable; the idealistic must conquer the realistic and not merely escape from it.

Besides these habits of thought, a new total pattern for the organization of the different emotions into the sentiment needs to be established. Further, the transition from an established home condition to a new home situation involves rather difficult adjustments. One may get an habitual set and expectancy in regard to a home situation and find it difficult to fit into a new situation of a similar sort; habits conflict when they have part of their elements in common. The psychoanalysists speak of what they term mother-fixation-the child's idea of what a woman ought to be is taken so distinctly from his mother that he attempts to make every other woman fit exactly into this individual pattern. Groves speaks of a case in which he says, "Hardly had the ceremony been completed when the mother's influence began to be felt adversely in the new family. The husband constantly compared the wife and mother to the disadvantage of the former. He complained of every variation of the wife from the routine of the mother and quickly made clear to the mother that he was dissatisfied". (59:201). Such case points to the difficulty of emotional adjustment. It is evident that it has a much wider field of application than this. A superintendent might become so used to the individual ways of a secretary as to be dissatisfied with every other good secretary whose individuality was somewhat different. The main point to recognize is that the formation of a sentiment constitutes the formation of a series of complicated habits; some of these habits have to be reformed, which makes their establishment still more difficult. The formation of any new habit such as handwriting, typewriting, mirror tracing, piano playing, is developed only after months of persistent effort. It should be recognized that vividness is a definite factor in habit formation; in cases where a strong impulse functions the new set may be secured much more quickly. The factor of sex in the intersex relation may give vividness to the adjust-

Social Psychology ment tendency. But such an adjustment is apt to be deceptive as it does not make for the total adjustment needed for the sentiment; part of this adjustment involves cognition and self-assertion. Because of these considerations we conclude that favorable relationships covering some months at least constitute a minimum requirement for the development of those phases of the sentiment which should be developed during the free period. 

We offer a few practical suggestions as to how to further desirable developments of the sentiment during the free period. There is need of what Griggs calls "community of 

How promote friend. experience" (58:24) in order to ship during the free develop what King calls “mutual self-revelation and period? answering trust". (79:55). Miss Condé says, "Daily life with others, in the home, school, or college, business or church work, brings to light hidden treasures of personality that help and hold us". (29:26). Or even if undesirable aspects of individual life are brought out in such contacts it is better than such be done during the more idealistic free period. Experience of this sort should cover as wide a range as possible and thus bring out as much mutual revelation of character and individual tendency as possible. The experiences should be sufficiently varied as to reveal practically all the strong impulses and emotions of life. It is especially important to secure mutual selfrevelations on matters of sex, money, self-assertion, morality and religion. It is well also to reveal party attitudes, family traditions and loyalties, national and racial prejudices. It is not to be supposed that all such revelation will be an immediate help to the progress of the friendship; our concern is not with the immediate progress but with the permanent growth and development of the sentiment. Such revelations as develop from intimate contacts are certain under conditions of marriage or other close group relations and should be made during the free

period. This is true because the free period is the more idealistic and the jar and jolt of conflicting revelations can be absorbed more readily here. In certain instances the urge of suppressed sex and the uncertainty of the attainment of the friendship is likely to produce a more earnest effort to make the personal adjustments necessary. There is special need to face squarely the problems relating to money and to sex. In the early idealistic glow of friendship it may appear that the financial status of the parties involved has but little bearing. But our economic interests are too closely bound up with our strong tendencies of gregariousness and self-assertion to be considered of minor importance. Definite consideration must be taken of the probable family income and how it shall be handled and distributed. It is especially important to determine whether it is a joint income of equal partners or whether, as is sometimes the case, it shall be thought of as the husband's income and the wife be listed as having no occupation and no income. The various problems connected with sex must be faced openly. Especially such problem as those relating to conceptive control and the desirability of children in the home must be faced and settled. Excellent books are being produced which set forth these problems in a clear way and with a moral and spiritual setting that cannot be condemned. A definite attempt should be made to keep the friendship free from the domination of external pressure and of wrong individual motives. The date of a marriage frequently is determined by professional and financial considerations and without regard for what should happen during the courtship period; the marriage is unduly delayed on account of professional preparation or unduly hurried on account of an opportunity to enter into business. Individuals who in reality do not love convince themselves that they ought to love this person and probably that they do care sufficiently for him; the attachment always should be definite and personal.

Social Psychology 

Many such forms of pressure are subtle and cannot be detected easily. But wherever they can be detected the individual should face the actual facts and not permit himself to drift into fantasy or rationalization. Probably the happiness of the total life period is at stake. No opportunity should be lost for utilizing the period most favorable to the formation of the friendship. Every effort should be given to arranging conditions so that this free period will fulfill its real function in laying permanent and sure foundations for the friendship. 

4. The Maintenance After Public Committal of the 

Friendship Formed During the Free Period. 

We consider here: (1) the possibility of the disintegration of friendship after its establishment during the free period; (2) how its disintegration under conditions of group relations may be prevented. we expect

We have spoken of the automatic and conscious disorganization of a friendship after its formation. When a definite mood is upon one it is easy to believe that it will Should persist throughout life; the glow of friendship to disinter- a religious conversion is apt to grate during during close give this effect. (See AAA128:138). In like manner any early exgroup contact? perience of love is apt to be deceptive as to its permanency-as a mood and feeling attitude it cannot last very long. Aristotle raises the question concerning a developed friendship, "suppose that one party remains what he was when the Friendship was formed, while the other becomes morally improved and widely different from his friend in goodness; is the improved character to treat the other as a friend? May we not say it is impossible?" (4:215). But in a fine passage Aristotle says that we always should remember the previous friend-

ship and "to those who have been friends and are so no longer we should allow somewhat on the score of previous Friendship, whenever the cause of severance is not excessive depravity on their part". (4:215). We should keep in mind that a friendship may disintegrate the same as any other character tendency; the reversal of the same influences which built it up will tear it down. The ideal of the sentiment-its object-may be forgotten or disregarded the same as any other idea or ideal; or the friend on account of certain changed tendencies may cease to suggest the ideal. Moreover the close contacts of intimate relations may call to attention certain traits in the character of the individual to which the friend previously was blind. The continuance of attention on such traits may act as a constant interference with the normal functioning of the sentiment until the sentiment itself is practically destroyed. 

Suppose a case in which after marriage the husband develops drunkenness or some extreme type of sensuality.

The disgust and repulsion aroused by such a condition may at first cause the friend to question the accuracy of her senses but soon it will produce reproach, horror and finally primitive disgust and repulsion which indicates that the sentiment has been broken down; or we may say that the real object of the sentiment-the ideal-has ceased to be suggested by this drunkard or libertine. Obviously one should not anticipate a result like this but should realize its possibility in order to avoid it. The realism of close group contact puts a special strain upon a friendship and for that reason we should expect to use special efforts in order to maintain and develop it. 

A friendship never is completed in the sense that it reaches and automatically maintains an unchanged status. 

Social relationships never become static after any such fashion; neither does an individual life maintain its set in any such way. So the only possibility of keeping the

Social Psychology friendship alive is to keep alive the conditions which develop it; moving bodies, to keep together, must move in parallel lines or in curves maintaining the same relation to each other. Friendship, like any other living thing, must feed upon 

How may friendship be kept alive during close group association? its natural food in order to maintain its life and vitality. Griggs says, "To keep the love of one's life-comrade, one must truly win it over again every day". (58:36). Conditions favorable to a normal and wholesome growth of friendship as members of a small group must in some degree approximate the conditions of the free period. This does not mean that the conditions of marriage, for instance, can be assumed to be the same as those of courtship; the conditions are different and it is folly to assume that they are the same. But much can be done for the maintenance of the friendship which is not likely to be done unless the interested parties think of the problem properly and both strive to solve it. Success must depend basically upon a mutual desire for friendship, a willingness of each to pay the price in labor and suffering which a friendly adjustment demands, and a thoroughgoing respect for the personality, individuality, ability and rights of the partner. Many traditional conceptions of proper home relationships must be faced squarely as enemies of home life today. Gray says, "The conception of the husband as a sort of Czar within his own home still lingers,— Men still grow up with the idea that a wife should be a sort of submissive and very charming slave, honored by occasional demonstrations of affection, and that the whole household should be ordered to suit his lordship's convenience". (55:137). It is an open question whether men or women do most toward keeping this foolish and destructive tradition alive. Judge Lindsey mentions frequently the tradition that each of the married partners must watch the

other carefully and avenge any tendency toward disloyalty; he speaks of this as the cause of the jealousy which disrupts the home so often. This tradition is just the opposite of what is needed to develop friendship. Instead of a suspicious oversight there must be a mutual trust and confidence. Love flourishes only under conditions of freedom. 

Where the group relation limits freedom the partners to the group must see that they do not extend such limitation further. 

In order to present the dangers to friendship adhering in the group relationship we mention certain obstacles more definitely. It is impossible to mention all the stumbling 

What interferes with blocks but the reader can help friendships in group supply the omissions. Black conrelations? cludes that the most basic principle of friendship is that of the Golden

Rule. 17:49). It might seem that the truth of this principle would be conceded readily and universally. That such is not the case, points to the distinct overemphasis on the affective and mystical aspects of love. Some come to think of love as being so unique and mysterious in its nature as not to conform to the ordinary laws of cause and effect. Indeed some seem to have the idea that to suggest the laws of cause and effect in relation to love is in some way to minimize its worth or to assign it derogatory relationships. On account of this false conception it needs to be emphasized that the relations existing between husband and wife or any close associates in a group-must be fundamentally just. Friendship will not thrive unless each party to it treats the other as a person of equal rights and privileges. Berl says, "Love presupposes a certain identity between the subject and the object it brings together; an identity that we discover or one that we work out". (13: 

249). Such recognition of identity must apply to the essentials of human life. For instance, economic servitude

Social Psychology or dependence on the part of the wife obviously is unjust and bound to be a factor disturbing of marriage friendship. 

Whatever scheme of administering the family income to meet the family needs is adopted, it must presuppose that husband and wife are equal partners and not the husband a business man and the wife a hired servant. To designate a certain allowance to the wife may supply her economic needs but does not treat her as an intelligent partner who deserves a place of equal trust in deciding how the family income shall be spent. Further, any evidence of sexual or social disloyalty strikes at the very foundations of friendship since it is evidence of betrayal of a trust made necessary by the marriage relationship. Failure to understand the sex life of the partner and to observe reasonable sex relationships acts as a powerful irritant which is likely to disturb seriously if not to disrupt the friendship of the home; such irritation is deep-seated and may express itself in ways which seem not closely related to its real cause. 

Evidence seems to show that most of the disturbing influence which results in the breaking up of homes has some connection with either the problem of money or that of sex adjustment. Lack of satisfactory arrangements and adjustments produce basic irritants which cause open conflicts of various sorts to appear. The irritating factors in other small group relations are likely to be of somewhat different sorts but have an essential bearing on justice, selfrespect and principles of morality. 

In addition to avoiding the miscarriage of justice, it is well also to make a positive effort to cultivate the idealistic phases of life during marriage or other such close group alignment. One of the most frequent jests (intended to amuse but which probably should disgust us) is based upon the contrast of the pseudo idealism of courtship to the commonplace realism of marriage. Because of the intimacy of the contacts of marriage its relationships need a constant

leven to make then tolerable. Gray says, "So close an intimacy as marriage involves is really only tolerable when love constantly supplies reasons for patience, generosity and forgiveness". (55:157). While love should be used to its full capacity to absorb the jars and jolts consequent to the intimate adjustment of two lives, at the same time positive efforts should be made to maintain idealistic attitudes and conditions. In fact, we cannot expect to use the potency of love without giving it food upon which to renew its life, and one of its chief types of food is aesthetic idealism. Hear Emerson the idealist speak of his friend! "Happy is the house that shelters a friend! It might well be built, like a festal bower or arch, to entertain him a 

How make group friendship more idealistic? single day". (41:116). If such thought of a friend involves a consequent planning for his happiness and welfare it tends to keep the friendship alive and growing. We make the following suggestions as examples of certain conditions favorable to idealism. Vacations apart as well as together should be arranged if at all possible; experience apart for a time tends to renew the conditions and something of the remembrances of the courtship period. Also vacations together if they are used to visit some of the old scenes of the courtship days are likely to stir again idealistic attitudes. Imagination may be stirred and idealism renewed by reading novels together or by attending dramatic or athletic entertainments together. Common interests in literature, philanthropy, education or religion should inspire idealism of the right sort. The observation of anniversaries may be so used as to call to mind previous visions and hopes. A real union of economic interests in the promotion of some worthy end is highly desirable. Such planning for conquest and achievement renews life and stimulates vision. Probably no line of common endeavor is so natural or beneficial as

Social Psychology assuming the responsibility for the care and direction of the life of a child; close association with the life of a child is a great stimulus for creative imagination and planning. 

The life and thought of a child is definitely idealistic and hence no parent, whether real or foster, plans other than idealistic achievements for his child. The home which plans deliberately to remain childless cuts the power line which maintains its life; a former class mate recently reiterated his growing conviction that it is a distressing thing to grow old without children. These suggestions might be extended greatly but sufficient have been given to show the possibility and something of the methods available for the development of idealism within the home. 

The reader can think of kindred methods for developing idealism and vision among the members of a faculty, a dramatic cast, a Y. M. C. A. cabinet, a grange, or any such small group. Even the United States senate might to advantage spend some thought as to how to develop a higher type of friendly morale among its members. All such problems can be solved when stated definitely and effort put forth for their solution.

IV. The Development of Positive Social Sentiments among All the Members of the Small Social Group. 

We consider here: (1) the development of friendships under certain special individual relations; (2) the development of friendships under special environmental or genetic conditions. 

1. The Influence of Special Relationships Existing Be tween the Individuals Constituting a Small Social Group.

To be stable, the small social group must have all of its individuals bound together by ties of friendship. We con-

sider in this connection special conditions involving the relationships of all of the members of small social groups. 

Again the home is used as a type example. 

What effect has kinship upon friendship?

We consider first the essential effect of kinship upon the growth of the friendship within the small group; this may be extended in its meaning to the religious, social, political or other bonds of likeness. Cicero says, "The closest and nearest of all societies is between man and wife; then follows that between them and their children, and afterwards that of the whole family, who inhabit together and have all things in common;-". (A27:25). If the closest bond is between man and wife then kinship of blood cannot be considered a major factor. But Bogardus says that, "The love of a mother for her child is the most enduring;-". (A18:18). Ellwood asserts that, "The 'blood bond' between the members of uncivilized, and even of civilized, groups has often been the most powerful expression of their solidarity;-". (40:258). It must appear to all only natural that the bonds of love should bind parents to their children and children in turn to their parents and to each other. But that children are not as certain to feel love for their parents as parents for their children must be evident to any careful observer. 

Roman moralists had trouble in explaining the facts that during the Sullan persecutions many sons denounced their fathers but no father was known to denounce his son. If kinship or likeness were the secret of the attachments that attachment would be strong equally in the case of child for parent as that of parent for child. The helplessness of the human infant and the consequent care and attention of the parent given to it, is practically certain to develop that measure of tenderness and parental love of which the parent is capable. But children for instance, who are treated in ways that are basically unjust, may develop first The

Social Psychology fear and then hatred for their parents but certainly will not develop love for them for the simple reason that they are their children. In the case of racial, religious and even political kinship, the factors of likeness function to some extent as aids toward the building of the sentiment; it is easier for me to understand another person who is akin to me in some way. Also those who are akin are likely to have common interests and because of such interests associate together more frequently and thus build up a friendship. The fundamental fact that sentiments are acquired attitudes must be kept in mind. Special forms of likeness may make for mutual understandings, mutual interests and confidence one in the other but cannot, on that account, produce a sentiment by other than its normal processes of growth; a son of a missionary family reports that he had little love for his older brothers until he enjoyed actual assocation with them. It follows that kinship and likeness make the growth of friendship easier but do not guarantee its development; friendship has normal processes of growth through which it must go.

It is best that we consider kinship an opportunity for the development of friendship rather than a substitute for it. There are several conditions which may hinder the de- 

What may hinder the velopment of love on the part of love between kinsmen? those who receive benefits and who normally should respond with appreciation and gratitude. This applies not only to the relation of children to parents but also of pupils to teachers, employees to employers, patients to physicians, etc. We are thinking of the relation of children to parents primarily but keeping in mind the broader applications of the principle. Favoritism shown by the parents to one child in the family may not only make the child unpopular with the rest of the children but may make for a feeling of resentment on the part of the other children towards the parents.

Also there is danger in parental stoicism. The parent may in reality care deeply for the child but may not incline to show the affection openly. Thus there may fail to be given to the child the proper stimulus to cause him to love the parent. The opposite extreme of this defect is a defect of equally serious nature. The parent may display a primitive form of tenderness which ministers to the immediate and selfish whim and caprice of the child. This may be done as a real sacrifice on the part of the parent and may have back of it the best altruistic motives. But as 

Griggs says of the mother, "When the sacrifice is made blindly, the woman is apt to awaken, years afterward, to find her children selfish and her husband ungrateful". (58:38-39). Such a parent may sacrifice her parental standing and take in its place that of a servant and thus not only sacrifice the best in her own life but also deprive her children of a real mother who can develop their filial love. This type of sacrifice is not helpful but destructive of both parental character and filial love; the attitude of some parents constitutes an effective training school for narrow selfishness on the part of their children. Parents must come to understand the nature of sentiments and to realize that parental tenderness stands in need of proper sublimation as much as any other primitive life tendency. 

Turning to the positive side of this problem, let us attempt to determine the types of influence which will stimulate a responsive love between or among kinsmen. The conditions of early child life give many 

How cultivate responsive love between kins- opportunities for the arousal of tenderness and filial love on the men? part of children for their parents and children for each other; the dependence of pupils upon teachers, prisoners upon their keepers, certain employees upon their employers, etc., constitute analogous situations. 

In such cases an unequal amount of responsibility for the

Social Psychology initiation of the friendship must rest upon those who have the superior control; the dependent individual is not in a position to initiate friendship because of the likelihood of his motives being misunderstood. Under such conditions there is need to recognize the fact that friendship develops to the best advantage when the parties to it are free and equal. It seems evident that in many ways the dependent individual cannot be on a plane of equality with the one upon whom he depends. But freedom and equality should be stressed along the lines in which they do exist and an attempt may be made to develop equality along other lines; this is the reason for placing the larger burden of initiative upon the party in a position to confer benefits. 

If a child cannot be on an equality in the way of knowledge and skill, the most should be made of his relative equality in morality and self-respect. Financial arrangements can be made to the end that the child have some property of his own so that he can confer favors and give gifts; to have to ask for special money to give a gift is to destroy much of the benefit of the giving. In dealing with prisoners many times we pursue a policy calculated to break down, rather than to build up, their self-respect; how can one grow to be a self-respecting citizen with shaven head and horrible striped clothing and at times made an exhibit of everything wrong in human life? We should remember that to promote friendship it is much more important to give than to recive; where the treasure is placed there will the affection of the heart go. The majority of homes could take a larger responsibility in teaching their children concerning the acquiring and spending of money; there is real need that they learn to relate money to its source in labor and learn to spend it to advantage as well as to accumulate a reasonable store of it. Many times financial failure or extravagance seems due to lack of early training in the actual handling of money. A child early in his developing life should

have some control of property so as to strengthen his selfrespect and give him the power to confer benefits; this is needed by all dependents in order to enable them to love those upon whom they depend. 

2. Special Conditions Demanding Care in the Adjust ments Leading to Friendships within Small Social Groups. 

We consider here: (1) special conditions involved in growth; (2) special environmental conditions making difficult the development of friendship. 

(1). The Relation of Infancy and of Adolescence to the Development of Group Friendships. 

When thinking of the development of friendship the genetic periods of infancy and adolescence seem to demand special attention; these periods are somewhat akin in that they imply natural and spiritual birth. The social impressions of infancy become very 

How cultivate group friendships during in- deep-seated and lasting. The genfancy? eral principle of primacy causes us to give special attention to experiences which are the first in a series as well as the absolutely first; the first day in school, first public address, first intercollegiate game, etc., make vivid impressions. Increasing emphasis is being placed upon the importance of social heredity; by this we mean the very early environmental influence. Bagley says that the "great essentials of human life are transmitted, not directly through the germ cell, but indirectly by social contact-culture, 'education' and civilized habits". (6:9). Social heredity should aim to develop character stability and to lay a sound foundation for friendship by impressing major premises of thought. Also there should be a basic sense of loyalty established to parents

Social Pyschology and family. During the first few years such responsiveness to parental love should be aroused as to establish permanent bonds of friendship for parents and members of the family. Such basic attitudes must be established more by example than by precept. Racial, industrial and national attitudes are established mainly through imitation of the basic attitudes of parents. Unguarded or habitual expressions in word or deed on the part of the parent are very potent influences over the life of the child. Children distinguish very quickly between the real and the superficial attitude of the parent and always will reflect the real character trait of the parent. The parent whose child develops fundamentally wrong attitudes toward life had better examine again his own character traits. Whenever parental attitudes differ on basic moral and religious questions this difference is almost certain to be reflected in the lives of the children; if there are several children some will follow one parent and some the other, or there may be diverse tendencies within the traits of the life of one child. Just as in physical heredity two types of germ cells contribute determiners, so in social heredity the basic moral and spiritual attitudes of each parent will leave an indelible imprint on the life of the child. The main need of the little child is a spiritual atmosphere of quiet confidence, an example of personal attitudes that are fundamentally right, and a steady guidance toward a proper expression in his own life; irregularity of eating and sleeping, excessive indulgence mingled with occasional severity, instruction contradicted by example, is the type of thing which causes the parents to be surprised to find that their child is not what he ought to be. We must come to realize that probably the most important period of education precedes entrance into the kindergarten; one reason why we hold women to a higher status of personal morality is because we have en-

trusted them to so great an extent with the social heredity of children.

Adolescence has been termed a second birth and consequently involves a period of development akin in importance to the infancy period; it has been suggested that this is the birth in which we develop 

What training in friendship does the not mere existence but real life. adolescent need?

The significance of the adolescent period has now come to be recognized rather generally. Paul Monroe says, "It is now known that during the adolescent period the child undergoes such a radical change, physically and psychically, that education can find in these changes the sufficient basis for a differentiation between the earlier and secondary stages of education". (D92:9). Certain dangers to family or other small group relations are involved in these violent changes in the growing life. It is not easy to bridge the chasm between the attachment of the child and that of the adult. There is danger that the adolescent depreciate the wisdom of the parent and consider him old-fashioned and out-of-date; this is true especially in cases in which the parents are foreign by birth and hence have ideas different from those of the young people of the new country. It is probably true that we are overemphasizing the present "revolt of youth"; in a sense every generation does and must present a mild type of revolt and very ancient history records this contrast between generations and the fear it occasioned on the part of certain elders. Especially on account of his intersex friendships, but also on account of all his friendships with his own age, is the adolescent likely to strain the bonds of attachment with his own family. 

To meet these difficulties parents should become interested definitely in the problems of adolescents and thus aware of the conditions which confront each new generation; the parent should have the background for understanding the

Social Psychology sudden changes in the life of the adolescent and should be able to make due allowance for certain eccentricities. The parent can afford to spend much time and thought in providing opportunities for wholesome lines of friendship for the adolescent; let the parent welcome these new friendships of the adolescent and try his best to give them intelligent guidance. There must be a willingness to release the young life in order that it may prepare to meet the problems of its own generation; it is only through this voluntary release that equality can be achieved and the friendships within the circle of the home made permanent. But the adolescent should understand that he is in a period of life that he can adjust more readily than can the parent. 

Especially if he has enjoyed more opportunities in the way of education, he can be expected to appreciate the value of friendship and perhaps to know how to make the adjustments needed. 

(2). The Development of Small Group Friendships Under Unfavorable Environmental Conditions. 

There is a strain imposed on friendship not only by special conditions of growth but also by special conditions of environment. There is little question but that friend- 

How cultivate friend. ships prosper better under encourships in unfavorable aging conditions of environment. 

Conditions of wealth may seem to environs? make the individual free from the need of friends. Conditions of poverty may lower selfrespect, may interfere with the giving of favors and may so harden and burden the life as to make friendship seem a secondary matter. Conditions produced by strikes or war emphasize material and selfish considerations to such an extent as to make development of an idealistic friendship difficult. Any serious form of failure may so discour-

age the individual as to interfere with the assertion of the idealism needed for friendship. Such conditions place a severe strain upon the voluntary factors of friendship; the individual can by effort maintain loyalty until he can renew the basic life impulses which prompt friendship. There is need that friendships be established deeply enough to withstand these periods of special strain. Or we may say there is need that we strive to adjust the social and economic order so that friendships will have a chance in it; as long as there exist such conditions as Upton Sinclair describes in "The Jungle" we have phases of the social order in which friendship has little chance. A very good standard for judging the rightness of the social order is to ask whether it is favorable to the development of all kinds of small group friendships.
