CHAPTER XII Friendly Sentiments Relating the Individual to the Social Group. 

Our consideration in the previous chapter concerned the relation of individuals within small social groups. In this chapter we speak of the relationship of the individual to the institution, which may involve either a large or a small group. What institutional alignments does the individual normally have? To what extent does the individual as such choose these alignments? How may conflicting institutional loyalties be adjusted? How may the individual develop desirable civic sentiments? What attachments to purely social groups are desirable?

I. Types of Individual-Group Alignments Possible and 

Actual. 

Besides friendly attachments to individuals we form attachments to groups. These may include beside family attachments, industrial, professional, educational, civic, What institutional at. religious, athletic or purely social tachments form? do we attachments. These attachments may be to small or to large groups; to a family, a neighborhood, a township, a city, a county, a state, a nation, western civilization, humanity. In school life one has his attachment to his class, his social group, his department, his school or to the ideal of getting an education. We have indicated that sentiments may be individual, collective or abstract; this classification depends upon the nature of the object of the sentiment. One may be attached to a particular home, to a particular type of home such as a country 333

Social Psychology home, or to home in the abstract. We speak in this chapter of how these institutional adjustments are built up on the part of the individual; our concern includes the friendly adjustment of the individual to any type of institution. 

II. Some Principles Governing the Alignment of the Individual to Any Type of Social Group. 

Under this heading we consider: (1) The determination of social alignments by the individual; (2) the formation of an individual scale of values. 

1. The Selection of Social Alignments by the Individual 

Himself. his own determine social loyalties?

In the determination of the relations of an individual to the various institutions there is sure to arise the question of conflicts of loyalty. In the case of such conflict Royce 

Can the individual gives the advice following: "Prevent the conflict of loyalties when you can, minimize such conflict when it exists, and, by means of fair play and the chivalrous attitude towards the opponent, utilize even conflict, where it is inevitable, so as to further the cause of loyalty to loyalty". (A116:158-59). But it may not appear evident to all that the individual is capable of passing judgment on such important matters. McMurry says that even the young student must pass upon the worth and truth of ideas. He says, "The student, therefore, must set himself up as judge of whatever ideas appear before him. 

They are up for trial on their soundness and worth; he must uncover their merits and defects, and must pass judgment on their general value. If he is hasty and careless he suffers the penalty of bad judgment; and if he refrains from judging at all, he becomes one who 'does not know his own mind', a weakling". (AA92:139-40). But can the

Individual-Group Sentiments 

335 individual do otherwise than pass judgment for himself? 

He may entrust the decision to the Pope, the Bible, the surgeon, the parent or teacher but in fact he has made his own decision by selecting some particular individual or book to make it for him. It is easy to assume that a high official of church or state can determine with accuracy and finality as to what constitutes orthodoxy or patriotism. 

But if one were to go to an official of a different type of church or state he would be likely to get a different idea as to what constitutes his duty. Therefore the individual who has normal intelligence must accept squarely the task of determining his own loyalties. In any practical case such determination is within limits; the young child in the home is not likely to have before him serious questions for his determination and decision. Our party alignments and major premises come to us for the most part through social traditions and our decisions commonly are within the bounds of such major premises. But it is possible that major issues may arise at any time and the individual should accustom himself to rely upon his own judgment for the decision of such issues; what seems to be an escape from the meeting of individual responsibility is for the most part an illusion. On the whole the conscious acceptance by the individual of such responsibility is likely to result on his part in a higher degree of moral dependability. 

Becaues the individual is related to various institutions at the same time conflicts are practically sure to arise. For instance he is under different forms of government at the 

How solve individual same time: he is asked to comply conflicts of loyalties? with moral, civic, home and school laws besides observing traditions and fashions. But teacher and parent possibly may give different directions as to how to solve a problem. The pressure from the gang may go counter to the laws of the policeman. The school or civic law may demand a report

Social Psychology of misconduct on the part of a brother or schoolmate which loyalty to the group does not permit. The state may demand service in war which conscience based upon morality condemns. How can the individual serve all these masters?

The most inclusive organization has the right to the first claim on the loyalty of the individual. Royce advocates that the individual choose a good cause and give himself loyally to the furtherance of it. But how determine the cause that is good? Royce says, "And so, a cause is good, not only for me, but for mankind, in so far as it is essentially a loyalty to loyalty, that is, is an aid and a furtherance of loyalty in my fellows". (A116:118). But what is loyalty? Royce says when it is properly defined loyalty is understood to be "the fulfillment of the whole moral law”. (A116:16). Loyalty to moral principles must have first place because these are the principles or "mores" affecting all social life; possibly the total human life of the past has contributed in a way to the formation of these moral principles, hence they represent the most distinct majority view. Thus it is that all promises of immediate returns and all calculations of results must give way before a principle that is distinctly moral. Socrates in facing the death sentence says, "a man who is good for anything ought not to calculate the chance of living or dying; he ought only to consider whether in doing anything he is doing right or wrong-acting the part of a good man or a bad". (B106:116). Or hear Saleeby speaking from the point of view of the race: "Every human being that exists or ever has existed or ever will exist is a product of mother-love or foster-mother-love, and I am well entitled to say, as I have so often said, no morals, no man". (118:57-58). We must assume that the following of moral principles will, in the end, develop the most desirable results. It may seem for the time that better results can be gotten for the individual or party through the sacrifice of moral principles but such

Individual-Group Sentiments 

337 can be only an appearance and not a reality. 

If there is any doubt as to what constitutes the moral, we can assert the principle that the more inclusive attachments must receive our support; the mores are developed over long periods of time by many 

How evaluate wider alties? and more narrow loy. nations and peoples. Bogardus says, "True national patriotism is national love divorced from all narrow desires. It urges that one's nation group play a role of wholesomeness in the world.-Besides loyalty to family, to community, to nation-state, the trend of social evolution is producing another type of group loyalty-internationalism. The world is on the verge of forming an international consciousness and a sense of planetary values". (A18:307-08). O. S. Davis in speaking of the social gospel says, "Therefore the moral idea must be socialized. We must see that there is a moral standard to cover corporate action as well as individual behavior. Groups, as well as single persons, are amenable to the moral law". (33:4243). It must be recognized clearly by all that loyalty to the interests of a party cannot be right if that party is promoting tendencies which are definitely antisocial. 

Further, the interests of a state must be subordinate to that of a nation of which it is a part and the interests of a nation subordinate to that of a group of nations to which it belongs; this subordination is necessary for the good of the part, the whole cannot suffer and the part prosper. 

Tranquility in a home must not be allowed to prevent the development of justice. If hasty suppression of filial initiative means injustice to the growing child life it is certain in the long run to injure the best interests of the home and hence of the parents as well as of the children. In every case the wider loyalty must prevail over the more narrow, and the widest loyalty of all is that to the general moral principle.

Social Psychology alties?

The individual constantly is confronted with traditional and formal conceptions of loyalties. We have mentioned the fact that the life and stability of an institution is not How evaluate tradi- helped by blind conservatism; all tional and formal loy. conservatism must be intelligent and in accord with present needs. 

Certain basic ideas-in religion, law, medicine, education, government-persist but are likely to need restatement in every few generations. Fosdick says that we should think of "abiding experiences in changing categories". Careful consideration needs to be given to the separation of these abiding principles from the particular form in which a previous generation set them forth; there is great need of conservatism, all progress depends upon it, but it must be of stable values and not of external forms. The worship of form prevents the application of the vital principle under new conditions; in the new day of communication and transportation the attempt to avoid "entangling alliances" may work against the common good rather than advance it. The habit of speaking in favor of large families may in a day of scientific machinery make for the impoverishment and degradation of the race. must keep the objective of human good in mind and make sure that methods which previously promoted it will, in the present changed environment, have the same effect. Where form opposes itself to spirit, and ceremony to human interests, the right-minded individual need not be in doubt as to which deserves his support. Traditional form must in all cases be subordinate to stable character and social values. Let the form be preserved if it will make for the real values, otherwise let it be changed. 

2. The Formation and Application by the Individual of a Scale of Value Judgments. 

The individual must formulate and apply a scheme of

Individual-Group Sentiments 

339 value judgments to his institutional relations and loyalties. 

His final dependence must be upon his own conscientious judgment as to what type of loyalty is of supreme value. 

How formulate and He should give careful heed to such apply a proper scale teachers as are sincere and disinof values? terested, who have had access to the facts involved, and who love truth and goodness above any of their particular applications. He should give careful attention to such values as have stood the test of time and have come to him with the recommendation of several generations and races back of them. On the whole he should give the interests of the large, more inclusive institutions right of way where they seem to conflict with more narrow and perhaps more personal interests. He should seek earnestly to differentiate the vital content of the traditional loyalty from the particular expression in which it was set forth by a previous generation. He should refuse to be cowed or stampeded by the mere assertion that his decision is not in accord with orthodoxy or patriotism; his own careful conscientious judgment must be the test of what it is proper to call orthodox or patriotic. So long as the propagandist can cow and intimidate the free citizen by merely saying that his position is the only patriotic and orthodox one, that long will the main values of individual thought and free citizenship be unrealized. Each age has as much right to interpret its patriotism as it has to translate its sacred literature into a form that its generation can understand. Certainly careful thought should be given by every individual as to what constitutes fundamental principles; such principles must be applied but there is need of much thought and comparison of view as to what constitutes them. Our confidence in the basic principle of democracy leads us to believe that a free and educated citizenship that arrives at its own decisions will promote the social good; if we cannot believe this, we

Social Psychology had best substitute the rule of the few, for the rule of the many and stop public education. So long as we advocate education for all and a general participation in government, we must be concerned deeply that the individual develop and apply a proper scale of values to his social loyalties. 

III.—Illustrations of Individual-Group Alignments in the Civic Realm. 

The institutional attachments and loyalties of the individual are so many and varied that we cannot give detailed attention to them all. In this chapter we illustrate these alignments in the civic, educational and social realms; on the basis of such type cases it is hoped that the reader will apply the principles involved to other forms of loyalty in which he is interested. We consider here: (1) needed civic sentiments; (2) how such can be developed. 

1. The Civic Sentiments Needed by the Individual. 

What civic sentiments should be encouraged?

There should develop on the part of each citizen friendly sentiments for each of the civic organizations with which his life is associated. Patriotism is a prime requisite for the life of any civic organization; in like manner every professional or business organization must be concerned with the status of its morale. Berl says, "The State insists on being obeyed, but the fatherland insists on being loved. It will punish not only the deserter and the traitor, it will also punish the défaitiste: the one who doubts of his country, who doubts of victory in time of war. It will punish-if not by law, at all events by social custom-the internationalist who declares that he loves other nations as dearly as his own". (13: 

105). There is justification for insisting-by right methods, of course that the love of one's own nation be greater

Individual-Group Sentiments 

341 than that given to any other nation. But this must not imply that the interests of the part are superior to those of the larger whole of which it is a part; our love for America causes us to desire to see her first in social service but last in selfishness, tyranny and greed. Imprisonment and banishment inflicted because of love for the larger interests of humanity certainly is not for the good of the state or the advancement of the spiritual life of its citizens. Even 

Plato seems to forget the importance of the larger order of things when he says: "And may I not observe with equal propriety that the Hellenic race is all united together by ties of blood and friendship, and alien and strange to the barbarians?" (A106:207). Why think of others as "alien and strange" and as "barbarians"? We are coming, at least in theory to think of the relationship of the wider and more narrow civic loyalties in accord with the general principles of loyal attachments; the broader interests must have the support of our loyalties in case of conflicts. Hear Fosdick as he speaks of the right of the citizen to conform to the international and moral rather than exclusively to the national. "Against foolish chauvinism, competitive armaments, secret diplomacy, imperialistic experiments, against endeavors to play lone hands, when, by cooperation, international agencies could be set up to solve the problems which war never solves but only makes the worse, we now must lift our protest and launch our crusade". (A97:3).

This is the assertion by the individual of the right to support the broader interest and loyalty in case the two, in his judgment, come into conflict. This conforms to the usual principle of deciding in accord with one's best judgment. Royce says, "If one asks, How decide? conscience further urges, Decide as I, your conscience, the ideal expression of your whole personal nature, conscious and unconscious, find best". (A116:195-96). It is for the good of the nation that the citizen form these larger attachments

Social Psychology as well as attachments to the civic units within the nation. 

A particular civic unit is not likely to prosper until its contained units as well as the larger units of which it is a part enjoy the support of the friendly loyalty of its citizens. 

2. Methods for Developing Civic Sentiments on the 

Part of the Growing Citizen.

We consider here: (1) progress from smaller to larger units; (2) civic knowledge needed; (3) development of the feeling elements; (4) development of the loyalty aspect of the sentiment. 

(1). The Development of Sentiments from the Smaller to the Larger Civic Units. 

units?

It is in accord with the present day principles of pedagogy to say that the genesis of loyalties should be from the smaller to the larger civic units; the principle is to proceed 

Why develop first loy. from the well to the less well alty to smaller civic known. The near-at-hand object of loyalty, since its better understood, is more suited for a point of beginning for the young child. The young child must come to respect and love his local community before there can be any real content to his love for his nation; the postman, teacher and policeman, as representatives of the state may serve as a starting point from which larger conceptions and loyalties develop. This certainly is a more wholesome and desirable foundation for patriotism than the expression of formal reverence for the flag of the nation without any intelligent appreciation of the meaning of the word nation. 

Probably the value of flag drills and salutes is aesthetic rather than patriotic; if any actual patriotism is developed, it is of the superficial, unthinking kind. The citizen needs to learn that government is good, in spite of the

Individual-Group Sentiments 

343 fact of certain present defects. It is best to learn this in relation to the local community where both the defects and benefits of government are evident. Whenever the young citizen becomes an intelligent and loyal supporter of his local community he is ready to advance to the support of the larger units; this progress should develop by gradual stages from town to county, state, nation, civilization, humanity. Probably it is dangerous to try to jump from a strictly local to a universal loyalty; the jump is too long and is likely to be made in imagination only and not in reality. Much so-called patriotism is merely thoughtless chauvinism and bombastic dogmatism. Certainly one of the reasons for this is that the loyalty has not been kept intelligent and practical in its development from step to step. The loyalty, of course, should be to the ideal but there must be such a recognition of the present actuality as to encourage its development in the way of the ideal. 

(2). The Knowledge Needed for the Development of Civic Sentiments. 

The growing citizen should come to understand at least certain of the main facts concerning government. There should be a realization of the significance of national 

What knowledge is es- government. Bogardus says, "Govsential to right civic ernment, inclusive of legal machinsentiments? ery, is a mighty engine of control.

National governments are especially omnipotent". (A18:356). Bogardus points out how government may tend in the wrong direction and insists that we must educate leaders with such a sense of responsibility that they will put public interests ahead of private advantage. A part of the program for securing such leaders is to cause the growing citizen to understand the nature of government and the importance of right gov

Social Psychology ernment. Much knowledge of the nature of government is desirable but a certain minimum is essential for good citizenship. The primary necessities and benefits of government should be understood. Government makes possible private property, the development of stable institutions, and the freedom of each to give his attention to chosen phases of industrial, artistic and social development. Each individual should realize something of the probable result of the breakdown of government; anarchy and degeneracy in all phases of social life is practically inevitable. Further, there should be a realization of what is possible under better forms of government than have, at present, been developed; probably many do not realize what actual advantages are, at present, secured through the paying of taxes. We are likely to take our public schools, roads, sewers, streets, state institutions, etc., as matters of course without realizing their essential connection with government. Civil government has in it almost unlimited possibilities for good; what can be had in the way of public parks, forests, play grounds, wild life preserves, transportation systems, hygienic measures, etc., probably is not known. These varied possibilities should be in the minds of all citizens as something to be striven for through government. Also there should be a definite knowledge of the present defects and limitations of government and of what happens when unprincipled men get into governmental control. Such knowledge is sure to come to the citizen in his contacts with life and should be given him in the best possible setting and with the suggestion that such conditions can be overcome. 

(3). The Development of the Feeling Side of Civic 

Sentiments.

But knowledge alone never is sufficient to develop a sentiment. There is no sentiment until there is an organi-

Individual-Group Sentiments 

345 zation of the emotion-instinct patterns from which it de- The permanent attitude of the citizen 

How develop the af. fective side of civic sentiments? sub- rives its power. should involve both respect and love for his government. 

The sentiment of respect involves considerable emphasis on mission and admiration. Admiration frequently is analyzed as a compound emotion made up of wonder and negative selffeeling. These two component elements need to be developed at the same time before the result will be the emotion of admiration. It is not difficult to develop wonder; contact with seals, uniforms, symbols, of government develop it readily. The feeling of subjection or submission is not developed so readily. Wonder on the part of the more intelligent citizen may be stirred by secret service agencies, naval reviews, reclamation of desert regions, etc. But this tends to develop from a realization of the power of government as compared to that of the individual; also a consideration of the continuous nature of government as compared to the length of individual life. 

Admiration develops into awe by the addition of the element of fear. While a pronounced development of awe may be to the detriment of free citizenship a touch of this feeling attitude toward government is not out of place. The best type of fear is that the government fail to bear its responsibilities and live up to its possibilities; this type of fear may be developed by a revelation of what has happened and is happening under different governments. But the element most needed in a friendly sentiment is tenderness. 

Considerable discussion has been, and could be, given to the question as to whether tenderness is developed best by benefits received or benefits given. Certainly benefits given have a part to play as well as benefits reecived; the giving always should be done in a generous spirit. Every citizen should be brought to recognize that he is receiving

Social Psychology benefits and that he is receiving them on account of the existence of government. Also we must interpret government as in need of the protection and benefits which the individual citizen can confer; the idea that each citizen is a part of the government gives support to this. Also the idea of defects in government which can be remedied by individual interest and effort calls forth the protective impulse which is basic to tenderness. Any actual contribution given by the individual to government makes not only an economic but an emotional investment which extends the protective care of self over government and hence develops a type of protective tenderness; the reader can think of numerous forms of desirable governmental service. It is of special importance that the individual develop an attachment to a local and real government rather than to a more distant and rather imaginary government; this local attachment must tend to make the local government more idealistic. Special national days should be utilized to greater advantage than is now the case in arousing a proper feeling attitude on the part of the citizen towards the different units of his government. 

(4) The Development of the Conative Phase of Civic 

Sentiments.

Also a sentiment binding an individual to an institution is not secure until there is a development of its conative side. Such development consists in the registering and 

How develop the loy. maintaining of a series of minor alty needed in civic and major decisions; what is true of civic sentiments is true as well sentiments? of any other form of individualgroup alignments. Such decisions as cause the individual to participate in the rights and duties of suffrage or to report correctly the value of invisible property owned, may

Individual-Group Sentiments 

347 in a way be considered minor decisions but they aid greatly in binding the individual to his government. An important major decision is that I as an individual will support the will of the majority. Such a decision need not interfere with the exercise occasionally of the right of conscientious objection; certainly the mores, upon which conscience is based, as to its content, represent the conclusion of the largest possible social group. Another important decision is that I will either obey the law or aid in bringing about its reformation by constitutional methods; there can be no resort to violent, direct methods on the part of the intelligent loyal citizen. The decision to accept the will of the majority certainly should involve a courteous, willing cooperation with those who hold positions of governmental authority. Certainly the loyal citizen should support the officers of the law in the exercise of their duty; the traffic official is not the enemy but the friend of those who value their lives while traveling the highway. Many otherwise good citizens are very lax in reporting infringements of the law or in assisting in the detection of law breakers; this indicates a lack of value judgments and a confusion between minor individual loyalties and major institutional loyalties. Even the individual who is honest in his objection to the obeying of a particular law may conform obediently to the government's regulations for such cases; Gandhi submitted calmy to arrest and insisted that his prison sentence be adequate to the extent of his opposition to the British Government. The cases are few in which the citizen cannot find definite ways of cooperating with the present administration which represents the background government; the decision that a law is unsatisfactory or unwise does not constitute a ground for conscientious objection to it. The conscientious objector must be unable to obey the law because of its conflict with a higher moral law; certainly the issue should be rather

Social Psychology plain before the individual determines that it is a situation for conscientious objection. The pledge of citizenship should involve cooperation with the present representatives of government wherever such is possible. 

IV. Illustrations of Individual-Group Alignments in the Educational and Social Realms. 

We consider here: (1) something of the extent of social groupings; (2) advantages and dangers of purely social loyalties. 

1. The Extensive and Varied Nature of Social Groupings. 

At the beginning of this chapter we mentioned something of the scope of individual-group alignments possible and actual. Even in the educational and general social realms 

What is the extent of the scope of such alignments is so social loyalties? extended and varied as to make treatment of each of them in detail impossible. The student in the larger educational institution feels the pull of loyalties in various directions. 

We have come to think of so many life experienecs as being definitely educative as to make the student's choice among educational groupings rather difficult. To what extent shall he go in for dramatics, athletics, forensics, oratory, special departmental clubs, church work, philanthropic work, literary work, etc., becomes a real problem. Many such adjustments are not intended to be permanent and hence never come to involve completely formed sentiments. 

If one group alignment is chosen as a permanent, life alignment, the building of a sentiment to it becomes a matter of importance. In general the principles and methods involved in such an alignment are the same as those discussed concerning the civic alignment. We shall not discuss these

Individual-Group Sentiments 

349 further. However, there are certain social groups which involve special problems and to these we now turn our attention. 

2. Advantages and Dangers of Individual Loyalties to the Purely Social Group. 

By a purely social group we are thinking of a selected group the formation of which is determined largely for purposes of mutual association and contact; some external 

What are the values interest or activity may be menof the purely social tioned but seems not essential to group? the organization. Such social clubs as fraternities and sororities are frequent in the higher educational institutions and take varied forms in the general social order; their activities may stress anything from golf to fancy work but usually involve a selected group of members and a greater or less amount of exclusiveness and secrecy. Some such associations emphasize the feature of secrecy and involve a great deal of mystery and ritualism. Association with such group may lead to the augmenting of a worthy form of happiness on the part of the individual; individuals differ greatly as to their feeling of need for this type of association. Comradeship in a select group frequently leads to an augmented enjoyment of the special interests of that group; usually there is some extension of knowledge, though the club be purely social. Under favorable conditions of club life the social environment of the individual may come to be more stimulating and wholesome. Possibly the club member will develop a pride in some worthy endeavor. 

Further, the club may promote among its members a tendency to excel along a chosen line. Or the fact of club membership may stimulate the individual to excel in some form of social endeavor.

Social Psychology 

There are certain dangers involved in the purely social group or at least in the functioning of such group under certain conditions. The needs of friendship may be met fully by such group relations and the individual feel no What are the dangers need for asserting friendly relaof the purely social tions outside the group; on the other hand there is the possibility group? that the close friendship of the group so increase the evaluation of friendship that the individual will be more responsive to friendly advances. It is easier to maintain loyalty to a small than to a large group; it is easier to secure the knowledge needed and to appreciate the values of the small group. There is a blindness in personal friendship which makes it easy to hold to the small social group where moral character is sacrificed by so doing; even religious fraternal groups have found it easy to use wrong means in order to promote the end of group endeavor. Many things have been done which the group assert to be "for the glory of God" but which seem to the onlooker to detract from God's glory; reports come of immoral conditions existing in ancient monasteries and in modern fraternity houses. There is also a danger of developing group antagonism and hence social disruption; the selection of rather exclusive social groups must constitute some danger to belief in the principle of democracy. The developed anti-fraternal group may be more narrow and bigoted than the fraternal group but the organized group must bear the responsibility for calling forth the antagonism of the anti-group. Further, there is grave danger that the principles of a secret organization be misunderstood or misrepresented and hence that there develop various forms of suspicion and antagonism; the fact that the constitution of the organization is secret makes it impossible for the members to inform the general public of their exact purposes and hence to counteract any misrepre-

Individual-Group Sentiments 

351 sentation. Where the organization is secret, the age of the members is a matter of considerable concern; for the most part the secret fraternity has been condemned in the high school while it has established itself rather generally in the college and university. 

3. General Principles which Should Govern the Organization of the Purely Social Group. 

What principles should govern purely social groups?

Certain principles should guide the organization and maintenance of the purely social group. It seems reasonable for society to demand that a special social group perform a special social service. 

Democracy can demand that, if a special social group be sanctioned, out from the organization of that group there develop a general social good. The nature of such general good need not be restricted unduly; if only the character of the members be improved, then an important social contribution has been made. Or if in any way the general public can be brought to realize the value of friendship and hence that friendships come to exist between larger units in the social order, an important social contribution has been made. 

Further, in behalf of social peace and cooperation it is highly desirable that the secret organization do not parade certain aspects of its life in the eye of the general public. 

A private organization certainly should keep its affairs, for the most part, in private and not arouse a jealous selfassertiveness on the part of others by frequent public parades with uniforms and banners. The main social disturbance may be caused by those who in reality desire to be members of the organization but the omission of the public parade would at least be desirable socially; let courtship be carried on in private rather than under the eye of the general public. Perhaps the most important

Social Psychology principle is that the interests of the special social group must in all instances be subordinated to the general social good. This must be recognized distinctly in theory and applied consistently in practice; many times it is apt to be conceded in theory but in practice to be but little heeded. 

A thoroughgoing recognition of this principle is bound to go a long way toward preventing much of the clash and discord produced in the social order by antagonistic secret organizations. Let us remember that the good of the part can be maintained only by maintaining the good of the whole.
