CHAPTER XIII Friendly Sentiments Existing Between Social Groups. 

In previous chapters we spoke of individual sentiments; these related individuals, apart from or within groups, and individuals to groups. In this chapter we speak of sentiments relating groups to each other. As you read you will do well to keep such questions as the following in mind. 

Can social groups form sentiments among themselves? 

How do group relationships compare to individual relations? Are group reactions prevailingly feeling? Can the impulses and emotions of groups be sublimated? What at present is being done to develop better group relationships? 

I. Comparison of the Attitudes of Groups to Individual 

Sentiments.

We consider here: (1) the actuality of group mind and group sentiments; (2) the comparison of group and individual sentiments. 

1. The Similarity of Inter-Group Attachments to Other Types of Sentiments. 

It may seem at first that there is no possibility of group sentiments because of the lack of unity in group conscious- 

Can groups mainness. McDougall quotes Barker as follows: "There is no group tain sentiments? mind existing apart from the minds of the members of the group; the group-mind only exists in the minds of its members. But nevertheless it exists". (B90:25). Also Barker says further, "In a word group- 355

Social Psychology persons are, real persons; and just because they are so, and possess such attributes of persons as will and character, they cannot have been made by the State". Following this quotation McDougall adds, "I am not alone, then, in postulating the reality of the group mind". (B90:26). We sometimes think of our nation as having decided once for all an issue, such as the slavery issue. We at times speak of the mind of the nation as we do of that of an individual. 

Again we may speak of the heart and soul or feeling life of the nation or other such group. We are not contending for any particular conception of group mind. We are asserting merely that groups develop attitudes which have about them all the essential characteristics of sentiments. 

These attitudes are likely to pass from generation to generation as a part of the social heredity and thus become dominant major premises in the minds of each generation. It is in this sense that the attitudes or tendencies belong to the group and not to any particular individuals or generation of individuals. It always is possible for any individual to examine such products of social heredity and to set his will against them. But to resist such tendencies successfully is very difficult and not to be expected on the part of the majority of any one generation; only a gradual breaking away from any such tradition is all that we have reason to expect. Through its institutional and traditional life a group maintains the essentials of a personality and hence develops the normal personal relations. 

So as to type, group sentiments are in accord with individual sentiments; the terms which express the prevailing 

What types of group sentiments exist? motivation of the sentiment are love, hate, and regard. To constitute a permanent group there must be a considerable degree of unity among its members; the crowd or mob which gathers by accident or with a temporary purpose is not sufficiently stable to constitute a group

in the sense we are using the word. Ellwood says, "The individuals of a group must form a stable environment with reference to each. They must be able to count on the right conduct of their fellow members under all circumstances, or the group will tend to disintergrate". (40:105). Because of this necessary unity there comes to be built up prevailing group attitudes which have in them all the characteristics of sentiments. Friendly relations come to be traditional between certain groups. Nations, business organizations, educational institutions, etc., may come to have alignments of a friendly type. Or, on the other hand, hostile relations may persist between groups for long periods of time. Williams says, "If the party organization is to make profits, if it is to win offices and large campaign contributions, it must beat the rival party in the struggle for political control. Consequently the essential aim of a party organization is to defeat the opposing organization". (141:196).

Possibly a particular party was not organized with the conscious purpose of defeating an opponent-for example it may have been the first of the parties organized—but certainly where party contests are involved each party must win an occasional victory in order to maintain its life. If such parties are somewhat removed from each other so as to permit misunderstandings and deceptive propaganda, rivalry may develop readily into real hostility and hatred. 

Especially is this true if vital interests are at stake between the contending parties; differences in race, occupation and religion, also make misunderstandings and propaganda easier and hence augment the tendency toward hatred. Besides the stable group attitudes of friendship and hatred, groups may hold each other in high regard. One faculty or labor union may have greater or less respect for another faculty or labor organization. Certain business concerns are fully recognized and in good standing with all their associates; certain others have but recently organized, per-

Social Psychology haps are attempting the application of principles not generally approved, and hence receive but scant respect from the older and better established organizations. What is true of such groups holds as well for all types of social groups. The conclusion is that groups can maintain the ordinary types of sentiments. 

2. Variations Involved in the Constituent Elements of Inter-Group as Compared to Personal Sentiments. 

We consider here the place in inter-group sentiments: (1) of understanding or cognition; (2) of group feeling; (3) of group conation in the form of volition or assertion. (1). The Demand of the Inter-Group Sentiment for a 

Large Measure of Cognition. 

There are several needs for cognition in inter-group relations. These will be developed in suceeding paragraphs. 

The object of a group sentiment is likely to be understood 

Why so difficult to very imperfectly. The difficulties understand a group? are increased if the group toward which the attitude is held, be of a different race and be living at a considerable distance. 

McDougall concludes that racial tendencies produce pronounced differences. He says, "And, even in intellectual quality, there appear to be not only differences of degree, but also differences of kind, inexplicable save as racial differences. The logical deductive tendencies of the French intellect and the impirical inductive tendencies of the English, seem to be rooted in race; though here of course tradition accumulates and accentuates such differences from generation to generation". (B90:156). Also it is more difficult to interpret a large organization than it is the major tendencies of an individual; it should be kept in mind that the basic tendencies of the individual life are not discerned

easily. These difficulties are further increased when it is a group trying to understand another group. It is difficult to separate the essential from the accidental in group life; it is hard to detect basic human likenesses on the part of those of a different race and tradition, when they eat different food and dress so differently from us. This is true especially if our contacts are limited and hence we meet only a few of the group in question. Further, there always are some in every group who misrepresent its central aim; they may disagree with the aim or they may interpret it on a low or crude level. Also, many who lack contact with the group are forced to take their interpretations of it second hand. This may lead readily to misunderstandings and false interpretations; the original interpreter of the group may be prejudiced toward it or his interpretations may be misunderstood or misapplied. A leader may have selfish reasons for opposing a group or he may fear the aim of a group because he has misunderstaood that aim. 

Of course, there are certain leaders of different groups whose essential aims do in reality conflict; the group that defends itself by militaristic methods is apt to be of a very different kind from the one that believes in non-violence and the assertion of soul force. It is well to keep in mind that the understanding of a group by a group is an undertaking of considerable difficulty.

Also the necessity of developing and maintaining friendly relations between groups is not apparent to every one. It 

Why hard to under. is easy to assume that we can just stand the need of as well live as a little friendly group and remain hostile to all outgroup friendships? side. Just as Jacob and Laban thought they could end their difficulties by drawing a line between them that neither would cross, so the Greeks thought of the barbarians and the Jews of the Gentiles. 

Today it is easy for many citizens of America to believe

Social Psychology that this nation can live her life apart from all other nations. The danger of an hostile act against a group is not brought home to an individual because the retaliation is not as immediate and personal as though the act were against an individual. The resultant group strife which comes from a multitude of such acts may be destructive in a marked way of the interests of the individual but his own part in bringing about the effect may not be noted. Large bodies move slowly and hence individual differences may be operative for a considerable time before definite effects are made on group attitudes. So the individual who does but a limited amount of thinking in the abstract may find it very difficult to understand why he should be concerned with the promotion of inter-group friendships. 

Again it is difficult to trace in mind the cause and effect relations as they operate between large groups. An individual is apt to make it plain that his reaction is in response to the action of another individual. But when a 

Why hard to under. group action occurs it is a matter stand group of interpretation as to what relation it has to previous group action. and reaction?

One of the difficulties arises from action the fact that group activities frequently are based upon feeling rather than knowledge: Group feeling consciousness is very sporadic and uncertain in its expression. As is the case with the mob, so is that to some extent with the general public; a wave of public sympathy may be followed quickly by one of resentment and anger. Such expressions, varying as they do in their nature, make it very difficult to interpret the mind of the public. Thus it is very difficult to predict a definite tendency in the life of a group. Political landslides, migration movements, crime waves, religious revivals, are hard to explain after they have occurred and practically impossible to predict. Any such movement is too complex and big to be grasped as a whole. Hocking,

What should be taught about group friendships? in speaking of realizing the great war, says, "the war is not a thing that can be seen; it must be thought. And if physical vision hinders or preoccupies the free flight of thought it may be the very thing that prevents the realization of the war". (67:49). Just as the cause and effect relations of the great war are difficult to comprehend, so also is any tendency that finds expression in a large group. 

Because of the greater need for cognition in group friendships we need to give special emphasis to teaching. A definite attempt should be made to understand the central aim of a group. The aim of a group like that of an individual ought to receive a favorable interpretation. 

It is best to try to see a definite interest in, and quest for, truth and right on the part of different religions, labor labor organizations, philosophies, schemes of education, etc. It is possible to stress the cooperative features of group relations as well as the antagonistic tendencies. It is possible to think of another nation as furnishing a basis for the extension of our trade, as supplementing our labor in various ways, or as stimulating and arousing ideas on our part. Many of our Christian missionaries are coming to realize that their first task is not to tear down heathen idols but rather to build upon the religious foundations which crude heathendom has laid. 

Our interpretation of institutional aims should be as favor-· able as we can make them; such a favorable interpretation should be taught to our children. Through channels of social heredity it is easy to build up friendships or hatreds on the part of our children for various religious, racial, civic and social groups. Also we should teach the necessity of developing group friendships. The terrible destructiveness of industrial strikes, racial hatreds, religious antagonisms and national wars should be borne home to

Social Psychology the consciousness of every group. But group attitudes must not be separated from individual responsibilities. 

This implies that the individual must learn to relate his conduct in an intelligent way to that of the group. Many good citizens are not likely to realize that they can and should have a definite part in the development of intergroup friendships. But in fact, there are few members of any important group who are not in a position to influence the attitude of that group or to help interpret the motives of their own or other groups. There is positive need of a more favorable press attitude towards certain nations, races and religions. In one of our cities a group of Negroes have considered it worth while to organize in order to bring pressure to bear upon the papers of that city to publish worthy acts of Negroes in addition to their criminal acts. 

Organizations with an economic, religious or national bias may do great injury by augmenting group antagonisms; the effects of cartoons, motion pictures and the press and theatre in this regard are very powerful. Secret organizations especially should take great pains to make it plain that their social attitude is friendly. The factors which make for and against group friendships must be understood more thoroughly. Toward the latter part of the chapter we speak of certain movements at present operative towards the development of better international relations. 

(2). The Nature of Group Feeling and Its Place in the Group Sentiment. 

We consider next the place of feeling in the group sentiment. Ross says, "To unify men touch the chord of feeling". (115:130). Gault says, "The solidarity that is of

What place has feeling in group sentiments? greatest interest in our connection is the sense of unity or feeling of belonging-together that makes every member of a group seem to himself to be kin to every other member:-". (51:14). Ellwood says, "Some feelings draw individuals together; others pull them apart, as it were. Thus feelings of antipathy and hatred, reenforcing instincts of conflict and avoidance, tend to dissolve social bonds. Feelings of love and sympathy, on the other hand, reenforcing altruistic impulses, greatly strengthen social bonds". (40:103). Such quotations might be extended greatly as it seems to be a general conviction that emotion and impulse have much to do with the unity of groups. It is just as true that feelings dominate in group relations, as it is that they are a major factor in producing the unity of a particular group. A certain feeling attitude towards another group or groups is implied in membership in a particular group; social tradition has established a certain feeling attitude which the individual absorbs with his membership into the group. Certain sections of our country have definite feeling attitudes toward certain races; one seems hardly a loyal member of that particular section without hating a particular race. To become a member of a particular school, labor union, religious group, medical group, nation, means to adopt a feeling of opposition to certain groups. Manifest feeling attitudes on the part of the majority may be taken as the feeling attitude of the group. The trend of a nation's feeling attitude may be noted in its press or in the waves of anger, fear or tenderness which sweep over it. If the feeling is hostile towards a certain group then destructive propaganda will be believed readily concerning that group. There is little to indicate that particular feeling attitudes are inherent in certain race relations; there is no reason for thinking that one race naturally hates another. Gandhi says, “Any one

Social Psychology who is fairly on the road to renunciation will not need to be told that ahimsa (love), not himsa (hate) rules man, I was almost about to say, the world". (BB50:951). Feeling relationships are developed by environmental conditions and maintained mainly through social heredity; the expression of the feeling attitude by parents and teachers causes it to develop on the part of the young. It is desirable to keep in mind: (1) that feeling is an important factor in group relations; (2) that feeling attitudes are acquired by groups and can be altered. 

How potent is group feeling in producing group sentiments?

Group feeling is a potent factor in group relations and deserves on that account our most careful consideration. 

Group antagonism may develop such results as strikes and labor disturbances, party frictions in all types of parties, racial propaganda and national wars. Also, such group feeling may influence greatly the cognitive tendencies of groups; strong group feeling may be a definite interference with clear individual thinking. Racial or other forms of group hatred blind the individual to the good points of the group hated; group love also is blind in an optimistic way kindred to individual love. Because of such predominance of group feeling, its influence over the thinking and deciding of the individual is pronounced. The basic principles of the individual's thinking come to conform to the prevailing tendencies of group feeling. The feeling of race superiority makes it easy for the individual to justify in his thinking an injustice to a supposedly inferior race. Because of prevailing group feeling it has been easy for individuals to interpret the Bible in a way favorable to polygamy, slavery, war, etc. 

It is comparatively easy to raise up a generation of children either to favor or to oppose any particular institution; we can teach them to think of war as evil in the same way that we were taught to think of slavery or the saloon. Let group feeling become intense enough and sufficient indi-

viduals of the group will be ready for group assertion in order to satisfy the impulse of the feeling. Such assertion may take the form of a strike, rioting, war or tenderness may prevail and in response to group sympathy there may be expression in gifts, social service or cooperative action. 

If the individual becomes a real part of any group he is bound to be influenced greatly by group attitudes along the lines of his thinking and deciding. 

How cause group feel- ing to promote friendly sentiments? sources.

Let us consider how the power of group feeling may be turned towards the development of friendly group sentiments. One of the first essentials of right group feeling is to establish a proper cognitive basis for its development. We should keep in mind that the decisions of the individual or group are determined by the selection of particular ideas; multitudes of ideas crowd into consciousness and particular ones are sanctioned. Dewey says, "Thoughts sprout and vegetate; ideas proliferate. They come from deep unconscious 'I think' is a statement about voluntary action.

Some suggestion surges from the unknown. Our active body of habits appropriates it. The suggestion then becomes an assertion. It no longer merely comes to us. It is accepted and uttered by us". (B34:314). A group may impress upon its membership ideas by first arousing the feeling responses and then offering the positive suggestion. 

So it is important that ideas favorable to right group feelings prevail in the public mind. There should be a general distribution of knowledge concerning the better qualities and traits of groups with which wrong relations are possible. 

It is practically impossible to develop admiration or gratitude towards a group without a knowledge of their better traits. One of the best ways to present the knowledge of desirable traits is to put favored individuals of the despised race or group into positions of prestige. Any person who

Social Psychology has heard Booker T. Washington speak or a group of fine 

Negro voices sing must find it difficult to keep on thinking of the Negro as belonging to a distinctly lower race; the persistent assertion of the race hater that these Negroes are dominated by white blood shows the potency of such presentation. Tenderness, which is at the basis of friendship, develops at least in part from the knowledge of benefits received. On account of this, it is important to think of all groups as contributing in a positive way to the welfare of our common civilization. It is just as important for the development of tenderness, to know that other groups need our ministrations, as to realize that we are receiving assistance from them. But the thought of certain groups as "dogs", "brutes", "yellow perils", "Huns". "Wops", "Yaps", or "Niggers", tends to prevent the feeling of respect which is necessary if we are to have friendships towards all races; H. G. Wells said recently that race hatred is the most evil of all social influences. Specific knowledge of the needs of others is desirable as it aids in arousing our altruistic impulses and leads the way to the development of friendly attitudes. It is highly important that a constant stream of right ideas be brought to the attention of the general public. 

(3). The Nature of Friendly Forms of Group Conation. 

Conation in group friendship means group decision in favor of another group. Group feeling usually has a potent influence on group decision or volition. In an organized group such as a nation, a landslide of votes may be taken as an indication of the will of the group; it must be 

How develop group conation favorable to inter-group friend- ship? understood that a group, just as an individual, can later reverse its decision. 

Also a group decision may be indi-

cated by a fixed attitude of press or platform; we assume that we have settled the saloon and slavery questions but all are not sure that we have settled the question of the league of nations. The decisions expressed by large groups are likely to be rather thoroughgoing and primitive in their nature; they are not likely to be complicated or marked by reservations or restrictions. Many times we are surprised that individuals with a high level of intelligence find expression as a group in such primitive acts as lynching. In case of lynching and panic it seems almost that the ideas and principles of the individual members of the group go for nothing. Even the action of an individual dominated by the spirit of a group, but for the time being acting alone, may be very different than if he had no connection with the group. Eddy mentions a Christian leader who says, "I admit that war is unchristian, but when war comes, I put my Christianity in my pocket and go out and fight". (AA38:25). It is not advisable to put dependence upon moral ideals as directors of group action. Our most assured dependence must be placed on the moralization or sublimation of group feeling and impulse; as we suggested above, numerous right ideas should be available for the use of right impulses when such can be brought to prevail. 

The method of Jesus seems to be that of positive suggestion of right principles rather than extended argument concerning the rightness of major premises. In dealing with groups the demand is for moral rather than for intellectual education; we are not assuming that the contrast here is in any sense thoroughgoing. But right group feeling is a more effective basis for their decisions because feeling and impulse are the main types of immediate group motivation. The greater part of the remainder of this chapter will be devoted to this educational problem.

Social Psychology II. The Development of Friendly Group Sentiments. 

We consider here: (1) a statement of principles involved; (2) a program for sublimating group impulses; (3) present day movements making for better group relations. 

1. Some Principles Relating to the Development of Inter-Group Sentiments. 

Why assume group sentiments to be primitive?

The first principle has been suggested already but perhaps should be given a more definite statement. Definite allowance should be made for the primitive nature of group sentiments. It is only a reasonable caution to assume that group impulses and emotions will function on a crude or primitive level. Original human nature as such does not prompt the individual along lines of modern civilization but, in so far as its promptings are definite at all, along very primitive lines; this must not be taken to imply a racial fatalism to live on low levels. Certainly we should be glad to find any expression of group life more developed than we expected to find it, but never is it safe to count upon such high level types of expression. Much of our educational emphasis up-to-date has been cognitive and very little of it directed primarily to the moralization of our feeling nature. Our main educational aim has centered about the acquisition of knowledge and the proper voluntary use of it. Usually we have been content to emphasize knowledge, wisdom and self-control. We have, on the other hand, distinct evidence of the ready response of the public to the narrow and partisan as well as to the vulgar and cruel. It is very easy indeed to arouse racial animosities and hatreds. Recently in the United States we saw what a tremendous at least temporary-following an organization can secure that is based squarely on racial and religious intolerance. Re-

ligious, political and industrial discussions tend to degenerate to the most primitive and crude levels. The methods of the Spanish inquisition no longer are available for dealing with religious opposition but as close an approach to such methods as possible seem to be a frequent group tendency; every one perhaps has in mind the utter barbarity of group actions during the World War. The harshness even of those who came to the new country for religious liberty is indicated by Thomas Jefferson who says, "Several acts of the Virginia Assembly, of 1659, 1662, and 1693 had made it penal in parents to refuse to have their children baptised; had prohibited the ‘unlawful' assembling of Quakers; had made it penal for any master of a vessel to bring a Quaker into the state; and ordered those already there, and such as should come hereafter, to be imprisoned until they should abjure the country-provided a milder penalty for the first and second return, but death for the third". (B131:15). Such actions as these must be toned down in our day but it seems evident that many groups still have the spirit which made such acts possible. Because apparently it is a tendency of groups to act on primitive levels, it is well to expect such expression in order to prepare to alter it. 

Why assume group expressions affective in nature?

Further, it is well to assume that group decision and action develop from the immediate background of group feeling; this implies that group thought is not the most important factor leading to group expression. 

Such assumption should in no wise detract from our effort to promote a higher level of knowledge among the members of the group. Nor should it minimize our insistence upon self-control based upon knowledge. 

Every advance made along the lines of knowledge and selfcontrol is for the good of all concerned. But always is there danger that we assume an extent of knowledge lead-

Social Psychology ing to self-control in excess of that actually possessed by the group. There were many who thought that our civilization had advanced to such a place of wisdom and selfmastery as to make the development of such barbarism as we witnessed in the World War utterly impossible. We need not regret any effort that has been made to improve our people in knowledge and its proper application. What we need is care not to build up a false system of dependence upon logical reasoning and judgment to give right direction to group action. For as a matter of fact group decisions are not made on this basis. Dresser says, "After a period of dominancy of the old faculty psychology, with its over-emphasis on the intellect, psychology has been steadily confirming the primacy of the will or love". (36: 

441). We understand this to mean that the will is motivated more by the feelings and impulses connected with the sentiments than by the implications of reason. The need of emphasis on feeling, as a dominator of decision, is much more as regards groups than as regards individuals. Human expression of emotion is one of the most effective stimuli for the arousal of that emotion on the part of others; this is true especially if all the individuals involved belong to the same crowd. We have been accustomed to apply this thought of group influence only to crowds or groups who are in actual physical contact. But due to the development of ways of ready contact over wide areas in practically an instant of time, the actual physical nearness of groups is not an item of such importance as formerly was the case. 

Any one who will notice how readily "good Christian people" engage in lynching, carry filthy stories in their minds, sanction racial hatred, promote the most primitive of church quarrels, in spite of their excellent principles and self-control, will realize that the determiners for such conduct must not be cognitive. Even in the face of the terrible disaster which the recent war has visited upon our

civilization, it is folly to assume that even this generation has learned the futility and suicidal nature of war, to an extend sufficient to make our civilization safe from the ravages of this monster; the suffering and degradation produced by war tend more to produce, than to stop war. 

Enlightened self-interest will not stop war because group actions are not motivated in that way. Nor are we safe in assuming that people who understand Christian principles and who give their intellectual assent to them will not harbor jealousy, malice and hatred toward their fellows.

We are foolish to assume that a high civic office always raises a man above expressions of jealousy and primitive selfishness. Indeed we have little ground for assuming that the man who has a fair understanding of the principle of honesty and applies it in four cases will apply it also in the fifth; the fifth case may be the only one of the group in which he has a real temptation to be dishonest. What applies to the individual applies much more definitely to the group. Group decisions are far more likely to be feeling decisions. On this account the only safe procedure is to assume that groups will make their decisions prevailingly on the basis of feeling. This recognition should prompt us to give more definite attention to the endeavor of promoting right feeling attitudes among groups. The impulse itself must be so cultivated as to prompt right group action without regard to cognition and self-control. 

2. The Need for Sublimating the Emotions and Impulses Connected with Group Sentiments.

We consider here: (1) the need for the sublimation of impulses in connection with group relations: (2) the group impulses in special need of sublimation.

Social Psychology (1). The Social Need for the Sublimation of Group 

Impulses.

Why sublimate group impulses?

We have pointed out that feeling is likely to be a very potent element in group expression and further, that such group expressions tend to be very primitive in their nature. 

For this reason the moralization or socialization of a group of people must be considered one of the most important elements of their education. Bogardus points out that, "Socialization does not imply an objective social control so much as socialized self-control. A person achieves habits of acting for the welfare of others, and comes to occupy a position inclusive of social control. 

Personality moves up from a lowly position under social discipline, law and control, to a rank far above". (A18: 

236). To indicate such automatic socialized reaction, the term sublimation is coming into rather common use. The 

Freudian writers frequently connect the term with the sex impulse (see A20:328, B49:118) but there is no essential reason for such reference unless sex is supposed to be basic to all impulses. Dresser says that any form of conflict and unhappiness indicates a life urge which needs a creative form of expression. (36:343). It is rather evident that sublimation can apply to any form of human instinctive or emotional tendency. We spoke on page 49 of the perceptual and motor implications of sublimation and of its relation to self-control. If life can be given a satisfactory direction by sublimated impulses it has that much less need for voluntary self-control. As we indicated in Chapter VI, the basic human tendencies are very susceptible to modification. Hence they may be sublimated-moralized, socialized, humanized, cultured-both as to the perception which arouses them and as to the forms of expression they take. It is especially important that every effort be put

forth to sublimate group impulses. In their subhuman form they are a constant menace to the social order and frequently defy all efforts at voluntary control. Their proper sublimation constitutes the best guarantee of stability and cooperation within the social order. 

(2). The Group Impulses in Special Need of 

Sublimation.

It is highly desirable that every impulse of life be in accord with the demands of moral character and social good. But the degenerate expression of certain group im- 

How sublimate the pulses is so destructive to the social order that their sublimation fighting impulse? is of special significance. Certainly no one needs to be told that the human fighting impulse needs to be given a different turn. We do not assume that anger in distinction to fear, jealousy or greed, is the outstanding cause of war, but are speaking of the sublimation of all tendencies which lead to fighting. Page in speaking of the World War says, "it cost 26 million lives, 337 billion dollars, the moral deterioration of whole nations, spiritual tragedies beyond computation, and the sowing of the seeds of future wars". (A97:50). It is not necessary to have an especially active imagination to realize keenly that we must have a moral substitute for war. The national fighting impulse should be aroused only when the interests of all nations and races are in danger. As nations we must learn to fight together against our common enemies rather than expressing our energy in the slaughter each of the other. 

We should fight for the common mastery of land, water and air transportation, for the subjecting of the forces of steam, electricity, heredity, etc., to our control, and for the destruction of ignorance, superstition, prejudice, bigotry, and all such forces that are detrimental to the whole race.

Social Psychology 

Certainly our expression of the fighting impulse must be along such lines as not to injure ourselves along with the enemy. An increasing number of people recognize the truth of what Page suggests when he says that war "is self-defeating in its very nature". (A97:79). The present action of the civilized nations in condemning and outlawing it indicates that its evil nature as an institution is being recognized. We must learn ways of defense and offense which do not cripple and destroy both friend and enemy alike as do military methods. We need to take more seriously the suggestion that the social and spiritual forces of the world are its strongest forces and if harnessed and applied will win human victories without destruction to the character of the fighter and without social deterioration. 

In home and school, children should be trained to become angry only when major character and group interests are involved and to express their anger in constructive measures for the elimination of the wrong and the furtherance of the social good; the social good also involves the individual and character good. We are convinced that the sublimation of the fighting impulse will serve the race better than an ascetic attempt to eliminate it or a Stoical attempt to hold it rigidly subjected to self-control; impulses must be directed so as to make self-control easier and not harder. 

The innate tendency which leads to the protection of self or that which in any way is associated with self-as my family, nation, philosophy, civilization-needs sublimation 

How sublimate the protective impulse? on both its perceptual and motor aspects. We need to come to fear that which is actually dangerous and to express that fear in a way which actually makes for the protection of the interest involved. Many times we fear that which does not exist demons, ghosts, witchesor that, which for the individual who fears, is merely

verbal; much of the fear of higher criticism, bolshevism, evolution, etc., is for the most part a fear of words. Mc- 

Dougall speaks of "that terrible threat under which the people of Europe are even now, in spite of the League of Nations, suffering accutely, the threat of attack from the air.-". (E90:233). Certainly we should strive to unify our social order to the place where one group of people need not fear that their neighbors suddenly will attack and destroy them. Our social fear rather should be of disease, degenerate heredity, international or inter-racial commission of offenses, or of ignorance, superstition or such foes. Just as we need to give up imaginary and verbal fears, so we need to add the fear of certain actual dangers which now exist in a form which are not recognized. War is more to be feared than the hostility of a neighboring nation. The microbe is much more dangerous than the lion. It is hard to fear that which does not appeal to primitive impulses; the automobile is killing twenty to twenty-five thousand people a year in the United States yet we have developed little fear of it. Further, the expression of fear in a primitive form may do injury rather than social good; fear may lead to trembling, fainting, panic, and blind retaliation leading to social antagonism and further fear. Thus in a civilized social order primitive fear defeats its own purpose. This does not imply that we should have no fear or have it and keep it under rigid control constantly. We must learn to express our fear in right ways, such as social action which gives relatively permanent relief from the danger. Fear should prompt the diffusion of education, morality, hygienic living, friendliness and kindness, or kindred measures which do in reality relieve us from the cause of our fear. Another protective tendency of the first order is disgust or repulsion. This impulse seems to protect mainly against wrong-crude, animal like -forms of food and tactual contact, as with wet slimy

Social Psychology objects. This form of defense, even in its primitive expressions, is for the most part good. But it may do harm if it prevents chemical experimenting with nauseous gases or surgical work involving contact with blood. But it may perform a much more important human service than guarding the gateways of taste, smell and touch. We should. come to be repelled by the crude and vulgar in music, literature, drama, the cartoon, etc., and to take such measures as will put out of our environment these demoralizing forces and introduce in their stead moralized types which are individually wholesome and socially good. 

The attachment to the worthy and good must be the counterpart of repulsion from the crude and vulgar; there is need for both a pull and a push to keep life on the upward grade. Also sublimation here means that I come to protect my family, my school, my race, my civilization, my philosophy as well as my body and my finances. We must so train our children that fear and disgust will function to the highest individual and social good. 

How sublimate group tenderness?

Also we assert, contrary to the opinion of some, that the tender emotion or emotions are in need of sublimation; the reader should keep in mind that we are thinking now from the point of view of group relations. Tenderness needs sublimation because it tends to care for the immediate, physical needs of the object, rather than for his future, mental, moral or social needs. The arousal of public sympathy for one man in a cave, for a murdered or kidnaped child or for a particular prisoner, probably on the whole does social harm rather than good; the emphasis is likely to be so distorted as to lead away from social justice rather than towards it. The primitive parent may be satisfied if the child's crying is stopped whereas his more basic and permanent needs-education, moral training, social outlook- which do not cause him to cry may

go uncared for. The wave of public feeling in favor of the criminal may do nothing more than cause his release and possibly encourage his tendency to further crime; sublimated tenderness tends to reform criminal procedure and treatment to the individual and social good. Primitive public tenderness is greatly aroused by the kidnaping of one child; sublimated public tenderness cries out against child labor and conditions of social hygiene which cause hundreds of children to perish. We should come to feel tenderness in response to mental and moral weakness because these indicate very definitely a demand for our protection. Unless the tender emotions are sublimated, the expression of mental and social need is apt to arouse derisive laughter from the crowd. There must come to be a public consciousness which recognizes the more basic types of needs and thus comes to assert its protective care in ways which in reality supply the need; if there really is a white man's burden it must find expression in the tender care of all forms of social weakness for the general social good. In all forms of teaching, emphasis must be placed upon real social need, and ways suggested which in actuality will bring relief; such teaching must finally sublimate the expressions of tenderness on the part of groups. 

The native tendency of gregariousness is another innate tendency definitely in need of sublimation. Primitive gre gariousness means only a tendency to group together in 

How sublimate gregariousness? physical proximity; the musk ox is said to be restless when separated but to show no interest in other members of his species when in a group. Primitive human gregariousness is satisfied to be one of a crowd without forming any personal relationships or friendships with individuals within the crowd. The grouping together of great masses of people may do very little toward the building of a better social order. At its best gregariousness

Social Psychology ought to develop a friendly give and take relationship between all types of human minds. The unity need not be in the way of physical contact but should involve social interest and cooperative efforts. There should develop a readiness to learn from all races and classes and to discover the good in all forms of expression in both the old and new. Gregariousness must make a place for all forms of self-assertion and demand from all proper and right forms of self-submission; there can be no unified social order without moral submission by all and the right of all to friendly assertion. Gregarious self-assertion should take the form of modest and worthy contributions to the good of all and certainly not brutal domination; is there any reason why a strong cannot help a weak nation without assuming civic control over it? Submission by groups to right forms of government and to true ideals will further the interests both of the group and of society in general. 

Sublimated gregariousness seeks contact with humanity and with truth and goodness. Confidence in truth, in goodness and in humanity must be developed; the real infidel is the one who fears to have his present beliefs examined, the real cynic is the one who resists the enlargement of the present realm of truth. The impulse of gregariousness can be made to work against narrow prejudice and dogmatism and to favor open-mindedness and world consciousness. 

Friendly group sentiments need to be built up so as to favor the higher promptings of gregariousness toward a more unified and coordinated social order. 

3. Some Recent Movements Intended to Promote

Better Group Relations. 

In order to give the chapter a somewhat more practical · turn we mention certain present day tendencies intended by their promoters to make for better group relations; the

reader will have to decide for himself whether these movements do in actuality make for more friendly group relations. The list is intended to be suggestive and not complete.

The For some time there has been an attempt to extend the scope of both civic and judicial control over groups. The linking of states under a federal government or the build- What civic and judi- ing of an empire of cooperative cial organization is be- states is along this line. ing attempted?

League of Nations is an attempt to get better relationships between certain outstanding nations; we do not attempt to say to what extent it is altruistic, whether it will destroy the disastrous attempt to balance military power, or how successfully it grants justice to the smaller nations. The Hague 

Court and the World Court are attempts to extend the judiciary privileges to nations; these differ mainly in that the latter has a permanent panel of judges at the disposal of any nations desiring their service. Many are now advocating a world court with positive jurisdiction-the right for any nation to secure judgment from the court against any other nation whether the two previously agreed to arbitrate or not. This would be a court very similar to the supreme court of the United States with the nations occupying a place analagous to that of the states of our union. 

The acceptance by the states in the union of the decisions of our supreme court indicates, that such a court has back of it a very powerful influence in the way of public opinion; many think that a world court with positive jurisdiction would function in a way very analogous to that of the 

United States' supreme court.

The outlawry of war is a recent movement which at present is meeting with favor among the administrators of nations. The main genius of this movement is its simplicity; the nations declare war as a practice to be wrong

Social Psychology of war? and pledge themselves to settle all controversies by other than military means. President Coolidge in his message to congress in December, 1924 said, "Our country has defi- What is the outlawry nitely relinquished the old standard of dealing with other countries by terror and force, and is definitely committed to the new standard of dealing with them through friendship and understanding". A joint public statement of President Hoover and Premier MacDonald commits the future policy of the two great English speaking nations to the plain meaning of the Paris pact; the policies of the United States and England are to be based on the pact and not on the varied interpretations of the diplomatic correspondence which preceded it. The hope of this proposal is, that confidence between the nations will be so strengthened by such a treaty that the international machinery for settling difficulties will function without war. 

Patriotism will be summoned against war, rather than in favor of it and the warmakers, rather than the pacifists, will be under the suspicion of government. After the nations have taken their stand against war certainly many organizations and individuals will be encouraged to take a similar stand. War needs to be recognized as an evil institution much as dueling, slavery, the saloon, polygamy, infanticide, etc., have been recognized as evil and for the most part abolished. No one should associate the abolishing of war as a national practice with the elimination of the fighting impulse; war is just one of the many varied ways in which fighting occurs. The peace forces at present seem agreed that the outlawry of war movement should be consummated by its hearty approval in public opinion. 

A great many societies are working for better relations between the races and nations; these are too numerous to be listed in this connection. (See A17:509-511). Some concentrate their efforts through the churches while others

have various schemes for bringing about a better social order. Forms of the youth movement try to enlist the young in some special way to bring about larger social 

What special influ- coordination. Various conferences are held in which the young of difences are working toferent nations and races mingle in wards group friendships? a friendly way; some of these are special conferences and others are connected with church, Y. M. or Y. W. C. A., religious education, missionary work, etc. The exchange of faculty members and the sending of students from one country to another for their education help in the understanding of group by group. There is developing a recognition of the fact that much can be done through ordinary educational channels for the development of better group relations. 

History needs to be taught so as to make for, and not against, friendly group relations. Geography can do much toward better understanding of group relations and group needs. Supplementary readers can take the child around the world in imagination and introduce him to the favorable aspects in the life of other peoples. Recognition can be given to the contributions that have come to the whole race from various national and racial groups. The financial and industrial inter-dependence of the world can be stressed. The better phases of the life of other groups can be emphasized and thus the basis for understanding and cooperation established. The various possibilities for education in the direction of a friendly cooperative world seem almost endless.

Various religious groups as well as individuals have recently made public their assertions of supreme loyalty to morality and hence to a friendly world order. Representatives of a great many church organizations have condemned hatred and war and, as far as their resolutions indicate, have practically eliminated their support of a na-

Social Psychology tion at war. While this form of excommunication may not tend to destroy war, as outlawry attempts to do, it serves as a warning to any civic administration as to what to 

What is a supreme moral loyalty? expect from a church organization, and it helps to mould public sentiment against war as an institution and in favor of peaceful methods of the settlement of disputes. In addition to the action of such groups there has been a considerable number of individuals who have declared their conscientious objection to war and their determination to have no personal part in its furtherance; such enroll themselves as conscientious objectors but make known their objection before the actual stress of war. Even in the last war governments felt constrained to make reasonable provisions for conscientious objectors. For those who believe the practice of war is counter to their morals and religion, such straightforward announcement seems nothing more than an honest way of dealing with the administration which, for the time being, may head their government.

One of the most far-reaching tendencies of our time is the development of a new conception of force available by groups for offense and defense; perhaps it is only the ap- 

What is the nature of plication of this idea that is new, as the idea itself seems to be rather super-resistance? plainly taught by Jesus and other great religious teachers. Passive resistance was used by the Germans to some extent during the French occupation after the World War. But it is in India, under the leadership of Mahatma Gandhi, that we see the most outstanding movement of this type. Many have difficulty in understanding how active resistance is possible without violence. 

Gandhi says that his conception of non-violence involves actual opposition to wickedness. He adds, "The Nonviolence of my conception is a more active and more real

fighting against wickedness than a relation whose very nature is to increase wickedness. I contemplate a mental and therefore a moral opposition to immoralities". (BB50: 

864). Again he says, "in my scheme, love is presumed to be a more active and potent force than brute force. He who has not the love and remains passive is a coward. He is neither man nor brute. He has proved himself unfit to protect". (BB50:867). In another place Gandhi speaks further as to the nature of this love on which he depends for defense and assertion. "The law of love,-call it attraction, affinity, cohesion, if you like,-governs the world. 

Life persists in the face of death. The universe continues in spite of destruction incessantly going on. Truth triumphs over untruth. Love conquers hate. God eternally triumphs over Satan". (BB50:884). This is the faith that God is love and that he who acts with the motivation of love acts with the power of God. Such program assumes great power of self-control on the part of those who use it; instead of calling for less courage, it calls for not only more courage but more faith and self-control. 

It assumes that there is essential goodness in all groups and that an expression of love will call out such a response from the group as to overcome vengeance, malice and hatred. 

Socrates said, "I have left my sting in them and they cannot get away from it". Jesus said, "I have overcome the world", "I if I be lifted up (be crucified in suffering and shame) will draw men". Confidence seems to be growing that the strongest social forces are after the nature of public opinion; even in the use of militaristic forces a chief concern was with the morale of groups. If war can be outlawed, excommunicated and individually denounced, if a world court can establish machinery for national arbitrations, if education can convince large numbers of people of the superiority of the forces of love over those of hate, then a new day may enter into social relations. We think it

Social Psychology scarcely necessary to mention societies and tendencies working toward group isolation and opposition and loudly proclaiming that the only patriot is the one who trusts in brute force; these individuals and groups seem sufficiently vociferous to speak for themselves. We have been concerned merely to illustrate the definite tendencies which are at work trying to secure more friendly sentiments between groups.
