CHAPTER XIV Friendly Sentiments Relating the Individual to the Broader Religious or Cosmic Order. 

This chapter attempts to relate the individual to the broadest possible aspects of his environment. We have spoken of the different forms of social relations. We speak now of religious or cosmic relations. What types of sentiments are involved in religion? What emotions are prominent in religious attitudes? What are the most desirable objects of religious sentiments? What is needed for the development of religious sentiments? How stimulate the growth of religious sentiments on the part of the child? 

The reader should be thinking of religion in a broad rather than a narrow sense.

I. The Nature of the Sentiments Involved in Religion. 

To determine the nature of the sentiments we consider: (1) the type of sentiments involved; (2) their intellectual and emotional contents; (3) their object or objects. 

1. The Major Place in Religion of the Sentiments of 

Love and Regard.

Let us consider first the possibility of the relationship of hatred existing between the worshiper and the object of his worship. (1). The Possibility of the Functioning of the Sentiment of Hatred in Relation to the Religious Life. 

It is only in the case of the more primitive religions that there seems to be the possibility of hatred being considered 887

Social Psychology 

Is hatred ever a religious sentiment? a religious sentiment. In the more developed religions the higher conception of the Deity precludes this. But in cases in which the object of worship is but little above the status of the worshiper, or in cases involving placation or approaching the borderland of magic, there is the possibility of hatred entering in. In some of the more primitive types of religion there is demon worship and the placation of the Deity; the placation may be in the way of gifts and the intention to ward off such evils as famine and pestilence. If the Deity which is supposed to send the evil is invisible and supposed to be but little superior to the worshiper the placation may be offered in a spirit approaching hatred; for the most part the human could not assume to impede the welfare of the superhuman. On this low level placations of the Deity are more likely to be of the nature of magic. McDougall says that the attitude of religion is that of submission and the attitude of magic that of self-assertion. (90:313). If the objects responds as does a controlled force then the expression takes the form of magic, while if the worshiper petitions the will of the object it takes the form of religion. In the lower religions the motives of the Deity are considered to be very primitive. Westermarck says, "They know, or believe, that on some certain occasion, they are in danger of losing their lives; they attribute this to the designs of a supernatural being; and, by sacrificing a man, they hope to gratify the being's craving for human life, and thereby avert the danger from themselves". (C138:440). worshiper may conceive of such a Deity as being worthy of hatred but is not likely, on that account, actually to hate him because of his superior position and power. Certain lives become very bitter toward the whole of the cosmic order on account of misfortunes and consequent pessimism and cynicism. But this attitude is not primarily hatred but The

rather primitive self-assertion without hope or trust. The development of a thoroughgoing pessimistic attitude is likely to do great harm to religious love and faith but lacks the positive aggressive elements characteristic of hatred; there is little persisting tendency to do injury to the object. 

(2). The Prevalent Positions of the Sentiments of Re gard and Love in the Relation of Worshiper and Deity.

It seems only natural for the worshiper to hold the object of his worship in a place of high regard. Westermark in speaking of the relation of the worshiper to his Does the worshipper gods says, "He always ascribed to highly regard the ob- them a superior power of action; otherwise they would have been no ject of his worship? gods at all. In many cases, at least, he also attributed to them a superior knowledge". (D138:599). We are not, however, to suppose that because the Deity is thought of as being superhuman he is on that account considered to be good. We quote further from 

Westermarck, "We have seen that the gods of uncivilized races are to a very large extent of a malevolent character, that they as a rule take little interest in any kind of human conduct which does not affect their own welfare, and that, if they show any signs of moral feelings, they may be guardians either of tribal customs in general or only of some special branch of morality". But he says that they "have thus experienced a gradual change for the better; until at last they are described as ideals of moral perfection-". (D138:728). Regardless of the quality for which the god is admired by his primitive worshiper, the persistence of such admiration tends to build up a sentiment of respect or regard. Whether the worshiper submits willingly or not, the very act of worship indicates a type of

Social Psychology submission to the will of the Deity. The central emotion of worship is reverence which is a further extension of admiration and awe. Add fear to admiration and the result is awe. Combine tenderness with awe and the resulting compound emotion is reverence; where tenderness enters in there is the likelihood of the development of the sentiment of love. But tenderness is not likely to be added unless there is a realization of benefits received as well as given. So it may happen that the emotional attitude of the worshiper toward his Deity may become stabilized without the emotion of tenderness and hence manifest the sentiment of regard. The worshiper submits to the power and will of the Deity but, because of conceptions held of his character and motives, does not develop the positive sentiment of love. re- It is rather evident that the relationship between worshiper and Deity is not what it should be without the positive social sentiment of love. Religion needs the domi- 

What need has nance of tenderness in it which is ligion for love? introduced only by the sentiment of love. We have said that this develops in conditions in which there is a realization of need and of service; it probably is better that there be a consciousness of both giving and receiving in the way of service. The need of the Deity is usually considered that of human co-workers in his interprises; he may be thought to need attendants, someone to understand him or to carry forward his will upon earth. Further, a sentiment that has tenderness in it demands more of anger and fear than one, such as regard, that lacks this element; it takes the active assertiveness of tenderness to bring these powers to the front. The cherishing of all the interests of the object of love gives to fear a much wider scope and directs its energies along altruistic lines. Also love promotes a fuller degree of religious service. Black says, "All religious ex-

perience has had this in common, that somehow the soul is so possessed by God, that doubt of His existence ceases; and the task of life becomes to keep step with Him, so there may be correspondence between the outer and the inner conditions of life". (17:230). But there is no possibility that the soul become so possessed by God unless the condition of positive love exists. Only love is potent enough to overcome the normal self-seeking tendency of the individual human. Love identifies the interests of self with those of the beloved. Love tends either to possess its object or to take it as an ideal; in the case of the Deity the latter is the more probable. Such tendency makes for a positive enlargement of life. Swain in speaking of Paul says, "His friendship with God gave him a new conception of, as well as a new interest in, society.-Here was personal assurance of God and immortality deep, strong, and jubilant. Whence came it? Such assurance is inherent in a life spiritually nourished and divinely employed. Hope simply comes to such a soul, like color to the ripening apple". (AAA128:175). The qualities which are inherent in the sentiment of love at its best, soon come to be qualities of character. If the object of love is human it is not possible to idealize it beyond human limits. But if the object be the Deity the limits are much enlarged; there remain certain limits-actuality, morality, rationality-but these are very generous when interpreted by the lover. It is well known that we tend to become like the object of our love. Thus it happens that the assumed qualities of the Deity becomes the ideal qualities of character towards which the worshiper strives. Because of these personal and social benefits we say that there is need that the relation of the worshiper to the Deity be characterized by positive love.

Social Psychology 2. The Status of Intellectual and Emotional Elements 

Involved in the Sentiments of Regard and Love for the Deity.

The organization of feelings and impulses which make up a sentiment center about an ideal. McDougall insists that it centers about the idea of an object. (90:164). Givler says that a sentiment is always 

What place has cognition in religious sentiments? an acquirement of education. (A51:145). As one of the elements of a sentiment, Major points out that it "involves a judgment of worth in regard to a given object or group of objects";-. (A91:334). Thus, it seems that cognition must have a considerable place in directing the growth of a sentiment, as it has a place in directing all reactions of experience, and interpreting the nature of its object. If the object of the sentiment is in the superhuman realm certain effects are likely to result. In such case we can hardly assume that friends have all things in common. The superhuman may be willing to share but the human is not able to receive; think of trying to share your college education with a favorite horse or dog. Sabatier says that man "needs a universal being on whom he feels himself to depend, and on whom he may equally make to depend the whole universe". (117:290). But such a being must be of a different order than man himself and consequently must be thought to possess knowledge and power which he is not able, however willing, to share with his worshiper. For the same reason there cannot be conceived to be an equal exchange of benefits as measured in a common medium of exchange. The worshiper may sacrifice everything that he possesses but is not in a position to purchase his salvation by so doing; hence the common assertion that we must be saved by the gracious will of the Deity. Fosdick says, "Gravitation is no more deeply built into the structure of the physical universe than is

vicarious sacrifice into the essential nature of the moral world". (A47:230). As far as this concerns the point at issue here, it means that man strives for ends and realizes needs that he cannot attain for himself and hence must depend upon a being whom he worships to supply. The consciousness of these relationships gives a somewhat different turn to the friendship existing between worshiper and Deity. in common?

But perhaps there are certain tendencies or mutual expressions which may be common between a worshiper and his Deity. Good will is an attitude or conscious expression 

What may a worship. which may be mutual between beer and his Deity have ings on different genetic levels; this could not be applied to levels too low in the genetic scale. James quotes Mr. Vorsey who says, "It is the experience of myriads of trustful souls, that this sense of God's unfailing presence with them in their going out and in their coming in, and by night and day, is a source of absolute repose and confident calmness. It drives away all fear of what may befall them". (C73:275). This mutual relationship of the saint with the Deity may be thought of as an interchange of spiritual good will. The association is, however, more that of a covenant than that of a partnership. The effect is to make the will of the worshiper subservient to that of the Deity. James also quotes Mrs. Edwards who says, "I also thought how God had graciously given me an entire resignation to his will, with respect to the kind and manner of death I should die; having been made willing to die on the rack, or at the stake, and if it were God's will, to die in darkness". (C73:277). In the fulfillment of the covenant relationships there may be an earnest good will on the part of the worshiper but scarcely can he hope to be as successful in carrying out his part of the covenant as is the Deity. Worship, whether primitive or more developed, has in it the essential elements of dependence upon

Social Psychology the Deity on account of the inherent weakness of the human. 

The great religious teachers have pictured the Deity as being willing to bear with human weakness and to forgive human failures. Fosdick says, "So always it is the greatest minds that see the greatest possibilities in the most unlikely places. The Master exercised this insight supremely on men, and the worth which he saw hidden in immature, perverted, wronged human nature he was sure God saw too, so for those who could fulfill the conditions were awaiting forgiveness, reconciliation, and moral power to become sons of God." (A47:225). Even supposing an initial goodwill there is likely to develop on the part of the worshiper much pseudo-loyalty and some actual disloyalty. This is due in part to lack of understanding. The worshiper is likely to become interested in form or standardized procedure, while the Deity, according to his best interpreters, always is interested supremely in spirit. Very frequently the worshiper desires to be loyal to a position attained whereas the leading of the spirit demands an advance. But if the worshiper remains true to the friendship he is likely to grow in insight and in spiritual power. This does not mean that the essential genetic nature of the friendship is changing; the status of difference between human and divine is not becoming less. Nothing will spoil the relationship more than for the worshiper to lose his submissiveness and to assume that his will is the will of the Deity. The relationship is essentially that of child and father and must remain so. Where the god is supposed to be intimidated by the acts or attitude of the worshiper the procedure either has lost its religious nature entirely or has degenerated to a very low scale of religion. The individual who supposes that our advance in knowledge and skill will spoil our conception of divinity of Jesus has a very crude conception of divinity. A father always desires all possible growth on the part of his children. Right growth on the part of

the worshiper only tends to improve his relationship with his god and never to injure it.

What secondary emo tions are prominent in love for the Deity?

The interpretation by the worshiper of the exalted status of the Deity makes more prominent the position of certain emotions in this type of love. The same situation minimizes or even excludes the incorporation of certain other emotions in such sentiment. The secondary emotions which are apt to be most prominent in this form of sentiment are those which involve subjection and fear. 

We are told on good authority that perfect love casts out fear. We take this to mean that tenderness demands that intimacy with, and appropriation of, its object which excludes the possibility of fearing that object. This may have one of two results: (1) fear may cease to be aroused by the object and come to be aroused by that which endangers the object; (2) fear may cease to be felt in its primary form and come to find expression in such sceondary emotions as admiration, awe and reverence. In the case under consideration probably both tendencies are involved. The more the worshiper comes to trust in the goodness of the Deity, the less reason he has to fear as he did when he conceived the Deity to be malevolent. He now assumes that the Deity desires his welfare and will not punish him in horrible ways for offenses of which he is not conscious. 

But as the conception of the superiority of the Deity grows upon him his fear tends to pass into admiration and awe. 

Moreover the worshiper realizes his utter dependence upon the Deity and thus develops the emotion of negative selffeeling or subjection. Regardless of his own growth, he sees no possibility of becoming equal to the Deity but only of understanding the Deity better and of doing his will more perfectly. Since admiration precedes awe and reverence in this emotional series, we can expect to find it in

Social Psychology this type of emotion. There is likely to be a positive admiration for the Deity because of his power, patience, selfcontrol, goodness, etc. So we are likely to find the secondary emotions of admiration, awe and reverence in the sentiment of the love of the individual for the Deity. 

In this type of sentiment under consideration, certain of the derived emotions are likely to be prominent and others to find scant place. Westermarck says, "Religion may be defined as a belief in and a regard- 

What derived emotions are in love for ful attitude toward a superhuman the Deity? being on whom man feels himself dependent and to whose will he makes an appeal in his worship". (D138:584). Belief or feeling of confidence in the existence of the Deity and in the reality of the attitude he is supposed to possess, are recognized generally as basic to a religious life. This implies that such emotions as anxiety and despondency cannot find a place in the religious sentiment. It is notable how long religious belief will be maintained even in the face of apparent lack of response from the Deity. Always there is some way to explain why the Deity does not respond. The present failure is only temporary and intended to try the faith of the worshiper. The Deity thinks differently from the human and will defend the right in his own time and way. The Deity may be punishing the worshiper for some offense. The greater prosperity of the wicked man is an illusion only, and great and certain punishment await him later. The worshiper is likely to believe his Deity is equal to every situation. When man has reached his full extermity then God will come to his rescue. Even the dry bones will return to life again, the holy city will be restored and the apparent defeat and destruction will be turned to joy and victory. So hope, faith, belief and confidence, rather than despondency and discouragement, mark the attitude of the worshiper toward those problems which

directly concern his religion. Because the Deity is conceived to be unlimited in power and because his methods of expression are different from those of humans, there is no limit to which belief can be carried. All injustice will be righted in a future life and all apparent injustices there will be made plain. So the hopeful type of derived emotion is likely to be very prevalent in religion; there is no looking forward to defeat and the apparent defeat exprienced tends to be minimized. 

3. Objects Which are or May Be Associated with

Religious Sentiments. 

We have spoken of the types of sentiments involved most frequently in the relation of the worshiper to the Deity. Also mention has been made of the intellectual and emotional elements involved in such sentiments. We give some consideration now to the objects of religious sentiments. 

(1). The Primary and Rightful Objects of Religious 

Love.

The primary object of a religious sentiment is a Deity or a group of Deities. But soon the attributes of the Deity come to be objects of religious sentiments. Practically 

What are the real ob. every quality found in human life has been attributed to the life of jects of religious sentiments? some god. But as religion advances from its more primitive status the conception of the Deity comes to involve always the idea of certain qualities. Abraham speaks to Jehovah in the following manner: "That be far from thee to do after this manner, to slay the righteous with the wicked, that so the righteous should be as the wicked; that be far from thee: shall not the judge of all the world do right?" (Gen.

Social Psychology 

18:25). And the answer of the religious worshiper who has advanced beyond the primitive stage is, The God of all the world must do right or I will not acknowledge him as judge. There have been at times various conceptions as to what is right and also very pronounced distinctions as to whom the god considers friend and whom enemy. But there is or has been little disagreement concerning the assumption that the Deity must have in his character justice and goodness, and in the more developed religions positive love is attributed to him. The association of an attribute with the life of the Deity gives to that attribute a special place of reverence in the thought of the worshiper. 

Ordinarily at first we love a friend and then the essential attributes of his life. The lover and worshiper of the Deity come to both love and reverence those attributes of life which he believes to belong to the Deity. Westermarck says, "The belief in a god who acts as a guardian of worldly morality undoubtedly gives emphasis to its rules. To the social and legal sanctions a new one is added, which derives particular strength from the supernatural power and knowledge of the Deity". (D138:734). Because the

Deity gives his sanction to goodness and justice these and such virtues become objects of religious sentiments. We think of such tendency as desirable because a new sanction is given to moral character which makes it more likely to prevail in the life of the individual. (2). Objects of Religious Sentiments Likely to Involve 

Wrong Religious Attitudes and Effects. 

But certain ideas become the objects of religious sentiments which do not benefit religion or further the moral growth of worshipers. Sacred literature may become an object of religious sentiments. Such literature may come to hold a high place as an object of religious reverence be-

literature? cause of its close association with the Deity. All the traditional blindness of love is likely in this case to become associated with the interpretation of the sacred literature. 

What dangers in the The reader may become blind to worship of religious the obvious meaning of the text and find in it meaning which tradition assures him ought to be there. He may come to be satisfied with any curious interpretation or even contradiction without expecting sense or demanding consistency. In fact any peculiar element or assertion may be taken as evidence of the non-human, hence divine, nature of the writing. Or the worshiper may come to treat such a book as a sacred relic and hence depend upon it for protection much as the ancient Jew did upon the sacred ark. Many like to recount how Bibles have stopped bullets and hence saved the lives of soldiers.

The writer heard a churchman recount how a Bible floated for two days in the water without injury to the book. 

Such bibliolatry makes the book a sacred relic and hence an instrument for works of magic rather than religion. 

Or all forms of crude paganism may be tolerated because of their supposed relation to the Bible. The spectacle of devout Puritans torturing to death helpless old women of their community should long be remembered as a horrible example of what bibliolatry may produce. Orthodoxy based upon an interpretation of sacred literature has at times brought about the most fiendish type of barbarism. 

One need to think only of the horrors of the Spanish inquisition to realize what is possible in this connection. Surely it is incumbent upon every friend of the Bible to see to it that it is not made a basis for gross superstition, for the increase of magic, or for the return of crude barbarism. 

But these results are all possible if the literature itself becomes the object of worship. Jesus says, "Worship God".

Further a church or religious denomination may become

Social Psychology the object of a religious sentiment. The church is loved by the religious worshiper because it seems to embody the spirit of the Deity whom he worships. As in the case of the sacred 

What danger in the book, so here, in the case of the worship of a church? sacred house or institution, there are dangers involved. The unity and sufficiency attributed to the Deity are likely to be assigned to his church; in such case the church is supposed to be the single instrument through which the Deity expresses himself. This is likely to lead to various forms of bigotry and hence to a spirit which is distinctly antisocial. Robes and ritual may be suggestive spiritually or they may conceal much immorality and crude selfishness. It is really surprising, the extent to which bigotry and narrowness may dominate the mind without the fact being detected by the honest and earnest membership of the church; the most narrow type of partyism may within the group be assumed to be a form of universal belief and practice. The early seekers of religious freedom in our country found it easy to suppress the very quest for freedom on the part of others who differed somewhat from them. The wider interests of the Kingdom of God often are subordinated to the narrow interests of a denomination. In a great many small communities the whole religious life is hampered and hindered in its development by overchurching. The religiously inclined people of the community are so distributed among little churches that the community as such has little effective religious life; so far it seems that we are able to count upon only about one-third of the people of a community being active in church work. Thus narrow loyalty interferes with broader loyalty. The loyal churchman forgets that the searching test for an institution as well as for an individual is whether it is willing to die for its cause. 

The truly religious attitude is shown in the sentences following from one who the world around has come to be

called Mahatma or Great Soul. Gandhi says, "I claim to be a simple individual liable to err like any other fellow mortal. I own, however, that I have humility enough in me to confess my errors and to retrace my steps. I own that I have an immovable faith in God and His goodness and unconsumable passion for truth and love". (BB50:869-70).

This must be the attitude of a church or any such institution if it is to serve God rightly. The church must be a servant of God and not a substitute for the Deity. to As a fine example of an undesirable object of a religious sentiment we consider the theological system. Thought of individuals concerning God, at times is given a place of 

Why dangerous reverence akin to the place properworship any theologi. ly held by the characteristics of the cal system? 

Deity himself. Mathews thinks it necessary to warn us that, "To be religious in the Christian sense is something more than to assent to theological definitions". (A92:196). Josey asserts that man "claims considerable knowledge about the secrets of God's mind". He says further, "For the difficulties of investigation are substituted acts of faith". (76:43). Here also the blindness of love and reverence is apt to lead to confusion: man's thought concerning God is substituted for God's thought. Often it is the case that little distinction is made between the true and false prophet; any prophet with the courage or arrogance to say "thus saith Jehovah" is assumed to be a true prophet. Theology has been called the queen of the sciences. This has been done in face of the fact that it is not a science but a speculative philosophy. Its speculations, because they concern divine issues, are no less human and fallible speculations. 

Robinson says that history has demonstrated "that religious dogma can be neglected in matters of public concern and reduced to a question of private taste and preference". (114:136). There can be no question that it is of great

Social Psychology value to attempt to ascertain the will of the Deity. Also those who have religious insight should make such available for those who have but little. But a heavy responsibility rests upon him who speaks in the name of the Deity. This responsibility instead of freeing such a one from the criticism of his fellows should cause him to welcome every aid tending to further truth in this field. Worship of theology is just as destructive of spiritual values as is bibliolatry. 

Belief in orthodoxy readily may become a substitute for belief in God and spiritual values. The orthodox man may be doing nothing more than to let another think and speak for him in the field of religion. Exactly why this should be to his credit is not apparent. Many times the bare assertion of right belief has been taken as a substitute for Christian love and service. The belief itself may be wholesome and right but its substitution for the love and service of God certainly is unwholesome and wrong. The submissive and loving servant of God must give spiritual values first place. Let us repeat, "Worship God".

II. The Development and Maintenance of Basic Religious 

Sentiments.

We consider here: (1) development along lines of the different aspects of consciousness; (2) methods to be used in the case of the growing child; these points are considered together under the headings given. 

1. The Involvement of the Major Phases of Consciousness in the Love of the Individual for the Deity. 

In the consideration of the development of desirable religious sentiments we give attention to the understanding, the feeling and the assertive volition involved.

(1). The Development of the Cognition Needed in the 

Intelligent Love of the Individual for the Deity.

We said above that if religious sentiments are to be beneficial in their social and character results they must relate definitely to the Deity as a virtuous being. Let us consider 

What cognition is im- first the cognition involved in the plied in my love for love of the individual for the Deity. 

Saint Augustine says that, "the soul is so much the less subject to God God? (A110: as it is less occupied with the thought of God;-". 

182). It is evident that the love of the individual for God is a matter of growth and must involve changing ideas. 

The child comes to a fuller conception of God through something of the same process as that by which the race has built up its conception. Fosdick says that the primitive conception of God was "like a man who walks in a garden in the cool of the day, or as one who comes down from the sky to confuse men's speech lest they should build their tower so high as to reach his home;-". From this the conception has grown "until in the New Testament God revealed in Christ is the Spiritual Presence in whom we live, and move, and have our being, whose name is love and whose temples are human hearts:-". (A47:22). To cultivate the growth of the right kind of thought toward the Deity there is need that we associate the idea of the Deity with ideas that are supported already by friendly sentiments. Thus the idea of God should be related with that of life, strength and beauty, or with such ideas as already call for a favorable response from the child. God should be related definitely in our thought to values of this world as well as to purely spiritual conceptions of life to be attained in the future. Rauschenbusch says, "the old religion of other-worldy salvation necessarily diverted immense currents of energy toward actions that were apart from daily life". (111:98). Our thought of God should

Social Psychology include the idea of the giver of food and the creator of light and beauty. There should be a keen realization of the physical as well as the mental or spiritual dependence of the individual upon the Deity. The conception of God as an ever near and ever providing Father is a conception calculated to stimulate a real love for God. 

(2). The Development of the Feeling Elements Implied in the Best Form of Love of the Individual for the Deity.

But the love of God must have in it elements of feeling as well as understanding before it can be reckoned as a real love. Plotinus says that the love of God should affect the soul in a "most felicitous manner”. 

Miss Condé says, (A110:172). 

How develop a proper feeling of love for God? "As the love of God grows within us and holds us with its wonder and satisfying power, we find that our friendships are kept sweet and strong". (29:79). It takes the feeling element of tenderness to give reality to the relationship of the individual to God. James says, "When I read in a religious paper words like these: 'Perhaps the best thing we can say of God is that he is the Inevitable Inference,' I recognize the tendency to let religion evaporate in intellectual terms. 

Would martyrs have sung in the flames for a mere inference, however inevitable it might be?" (C73:502). To develop this feeling of reality toward the Deity it is well to transfer to the Heavenly Father the tenderness that has developed for the human parent. Our expectancy should be that development take place from the concrete to the abstract; awe and reverence probably should begin in the natural and lead to the spiritual world. It is well that the child's conception of the perfection of the Deity does not exclude the idea that the Heavenly Father appreciates benefits received. The child should come to think of the

Heavenly Father who desires the notice and love of his children and who makes the extension of his kingdom depend upon the services of men. Also the interest of the child in Jack Frost, Santa Claus, or other products of the world of imagination, may be utilized as a way of approach to the Deity. As the child develops, Santa Claus should be spiritualized rather than ruled out of existence. The wonders of Jack Frost or the Magic Rabbit should be capitalized to further a friendly attitude toward, appreciation of and love for the Great Spirit who makes all wonders possible. This manner of sublimating the products of the fairy world certainly is better than the production of a rude shock to the child's idealism which comes with the assertion that there is no Santa Claus; a friend reports that a child on being told that there is no Santa, afterwards said, "And I am going to look into this Jesus business". Santa

Claus is the most worthy product of the child's imagination up-to-date and instead of being destroyed should be replaced by higher and more spiritual conceptions. As the emotions of awe and reverence develop there should be in the child's mind images or ideas referring to the unseen world which serve as a stimuli for these emotions. All the distinctive religious emotions confidence, belief, hope,should be related gradually to this increasingly spiritual conception of the unseen. The culmination of such growth and education should be a real love for the Deity. 

(3) The Development of the Loyalty Implied in the Sublimated Love of the Individual for the Deity. 

Lastly, love for God should have its conative side developed and thus come to manifest itself in abiding and worthy loyalties. Rauschenbusch points out that the

Kingdom of God "insists solely that the natural relations among men which God has created shall be ruled by God's

Social Psychology will.

It demands an organized fellowship of mankind, based on justice and resulting in love, binding all men together in strong bonds of trust, helpfulness, purity, and 

How develop the loy. alty implied in love for the Deity? good will". (111:98). There is real need that the love of God be given definite content and thus harnessed to the doing of actual social service. Religious worship and service needs in it something of the stern justice of which Cicero speaks. "There came a deserter out of Pyrrhus' camp, and offered the senate to dispatch him with poison; which they and 

Fabricius were so far from accepting of, that they gave him up again as a traitor to his master". (A27:19). Unless religion has in it something of the sterner qualities of sacrificial justice it is likely to waste its power either in vain speculation or equally unproductive feeling. Loyalty to the broader and more abstract interests must be introduced by the development of loyalty in more concrete relations; it is of special importance that we realize that the Deity never desires that which falls short of the best product of human will. With the child a loyalty which is rather objective and close at hand is desirable. Royce defines the essentials of loyalty as, "the willing, the thoroughgoing, and the practical devotion of self to a cause. And a cause means, in these lectures, something that is conceived by the loyal servant as unifying the lives of various human beings into one life". (A116:252). The first cause to which the child pledges loyalty should be concrete, easily understood, and its obligations fulfilled for the most part in objective actions. Loyalty to the family honor and to the good name of the parents constitutes and excellent beginning point for the child. From this there should be a gradual broadening of the scope of the cause to include the local church, the denomination, Protestant-

ism, Christianity, the Kingdom of God; we do not mean to suggest that these particular units of loyalty be included but only that the development take some such expanding course. The danger of the growing life of loyalty is that it fix its attention upon a particular cause and by so doing stop the tendency of its growth toward idealism. The child readily becomes dogmatic within the field of his own experience. So with the presentation of larger and more vague causes for loyalty there must be a constant emphasis upon the moral and spiritual in principle in order to prevent the fixation of attachment upon externals. We cannot begin too early in the life of the child to impress the lesson that God is Spirit and righteousness, and that any form of loyalty to God must involve loyalty to truth and righteousness. Once we permit attachment to become fixed upon certain forms of words and ritual we lay the foundation for partisanism and hence for social disruption. 

Much of the tragedy of spiritual shipwreck is due to the formation of early attachments which should never have been formed in such fashion. Loyalty must be subjected to creative idealism.

CHAPTER XV A Supplementary Chapter on The Sex Impulse and Its Relation to Friendship. 

This final chapter is supplementary. It can be omitted without loss to the continuity of the remaining portion of the text. What meanings have been assigned to sex? What is the best meaning to assign to it? What is the basic nature of its impulse? Why should the sex impulse be sublimated? What is the perceptual sublimation of sex? Is sex susceptible of motor sublimation? Is there an essential connection between sublimation and repression? 

I. The Nature of the Sex Impulse. 

The term "sex" is making its way out from the realm of social disapproval into a more general usage. Bigelow says that we have nothing to fear from this tendency. He says, "It is absurd to suppose that the free, dignified and scientific use of the word 'sex' is going to make people more sensual, more uncontrolled and more immoral". (16:8).

In this chapter we inquire as to the place of sex in relation to friendship. Our first concern is with the meaning of the term. 

1. Differing Interpretations of the Term "Sex". 

We consider here: (1) the narrow conception of sex; (2) the broader use of the term. 

(1). The Narrow Conception of Sex. 

We tend to object to the word "sex" because of the implications that have been read into it. Ellis says, "We 411

Social Psychology 

What is the narrow use of the term "sex"? have so deeply poisoned the springs of feeling in these matters with mediaeval ascetic crudities that all our words of sex tend soon to become bespattered with filth; we may pick them up from the mud into which they have fallen and seek to purify them, but to many eyes they will still seem dirty". (F39:133). Many still think against the backgrounds of contrasts which the ascetics made between organic sensations and derived emotions. The monk thought that hope, faith and love must be secured by the elimination of hunger, thirst and lust. The Roman monk had seen such devastations wrought by crude sensuality that it was only natural for him to condemn all desires of this type. But a certain type of religious temperament seems to turn deliberately against sex. Gandhi says, “I fear, I shall preach to the end of my day complete renunciation of sexual desire". (BB50:951). In this sense sex is in some way connected with the crude in sensuality and impresses the individual as the opposite of the spiritual tendencies of life. While we are seeking to secure a better background of thought for these terms we should bear patiently with those who are not able to make this adjustment so quickly and easily. 

(2). The More Inclusive Use of the Term "Sex". 

The broadest use of the term "sex" or "libido" is its use as equivalent to all the basic impulses of life. White 

What is the most in- says, "The great creative force, clusive meaning of the libido, in the last analysis, has only two problems-the problem of self-preservation and the problem of the perpetuation of the race. The libido devoted to the solution of the first of these problems is the nutritive libido, that devoted to the latter is the sexual libido". (140:153). Freud says that psychoanalysex?

sis is simply using the word "love" in the same sense in which the Apostle Paul used it. (B49:38-39). It is evident that Paul includes the higher reaches of love but not quite so evident that he is conscious of implying any sex element in it. Ellis insists consciously upon the synthesis. 

He says, "Love, in the sexual sense, is, summarily considered, a synthesis of lust (in the primitive and uncolored sense of sexual emotion) and friendship". (F39:133). To show what Ellis means by the "uncolored sense" of lust we quote further. He says, "Properly speaking, 'lust' is an entirely colorless word and merely means desire in general and sexual desire in particular; it corresponds to 'hunger' or 'thirst'; to use it in an offensive sense is much the same as though we should always assume the word 'hungry' had the offensive meaning of 'greedy'". (F39:132). Gulick says that, "sex and sex activity are to be viewed and used for spiritual purposes, quite independently of their use for reproductive purposes". (B60:93). The broader use of the term "sex" seems to be coming into more general use. In this sense we are to think of sex as implying creativeness along both physical and psychic lines. It furnishes much of the basic drive which gives a power and creative tendency to love. Many use these terms with a primary reference of the home and race in mind but with something of a broader background and implication. To make the meaning of this broader usage plainer we give a few paragraphs showing its implications in the world of social and psychic contacts.

One form of sublimation of which sex love is capable is that of becoming attached to the beautiful in the broader sense of the term. In its most primitive form sex beauty is connected with the primary qualities of masculine and feminine; these are largely physical qualities determined by the functioning of the gonad or closely allied endocrine glands. But in a more sublimated sense attraction comes to

Social Psychology be for the more psychic elements of masculine and feminine life. Ellis insists that, "So far from being animal-like, the 

What is the aesthetic human impulses of sex are among the least animal-like acquisitions implication of sex? of man". (F39:130). Certainly there is much truth in this assertion. There is no basic tendency in human life which comes to have a more diffuse psychic implication than that of sex. This is indicated by the fact that readily it comes to prompt attachments to such abstractions as beauty and goodness. Berl in speaking of love says, "When narrow and restricted it clings to immortality. When it widens out, it wants the Kingdom of God". (13:54). But the Plantonic Socrates insists that the object is not merely beauty but generation and birth in beauty or the everlasting possession of the good. (B106:337). Care for the personal appearance and for aesthetic possessions seems in some way to have its roots in sex. From this it is easy to go up the scale of sublimation to all fair forms connected with the creative arts; see the upper reaches of the chart of human impulses connected with Chapter IV. Attraction to the good and beautiful and the creative tendency to produce the good and beautiful have their beginnings in the impulse of sex. The use of the term in its broader sense relates it not only to its primary beginnings but to its more developed applications.

Also the sex impulse is capable of sublimation along lines of self-assertion or the psychic outreach of self. Every 

What does sex imply one recognizes that sex may take as to psychic assertion? a selfish turn and thus become one of the most destructive forces in life; it seeks the possession of the good and may make a right or a wrong use of it. Possession may be for the sake of careful protection as well as for selfish enjoyment. Why does the mother demand her

right to be present in the sick room of her suffering child? 

This is a type of self-assertion and possession that we are inclined to praise and not to blame. Primitive reproduction is a sort of unconscious extension of self; thus certain of the characteristics of the individual are kept alive after his death. But if there is an active interest in and care for the life of the child the parent has through social heredity an added chance to extend his personality and to preserve it. This is a step towards the sublimation of the self-assertive tendency; there has to be a positive altruism and tenderness or the child will not be willing to receive the ministrations and hence the impressions of the parent. But an actual child may be lacking to the individual who has within him the power and desire for creative self-assertion. Does this imply that the impulse must languish and finally die? Such seems not to be the case. Here is a manifestation of the freedom of each human life from the iron-clad chains of heredity. One may exercise creative self-assertion upon the child of another. The child begotten in the spirit may carry in its life more of the individual characteristics of its foster parent than the one begotten in the flesh may represent its real parent. But sublimation of the creative impulse may go even further than this. Creative self-assertion may find expression in a story, poem, painting or any form of lasting art. In such case it is not so manifest that the sex impulse is at the root of the creative tendency. But whether we say it is or is not, depends for the most part upon arbitrary meanings assigned to terms. 

If we agree to call this creative impulse in its primitive form the sex impulse then there need to be no objection to speaking of this as a sublimated form of sex. From this point of view it is easy to see how sex may be considered a basic element to most of the primary impulses. If sex is creative self-assertion then anger as an impulse is simply this self-assertion as it strives to overcome difficulties. Fear

Social Psychology is the protective tendency of self-assertion and functions to the end of preserving the values created. The main implication of sex in the psychic realm is that the individual is assertive along creative lines; certainly no one can see evil in such creative assertiveness. 

After the beginning of adolescence there is a positive and definite attractiveness between the sexes due to the development of sex-characteristics. Darwin speaks of the attempt on the part of both sexes to augment the factor of sex attractive- He says, "it is a remarkable 

What place has sex in social attractiveness? ness. fact that the same fashions in modifying the shape of the head, in ornamenting the hair, in painting, tatooing, or perforating the nose, lips or ears, in removing or filing the teeth, etc., now prevail, and have long prevailed, in the most distant quarters of the world". (A31:658). Westermarck says, "I think that it is an indisputable fact that savages, at present at least, practice ornamentation on a large scale as a sexual allurement". (138:499). Certain forms of such ornamentation come to be considered especially alluring. 

Darwin says, "A star tattooed on the forehead and a spot on the chin are thought by the women in one part of Africa to be irresistible attractions". (A31:657-58). With the advancement of civilization such forms of attraction come to be symbolized into ideal characteristics for each sex. 

Mrs. Spencer in speaking of the prevailing custom of embodying truth, justice, knowledge and charity in feminine forms says, "These bear witness to the fact that even when men were most insistent upon father-rights they were moulded by intimate companionship with women in the home to some appreciation of the value of feminine personality". (126:84). There can be little doubt that in the intersex friendship the factor of sex has a very definite and positive place; this is true whether it functions to the good or injury of the race. We recognize this very clearly so

long as we are thinking of the intersex friendship that is preparatory to marriage and the forming of the home. But there are some who do not recognize the functioning of sex in the friendship between mothers and sons and fathers and daughters; this is due to thinking of sex in too narrow ways. 

Groves says, "The child does not react the same to both his parents. Very early, much earlier than common thought in the past has been want to suppose, the child distinguishes between the mother and father in such a way that his behavior is slightly modified because of his recognition that they are in some sense different and demand from him different treatment". (59:193). In order to detect the functioning of sex here, it is necessary that we free ourselves entirely from the conception that there is something vulgar and sensuous in sex. So long as sex implies such conceptions, of course, we shall not be able to detect its manifestations in the relations of parents and their children. 

Masculine and feminine may refer quite as much to qualities of mind as to those of body. Let this fact be understood and we can detect manifestations of sex where formerly no such relation could be recognized. Once we have grasped a broader conception of sex we are likely to grant to Darwin something of his claim for the potency of sex selection. 

In its psychic manifestations the sex impulse may influence the normal intrasex as well as the intersex friendships. 

Jesus in replying to the Sadducees concerning a marriage How does the subli- problem in the world of the spirit mation of sex broaden says, "they that are accounted worthy to attain to that world, and its social power of the resurrection from the dead, selection? neither marry, nor are given in marriage:-for they-are sons of God, being sons of the resurrection". (Lk. 20:35-36). The implication seems to be that in the psychic realm there may be a breadth and

Social Psychology scope of friendly relationships much beyond that which is possible in the physical-psychic relations. The freeing of women from the harem, the development of coeducation, the tendency toward social and economic equality between the sexes, all lead towards the control and sublimation of sex. Under primitive conditions sex relations are restricted necessarily because practically the sole manifestation of sex is along racially creative lines. But with a broadening mental culture there must of necessity be a sublimation of sex tendencies. Just as a man of mental culture can no longer throw prayer wads at a hideous idol so also the same man cannot be attracted permanently to an object that does not reveal psychic elements of attraction. Plutarch gives the reply of Aristippus to one that said that, "Lais loved him not, 'No more', he said, 'do meat and wine love me, but 

I gladly enjoy both'". (107:32). Such a statement is mere rationalization; the desire that it might be thus, is father of the thought that it is. Human life is unified to too great an extent to permit of such detachment of the psychic from the physical. The continued expression of a crude physical desire calls for a degenerate frame of mind to give it an appropriate background. The fact that homosexual attractions develop occasionally indicates that even on a primitive level sex attractions are broader than the intersex. Sex in some of its ramifications may play a part in any type of social attachment. After friendly relations become sublimated there is developed a situation in which it is difficult to discern a sex element even when present. 

We have no particular quarrel with those who assert that sex has a part in the functioning of all types of friendship. 

2. The Meaning of the Term "Sex" as Used in the

Present Chapter. 

We indicated above our agreement in a general way with the broader use of the term "sex". To make the matter a

How think of the sex impulse by itself? little plainer let us attempt to differentiate the impulse of sex from the affective and cognitive elements which commonly are associated with it. When we speak of such differentiation of the elements of a complex it should be understood that this cannot be done by introspection. The complex becomes a unitary type of organization, all of the different parts of which function together in a way which makes it impossible to distinguish them by introspection; it functions as a whole just as any machine, such as an automobile. Scarcely it is possible that there be a functioning of one element without there being some functioning of all. But this does not imply that the elements of the complex are of kindred nature or that they cannot be separated for purposes of examination. In every case of a complex we think of the impulse as the central drive or dynamic. We have been asserting that such a dynamic function is inherent in the nature of life itself and, of course, is to be found in connection with all emotions, reflexes and instincts. As we suggested before, we may think of any such tendency as having a neural pattern for its physiological basis. Or we may connect it with the influence produced upon the life by a particular hormone or associated group of hormones secreted into the blood by a certain or by certain endocrine glands. Or combining these, we may conceive that there is a particular neural pattern which functions only when stimulated by certain hormones; the future may reveal other possible explanations. Any of these conceptions will permit imagination to picture a concrete basis for an impulse. We are saying that it is best to think of sex as an impulse, rather than as an instinct or emotion; wherever there is a steady function of this impulse we can think of it as a desire. Our chart in connection with Chapter IV attempts to show the relation of the sex impulse to other human impulses; its place-

Social Psychology ment among the more psychic creative impulses is in accord with the broader conception of sex as presented above. 

What is the racial meaning of sex?

It appears that sex as an impulse is related rather closely to the tendency of each one to produce on his environment a lasting mark of his individuality. Some assert that the strongest desire in human life is the desire for immortality. 

Uusually this implies the desire to live in some form or other; it may be only to live on in the thought of others or to live as a part of the world soul. Hocking says, "Love is that region of life which exists in giving life; and this means developing the possibilities of a mutual existence both of sense and of idea. It is satisfied only when its power can work in a completely historic form. 

It ministers to the soul, but always by way of body and estate". (A67:366). Thus the identification of sex with racial reproduction is not false so much as it is crude and incomplete. Sex should be used to involve this form of creative reproduction but should not be confined to merely physical reproduction. As the Greeks assert there is an essential insistence in sex for production in the realm of beauty. Creation along lines of beauty saves the race from the abnormal and distorted forms of reproduction. 

The race is guarded against the abnormal and promoted along lines of right and safe tendencies by the insistence of sex upon masculine and feminine elements of beauty. That which the primitive man calls beautiful seems to us curious. 

But it takes a rare prophet to see in primitive beginnings that which is to develop from them. The connection of sex with the elements of color and rhythm give to these artistic tendencies a touch of creative life. The better expression of sex protects the interests of the species. Beauty and strength in sex attractiveness indicate that the interests of the race along the lines of its creative evolution are being preserved. By the power of sex selection there are de-

veloped supplementary tendencies of the strongly masculine and feminine to the good of the race. One of the major objections to ready divorce is that the developing child is deprived of the influence of one of its parents upon its life. 

Sex is the factor which has so functioned through the ages as to build up these supplementary masculine and feminine tendencies of life. Further, if it is brought to function at its best sex will support the mores of the race. These moral principles are substantially a creative product of racial life. If it can be brought about that the major sentiment in the life of the individual tends to further the idea of the supreme good, developed by the experience of the race, then we have a condition in which the driving power of sex is squarely back of the highest product of racial creativeness. Through its proper sublimation the power of the sex impulse can be brought to promote the highest interests of the race; on its racial side sex is not to be thought of as crude and primitive necessarily or as essentially destructive of spiritual interests. 

How is sex creative individually?

But in addition to being creative racially, the sex impulse is a potent factor in the production of desirable individual character traits. It is difficult to state briefly the basic distinctions between men and women; we are concerned mainly with the psychic rather than the physical distinctions. It is well known that the historic conception of women is that of an inferior creature whose main function was supposed to be to bear children and minister to the pleasure of men. (See 124:5). At present we seem to have agreed that there is no superior or inferior sex. agrees with Heymans that the "chief fundamental distinction between men and women is the greater emotionality (or as I have termed it, affectibility) of women". (39:169-70). Ellis says boys are superior in judgment and

Ellis

Social Psychology reasoning and asserts that there is "a deep feminine dislike to analysis, probably connected with the emotionality of women". (39:172). But we find that if a variety of mental tests are given that the general average for boys and for girls is as nearly the same as could be expected. 

But likeness of intelligence does not indicate that there is extreme likeness in the functioning of the two types of minds. In fact the extension of investigations seems to show that, while no power is possessed by either sex that to some degree is not possessed by the other, there is a distinct difference of emphasis. Hall says, "Woman is nearer the race in body and soul and is a better representative of the species than man. She is more phylogenetic than ontogenetic, more altruistic than egoistic". (62:111). This is not to be taken to mean that consciously she is more idealistic or more willing to suffer than her brother. She may not even be conscious of the racial ends promoted by her feeling judgments when she "blushingly turns from Adonis to Hercules" but, as Ellis suggests, "the emotional attitude is rooted in more or less unerring instincts". (C39:192). 

The following tendencies are frequently attributed to women or called feminine; on the psychic side a man may have a feminine tendency. Physiologically she is anabolic; she emphasizes intuitive judgment and emotional attention; she works normally under more neural tension; she has more power of aesthetic appreciation and emotional sublimation; she is interested in persons and is careful about details; she is courageous in sickness and optimistic in defeat. Man inclines to supplement these lines of emphasis. 

He is katabolic and capable of expressing much energy at one time; he stresses logical judgments and voluntary attention; he has strong powers of initiative and voluntary control; he is interested in machines of all types and the doing of big business; he faces the dangers of the frontier

with courage. These lists might be very much extended and detailed. But it must be remembered that these distinctive masculine and feminine traits are produced by gonad and related endocrine glands or can be traced back to their influence; we do not speak of a masculine and feminine nervous system. Berman points out changes in indi viduals due to changes in the endocrine glands. (14:248).

The study of the whole subject of the endocrine glands while securing much attention, is still only in its beginning and we may expect notable developments in the future. 

Some think the highest type of human life combines the distinctively masculine and feminine characteristics; this is possible in so far as these are psychic. Carpenter speaks of this type as the "Uranian Man" and says of him, "we find a man who, while possessing thoroughly masculine powers of mind and body, combines with them the tenderer and more emotional soul nature of the womanand sometimes to a remarkable degree". (26:135). Masculine dominance leads to extremes in harshness, Stoicism, asceticism, militarism, etc. Historically feminine dominance has not been given its chance to injure the race as has masucline but probably would find its extreme along the line of whimsical affectation; some think that in the 

United States we have approximately feminine dominance in our elementary schools. The main point to which we call attention is that sex is a very dominant factor in individual life and character. The differences between masculine and feminine do not imply superiority and inferiority but rather involve certain cognitive, emotional and dynamic tendencies of mental functioning. Whether we can as yet make a true list of these characteristics it is, nevertheless, true that the sex factor produces very distinct changes in the life of an individual. Sex is creative not only in the race but in the individual life,

Social Psychology II. The Direction of the Sex Impulse in the Interests of the Higher Forms of Individual Friendship. 

We consider (1) the desirability of sex sublimation; (2) forms of sublimation and resultant values. 

1. The Desirability of the Sublimation of the Sex 

Impulse. other basic impulses?

From what has been said it can be concluded that the sex impulse must have a prominent place in social relations. 

But whether that influence is for the elevation or degeneration of friendships depends largely 

How is sex related to upon the type of expression given the impulse of sex. Sex must assert great influence because it is so closely related to other impulses. As we have been saying throughout the text, all impulses differentiate from a basic life impulse without clear and definite distinctions. Probably it is not possible to make a clear-cut distinction between sex and self-assertion. Further, there is no necessity for a definite distinction as both are active in promoting the same ends. But sex emphasizes the more permanent element of individual creativeness; or perhaps we should say sex at its best inclines to emphasize this racial permanency. Self is assertive along lines of defense as well as construction. The fear and disgust impulses come to be important agencies of self-assertion along lines of defense. But along lines of creative assertiveness anger comes so readily to the aid of self-assertion and sex that it is practically impossible to determine at times when the more basic impulses are functioning alone and when supplemented by anger. Also it is obvious that the tendencies of sex and those of gregariousness overlap. But the social content impelled by sex is more personal and creative than that furthered by gregariousness. Gregariousness in itself seems satisfied with the mere grouping of individuals; the individuals when separ-

ated are restless and tend to group together but not necessarily to pay any attention to one another. Sex demands a more pronounced individual association and appropriation; the Platonic conception may make this an association of mind and think of the friend as an ideal but if sex is the motivating power the friend always is an individual and appropriated ideal. So we can say that between these associated impulses certain distinctions are possible but in the actual complex functioning of a human life there is so much overlapping and supplementing that in any particular case it may be practically impossible to distinguish and name a single impulse as a motivating force; for this reason we have been emphasizing the complex which lays the stress on the whole not on any of the parts. Reference to sex is likely to imply the presence of other kindred impulses.

In order to show the desirability of the sublimation of the sex impulse we point to certain dangers of the expression of the crude impulse. Certain social effects which are very undesirable accompany a crude or primitive expression of the sex impulse. Sex like any primitive appetite is likely 

What social dangers to seek its ends without regard to are involved in crude the best interests either of self or of others. This may lead to a resex expressions? production of offspring under very unfortunate social conditions; the parents may be unmarried, have no desire for the child, or have no ability to care for its physical, mental or moral needs. Or it may lead to attempts to gratify the impulse of sex through prostitution and other forms of promiscuity and thus produce accompanying antihygienic or antimoral effects. There is some disagreement concerning the exact status of venereal diseases and also concerning the best methods for their prevention but there is no disagreement as to their alarming prevalence nor as to their direct connection with

Social Psychology crude sex practices; it should be kept in mind that these diseases can be spread through various means and that the fact of such disease does not imply any moral or social offense. Also society is interested vitally in the institution of the brothel, the white slave and the supposed organization of the white slave trade. That the professional prostitute whether becoming such by her own decision or notis a slave of the worst type and as such a distinct social menace is coming to be recognized generally. We are not attempting to consider here the many historical, civic, social or moral questions connected with this deplorable situation. 

We are calling attention merely to the fact of the existence of such a situation and to the very obvious fact of its close connection with crude sex expression. 

Certain deplorable character effects are normal accompaniments of the primitive or crude expression of the sex impulse. Moral character consists mainly in a proper organization and unification of the basic life tendencies. But nothing endangers an organization of this type so much as the primitive and detached expression of a potent 

What dangers to moral character are involved in crude sex expression? impulse. Sex is one of the most powerful impulses in the life and when it comes to function as an outlaw to the organization its destructive power is very great. The assertion of self through the agency of volition is made increasingly hard in proportion to the extent of the detachment of the sex impulse from the hierarchy of the sentiments. Certainly it is true that a lot of foolish and false assertions have been made as to the destructive effects of masturbation; ordinarily it does not produce insanity and unless very excessive weaken materially the cognitive powers of mind. But the effects of masturbation, harlotry or other crude forms of sex expression, on self-respect and consequently upon self-assertion and the power of volition,

have not been overstated. There can be no more destructive form of "self-abuse" than a practice which lowers the self-respect of the individual. McDougall certainly is right in his contention that the power of volition comes from the instinctive impulses and through the sentiment of self-regard. A primitive form of sex expression does not need to injure the health or impair the cognitive powers in order to impair very seriously the functioning of the sentiment of self-regard. Any practice after the nature of a "secret sin" will do this. Further, the turning of the impulse of sex into wrong channels disorganizes the balance of the impulses entering into intuition, conscience or any form of affective judgment bearing on aesthetic or moral matters. 

Crude and distorted major premises of thought are developed. The lewd and vulgar imagination and thought on the subject of sex constitutes a rotten sore in many a character otherwise good. This is the primary source of the vulgar and profane in story, song or picture. The process of rationalization in self-defense causes the one who has such distorted conceptions of sex to read kindred conceptions into the words and actions of all others. Thus a vicious circle of vulgar imagination and crude sex expression gets started which soon becomes a major opponent to the better efforts of self to moralize the life. The difficulty of attaining desirable sex mastery is due mainly to wrong premises of thought and wrong forms of imagination concerning sex. This in turn usually goes back to some form of crude sex practice or to the assumption of such sex practice on the part of others, which may be due to a very defective sex education. There can be very little question concerning the truth of the assertion that crude sex practices have a very destructive effect on moral character. 

In general the best remedy for the dangers involved in the crude expression of sex is not its elimination or suppression but its sublimation. The ascetic vow of chastity

Social Psychology 

How avoid the dan. gers of the crude expression of sex? is not only the death sentence of the race but also constitutes the grounds for most of our wrong thinking on the subject of sex. That a religious vow should be taken against sex indicates that sex is thought of as something vicious and dangerous to the spiritual life of the individual. We are coming to see that sex is not only necessary racially but that it, when expressed properly, is wholesome individually and good morally. The suggestion of sublimation is that sex find expression on levels that preserve the racial, hygienic and moral values of all concerned. Its physical expression cannot be detached from its psychic. On the part of human beings its expression cannot be detached from an acceptance of responsibility both for the natural racial product and for the health and character of all those vitally concerned; in the case of the lower animals the absence of images and ideas makes it possible to consider sex only a vital physiological function. But in the case of human beings the sublimation of sex must mean its definite incorporation within the system of the self and its thoroughgoing subordination to the moral ends of the self. Thus it can be made to serve the real ends of friendship instead of hindering the development of such ends. The possible lines of sublimation and the resultant values need separate consideration. 

2. Forms of the Sublimated Sex Impulse and Resultant 

Values.

We include here: (1) the perceptual sublimation of sex; (2) its motor sublimation. Besides the possibility of its entering as a component factor into a larger organization, there are two ways in which human innate tendencies may be modified. These consist in what McDougall calls (90:-

429 

31) afferent and efferent modification. We consider first the afferent or perceptual form of modification. sublimation of sex?

The perceptual sublimation of the sex impulse means such modification of its afferent inlet as causes it to be stimulated by and only by a desirable form of percept; a percept is desirable when it is for the general social good What is the perceptual and for the good of moral character. This implies attraction to the more distinctive and worthy psychic attributes of the other sex; or if it be intrasex to desirable moral characteristics. Gentleness and care with details comes to attract the masculine initiative with its assertion of strength to the overcoming of obstacles. In like manner calm self-control coupled with intellectual wisdom, and youthful vision coupled with enthusiastic vigor to undertake new tasks, should be attractive mutually. 

Such lines of attraction are for the good of both parties and function to the strengthening of institutions and the social life of communities. Also perceptual sublimation of sex should cause, masculine and feminine alike to find attraction in worthy and permanent aesthetic qualities of life. 

Aesthetic manifestations of life may be either crude and superficial or worthy and lasting. A test of aesthetic sublimation is whether the individual is attracted by the beautiful in form and outline only, or by the beautiful in character and life primarily; it is true, of course, that a life beautiful in character may control a body beautiful in form and outline. But here as elsewhere there can be only one focal point of attention; either form or spirit must be in first place. And finally, as practically the whole literature on friendship is at one in insisting, the moral and idealistic elements of life must come to assert a positive attraction. 

Such attraction gives the best possible guarantee of the promotion by the impulse of sex of those tendencies of friendship which give it its most distinctive values. When

Social Psychology the power of sex can be gotten squarely back of the desire to promote the good in the character of another, then indeed will sex have lost its stigma and become the best guarantee of lasting and worthy friendship. 

What is the expressional sublimation of sex life?

Lastly we consider something of the implications of the efferent or expressional sublimation of sex. The main indication that sex has undergone sublimation on its expressional side is that it finds a factor of self. expression as rather than as a detached impulse which may or may not be in accord with the best tendencies of self. Ellen Key says that. "Whenever woman has taken the lead in erotic matters, man's love has been ennobled". (B78:13). This seems to be due to the fact, "That with women love usually proceeds from the soul to the senses and sometimes does not reach so far; that with man it usually proceeds from the senses to the soul and sometimes never completes the journey—this is for both the most painful of the existing distinctions between man and woman". (B78:51). One who comprehends fully the implications of the last quotation has made a large beginning in the way of sex education; the failure to recognize the fact stated here is an outstanding cause of the failure to make sex adjustment in marriage. The tendency for women to be attracted through the higher psychic realm gives her strong grounds for the claim that she is the natural guardian in the realm of sex. This perhaps is the meaning of the dual sex standard which holds women to a higher sex level but which gives her an equally high office for the control of social standards; but her libido must make its journey from the mystical and perhaps frigid regions of the psychic into the more highly colored areas of sense. This is entirely needful for sex adjustment between the sexes in marriage and should be thought of as a phase of feminine sublimation; frigidity ruins adjustment quite as

much as sensuality. But sex must come to be incorporated into self and for this reason before expression must be brought into the psychic realm; since it is already there in the feminine mind, Miss Key's conclusion that women should take the lead in erotic matters is justified. There are other reasons why the setting of high expressional standards in matters of sex should be entrusted to women. Her more pronounced rhythmic expression of the sex impulse sets desirable limits to sex expression under conditions of marriage. Her greater sacrifice in the production of offspring points to the right of a leading voice in matters of sex. But we are not to assume that racial reproduction is the single function of the sex impulse. If properly developed it can be made to constitute one of the most effective binding ties within the life of the home. Let it once develop worthy types of stimuli and at the same time be the channel of expression for the idealistic and aesthetic creativeness of self and we will find in sex a bond which holds together homes far more effectively than any possible divorce law. But to attain this, sex expression must find that adjustment between the psychic and sensuous to the end that neither is "bertayed" or its proper function impeded. If both the perceptual and expressional aspects of sex are sublimated properly it will make no more demands upon self-control than do hunger and thirst when these have developed righteousness as their object. Such sublimation should be the objective of sex education.

8. Has there been a "conspiracy of silence" concerning sex? Has it been a conscious conspiracy? Are there historical justifications for the sex taboo? Present day justifications? Should it be broken? Should the process be gradual? 

9. Carpenter says, "Our public opinion, our literature, our customs, our laws, are saturated with the notion of the uncleanness of sex, and are so making the conditions of its cleanness more and more difficult." (26:24).

Is this an overstatement? If such condition exists how do you account for it? Can it be remedied?

10. Galloway concludes: "Prostitution, therefore, either open or secret, is not a necessary institution". (A50: 

79). Does this mean that it can be eliminated?

Through what agencies do you think that the best progress can be made along this line? 

11. Gray says, "The function of the sexual elements in our physical frame is so central that unless they be truly managed health and strength are impossible". (55:xii). Is this actually a fact? Does "truly managed" mean consciously understood and managed? 

To what extent are these tendencies being rightly managed today? 

12. Ellen Key says, "Great love arises only when desire of a being of the other sex coalesces with the longing for a soul of one's own kind? (A78:74). Is this true? 

Does it mean that psychic attraction is to likeness only? Does it limit greatly the range of deep friendships?
